\title{Core-Sparse Monge Construction\\for Full Optimal Binary Search Trees\textsuperscript{*}}

\begin{document}
\maketitle
\begin{abstract}
We give an exact construction for optimal binary search trees with arbitrary
nonnegative integer weights on keys and on the gaps between them. For total
mass $W=O(n)$, a reduction to the core-sparse Monge product of Gawrychowski,
Gorbachev, and Kociumaka takes $O(n^{3/2}\log^{3/2}(n+1))$ arithmetic
operations and returns the complete tree. Terminal-penalty layers preserve
Monge structure; a mixed-weight subtree bound controls the number of layers,
and a dyadic boundary argument bounds every intermediate core. Charging
individual outcome weights to tiles bounds the sum of all layer cores.
Centering around separate constant key and gap baselines gives
$O(n(1+\sqrt R)\log^3(n+1))$ arithmetic work when their total nonnegative
deviation $R$ is polynomial in $n$.

A common lower-bound certificate also gives faster exact construction on
specified families. Independently, a common key/gap period $b$ permits
$O(nb)$ full construction by cyclic root amortization. A periodic middle of
length $m$ with $d=n-m$ arbitrary fringe keys admits $O(mb+nd)$ full
construction via Wilber's linear concave least-weight-subsequence primitive.
Positive affine translation of key and gap weights also gives $O(nb)$
construction, including geometric scaling and opposing arithmetic drifts.
A height bound charging disjoint ancestor increments to exceptional outcomes
turns the mixed run-vector construction into an unrestricted exact solver.
With a fixed number of bounded positive key runs and exceptional key/gap
weights, it takes $O(n^{\log_\varphi 2}\log(n+1))$ arithmetic work,
where $\varphi=(1+\sqrt5)/2$, independently of the exceptional magnitudes.
Compressing prefix vectors into valuation-class records removes explicit
perfect-tree expansion. With integer mass $W=O(n)$ and a fixed number of
normalized key runs and residual gaps, complete unrestricted construction
takes $O(n\log^4(n+1))$ arithmetic work, allowing zero-weight runs.
A fixed number of arbitrarily large integer outcomes preserves a bound
of $n$ times a fixed power of $\log(n+1)$ through their finite reward spectra.
We give the certificate once and derive the family
guarantees from it. Counterexamples delimit bounded-window relaxations,
endpoint-response composition, explicit density representations, and
additive separable transfers. Explicit prefix records can be exponential
even at the minimum optimal height. Separating each block's first entry
from its implicit prefix diagram gives $O(h(D+1))$ arithmetic and dictionary
operations per arbitrary mixed update, for height $h$ and input size $D$.
Including traceback and tree repair, complete construction takes
$O(h[n+\sum_kD_k])$ such operations. An explicit positive mixed family forces $\Omega(n^3)$
final diagram nodes and $\Omega(n^4)$ distinct nodes in shared prefix
history, even when blocks may share under positive affine transformations;
the optimum remains a directly certified right chain.
A contiguous odd ramp with total mass exactly $n$ has
$\Omega(n^{3/2})$ intermediate core entries with explicitly computed densities.
Thus the core exponent is necessary, up to a remaining logarithmic factor.
A separate positive ruler family has quadratic support at deviation $R=n^2$.
An adversarial integer-mass search and independent exact checks are reported
in an appendix. The unrestricted subquadratic problem remains open.
\end{abstract}

\section{Model and prior work}
\label{sec:legacy-1}

Weights are attached to fixed positions in key order; they need not be sorted.
Keys have fixed inorder positions $0,\ldots,n-1$ and nonnegative weights
$p_0,\ldots,p_{n-1}$. The $n+1$ gaps have weights $q_0,\ldots,q_n$.
A key at depth $d$ contributes $p(d+1)$; a gap at depth $d$ contributes $qd$.
Charging an extra comparison at each gap adds the tree-independent constant
$\sum q_i$.

For a half-open key interval $[i,j)$, write
\[
 W(i,j)=\sum_{r=i}^{j-1}p_r+\sum_{r=i}^{j}q_r,\qquad C(i,i)=0.
\]
The exact recurrence is
\[
 C(i,j)=W(i,j)+\min_{i\le r<j}\{C(i,r)+C(r+1,j)\}.
\]
Knuth's monotone-root optimization computes the table in $O(n^2)$
time~\cite{knuth}. The central obstacle here is the number of states, not
merely the cost per state. We retain both kinds of weights throughout.

Larmore gives subquadratic exact construction under restrictions on long
runs of very low frequency items, together with an approximation
tradeoff~\cite{larmore}. Karpinski, Larmore, and Rytter combine exact small
intervals with a bounded top-level construction~\cite{klr}.
All running-time bounds below count arithmetic operations and comparisons.
They are not bit-complexity bounds for unbounded integer weights.


For uniform key weights $a$ and uniform gap weights $b$, the external path
length equals $I+2n$, where $I$ is the internal path length. Thus
\[
 C(T)=(a+b)I+(a+2b)n.
\]
Every minimum-internal-path-length tree is optimal. Such a tree fills every
level above the last and is constructible in $O(n)$ time. When $a=b=0$,
every tree is optimal. Later baseline arguments use this exact identity.

\section{The main Monge construction}
\label{sec:legacy-19}

Let $n\ge1$, let all key and gap weights be nonnegative integers, and put
$W=\sum p_i+\sum q_i$ and $L=\lceil\log_2(n+1)\rceil$. Write $f(W)$ for
the first index with $F_{f(W)}>W$, where $F_0=0,F_1=1$. Set
\[
 H=\min\{n,f(W)+L\},\qquad M=(H+1)W+1.
\]
If every key weight is positive, $H=\min\{n,f(W)\}$ suffices.

\begin{theorem}[Integer-mass sparse-product reduction]\label{result:legacy-29}\label{thm:penalty-reduction}
Using the core-sparse Monge multiplication and witness algorithms
of~\cite{coremonge}, a full optimal BST can be constructed in
\[
 O\!\left(H\left[n+\min\{n^2,n\sqrt{WL}\}\right]\log(n+1)\right)
\]
arithmetic operations. Retaining the witness structures uses
$O(H[n+\min\{n^2,n\sqrt{WL}\}])$ words. In particular, $W=O(n)$ gives
$O(n^{3/2}\log^{3/2}(n+1))$ exact construction. Intermediate integers have
$O(\log(n+1)+\log(W+1))$ bits.
\end{theorem}
The dependence on numerical mass is essential to this statement. The
linear-mass case fits ordinary $O(\log n)$-bit arithmetic, but arbitrary
large integer weights do not give an unrestricted strongly subquadratic
bound. Knuth remains a fallback when the displayed bound is unfavorable.

\subsection{A full mixed-weight height bound}
\begin{lemma}[A sufficient mixed-weight subtree bound]\label{result:legacy-8}\label{lem:mass}
If $S$ is an internal subtree at edge depth $d$ in an optimal full OBST
of total outcome mass $W$, then
\[
 W(S)\le W/F_{d+1},\qquad F_1=F_2=1.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For a subtree $Z$, $W(Z)$ includes every key and external gap in it;
$Z$ may be a single gap. A one-level depth change changes cost by the
outcome weight for both kinds of outcomes. Write $v(L,R)$ for a key and
its two subtrees. Reflection reduces the straight and bent cases to the
following two transformations.

In the straight case, with internal grandchild $X$, rotate
\[
 v(y(X,B),D)\longrightarrow y(X,v(B,D)).
\]
Key $y$ and all outcomes of $X$ rise one level; key $v$ and all outcomes
of $D$ fall one level. Every outcome of $B$ keeps its depth. If
$O=p_v+W(D)$, the exact change is
\[
 O-p_y-W(X).
\]
Optimality implies $O\ge p_y+W(X)\ge W(X)$.

For the bent case write $X=x(B,C)$ and perform the double rotation
\[
 v(y(A,x(B,C)),D)\longrightarrow x(y(A,B),v(C,D)).
\]
The changes of edge depth, and hence of comparison length, are:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|rrrrrrr}
Outcome or subtree & $x$ & $B$ & $C$ & $v$ & $D$ & $y$ & $A$\\
\hline
Depth change & $-2$ & $-1$ & $-1$ & $+1$ & $+1$ & $0$ & $0$
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Every outcome in a listed subtree has the indicated change. Therefore
\[
 C_{\rm new}-C_{\rm old}
 =p_v+W(D)-2p_x-W(B)-W(C)
 =O-p_x-W(X).
\]
Optimality again gives $O\ge W(X)$. Both transformations preserve inorder
positions and every external gap; all other outcomes keep their depths.
These are transformations of complete trees.

Going upward from $S$, write $T_0=S,T_1,\ldots,T_d=T$. Nonnegativity
gives $W(T_1)\ge W(T_0)$, and the grandchild inequality gives
$W(T_i)\ge W(T_{i-1})+W(T_{i-2})$ for $i\ge2$.
Induction gives $W(T_d)\ge F_{d+1}W(S)$. Weak inequalities include
zero weights and ties.
\end{proof}
This lemma concerns internal subtrees. A heavy interior external gap can
have nearly all the mass at depth two; zero-key subtrees remain permitted.
The Fibonacci approach is prior work~\cite{hlm}; sharpness of this
particular sufficient bound is not claimed.


\subsection{An upper iteration that finishes in a bounded number of levels}\label{sec:legacy-19-1}
Initialize $A_0(i,j)=M(j-i)$ for $i\le j$, and iterate
\begin{align*}
 A_h(i,i)&=0,\\
 A_h(i,j)&=W(i,j)+\min_{i\le r<j}
       \{A_{h-1}(i,r)+A_{h-1}(r+1,j)\}\quad(i<j).
\end{align*}
This is an $h$-level partial search tree: explicit decisions incur the usual
interval mass, while each key still in an unfinished terminal interval
incurs penalty $M$. If $t$ keys are explicit, the cost is
\[
 M((j-i)-t)+D,\qquad 0\le D\le hW(i,j).
\]
At most $\min\{j-i,2^h-1\}$ keys can be explicit, and that number is
attainable with inorder labels. Since $M>HW$, every minimizer for
$h\le H$ uses that maximum number. Writing $c_h=2^h-1$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:penalty-residual}
 A_h(i,j)=M\max\{0,j-i-c_h\}+R_h(i,j),\qquad
 0\le R_h(i,j)\le hW(i,j).
\end{equation}
The residual $R_h$ is not asserted to be Monge.

The initial bound dominates every interval optimum: any complete tree on
$j-i$ keys costs at most $(j-i)W(i,j)\le M(j-i)$. The recurrence is
monotone, and $A_1\le A_0$ because $W(i,j)\le M$. Thus the iterates
decrease and remain at least $C$. Whenever an optimal full tree fits
within $h$ levels, it is feasible without a terminal penalty, so $A_h=C$.

The mixed Fibonacci mass lemma applies here to an \emph{actual optimal
full tree}. Every positive-mass internal subtree at depth $d$ satisfies
$F_{d+1}\le W$. Zero-mass subtrees can be replaced by balanced trees of
height at most $L$ without changing cost. Therefore some optimum has
height at most $f(W)+L$; the trivial bound $n$ also applies. When all keys
have positive integer weight every internal subtree has mass at least one,
so the extra $L$ is unnecessary. These arguments apply to every interval,
proving $A_H=C$ throughout the upper triangle.

\subsection{Finite Monge products, including the diagonal boundary}\label{sec:legacy-19-2}
For a triangular matrix $A$ with zero diagonal, define its interior density by
\[
 \Delta_A(i,j)=A(i,j+1)+A(i+1,j)-A(i,j)-A(i+1,j+1)
 \quad(0\le i<j<n).
\]
A \emph{core} lists nonzero densities. To use a finite matrix product, put
$P=4nM+4W+1$ and complete each layer by
\[
 E_h(i,j)=\begin{cases}A_h(i,j),&i\le j,\\P(i-j),&i>j.\end{cases}
\]
We prove by induction that $E_h$ is Monge. This is immediate for $E_0$.
Given the previous completion, multiply its first $n$ columns by its last
$n$ rows in the min-plus sense:
\[
 B_h(i,j)=\min_{0\le r<n}
       \{E_{h-1}(i,r)+E_{h-1}(r+1,j)\}.
\]
For $i<j$, every invalid root has a term at least $P$, whereas a valid
pair costs at most $nM$. Thus the finite product gives the restricted root
minimum exactly. The product is Monge. Adding $W(i,j)$, a row term plus
a column term, leaves density unchanged in squares entirely above the
diagonal. The only interior squares affected by resetting a diagonal entry
to zero have $j=i+1$. Their new density is
\[
 \min\{A_{h-1}(i,i+1),A_{h-1}(i+1,i+2)\}-q_{i+1}\ge0.
\]
For the first step both old singleton entries are $M$; thereafter each is
its singleton interval mass. The completion adds exactly the $n$ diagonal
densities $P+A_h(i,i+1)$; all densities below them vanish. This proves
Monge preservation, including the boundary cases. All entries are integers.

\subsection{A bound on every intermediate core}\label{sec:legacy-19-3}
Let $K_h$ be the number of nonzero interior densities of $A_h$.
A direct telescoping identity is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:core-mass}
 \sum_{i<j}\Delta_A(i,j)=A(0,n)-\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}A(i,i+1).
\end{equation}
For $h\ge L$, a balanced full tree is feasible, so $A_h(0,n)\le LW$.
Nonnegative integrality then gives $K_h\le LW$. This final-stage argument
alone would not bound the early layers, whose terminal penalties are large.

We first make the geometric step explicit, including non-power-of-two sizes.
\begin{lemma}[Dyadic boundary count]\label{lem:dyadic-boundary}
In the integer density grid $[0,n)^2$, retain cells with $i<j$ except
those on $m$ specified diagonals $j-i=d_t$. Embed the grid in
$[0,N)^2$, where $N$ is the least power of two at least $n$. Subdivide
aligned squares into four children, discard squares without retained
cells, and accept a square as soon as all its cells are retained.
At every dyadic side length $s$, the accepted squares number at most
$8(m+2)n/s$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The enclosing square contains the main diagonal, so it is not accepted.
Every accepted square has a parent of side $t=2s$ containing both a
retained and a forbidden cell. That parent either crosses $i=n$ or $j=n$,
contains a cell of $i=j$, or contains a cell of a removed diagonal.
These exhaust the reasons for subdivision. In a fixed row of side-$t$
parent squares, a diagonal's column indices form $t$ consecutive integers,
so it meets at most two parents. Thus each of the $m+1$ diagonals meets
at most $2N/t$ parents. Each outer boundary crosses at most $N/t$ parents;
if it is grid-aligned it crosses none. Each charged parent has at most
four accepted children. Their number is at most
\[
 4\{2(m+1)+2\}N/(2s)\le8(m+2)n/s.
\]
The boundaries at $n$ account for the padding when $n$ is not a power
of two. Forbidden unit cells are discarded. Every retained cell reaches
an accepted ancestor or is itself accepted, proving that these squares
partition the retained domain.
\end{proof}

For $1\le h<L$, the penalty term in~\eqref{eq:penalty-residual} has
density only on $j-i=c_h$. Account for that line using at most $n$ cells,
and apply Lemma~\ref{lem:dyadic-boundary} with $m=1$ to the remainder.
There are at most $24n/s$ accepted tiles of side $s$.

On a tile $[a,b)\times[c,d)$ the penalty density vanishes. Its density
sum telescopes to
\[
 R_h(a,d)+R_h(b,c)-R_h(a,c)-R_h(b,d)\le2hW.
\]
All densities in this tile are nonnegative integers. Negative residual
densities can occur on the removed line and are not used. The number
of nonzero entries in the tile is at most $\min\{s^2,2hW\}$. Therefore
\[
 K_h=O\!\left(n+\sum_{s\text{ dyadic}}\frac ns
                         \min\{s^2,2hW\}\right)
     =O(n+n\sqrt{hW}),
\]
capped by $O(n^2)$. Splitting the geometric sum at $s\asymp\sqrt{hW}$
introduces no extra logarithm. For $W=0$, only the removed-line entries
can remain. Combining the early and late bounds, including the $n$
completion entries, yields
\begin{equation}\label{eq:penalty-core-bound}
 \delta(E_h)=O\!\left(n+\min\{n^2,n\sqrt{WL}\}\right)
 \qquad(0\le h\le H).
\end{equation}
For completeness, the early-layer argument gives the explicit bound
\[
 \delta(E_h)\le 2n+96n\sqrt{2hW}\qquad(1\le h<L,\ W>0),
\]
since $\sum_{s\text{ dyadic}}\min(s,t^2/s)\le4t$ for $t\ge1$.
For $W=0$ only the exceptional and completion diagonals can contribute.
Every layer also has at most $n(n+1)/2$ completed core entries. The constants
are conservative; the sharper geometric count is retained in the proof.
This controls representation size without charging the large value of $M$
as though it represented many separate core entries.

\subsection{Compact computation and full reconstruction}\label{sec:legacy-19-4}
The precise published interface is as follows. Theorem 1.2 of
Gawrychowski, Gorbachev, and Kociumaka~\cite{coremonge}, p.~74:3, states:
\begin{quote}
There is a (deterministic) algorithm that, given the condensed
representations of a $p\times q$ Monge matrix $A$ and a $q\times r$
Monge matrix $B$, in time
$O(p+q+r+(\delta(A)+\delta(B))\log(1+\delta(A)+\delta(B)))$
computes the condensed representation of $A\otimes B$.
\end{quote}
This quotation is reproduced under the paper's CC BY 4.0 license.
The following definitions and guarantees specify the interface used here.
\begin{itemize}
\item Definition 2.5 uses precisely our density sign and defines the core
as triples $(i,j,\Delta_A(i,j))$ with nonzero density. Its size $\delta(A)$
counts triples, regardless of their magnitudes.
\item Definition 3.2 stores the topmost row, leftmost column, and core.
This is exactly the representation used here.
\item Theorem 1.1 bounds the product core by
$2(\delta(A)+\delta(B))$.
\item Theorem 4.7, p.~74:11, adds within the same construction time a
witness structure occupying $O(p+q+r+\delta(A)+\delta(B))$ space, with
$O(\log(2+\delta(A)+\delta(B)))$ query time. Definition 2.3 specifies
the smallest minimizing index, so ties are covered.
\item Fact 4.1 supplies logarithmic-time entry queries after preprocessing
from the condensed representation.
\end{itemize}
Here the operands have dimensions $(n+1)\times n$ and $n\times(n+1)$.
Every entry is a finite integer: the lower-triangle padding is $P(i-j)$,
not infinity. Invalid roots have value at least $P>nM$, whereas a valid
root pair costs at most $nM$. Thus the reduction uses neither extended-real
entries nor an unstated triangular version of the published theorem.

Use them on the two contiguous submatrices of $E_{h-1}$. To obtain the
next completion, retain the product core strictly above the first
superdiagonal, where adding interval mass has zero density. Recompute the
$O(n)$ diagonal and first-superdiagonal core entries, discard lower entries,
and recover the top row and left column. Filtering and $O(n)$ logarithmic
entry queries take $O((n+\delta)\log(n+1))$ work, including coordinate
sorting if needed. The product's temporary core is also bounded linearly
by the two input cores, by Theorem~1.1 of~\cite{coremonge}. No step
first enumerates a dense interval table.

Retain the product witness oracle for every layer. Starting from $(0,n,H)$,
retrieve a root and recurse on its two intervals at layer $H-1$.
There are $n$ nonempty calls. An unfinished nonempty interval at layer zero
would incur at least $M$, but the optimum is at most $LW<M$, since
$H\ge L$. Thus none survives; when $H=n$, completeness also follows from
depth alone. Witness recovery costs an additional $O(n\log(n+1))$.
Equation~\eqref{eq:penalty-core-bound}, summed over $H$ layers, proves
the general bound in Theorem~\ref{thm:penalty-reduction}, including
dimensions, representation conversion, storage, and reconstruction.
Section~\ref{sec:aggregate-cores} proves its sharper linear-mass clause. Since $H\le n$ and the penalties
are polynomial in $n,W$, the stated bit-length bound follows as well.


\subsection{A linear-mass family with \texorpdfstring{$\Omega(n^{3/2})$}{Omega(n to the power 3/2)} intermediate core}
\label{sec:ramp-tightness}
A contiguous ramp surrounded by zero-weight keys supplies a lower bound
on the representation size. We first identify the exact optimization
problem inside its height-limited intervals.

\begin{lemma}[Perfect-slot representation for zero gap weights]
\label{lem:perfect-slots}
If every gap weight is zero and $k\le c=2^h-1$, the optimal height-at-most-$h$
key cost equals the minimum over all ordered $k$-slot subsets of the perfect
$c$-key tree, assigning the input keys to those slots in order. The comparison
length of its slot $r$ is $h-\nu_2(r)$, $1\le r\le c$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Every tree of height at most $h$ occupies an ancestor-closed subset of the
perfect tree's slots, proving one inequality. Conversely, start from any
$k$ selected slots and delete all unselected keys by ordinary BST deletion.
For a node with two children, move its inorder successor to that node and
splice out the successor from its old position; otherwise splice out the
node directly. Every remaining key keeps or decreases its depth. Inorder
order is preserved. With nonnegative key weights and zero gap weights,
the resulting feasible tree costs no more than the selected-slot assignment.
This proves the opposite inequality. The ruler formula for a perfect tree's
inorder depths follows by its recursive halving.
\end{proof}

Let $v_u=\nu_2(u+1)$ for zero-based ruler positions $u$, and let $D$ bound
these coefficients. They alternate between zero and a positive integer.
For any consecutive segment of length $\ell$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ruler-segment-mass}
 \sum v_u\le \ell+D,
\end{equation}
because there are at most $\ell/2^d+1$ multiples of $2^d$ in the segment,
and $v_u=\sum_{d=1}^D[2^d\mid u+1]$.
Define the odd-ramp subsequence score
\[
 f_m(a,b)=\max_{a\le t_1<\cdots<t_m<b}
                    \sum_{r=1}^m(2r-1)v_{t_r}.
\]

\begin{lemma}[Rightmost-zero deletion]\label{lem:rightmost-zero}
If $b-a=m+s$ and $m>2s+D$, an optimum for $f_m(a,b)$ deletes the
rightmost $s$ zero coefficients and retains every other position.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For $s=0$ every position is retained. For $s>0$, examine the right endpoint.
If its coefficient is zero, some optimum omits it: replace a selected final
zero by an earlier omitted position. The inserted coefficient is nonnegative,
and the selected coefficients it passes receive larger odd multipliers.

If the final coefficient is positive, every optimum selects it. Otherwise
there is a selected zero, since the window has fewer than $m$ positive
positions. Let $u$ be the last selected zero. Every zero after $u$ is
omitted, and the final positive coefficient uses one more omission. As
zeros alternate, the distance from $u$ to the final position is at most
$2s-1$. By~\eqref{eq:ruler-segment-mass}, the selected coefficients after
$u$ have sum at most $2s-1+D$. Replace that selected zero by the final
positive coefficient. The latter receives multiplier $2m-1$; each selected
coefficient after $u$ loses two in its multiplier. The gain is at least
\[
 (2m-1)-2(2s-1+D)>0,
\]
a contradiction. Thus retain the final positive coefficient and omit its
preceding zero while omissions remain. Removing this pair changes $(m,s)$
to $(m-1,s-1)$ and preserves the required inequality. An initial zero
omission only strengthens it. Continue until $s=0$.
\end{proof}

The rule yields an exact density, rather than only a count. Suppose
$b-a=m+s$, $s\ge1$, $b$ is odd, and $m>2(s+1)+D$, so it applies to all
four windows below. Appending the positive coefficient $v_b$ deletes the
additional zero at $b-2s-1$. The $s$ retained positive coefficients after
that zero each lose two in their multiplier. Hence
\[
 f_m(a,b+1)-f_m(a,b)
  =(2m-1)v_b-2\sum_{r=1}^s v_{b-2r}.
\]
After deleting the left endpoint, the same sum has $s-1$ terms. Subtracting
these two increments gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ramp-score-density}
 f_m(a,b+1)+f_m(a+1,b)-f_m(a,b)-f_m(a+1,b+1)
       =-2\nu_2(b-2s+1)<0.
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Linear-mass intermediate-core lower bound]
\label{thm:ramp-core-lower}
Let $m=2^t$, $t\ge5$, $n=m^2$, $z=(n-m)/2$, and $h=2t-1$.
Set every gap weight to zero, and set
\[
 p_{z+r-1}=2r-1\quad(1\le r\le m),\qquad p_i=0\text{ otherwise}.
\]
Then $W=n$, and the terminal-penalty layer $A_h$ has at least
\[
 \frac{nm}{16}-\frac{9n}{64}-\frac m4
       \ \ge\ \frac{n^{3/2}}{32}
\]
strictly positive interior densities. Their values have the explicit formula
in~\eqref{eq:ramp-cost-density} below.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Put $c=2^h-1=n/2-1$. Every interval of at most $c$ keys is fully exposed
in layer $h$, so Lemma~\ref{lem:perfect-slots} applies. An interval
$[i,j)$ containing the entire ramp has $z-i$ leading zero keys and
$j-z-m$ trailing zero keys. Its positive keys can occupy any $m$ slots in
\[
 [a,b)=[z-i,\ c-j+z+m).
\]
The preceding and following zero keys fit exactly when the positive slots
lie in this window. Since the interval's mass is $W=n$,
\[
 A_h(i,j)=hn-f_m(a,b).
\]
Write $s=c-(j-i)$; then $b-a=m+s$.
The four original intervals defining a density correspond to the four score
windows based at $(a-1,b-1)$. Equation~\eqref{eq:ramp-score-density} gives,
when $b$ is even,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ramp-cost-density}
 \Delta_{A_h}(i,j)=2\nu_2(b-2s)\ge2.
\end{equation}

Specifically, take $s=1,\ldots,m/4$ and
$a=1,\ldots,c-m-s-1$ with $b=a+m+s$ even; then set
$i=z-a$ and $j=c-b+z+m$. These are valid, distinct density cells, and
all four intervals contain the ramp and have at most $c$ keys. The ruler
coefficients are bounded by $D=h-1=2t-2$. For $t\ge5$,
\[
 m>2(m/4+1)+D,
\]
so the deletion rule applies to all the windows used.
At each $s$ there are exactly $\lfloor(c-m-s)/2\rfloor$ permitted values
of $a$. With $u=m/4$ even, their sum is
\[
 \sum_{s=1}^{u}\left\lfloor\frac{c-m-s}{2}\right\rfloor
 =\frac{u(c-m-1)}2-\frac{u^2}4
 =\frac{nm}{16}-\frac{9n}{64}-\frac m4.
\]
For $m\ge32$ this is at least $nm/32$.
\end{proof}

All counted cells use fully exposed intervals and avoid the penalty diagonal.
Their growth is therefore independent of the numerical size of the terminal penalty.
Consequently a uniform $O(n\,\operatorname{polylog}n)$ bound on every
intermediate explicit core is impossible even at $W=n$. The polynomial
exponent $3/2$ in the upper bound is necessary for this representation;
a factor $\sqrt{\log n}$ remains between the core bounds. The lower bound
concerns a layer's density list, not the complexity of constructing an
optimal tree by a different representation. The original positive ruler
family below supplies the complementary quadratic-support result at
centered deviation $R=n^2$.

\subsection{Charging individual outcomes across all layers}
\label{sec:aggregate-cores}
The maximum core size need not be paid at every layer. The exceptional
penalty diagonal has distance $c_h=2^h-1$ from the main diagonal. Its
geometric growth allows a sharper sum of the representation sizes.

\begin{lemma}[Per-outcome tile overlap]\label{lem:outcome-overlap}
Let $N$ be a power of two and $0<c<N$. Partition the integer cells
$0\le i<j<N$, except $j-i=c$, into maximal dyadic squares avoiding both
$j-i=0$ and $j-i=c$. For a square
$T=[a,a+s)\times[b,b+s)$, let $J_T=[a,b+s]$ be its inclusive span of
outcome positions. At each side length $s$ there are at most $12N/s$
squares. Each position belongs to at most $60(1+c/s)$ spans $J_T$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The whole square meets the main diagonal. Thus every accepted square has
a rejected parent of side $2s$, with lower-left coordinates $(A,B)$.
The parent cannot lie outside the upper-triangular domain, since it
contains the accepted child. Its rejection therefore implies that it
meets a line $j-i=d$ for $d\in\{0,c\}$. In integer coordinates,
\[
 |B-A-d|<2s,\qquad |b-a-d|<3s,
\]
since each child coordinate differs from its parent's by either $0$ or
$s$. For a fixed row coordinate $a$ and a fixed $d$, at most six
multiples of $s$ can be the column coordinate $b$. There are $N/s$
row coordinates and two lines, giving $12N/s$ squares.

If $x\in J_T$, then $a\le x\le b+s<a+d+4s$. Hence $a$ is a multiple
of $s$ in an interval of length $d+4s$, with at most $d/s+5$ choices.
Multiplying by six column choices and summing over $d=0,c$ gives
$60+6c/s\le60(1+c/s)$. Counting a square under both lines only enlarges
the bound. Inclusive endpoints cover every gap weight, and also bound
the incidences of key weights, whose right endpoint is excluded.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Aggregate core bound]\label{thm:aggregate-cores}
For the layers of Theorem~\ref{thm:penalty-reduction},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:aggregate-cores}
 S:=\sum_{h=0}^H\delta(E_h)
 =O\!\left(nH+n\sqrt{WL}+WL^3+HWL\right).
\end{equation}
The complete construction takes $O(S\log(n+1))$ arithmetic operations,
and the retained succinct witness structures occupy $O(S)$ words.
In particular, if $W=O(n)$, these bounds are respectively
$O(n^{3/2}\log^{3/2}(n+1))$ and $O(n^{3/2}\sqrt{\log(n+1)})$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For the early layers $1\le h<L$, pad the input for analysis to
$N=2^{\lceil\log_2 n\rceil}$ keys, where $n\le N<2n$. Append zero key
and gap weights, retaining the original $q_n$ at position $n$.
Keep the same penalty $M$ and the same layer index. Every interval
contained in $[0,n]$ has exactly its original recurrence and value.
No assertion about the optimal height of the padded full input is needed.
The proof of~\eqref{eq:penalty-residual} applies on every padded interval:
its mass is at most $W$ and $M>hW$. Thus its residual is between zero
and $h$ times its own interval mass, and its layer is Monge.

Apply Lemma~\ref{lem:outcome-overlap} to the padded grid with $c=c_h$.
There is no outer cut through a tile at $n$: the entire padded tile is
used for the bound, even if only part of it lies in the original grid.
For a tile $T$ of side $s$, the two positive corners in the telescoping
density sum are residuals on subintervals of $J_T$. Its density sum is
therefore at most $2hW(J_T)$. Its number of positive integer densities
is at most both this sum and $s^2$. Charging each key and gap weight
separately to its covering spans gives
\[
 \sum_{T:\,\operatorname{side}(T)=s} W(J_T)
 \le60W(1+c_h/s).
\]
Writing $K_{h,s}$ for the number of nonzero original densities covered
by these tiles, we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:outcome-scale}
 K_{h,s}\le\min\{24ns,120hW(1+c_h/s)\}.
\end{equation}
The original exceptional line and completion diagonal add at most $2n$
entries. There are at most $L$ dyadic scales. Using
$\min(x,y+z)\le\min(x,y)+\min(x,z)$ and summing geometrically yields
\begin{align*}
 \delta(E_h)
 &\le 2n+120\sum_s\min\{ns,hW(1+c_h/s)\}\\
 &=O\!\left(n+hWL+\sqrt{nhWc_h}\right).
\end{align*}
Indeed, the $hW$ term contributes at most $hWL$, while
$\sum_s\min\{ns,hWc_h/s\}\le4\sqrt{nhWc_h}$.
If $hWc_h<n$, the latter inequality follows directly from
$\sum_s hWc_h/s\le2hWc_h$; otherwise split at
$s=\sqrt{hWc_h/n}$. Zero mass makes both terms zero.

Now $2^L\le2(n+1)\le4n$, and
\[
 \sum_{h=1}^{L-1}\sqrt{h\,c_h}
 \le\sqrt L\sum_{h=1}^{L-1}2^{h/2}
 =O(\sqrt{Ln}).
\]
The early layers consequently contribute
$O(nL+WL^3+n\sqrt{WL})$. For $h\ge L$, the earlier balanced-tree
argument bounds each completed core by $n+LW$; the initial core has
$n$ entries. Since $H\ge L$, summing proves~\eqref{eq:aggregate-cores}.

The product, representation conversion, and witness construction at
layer $h$ cost $O((n+\delta(E_{h-1}))\log(n+1))$. The product core is
bounded by its input cores. The $n$ root queries add $O(n\log(n+1))$,
and each completed core already has $n$ completion entries. Hence the
total time is $O(S\log(n+1))$. The per-product succinct witness space
is $O(n+\delta(E_{h-1}))$, so retaining all such structures costs $O(S)$.
This storage statement is for the published succinct witness structures;
it does not assert the same space for an arbitrary range-query implementation.

Finally $W=O(n)$ implies $H=O(L)$. Equation~\eqref{eq:aggregate-cores}
then gives $S=O(n^{3/2}\sqrt L+nL^3)=O(n^{3/2}\sqrt L)$, using
$L^{5/2}=O(\sqrt n)$. This proves the sharper linear-mass clause in
Theorem~\ref{thm:penalty-reduction}. The general maximum-core bound
remains available whenever it is smaller.
\end{proof}

\subsection{A full mixed-weight slot representation}
\label{sec:mixed-slots}
The key-only ruler subsequence extends to weighted gaps by retaining a
pair term. For this subsection, index keys by $1,\ldots,n$ and gaps by
$0,\ldots,n$. Fix a height $h$ with $c=2^h-1\ge n$, put $r=c-n$, and
write $v(t)=\nu_2(t)$ for $1\le t\le c$.

\begin{theorem}[Mixed slot representation]\label{thm:mixed-slots}
The minimum full mixed cost among BSTs of height at most $h$ equals
$hW$ minus the maximum, over all $1\le t_1<\cdots<t_n\le c$, of
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mixed-slot-score}
 \sum_{i=1}^n p_i v(t_i)+q_0v(t_1)+q_nv(t_n)
       +\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}q_i\min\{v(t_i),v(t_{i+1})\}.
\end{equation}
The selected slots need not be ancestor-closed. A minimizing slot
selection can be converted to an optimal height-limited tree in $O(c+n)$
operations. The weights may be arbitrary nonnegative reals in the exact
arithmetic model.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A slot $t$ of the perfect tree has comparison length $\ell(t)=h-v(t)$.
A gap between adjacent keys has depth equal to the maximum of their
comparison lengths; the endpoint gaps have lengths equal to their
neighboring key. The internal-gap identity is Lemma~1 of Becker~\cite{becker}.
Thus every actual height-at-most-$h$ tree embeds into the perfect tree
and has cost exactly $hW$ minus~\eqref{eq:mixed-slot-score} for its slots.

Conversely, select any $n$ slots and delete all unselected keys from the
perfect BST. Ordinary BST deletion never increases a surviving key's
depth: a successor copied into a deleted node moves upward, and any
spliced child subtree also moves upward. Inorder order is preserved.
The gap-depth identity then shows that every resulting gap depth is at
most the maximum of its neighboring selected-slot comparison lengths.
Nonnegative weights imply that the repaired tree's mixed cost is at most
the slot expression. These two directions prove equality of the minima.

For conversion, store parent and child pointers, inorder threads, and
the current node location for every original slot label. Delete
unselected labels in increasing order. A two-child
deletion obtains its successor from a thread, copies its label, updates
that label's location, and splices out the successor. Each deletion changes
only constantly many pointers. Building the perfect tree, performing
$c-n$ deletions, and writing the output take $O(c+n)$ operations.
For an optimal slot sequence, a strict cost decrease during repair would
contradict the equality of minima just proved.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Capacity-deficit construction]\label{thm:slot-deficit}
With $r=2^h-1-n\ge0$, an optimal height-at-most-$h$ mixed-weight BST
can be constructed in $O(n(r+1))$ arithmetic operations and words,
including all preprocessing and full reconstruction.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Write $t_i=i+y_i$. The exact feasible sequences are
$0\le y_1\le\cdots\le y_n\le r$. Let $D_i(y)$ be the best prefix
reward ending at $y_i=y$, including gaps $q_0,\ldots,q_{i-1}$. Then
\begin{align*}
 D_1(y)&=(p_1+q_0)v(1+y),\\
 D_i(y)&=p_iv(i+y)+\max_{0\le z\le y}
       \{D_{i-1}(z)+q_{i-1}\min(v(i-1+z),v(i+y))\}.
\end{align*}
Finish with $\max_y\{D_n(y)+q_nv(n+y)\}$. There are $n(r+1)$ states.

Within row $i$, sweep $y$ increasingly and insert predecessor $z=y$.
Maintain $B_d$, the largest inserted predecessor value of valuation $d$,
and $S_e=\max_{d\ge e}B_d$, with attaining predecessors; unpopulated
levels are omitted. For $e=v(i+y)$,
the transition maximum is
\[
 \max\left\{\max_{d<e}(B_d+q_{i-1}d),\ S_e+q_{i-1}e\right\}.
\]
Inserting a value of valuation $d$ updates $B_d$ and $S_0,\ldots,S_d$.
The query scans the $e$ lower levels and reads $S_e$ once. Generation
tags reset these arrays logically between rows, so no length-$h$ clearing
pass is required per row. The state costs
$O(1+v(i-1+y)+v(i+y))$ operations.

Across the entire table, a slot index occurs at most $\min(n,r+1)$
times in either valuation sum. Since
\[
 \sum_{t=1}^{c}v(t)=c-h<c,\qquad
 \min(n,r+1)c\le2n(r+1),
\]
all scans and updates take $O(n(r+1))$ operations. The second inequality
follows by considering $r+1\le n$ and $r+1>n$. Tabulating valuations,
initializing the arrays, and processing the first and last rows fit the
same bound, since $c=n+r\le n(r+1)$.

Store one predecessor per state and trace back the slots. Apply
Theorem~\ref{thm:mixed-slots}'s $O(c+n)$ repair procedure. All arrays,
traceback records, and the reconstructed tree fit in $O(n(r+1))$ words.
\end{proof}

Becker~\cite{becker} already gives a quadratic construction when the
height limit is a fixed additive constant above the minimum. The bound
here exposes the capacity deficit: constant $r$ gives linear work, while
$r=\Theta(n)$ gives quadratic work. A prescribed height remains a
constraint; unrestricted optimality requires a sufficient height bound
or a separate certificate.
For example, $p=(100,1,1,1)$ and $q=(1,1,1,1,1)$ have an unrestricted
optimum of cost $121$: the first key must be the root, since placing it
lower already costs at least $200$, and its uniform three-key right
subtree is balanced. This tree has height three. Every minimum-internal-
path-length four-key tree puts that first key below the root, so the
height-limited class includes necessary shapes beyond that earlier
certificate family.

\paragraph{The complementary penalty regime.}
For an interval with $n\ge c$, maximum exposure produces a perfect
$c$-key skeleton, and every gap reaches depth $h$. Consequently, when
$M>hW$, its terminal-penalty value is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mixed-slots-above}
 A_h(0,n)=M(n-c)+hW-
 \max_{1\le t_1<\cdots<t_c\le n}\sum_{j=1}^{c}p_{t_j}v(j).
\end{equation}
The feasibility of selecting the skeleton keys is exactly their increasing
order; remaining keys lie in its terminal intervals. At $n=c$ this agrees
with~\eqref{eq:mixed-slot-score}, since adjacent ruler valuations have
minimum zero and both endpoint valuations are zero.
In particular, ordinary density cells strictly above $j-i=c$ are
independent of all gap weights: the gap contribution $hW(i,j)$ and the
penalty's affine length term both have zero density there. Mixed gap/key
interactions remain in the fully exposed region and on the boundary.

\subsection{Exact gap-only mass and a sharp binary control}
\label{sec:gap-core-identity}
The mixed representation separates two sources of core growth. The following
identity settles the binary gap-only control case and identifies where its
argument uses the absence of key weights. Write $D_h(i,j)$ for the ordinary
density of $A_h$ at $(i,j)$.

\begin{lemma}[Gap-only core mass]\label{lem:gap-core-mass}
Let $p_i=0$, $h\ge1$, $c=2^h-1\le n$, and $M>hW$. For $0\le i<n-c$, put
\[
 Q_i=\max\{q_{i+1},q_{i+3},\ldots,q_{i+c}\}.
\]
Densities strictly above $j-i=c$ vanish, and those on that line equal
$M-Q_i>0$. The density mass strictly below the line is exactly
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gap-core-mass}
 S_h=(h-2)W+q_0+q_n+\sum_{i=0}^{n-c-1}Q_i.
\end{equation}
For integer gaps, let $X_h=\sum_{j-i<c}\max\{D_h(i,j)-1,0\}$. Then
\[
 \delta(E_h)=2n-c+S_h-X_h.
\]
In particular, binary gaps satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:binary-gap-core}
 \delta(E_h)\le3n-2c+(h-2)W+q_0+q_n.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A length-$c$ interval has the forced perfect tree and cost $h$ times its
gap mass. At length $c-1$, Theorem~\ref{thm:mixed-slots} selects all but
one perfect slot. Removing an even slot yields no gap reward. Removing
an odd slot yields one reward, the corresponding even-indexed gap's weight:
the neighboring valuations have minimum one, or the endpoint neighbor
has valuation one. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gap-one-slot}
 A_h(a,a+c-1)=h\sum_{t=a}^{a+c-1}q_t
       -\max\{q_a,q_{a+2},\ldots,q_{a+c-1}\}.
\end{equation}
When $h=1$, the empty interval gives $q_a-q_a=0$ directly.
Equation~\eqref{eq:mixed-slots-above} gives $M(k-c)+hW(i,j)$ at
length $k\ge c$. Four-corner subtraction now gives zero above the line
and $M-Q_i$ on it.

For each $i<n-c$, summing below-line densities in that row telescopes to
\[
 \sum_{j=i+1}^{i+c-1}D_h(i,j)
 =A_h(i,i+c)-A_h(i+1,i+c)-A_h(i,i+1)
 =hq_i+Q_i-(q_i+q_{i+1}).
\]
The remaining triangular corner has mass
\[
 A_h(n-c,n)-\sum_{i=n-c}^{n-1}(q_i+q_{i+1}).
\]
Its full interval has length $c$ and hence perfect-tree cost. Adding these
expressions proves~\eqref{eq:gap-core-mass}, including $c=1$ and $c=n$.
For integer weights, below-line support is $S_h-X_h$; the capacity line
contributes $n-c$ entries and the completion contributes $n$. Finally,
$Q_i\le1$ proves~\eqref{eq:binary-gap-core}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Sharp leading term for fixed-mass binary gaps]
\label{thm:binary-gap-leading}
Let $n=2^s$, $s\ge4$. Over all inputs with zero key weights, binary gap
weights, and $W=n/2$, the largest completed core over all layers satisfies
\[
 \frac{sn}{2}+1
 \ \le\ \max_{q,h}\delta(E_h)
 \ \le\ \frac{(s+1)n}{2}+4.
\]
In particular, this maximum is $\tfrac12n\log_2 n+O(n)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For $1\le h\le s$, use~\eqref{eq:binary-gap-core} and $q_0+q_n\le2$.
The resulting upper bound $U(h)=3n-2(2^h-1)+(h-2)n/2+2$ satisfies
$U(h+1)-U(h)=n/2-2^{h+1}$. Its maximum occurs at $h=s-2,s-1$ and
is $(s+1)n/2+4$. Layer zero has core $n$. For $h\ge s+1$, a perfect
$(n-1)$-key skeleton plus one bottom cherry has gap cost at most $sW+2$.
Whole-triangle telescoping gives ordinary density mass
$A_h(0,n)-\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(q_i+q_{i+1})$. Integer densities therefore
give $\delta(E_h)\le n+(s-2)W+q_0+q_n+2\le sn/2+4$.

For the lower bound, put $q_i=1$ for even $i<n$, and set every other gap
weight, including $q_n$, to zero. This has mass exactly $n/2$. Take
$h=s+1$. An interval avoiding $q_n$ has alternating gap weights. By the
parity contraction bound of Lemma~\ref{result:legacy-40}, its optimum is
\[
 F(i,k)=tQ+(k+1-2^t),\qquad t=\lfloor\log_2(k+1)\rfloor,
\]
where $k$ is its key count and $Q$ its positive-gap count. A minimum-path-
length tree attains it within height $h$. For $k\ge1$ and $i+k+1\le n-1$,
its four-corner difference is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:binary-gap-densities}
 D_h(i,i+k)=
 \begin{cases}
 1,&i\text{ even and }k=2^d-1\text{ for some }d\ge1,\\
 0,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
Indeed, away from $k=2^d-2,2^d-1$, the logarithms agree and both the
mass and residual terms cancel. At $k=2^d-2$, the longest interval has
$2^d$ alternating gaps, exactly $2^{d-1}$ positive, which cancels its
logarithm jump. At $k=2^d-1$, subtraction gives $2^{d-1}-Q_{\rm short}$,
equal to one for even $i$ and zero for odd $i$. This includes $d=1$,
where the shortest interval is empty with cost zero.

For each $1\le d\le s-1$, equation~\eqref{eq:binary-gap-densities}
counts $(n-2^d)/2$ positive entries. Summing gives $(s-2)n/2+1$;
adding the $n$ completion entries proves the lower bound.
\end{proof}

This establishes the binary gap-only asymptotic by explicit upper and lower
proofs. The fixed-mass search in Appendix~\ref{app:binary-core} supplies
finite controls. Key weights change the saturated perfect-skeleton cost,
so the telescoping identity does not extend unchanged to the full mixed
objective. The odd-ramp theorem already rules out an $O(n\log n)$ bound
for all linear-mass mixed inputs.

\subsection{Computing all intervals in a capacity band}
\label{sec:capacity-band}
The capacity boundary can also be evaluated directly across every interval
start. This complements the single-instance slot construction.

\begin{theorem}[Exact capacity bands]\label{thm:capacity-band}
Fix an integer $d\ge0$ and $M>HW$. For $h=0,\ldots,H$, all terminal-penalty
values with $|j-i-c_h|\le d$, where $c_h=2^h-1$, and minimizing
roots for $h\ge1$ can be computed in $O(n(d+1)(H+1))$ arithmetic operations and words.
Every retained fully exposed interval has a complete tree witness that can
be reconstructed in time linear in its key count. For $d\ge1$, these values
also give all ordinary densities with $|j-i-c_h|\le d-1$ within the same bound.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
At layer $h$, put $a=c_{h-1}$. For a parent of length $k$, write the child
lengths as $u,v$, where $u+v=k-1$. Maximum exposure is forced by $M>hW$.
If $k\le c_h$, it requires $u,v\le a$, giving the feasible range
\[
 \max(0,k-1-a)\le u\le\min(a,k-1).
\]
The nonnegative deficits $a-u,a-v$ sum to $c_h-k\le d$, so both children
belong to the preceding band. If $k\ge c_h$, maximum exposure instead
requires $u,v\ge a$, or $a\le u\le k-1-a$. Their excesses sum to
$k-c_h\le d$, again keeping both children in the preceding band.
Every split in these ranges attains maximum exposure. Thus the restricted
recurrence equals the full recurrence on every stored state. Initialize
$A_0(i,i+k)=Mk$ for $k\le d$, with empty intervals zero.

At each layer, process lengths from $k_{\min}=\max(1,c_h-d)$ through
$k_{\max}=\min(n,c_h+d)$. The first diagonal examines every feasible
split, at most $d+1$ roots per interval, costing $O(n(d+1))$.
For subsequent diagonals, intersect the feasible range with the monotone
leftmost-root bounds
\[
 R_h(i,j-1)\le r\le R_h(i+1,j).
\]
These roots are already available on the preceding length diagonal.
For completeness, if $r<s$, increasing $j$ increases the difference
between their candidate costs by the Monge inequality for $A_{h-1}$
on rows $r+1,s+1$. Increasing $i$ also increases that difference, by the
inequality on columns $r,s$. Hence the leftmost minimizing root cannot
move left when either endpoint increases. The common interval-weight
term does not affect root selection. Restricting to maximum-exposure
splits preserves the same minimizer.

For a fixed length $k$, set $b_i=R_h(i,i+k-1)$. Candidate counts telescope:
\[
 \sum_i(b_{i+1}-b_i+1)\le2n.
\]
Feasibility clamping only reduces this work. There are at most $2d$
further diagonals, so the whole layer costs $O(n(d+1))$. Prefix sums,
base values, and array initialization fit this bound. Retaining all values
and roots uses $O(n(d+1)(H+1))$ words. Root traceback reconstructs a fully
exposed interval in linear output time; band closure supplies every child
state. Four-corner differences give the stated densities.
\end{proof}

For $H=O(\log n)$ and $d=1$, this computes every capacity-line density
in $O(n\log n)$ work, with arbitrary mixed weight magnitudes. The result
computes a whole family of intervals; the earlier $O(n(r+1))$ slot bound
solves one height-limited instance. Unrestricted optimality still requires
an adequate height or a separate certificate. The main core bound does
not depend on an unproved estimate outside this band.

\subsection{Exact capacity densities and their interaction allowance}
\label{sec:capacity-interaction}
The capacity line admits a closed form that separates joint optimization
from the contributions of individual weight units. Write $P=\sum p_i$,
$Q=\sum q_i$, and $v(t)=\nu_2(t)$.

\begin{theorem}[Capacity interaction]\label{thm:capacity-interaction}
Fix $h\ge1$, $c=2^h-1\le n$, and $M>hW$. For $0\le i<n-c$, define
\[
 Z_{i,r}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{r}v(2\ell)(p_{i+2\ell}-p_{i+2\ell-1}),\qquad
 F_i=\max_{0\le r\le(c-1)/2}(-Z_{i,r}),\qquad
 G_i=\max_{0\le r\le(c-1)/2}(Z_{i,r}+q_{i+2r+1}).
\]
Then $D_h(i,i+c)=M-F_i-G_i$. Let $T_1(w)$ denote total ordinary density
mass strictly off this line, $e_x$ a unit on outcome $x$, and
$R_h(w)=A_h(0,n)-M(n-c)$. For arbitrary nonnegative real weights,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:capacity-interaction}
 T_1(w)-\sum_x w_xT_1(e_x)
 \le R_h(w)-\sum_x w_xR_h(e_x)\le(h-1)P.
\end{equation}
The middle expression is nonnegative. These quantities are independent of
the choice of sufficiently large terminal penalties in the unit instances.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Put $B_i=\sum_{t=1}^c p_{i+t-1}v(t)$. The two length-$c$ intervals are
perfect. Their inner length-$c-1$ interval has reward $B_i+G_i$, by the
one-missing-slot calculation in Lemma~\ref{lem:mixed-boundary-mass}.
The outer length-$c+1$ interval exposes exactly $c$ keys. Omitting its first
key gives reward $B_{i+1}$. Moving the omitted key to index $i+t$ changes
this reward by
\[
 \sum_{k=1}^t v(k)(p_{i+k-1}-p_{i+k})=-Z_{i,\lfloor t/2\rfloor}.
\]
Thus its optimum reward is $B_{i+1}+F_i$. All its gaps have depth $h$.
The four-corner difference cancels the interval masses and perfect rewards,
leaving $M-F_i-G_i$. For $h=1$, the inner interval is empty and the formula
holds with $F_i=0$, $G_i=q_{i+1}$.

The sum of all ordinary densities is the full interval cost minus the
singleton costs. Subtracting the capacity densities therefore gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:total-off-capacity}
 T_1(w)=R_h(w)-P-2Q+q_0+q_n+\sum_i(F_i(w)+G_i(w)).
\end{equation}
Both maxima are positively homogeneous and subadditive in the weights.
Apply this to $w=\sum_x w_xe_x$ and cancel the linear terms in
\eqref{eq:total-off-capacity}; this proves the first inequality.

For the second, the full interval's perfect exposed skeleton has reward
\[
 S_h(p)=\max_{1\le t_1<\cdots<t_c\le n}
                    \sum_{j=1}^c p_{t_j-1}v(j),\qquad R_h(w)=hW-S_h(p).
\]
All gap terms cancel from the interaction. Its value is
$\sum_{x=0}^{n-1}p_xS_h(e_x)-S_h(p)$, which is nonnegative because $S_h$
is a maximum of linear forms. It is at most $(h-1)P$ because each unit
reward is at most $h-1$ and $S_h(p)\ge0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Sharp key-mass allowance]\label{cor:sharp-capacity-interaction}
No uniform factor smaller than one can multiply $(h-1)P$ in
\eqref{eq:capacity-interaction}. In particular an $O(P)$ allowance independent
of height is impossible.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Take $h\ge2$, $C=2^h$, $n=2C$, $p_x=1$ for even $x$ and zero otherwise,
and $q=0$. Thus $P=C$. Each walk $Z_{i,r}$ is monotone: increasing for even
$i$ and decreasing for odd $i$. The maximizing endpoints of $F_i,G_i$ are
common to every positive unit key, so their sums are exactly additive.
Equality therefore holds in the first inequality of
\eqref{eq:capacity-interaction}.

The skeleton reward is $c-h$: no reward exceeds $\sum_{j=1}^c v(j)=c-h$,
and the contiguous subsequence starting at key index one attains it by
putting unit keys at every even skeleton rank. Hence
$R_h(w)=(h-1)C+h+1$. For a unit key at one-based position $X$, feasible
skeleton ranks form
$[\max(1,X-C-1),\min(C-1,X)]$. Among odd positions, $C/2+1$ can occupy the
root. The leftmost has comparison length $h$. The remaining positions
occur in pairs of groups with comparison length $h-t$ and $2^{t-1}$
positions per group, for $1\le t\le h-2$. Consequently
\[
 \sum_{x\ {
m even}}R_h(e_x)
   =C/2+1+h+2\sum_{t=1}^{h-2}(h-t)2^{t-1}=2C-h-1.
\]
The interaction is exactly $(h-3)C+2h+2$, whose ratio to $(h-1)C$ tends
to one. Scaling all positive weights by two gives $W=n$ with the same ratio.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Coupled stability of above-capacity rank rewards}
\label{sec:rank-reward-stability}
Let $p_0,\ldots,p_{n-1}\ge0$, $P=\sum_xp_x$, and let
$v=(v_1,\ldots,v_c)$ be any real template, with $1\le c\le n$. Set
\[
 S_v(i,j)=\max_{i\le t_1<\cdots<t_c<j}\sum_{r=1}^c p_{t_r}v_r
 \qquad(j-i\ge c).
\]
For integer $d\ge1$, define its upper tail by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rank-upper-tail}
 U_d(v)=\sum_{\substack{0\le i<j<n\\j-i\ge c+d}}
 [S_v(i,j)+S_v(i+1,j+1)-S_v(i,j+1)-S_v(i+1,j)].
\end{equation}
Each summand is nonnegative. Given optima for the outer and inner
intervals, their componentwise minimum fits the left interval and their
componentwise maximum fits the right interval. The sum of their rewards
is unchanged, proving the inequality.
For $c=2^h-1$ and $v_r=\nu_2(r)$, the above-capacity mixed-slot formula
identifies \eqref{eq:rank-upper-tail} with the ordinary upper density mass
at distance at least $d$. Its terminal-penalty and interval-mass terms
cancel in every four-corner difference, including all gap weights.

\begin{theorem}[Rank-coordinate tail stability]\label{thm:rank-tail-stability}
For every rank $r$ and $\Delta\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rank-tail-stability}
 -2P\Delta\le U_d(v+\Delta e_r)-U_d(v)\le P\Delta.
\end{equation}
Consequently, if $v'\ge v$ coordinatewise, then
$U_d(v')\le U_d(v)+P\sum_r(v'_r-v_r)$. For arbitrary templates the
absolute change is at most $2P\lVert v'-v\rVert_1$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Optima for a fixed interval are closed under componentwise minimum and
maximum. Both sequences stay strictly increasing; their total rewards
sum to the original total, so both remain optimal. Choose the unique
componentwise-leftmost optimum, obtained by taking all these minima.
When both interval endpoints move weakly to the right, its selected
positions move weakly to the right coordinatewise. Indeed, the minimum
of the old and new optima is feasible in the old interval, and their
maximum in the new. Reward preservation makes both optimal. Minimality
of the old optimum forces it to equal that componentwise minimum.

Put $k=c+d$. If $k\ge n$ the tail is zero. Otherwise $m=n-k\ge1$ and
row telescoping gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rank-tail-telescope}
 U_d(v)=\sum_{i=0}^{m}S_v(i,i+k)
       -\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}S_v(i+1,i+k)-S_v(0,n).
\end{equation}
Vary $v_r$ by $\lambda\in[0,\Delta]$. Each score is the maximum of
finitely many affine functions, so away from finitely many breakpoints
its derivative is the weight at its optimum's $r$th selected position.
Persisting ties have equal slopes. Write $a_i$ for this position in
$[i,i+k)$ and $b_i$ for it in $[i+1,i+k)$. Endpoint monotonicity yields
\[
 a_0\le b_0\le a_1\le b_1\le\cdots\le a_m.
\]
The first two sums in \eqref{eq:rank-tail-telescope} therefore have
derivative $\sum_i p_{a_i}-\sum_i p_{b_i}$. Equal positions occupy
contiguous blocks of this alternating chain. Cancelling each such block
leaves a coefficient in $\{-1,0,1\}$ at every input position, bounding
this derivative between $-P$ and $P$. The last term contributes
$-p_z\in[-P,0]$. Integrating the resulting almost-everywhere derivative
bound proves \eqref{eq:rank-tail-stability}. Successive coordinate
changes prove both extensions.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Clipping high ruler rewards]\label{cor:ruler-clipping}
Let $C=2^h$, $c=C-1$, $v_r=\nu_2(r)$, and
$v^{(t)}_r=\min(v_r,t)$ for $0\le t\le h-1$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ruler-clipping}
 U_d(v)\le U_d(v^{(t)})+P\bigl(2^{h-t}-(h-t)-1\bigr).
\end{equation}
For $t=\min(h-1,\lfloor\log_2d\rfloor)$ the error is at most $2PC/d$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The removed reward sums to
\[
 \sum_{r=1}^{C-1}[\nu_2(r)-t]_+
 =\sum_{s=t+1}^{h-1}(2^{h-s}-1)=2^{h-t}-(h-t)-1.
\]
Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:rank-tail-stability} to the coordinatewise
increase from $v^{(t)}$ to $v$. If $t=h-1$ the error vanishes;
otherwise $2^t>d/2$ gives the stated estimate.
\end{proof}
The remaining template has period $2^t$. The corollary controls the
high-rank contribution at the desired capacity-over-width scale while
retaining joint optimization of all weights. That error scale is necessary,
as the following exact family shows. The subsequent alternating-template
theorem controls the periodic part at level one. Higher levels remain open;
Lemma~\ref{prop:periodic-tail-barrier} shows that even level one
requires a capacity-dependent term.

\begin{theorem}[Exact full-ruler clipping tail]\label{thm:ruler-clipping-sharp}
Let $a\ge0$, $b\ge3$, $d=2^a$, $Q=2^b$, $C=dQ$, $h=a+b$,
$c=C-1$, and $n=2C$. Give unit weight to the keys at one-based positions
$x=C-3d$ and $y=C$, and zero weight to every other key. For
$v_r=\nu_2(r)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ruler-clipping-sharp}
 U_d(v)=\frac{5Q}{4}-3b+2,\qquad U_d(v^{(a)})=0.
\end{equation}
These above-capacity tails are unchanged by arbitrary nonnegative gap
weights. Since $P=2$, the ratio of their difference to the clipping
allowance $P(Q-b-1)$ tends to $5/8$ as $b\to\infty$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Put $\ell_r=\min(v_r,a)$ and $z_r=(v_r-a)_+$. The high reward $z_r$
vanishes away from multiples of $d$, and $z_{dt}=\nu_2(t)$.
We first verify compatible optimization on the boundary intervals in
\eqref{eq:rank-tail-telescope}. This step is needed because optimized
scores are not generally additive.

Consider an interval with slack $e=d$ or $d-1$ containing both unit keys,
and let $r$ be the first key's relative one-based position. Their selected
ranks have the form
\[
 r-u,\quad r+3d-w,\qquad 0\le u\le w\le e,
\]
with both ranks in $[1,C-1]$. If $r\bmod d\ne0$ and the two multiples
of $d$ in the feasible rank windows are valid, $u=w=r\bmod d$ selects
both. Every other rank has low reward less than $a$ and high reward zero.
A selection using one multiple can round the other rank to its multiple
without violating $u\le w$, so the same selection maximizes both parts.
For slack $d-1$ and $r\bmod d=0$, the unique feasible pair of multiples
has this same property.

For slack $d$ and $r=dR$, the pairs of multiples, after division by $d$, are
\[
 (R,R+3),\quad(R-1,R+2),\quad(R,R+2),
\]
with out-of-range pairs discarded. The boundary starts with both keys
present have $1\le R\le Q-3$, so the third pair is always valid.
If one selected rank is off the multiples, round it while retaining the
rank already on a multiple: the extreme shifts force the first two pairs,
and the intermediate shifts use the third. A pair with neither rank on
a multiple is dominated in low reward by the third pair. Thus a high-score
maximizer also has low score $2a$.

The remaining two-key case is $1\le r<d$. The first key's best low rank
lies in $[1,r]$; the second key's unique multiple is $3d$, with reward
$a$ and high reward zero. These choices are compatible: the first omission
count is less than $r$, and the second is $r$. With only one key present,
its feasible rank window either contains a multiple of $d$, giving maximum
$a$ plus the high maximum, or contains none and has zero high maximum.
On the full interval, two selected ranks are at distance at most $3d$,
so at most one is divisible by $4d$. Their reward is at most
$(h-1)+(a+1)=h+a$. Ranks $C/2,C/2+2d$ attain it, have low score $2a$,
and have high score $b$. They are feasible at $x,y$ because $Q\ge8$.
Consequently every boundary score and the full score satisfy
\[
 S_v=S_\ell+S_z.
\]
On these same intervals the low score is also the sum of the separate
one-key low scores. A one-key capped-ruler maximum is the sum, over
$1\le t\le a$, of the indicators that its rank window meets a multiple
of $2^t$. Its density cells have capacity distance $2^t-1<d$, by the
consecutive-multiple classification in Section~\ref{sec:local-tile-obstruction}.
Hence $U_d(\ell)=0$ and $U_d(v)=U_d(z)$.

For the high scores put $k=C+d-1$ and divide selected multiple ranks by $d$.
The coarse instance has length $2Q$, capacity $Q-1$, unit keys at
$Q-3,Q$, and ruler rewards $\nu_2(t)$. If $i=jd$, the long boundary
score $S_z(i,i+k)$ maps to the coarse interval $[j,j+Q)$. If
$i=jd+r$ with $1\le r<d$, it maps to $[j+1,j+Q)$. Every short score
$S_z(i+1,i+k)$ in this block maps to $[j+1,j+Q)$.
These are exactly the unique-pair and three-pair alternatives above.
The terminal high score is zero, and the full score maps to the coarse
full interval. Cancelling within each block of
\eqref{eq:rank-tail-telescope}, including the zero endpoint terms, gives
$U_d(z)=U_1$ for the coarse instance.

Set $V(r)=\nu_2(r)$ for $1\le r<Q$ and zero otherwise. In that coarse
instance write $R=Q-3-i$. The long interval has one omission and the
short interval has none. Their score difference is
\[
 \delta_R=\max\{V(R)+V(R+3),\ V(R)+V(R+2),\
                    V(R-1)+V(R+2)\}-V(R-1)-V(R+2).
\]
The nonzero range is $-2\le R\le Q-3$. For even $R$ this is $V(R)$,
whose sum is $Q-b-2$. For odd $R$ it is
$[V(R+3)-V(R-1)]_+$. The boundary values $R=-1,1$ contribute $1,2$;
values $R=3\pmod4$ contribute zero. Writing $R=4t+1$ for the others
leaves the positive differences $\nu_2(t+1)$ for odd $t$, except the
last $t=Q/4-1$, where $V(Q)=0$. Thus the odd sum is
\[
 3+\sum_{j=1}^{Q/4-1}\nu_2(j)=Q/4-b+4.
\]
The full coarse score is $b$: two ranks within distance three cannot both
be divisible by four, and $Q/2,Q/2+2$ attain $(b-1)+1$.
Subtracting this full score gives \eqref{eq:ruler-clipping-sharp}.
Corollary~\ref{cor:ruler-clipping} removes reward $Q-b-1$, giving the
stated ratio. Gap independence follows from \eqref{eq:mixed-slots-above}.
\end{proof}

This proves necessity of the $PC/d$ scale for full-ruler density mass.
Multiplying both key weights by $C$ makes total key mass $n$ and scales
the displayed tails by $C$; the number of positive cells does not scale.
The theorem therefore sharpens the tail analysis without changing the
current core-cardinality upper bound.

\subsection{A width-sensitive bound for the alternating template}
\label{sec:alternating-tail-bound}
The level-one clipped ruler has a fixed-cardinality path-matching
representation. Its tail can be bounded while retaining arbitrary
nonnegative real key weights and all their interactions.

\begin{theorem}[Alternating-template upper tail]\label{thm:alternating-tail-bound}
Let $c=2m+1\ge3$, $n\ge c$, and $v_r=1$ for even $r$ and zero for odd
$r$. With the scores and tails of \eqref{eq:rank-upper-tail}, for every
integer $d\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:alternating-tail-bound}
 U_d(v)\le P\min\left\{m,\,2+\frac{c-3}{d}\right\}.
\end{equation}
Thus $U_d(v)=O(P(1+c/d))$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The $m$ rewarded positions of a length-$c$ subsequence lie in
$[i+1,j-2]$, with at least one filler before them, between consecutive
positions, and after them. Conversely any $m$ indices in this range
separated by at least two admit those fillers. Regard index $x$ as
edge $(x,x+1)$ of the path on vertices $i+1,\ldots,j-1$, with weight
$p_x$. Then $S_v(i,j)$ is exactly the maximum weight of a matching
with $m$ edges.

Fix $j-i=c+d$ and put $\delta=S_v(i,j)-S_v(i+1,j)$. If an optimum
omits the first edge $i+1$, it remains feasible on the shorter path
and $\delta=0$. Otherwise remove that edge and the first vertex.
The remaining path has $2m+d-1$ vertices and a matching $M$ of size
$m-1$, hence exactly $d+1$ unmatched vertices $z_0<\cdots<z_d$.
Between consecutive unmatched vertices every interior vertex is matched.
Each of the $d$ intervening segments is therefore an augmenting path:
its edges alternate unused and matched, starting and ending unused.
Flipping it gives a matching of size $m$ on the shorter path. This is
the classical matching augmentation operation~\cite{berge}; here all
candidate paths and their weight accounting are explicit.

These paths can share unmatched endpoints, but their matched edges are
disjoint. Their removed weights sum to at most the weight of $M$.
One flip therefore removes at most
$\bigl(S_v(i,j)-p_{i+1}\bigr)/d$; its added edges have nonnegative
weight. Including the omitted-edge case gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:alternating-endpoint-loss}
 d\delta\le(d-1)p_{i+1}+S_v(i,j).
\end{equation}

For $k=c+d<n$ put $b=n-k$. Equation~\eqref{eq:rank-tail-telescope}
becomes
\[
 U_d(v)=\sum_{i=0}^{b-1}\bigl(S_v(i,i+k)-S_v(i+1,i+k)\bigr)
       +S_v(b,n)-S_v(0,n).
\]
The last difference is nonpositive by containment. Each score in the
sum is at most $\sum_{x=i+1}^{i+k-2}p_x$, and a fixed key appears in
at most $k-2$ such intervals. Summing
\eqref{eq:alternating-endpoint-loss} yields
\[
 dU_d(v)\le(d-1)P+(k-2)P=(2d+c-3)P.
\]
For $k\ge n$ the tail is empty. Finally, increasing the $m$ even-rank
rewards from zero to one and applying
Theorem~\ref{thm:rank-tail-stability} gives $U_d(v)\le mP$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact weighted cluster tail]\label{thm:weighted-cluster-tail}
Let $C=2^h$, $K\ge3$, $C\ge4K$, $c=C-1$, $n=2C$, and
$x=C-3(K-1)$. Place arbitrary nonnegative real weights
$w_0,\ldots,w_{K-1}$ at $x,x+3,\ldots,C$, with other weights zero.
For the alternating template, put $\mu=\min_r w_r$ and $P=\sum_r w_r$.
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:weighted-cluster-tail}
 U_K=N\mu,\qquad N=\lceil x/2\rceil-1.
\end{equation}
At fixed cluster mass $P$, the maximum is $NP/K$, attained exactly by
equal weights.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Put $k=c+K$ and $b=n-k=C-K+1$. We compute each loss in
\eqref{eq:rank-tail-telescope}. For $i<x$, the old interval $[i,i+k)$
can reward all $K$ cluster keys. With first local index $R=x-i+1$,
omit $u=0$ positions before it if $R$ is even, or $u=1$ if odd, and
one in each spacing-three gap. This uses $K-1+u\le K$ omissions.
The last rewarded rank is $C-K+2-i-u$, even and at most $C-2$.
The apparent exception $K=3,i=0$ forces $u=1$ and also fits.
Any remaining omission goes after the last cluster key. Thus the old
score is $P$.

The shortened interval has omission budget $K-1$ and first local index
$r=x-i$. If $r$ is even, all cluster keys can again be rewarded. If
$r\ge3$ is odd, rewarding all of them would require one initial omission
and at least one in each of $K-1$ odd gaps: $K$ omissions, too many.
The score is therefore at most $P-\mu$.

Every choice of the unrewarded key is feasible. If it is the first,
the next local index $r+3$ is even, and the $K-2$ remaining odd gaps
use $K-2$ omissions. If it is an interior key, use one initial omission
and one in each of $K-3$ ordinary odd gaps; the gap across the unrewarded
key has length six and needs no omission. Again the total is $K-2$.
In both cases the final rewarded rank is $C-K+2-i$, even and at most
$C-2$; $K=3,i=0$ belongs to the even-$r$ case instead. If the last key
is unrewarded, one initial omission and $K-2$ inter-key omissions give
final rewarded rank $C-K-2-i$. The unrewarded key can occupy an odd
rank, requiring no extra omission. The remaining omissions fit after
the final rewarded key. Choosing a minimum weight therefore gives
shortened score $P-\mu$.

At $i=x-1$, the first cluster key is the shortened interval's left
endpoint and can only occupy odd rank one. Its loss is $w_0$, and all
remaining cluster keys can be rewarded. For $i\ge x$, each interval has
at most $K-1$ eligible cluster keys. Their first local index is at most
four; the initial parity correction and inter-key omissions fit the
budget, and their final rewarded rank is at most $2K\le C-2$. Both
intervals reward every eligible key, so their difference is $p_{i+1}$.

Exactly $N$ starts $i<x-1$ have $x-i$ odd. The subsequent losses plus
$S(b,n)$ equal $P-w_0$, while $S(0,n)=P$. The telescope gives
$U_K=N\mu+w_0+(P-w_0)-P=N\mu$. Finally $\mu\le P/K$, with equality
exactly at equal weights, proves the extremal statement.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Both alternating-tail scales are necessary]
\label{cor:alternating-tail-scales}
The additive $P$ term and the $Pc/d$ term in
\eqref{eq:alternating-tail-bound} are both necessary up to constants.
The latter remains necessary when $d$ tends to infinity.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
For the capacity term, take the unit-weight instance of
Theorem~\ref{thm:weighted-cluster-tail}. It gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:alternating-cluster-tail}
 U_K(v)=\lceil x/2\rceil-1
       =\left\lceil\frac{C-3K+1}{2}\right\rceil.
\end{equation}
Taking $K=2^{\lfloor h/2\rfloor}$
gives $d=K\to\infty$, $P=K$, and $U_K/(Pc/K)\to1/2$.
Scaling each unit by $n/K$ gives integer total mass $W=n$.

For the additive term take $c=3$, so
$S_v(i,j)=\max_{i+1\le x\le j-2}p_x$. Put $K\ge2$ unit keys at
$1,1+s,\ldots,1+(K-1)s$, with $s\ge2$ and $n=(K-1)s+3$.
The positive upper densities connect consecutive unit keys $x,y$:
at $(i,j)=(x-1,y+1)$ the outer and side scores are one and the inner
score is zero. There are $K-1$ such unit densities, all at distance
$s-1$ from capacity. Hence $U_{s-1}=K-1$ with $P=K$. Letting $K,s$
grow rules out a bound $O(Pc/d)$ alone.
\end{proof}
For higher clipped levels, a matching edge's reward depends on its rank
among selected edges. Augmentation can change rewards on retained edges;
Section~\ref{sec:periodic-tail-barrier} gives a small explicit failure of
\eqref{eq:alternating-endpoint-loss} at level two. That additional rank
accounting is exact in Section~\ref{sec:rank-repair-barrier}, which proves
that even the best single-augmentation certificate can exceed the desired
tail scale by an unbounded factor. Joint rank changes remain necessary.


\subsection{A linear above-capacity core for one constant key block}
\label{sec:plateau-core}
A constant positive key block admits an exact one-dimensional representation
of its above-capacity densities, even with arbitrary nonnegative gap weights.
The representation retains the joint optimization over ruler ranks.
We first record an exchange fact also used in
Section~\ref{sec:binary-dilation}.

\begin{lemma}[Binary saturation]\label{lem:binary-saturation}
For a binary input word with $P$ ones and arbitrary nonnegative rank
rewards $v_1,\ldots,v_c$, some maximizing $c$-subsequence contains
$\min(P,c)$ ones.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If $P\ge c$, selecting $c$ ones attains the upper bound $\sum_r v_r$.
Otherwise, if a selected subsequence omits a one, it selects a zero.
Choose a selected zero nearest to the omitted one in physical order;
all selected entries between them are ones. Insert the omitted one
and remove that zero. Shifting the intervening ones changes the selected
binary word only by replacing one zero with one, so its reward cannot
decrease. Repeat until every positive key is retained.
\end{proof}


\begin{lemma}[Range maxima and nonadjacent visibility]
\label{lem:range-max-visibility}
For a sequence $R(0),\ldots,R(N-1)$, there are at most $\max(0,N-2)$
pairs $a<b-1$ satisfying
\[
 \min\{R(a),R(b)\}>\max_{a<r<b}R(r).
\]
These pairs and their positive differences can be enumerated in $O(N)$
arithmetic work and space.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Pairs $(a,c)$ and $(b,d)$ with $a<b<c<d$ cannot both satisfy the
inequality: they would require $R(c)>R(b)$ and $R(b)>R(c)$.
Place the vertices on a polygon in index order. For $N\ge3$ the
noncrossing proper diagonals number at most $N-3$; the closing edge
$(0,N-1)$ adds at most one. For $N\le2$ there is no nonadjacent pair.
This is the usual horizontal visibility geometry~\cite{hvg,hvgouter}.

For enumeration, scan left to right with a stack whose heights strictly
decrease. On reading a new height $x$, pop all smaller heights, pairing
each popped index with the new index; also pair the remaining top, if
any, with the new index. Replace an equal-height top by the new index:
that equal intervening height blocks every future strict visibility of
the old index. Discard adjacent pairs. The first popped index is the
preceding position. For each later candidate the last popped height
is exactly the maximum over its interior: previously removed indices
are shielded by a retained index of at least their height. Thus each
emitted pair satisfies the strict inequality, and the last popped height
supplies its positive difference. Conversely, a visible earlier index
cannot have been removed by an intervening height at least its own;
it is either popped now or is the first remaining top. This enumerates
every pair. Each index is pushed once and popped at most once, and
there are at most $2N$ pair candidates.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[One-block above-capacity core]
\label{thm:plateau-above-core}
Fix $h\ge1$, $c=2^h-1$, and a valid terminal penalty. Suppose keys
$z,\ldots,z+m-1$ have common weight $\alpha>0$ and every other key
has weight zero. Gap weights may be arbitrary nonnegative reals.
The ordinary core strictly above capacity, $j-i>c$, has at most
\[
 \max\{0,c-m-1\}
\]
entries. Their coordinates and values can be constructed in $O(n)$
arithmetic work and space. The leading coefficient one is
asymptotically sharp.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Above capacity, the layer cost is an affine terminal penalty plus
$hW(i,j)$ minus the optimum ruler reward. The first two terms have zero
ordinary density; arbitrary gap weights therefore cancel. Divide the
reward by $\alpha$ to obtain a binary input.

\emph{Partial-block endpoints.}
All four intervals of a cell with $j-i>c$ have length at least $c$.
If its removed left key $i$ is positive and its added right key $j$
is inside the block, every interval consists of positive keys and its
score saturates at $\sum_{r=1}^c\nu_2(r)$. If $j$ is outside the block,
its positive keys form a prefix. By Lemma~\ref{lem:binary-saturation},
the selected positive keys occupy the first $\min(P,c)$ ranks, where
$P$ depends only on the left endpoint. Its score is independent of
the right endpoint, so the density is zero again.
Reflection proves the same statement when the added right key is
positive: $\nu_2(2^h-r)=\nu_2(r)$ makes a positive suffix equivalent
to a prefix. Thus every remaining positive cell has all four intervals
containing the entire block. If $m>c$, these scores all saturate and
the above-capacity core is empty.

\emph{Exact range maxima.}
Assume $m\le c$. On an interval $[i,j)$ containing the block, an optimum
retains all $m$ positive keys. They occupy consecutive selected ranks
$r+1,\ldots,r+m$. Counting available zeros before and after the block
gives precisely
\[
 \max(0,c+z-j)\le r\le\min(c-m,z-i).
\]
The normalized score is therefore a range maximum of
\begin{equation}\label{eq:plateau-reward}
 R_m(r)=\sum_{t=r+1}^{r+m}\nu_2(t)
       =m+s_2(r)-s_2(r+m),\qquad 0\le r\le c-m,
\end{equation}
where $s_2$ counts one bits. Indeed,
$\sum_{t=1}^u\nu_2(t)=\sum_{a\ge1}\lfloor u/2^a\rfloor=u-s_2(u)$.
Equivalently $R_m(r)=m-s_2(m)+\kappa(r,m)$, where $\kappa$ counts
binary carries, one per column with an outgoing carry, in adding $r$
and $m$.

Put $l=c+z-j$ and $u=z-i$. If clipping freezes either endpoint of the
range, the four-corner difference is zero. Otherwise set $a=l-1$ and
$b=u$, with $0\le a<b\le c-m$. The interior is nonempty because
$b-a=j-i-c+1\ge2$. Write $Q=\max_{a<r<b}R_m(r)$. The normalized
density is exactly
\begin{align*}
 D_h(i,j)/\alpha
 &=\max(Q,R_m(b))+\max(R_m(a),Q)
     -\max(R_m(a),Q,R_m(b))-Q\\
 &=\max\{0,\min(R_m(a),R_m(b))-Q\}.
\end{align*}
Thus each positive density is one nonadjacent visibility pair, mapped
injectively by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:plateau-density-map}
 i=z-b,\qquad j=c+z-1-a.
\end{equation}
Conversely every such pair with $0\le i<j<n$ gives the indicated
positive density. Apply Lemma~\ref{lem:range-max-visibility} to the
$N=c-m+1$ values in \eqref{eq:plateau-reward} to obtain the bound.

\emph{Construction.}
If $c\ge n$ the region is empty. Otherwise compute the ruler values
in $O(c)$ work using $\nu_2(2r)=\nu_2(r)+1$ and zero on odd arguments.
Sliding length-$m$ sums produce $R_m$ in $O(c)$ further work, without
assuming a unit-cost population-count primitive. The lemma enumerates
all pairs and their density values in $O(c)$ work. Apply
\eqref{eq:plateau-density-map}, discard cells outside the input, and
multiply each remaining value by $\alpha$. Since $c<n$, the work and
space are $O(n)$.

\emph{Sharp leading coefficient.}
Take $h\ge3$, $m=1$, $C=2^h$, $n=2C$, and $z=C+1$. Every nonadjacent
visibility pair of $\nu_2(1),\ldots,\nu_2(C-1)$ maps inside the input.
These pairs are exactly consecutive multiples of $2^r$, for
$1\le r<h$: their interior valuations are smaller than $r$, whereas
a nonconsecutive pair has an intervening multiple of the smaller
endpoint's power. Their number is
\[
 \sum_{r=1}^{h-1}(2^{h-r}-2)=C-2h.
\]
The upper bound is $c-m-1=C-3$, and the ratio tends to one.
\end{proof}
\begin{corollary}[A boundary charge for one block]
\label{cor:plateau-square}
Under Theorem~\ref{thm:plateau-above-core}, any $s\times s$ square of
ordinary density cells strictly above capacity has at most $2s-1$
positive entries, independently of the block's length and weight.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Equation~\eqref{eq:plateau-density-map} maps the square to visibility
pairs between two ordered disjoint index intervals, each with at most
$s$ positions. For distinct left endpoints $a<a'$, every neighbor $b$
of $a$ must be at least every neighbor $b'$ of $a'$; otherwise
$a<a'<b<b'$ would give crossing visibility pairs. Order the nonempty
neighbor sets by increasing left endpoint. Their spans are decreasing
and have disjoint interiors; two consecutive sets can share at most
one endpoint. If $k$ left endpoints and $t$ right endpoints participate,
the total number of pairs is at most $t+k-1\le2s-1$. An empty support
satisfies the same bound.
\end{proof}
The theorem constructs this part of a layer's core, including its values.
The below-capacity mixed core and the general local hypothesis in
\eqref{eq:boundary-tile-hypothesis} require further arguments.


\subsection{The below-capacity core of one constant key block}
\label{sec:plateau-below}
The complementary side admits an exact classification when the gaps have
weight zero. This identifies a source of sparsity inside the region where
unequal ramp weights produce the lower bound of Theorem~\ref{thm:ramp-core-lower}.

\begin{theorem}[Complete below-capacity plateau core]\label{thm:plateau-below}
Let $p_z=\cdots=p_{e-1}=\alpha>0$, where $e=z+m$, and let every other
key and every gap have weight zero. At height $h\ge1$, put $c=2^h-1$ and
\[
 U_h(m)=\sum_{r=1}^{h-1}\max(0,m-2^r+1).
\]
Every positive ordinary density strictly below capacity equals $\alpha$.
There are at most $2(n-m)+U_h(m)$ such entries, and their coordinates and
values can be constructed in $O(n+U_h(m))$ arithmetic work and space.
Every dyadically aligned side-$s$ square wholly below capacity and in the
ordinary region contains at most $8s$ positive entries. The magnitude of
$\alpha$ is arbitrary, and $c$ may exceed $n$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Write $v(t)=\nu_2(t)$ on the one-based perfect-tree slots $1,\ldots,c$.
An interval $[i,j)$ containing the complete block, of length at most $c$,
permits its $m$ positive keys to occupy any $m$ slots in
$[z-i+1,c-j+e]$. The remaining slots accommodate its leading and trailing
zero keys. The slot-deletion repair therefore makes its exact cost
$\alpha$ times $hm$ minus the sum of the $m$ largest values in this window.
For any nonnegative integer word $v$, denote that sum by $\operatorname{Top}_m(v)$.
Threshold counting gives the identity
\[
 \operatorname{Top}_m(v)=\sum_{r\ge1}\min\{m,\#\{t:v(t)\ge r\}\}.
\]
If the interior of $[u,v]$ has at least $m$ positions, its negative mixed
difference is consequently
\begin{align*}
 &\operatorname{Top}_m([u,v-1])+\operatorname{Top}_m([u+1,v])
 -\operatorname{Top}_m([u,v])-\operatorname{Top}_m([u+1,v-1])\\
 &\qquad=\sum_{r\ge1}\mathbf1\{\nu_2(u),\nu_2(v)\ge r,
              \ \#\{u<t<v:2^r\mid t\}=m-1\}.
\end{align*}
Each clipped count changes only when the two endpoints compete for its
last available rank. This is an identity for the top sum, without a
separate-optimizer assumption. We classify the changed endpoint keys $i,j$.

\emph{Both outside, with the whole block retained.}
A nonzero density in this case requires $i<z$ and $j\ge e$. Put
$u=z-i$, $v=c-j+e$. The displayed identity gives density $\alpha$ exactly when
\begin{equation}\label{eq:plateau-below-pairs}
 2^r\mid u,\quad 2^r\mid v,\quad v-u=m2^r
             \quad\text{for some }r\ge1.
\end{equation}
At most one $r$ satisfies the distance equation. The capacity distance is
$c-(j-i)=m(2^r-1)$. Enumerate multiples of $d=2^r$ in
\[
 \max(1,c+e-n+1-md)\le u\le\min(z,c-md),
 \qquad i=z-u,\quad j=c+e-u-md,
\]
and retain ordinary below-capacity cells. There are at most
$\sum_{r\ge1}\lfloor z/2^r\rfloor<z$ candidates when $z>0$;
reflection also bounds their number by $n-e$.

\emph{Positive left endpoint, zero right endpoint.}
For $z\le i<e\le j$, put $k=e-i$, $b=c+e-j$. The cost is
$\alpha(hk-\operatorname{Top}_k([1,b]))$. Its density is $\alpha$ times
the increase in the $k$th largest prefix value when slot $b$ is appended.
That increase is one exactly when
\begin{equation}\label{eq:plateau-below-prefix}
 b=k2^r\quad\text{for some }1\le r\le\nu_2(b),
\end{equation}
and is zero otherwise. Indeed, at level $r$, the prefix count increases
from $k-1$ to $k$ precisely in this case. Different $r$ give different $k$.
With $t=j-e+1$, we have $b=2^h-t$ and $\nu_2(b)=\nu_2(t)$ for
$1\le t<2^h$. Enumerating those divisors and retaining $1\le k\le m$
uses at most $n-e$ candidates. The reflected case, a zero left endpoint
and a positive right endpoint, uses at most $z$ candidates. Reflection
maps a cell to $(n-1-j,n-1-i)$ and uses
$\nu_2(2^h-t)=\nu_2(t)$.

\emph{Both endpoints positive.}
All four intervals have uniform keys. Their minimum-internal-path-length
cost has increment $\operatorname{bitlength}(k)$ for the $k$th key.
Thus the density equals $\alpha$ exactly on
$j-i=2^r-1$, $1\le r<h$, giving precisely $U_h(m)$ cells. All remaining
endpoint cases have zero density. The first three cases contribute at most
$z+(n-e)+\min(z,n-e)\le2(n-m)$. Short ruler prefixes are generated by
$\nu_2(2t)=\nu_2(t)+1$ and zero on odd arguments; no array of length $c$
