is needed when $c>n$. Each divisor candidate and each uniform output takes
constant arithmetic work, proving the construction bound.

For the local bound, take at most $s$ consecutive positive integers and
remove their unique maximum-valuation position. At level $r$, at most
$\lfloor(s-1)/2^r\rfloor$ multiples remain, so
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ruler-remove-peak}
 \sum\text{remaining valuations}\le
 \sum_{r\ge1}\left\lfloor\frac{s-1}{2^r}\right\rfloor\le s-1.
\end{equation}
Uniqueness follows because between two equal maximum valuations lies a
larger one. In \eqref{eq:plateau-below-pairs}, each row has at most $\nu_2(u)$
neighbors. All rows other than that maximum contribute at most $s-1$;
the maximum row contributes at most $s$. Hence this case has at most
$2s-1$ cells. Equation~\eqref{eq:plateau-below-prefix} gives the same bound
using columns, and reflection gives it for the third case.

For the uniform case, write the dyadic square as
$[a,a+s)\times[b,b+s)$, $b-a=Ds$, $D\ge1$. If $s=1$ the claim is immediate.
If $D\ge2$, eligible powers $(j-i)+1$ lie in
$[(D-1)s+2,(D+1)s]$, an interval of width $2s-2$ containing at most
one power of two, since each eligible power is at least $2s$. Its diagonal
has at most $s$ cells. If $D=1$, powers $d\le s$ contribute $d-1$ cells
each and $d=2s$ contributes one, totaling $2s-1-\log_2s$.
Cropping to the positive block reduces this count. Summing the four disjoint
cases gives at most $8s$ entries. Cropping padded grids to the original
non-power-of-two input also only removes cells.
\end{proof}

Together with Corollary~\ref{cor:plateau-square}, this proves a boundary-only
local support charge for this key-only family on both sides of capacity,
without integrality or a weight-magnitude restriction. The below-capacity
formulas require zero gaps; the earlier above-capacity result continues
to allow arbitrary nonnegative gaps. Independent verification and the
separate fixed-tile excess search are recorded in
Appendix~\ref{app:plateau-below}.

\subsection{Finite slot-window products and a complete run constructor}
\label{sec:slot-run}
The top-sum identity also composes unequal constant runs, if the matrix
coordinates are slot boundaries and every input key is retained. This
complements the rank transfers of Section~\ref{sec:rank-transfer-barrier},
which select input keys for consecutive ruler ranks.

\begin{lemma}[Finite slot-window factor]\label{lem:slot-run-factor}
Fix $h\ge1$, $c=2^h-1$, and nonnegative key runs $(w_\ell,m_\ell)$,
$1\le\ell\le r$, with $m_\ell\ge1$. Put
$P=\sum_\ell w_\ell m_\ell$ and $K=1+(h-1)P$. For $0\le a,b\le c$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:slot-run-factor}
 Q_\ell(a,b)=K\max(m_\ell-b+a,0)
 -w_\ell\sum_{t=1}^{h-1}
 \min\left(m_\ell,\left\lfloor\frac b{2^t}\right\rfloor
                       -\left\lfloor\frac a{2^t}\right\rfloor\right).
\end{equation}
This is a finite Monge matrix with fewer than $2c$ core entries and an
$O(c)$ condensed construction. If $b-a\ge m_\ell$, it is the negative
maximum reward of the run placed in slots $(a,b]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The feasible-entry formula follows by counting valuation thresholds among
the $m_\ell$ largest ruler values. The signed counts in
\eqref{eq:slot-run-factor} also define all other entries. Write $m=m_\ell$
and $w=w_\ell$. The hinge contributes density $K$ on $b-a=m$.
For a threshold $d=2^t$, a nonzero mixed difference requires both $a+1$
and $b+1$ to be multiples of $d$. With
$x=\lfloor b/d\rfloor-\lfloor a/d\rfloor$, its density is
\[
 w\,[2\min(m,x)-\min(m,x-1)-\min(m,x+1)].
\]
It equals $w$ exactly when $x=m$, and is zero otherwise, including for
negative $x$. Hence the complete density list consists of
\begin{align*}
 &K &&\text{at }(a,b)=(a,a+m),\quad 0\le a<c-m,\\
 &w &&\text{at }(a,b)=(kd-1,(k+m)d-1),\quad
       1\le k\le\lfloor c/d\rfloor-m,\quad d=2^t,\ 1\le t<h.
\end{align*}
Zero-weight entries are omitted. These supports are disjoint: the second
line has $b-a=md$, which distinguishes its levels and the hinge. Thus
\[
 \delta(Q_\ell)\le(c-m)_+
       +\sum_{t=1}^{h-1}(\lfloor c/2^t\rfloor-m)_+<2c.
\]
Let $F(a)=\sum_{j=1}^a\nu_2(j)$. The left boundary is
$Q_\ell(a,0)=K(m+a)+wF(a)$. To construct the top boundary, sweep $b$;
threshold $t$ changes precisely when $t\le\nu_2(b)$ and $b/2^t\le m$.
The total threshold visits are $\sum_{b=1}^c\nu_2(b)=c-h<c$.
Generate the listed core directly and order it by two bucket passes.
All boundaries and core entries therefore take $O(c)$ work and words,
even when $m>c$ and no feasible window exists.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Balanced slot-window construction]\label{thm:slot-run-product}
For the runs in Lemma~\ref{lem:slot-run-factor}, let $n=\sum_\ell m_\ell$
and let $w_1,\ldots,w_n$ be the expanded weights. The finite Monge product
$T=Q_1\otimes\cdots\otimes Q_r$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:slot-run-product}
 T(a,b)=-\max_{a<t_1<\cdots<t_n\le b}
                 \sum_{i=1}^n w_i\nu_2(t_i)\qquad(b-a\ge n).
\end{equation}
Its core has at most $2r2^{\lceil\log_2r\rceil}c\le4r^2c$ entries.
The condensed product and an optimal selected sequence for any feasible
entry can be constructed in $O(n+r^2c\log(c+1))$ arithmetic operations.
The ordinary witness arrays used here take $O(r^2c\log(c+1))$ auxiliary
words.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A feasible product path partitions $(a,b]$ into consecutive windows, one
per run, each of length at least its run length. The best independent
selections within these windows concatenate in key order. Conversely,
any all-key slot selection admits such a partition: cut after the final
selected slot of each run except the last. Thus feasible paths give
exactly \eqref{eq:slot-run-product}.

An infeasible path has an integer window deficit at least one and incurs
at least $K$ penalty. Every threshold minimum in
\eqref{eq:slot-run-factor} is at most $m_\ell$, even for a reversed window.
Consequently the reward part of any path is at least $-(h-1)P$. An
infeasible path costs at least $K-(h-1)P=1$, whereas every feasible path
costs at most zero. This proves strict exclusion, also for zero weights
and ties. No infinite entries are used.

Multiply in a balanced tree using the published condensed product and
witness primitive~\cite{coremonge}. Its bound
$\delta(X\otimes Y)\le2(\delta(X)+\delta(Y))$ charges each leaf by at
most $2^{\lceil\log_2r\rceil}$, proving the final core bound. The sum
of core sizes over all nodes is $O(r^2c)$: at product-tree depth $j$
there are at most $2^j$ nodes with at most $\lceil r/2^j\rceil$ leaves
each, and the resulting quadratic bounds form a geometric sum. Matrix
boundaries contribute $O(rc)$ and the product logarithm is
$O(\log(c+1))$. Extracting the runs costs $O(n)$.

Retained witnesses recover each leaf's window in $O(r\log(c+1))$ work;
the penalty argument ensures that every recovered window is feasible.
Bucket its valuations into $h$ levels and select the largest $m_\ell$,
breaking cutoff ties by earliest slot. Disjoint leaf windows partition the
outer window, so this costs $O(c+rh)$ altogether. These costs fit the
stated bound, including the conservative space for ordinary witness maps.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Equal internal gaps at a prescribed height]
\label{cor:slot-run-mixed}
Let $n\ge2$, let $q_1=\cdots=q_{n-1}=b\ge0$, and allow arbitrary
nonnegative keys and endpoint gaps. Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:slot-run-gap-gauge}
 w_0=p_0+q_0,\qquad w_i=p_i+b\ (1\le i\le n-2),\qquad
 w_{n-1}=p_{n-1}+q_n.
\end{equation}
If these weights have $r$ constant runs and $c=2^h-1\ge n$, a complete
optimal tree of height at most $h$ can be constructed in
$O(n+r^2c\log(c+1))$ arithmetic work and
$O(r^2c\log(c+1))$ auxiliary words. At the minimum feasible height
$h=\lceil\log_2(n+1)\rceil$, the work is $O(n+r^2n\log(n+1))$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
If $\ell_i$ are key comparison lengths, the total external path length is
$E=I+2n=\sum_i\ell_i+n$. The endpoint gaps have depths $\ell_0$ and
$\ell_{n-1}$. Subtracting their two contributions from $bE$ and restoring
the actual endpoint weights proves, for every tree,
\[
 C_{p,q}(T)=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}w_i\ell_i+bn.
\]
All transformed weights are nonnegative, even when an endpoint gap is
smaller than $b$. Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:slot-run-product} and the
$O(c+n)$ slot repair of Theorem~\ref{thm:mixed-slots}. The original mixed
optimum is $h\sum_iw_i+T(0,c)+bn$, and the repaired tree attains it.
For minimum feasible height, $c<2(n+1)$. For $n=1$ the unique tree has
cost $p_0+q_0+q_1$ and needs no product.
\end{proof}
If the original keys have $r_0$ runs, the transformed sequence has at most
$r_0+2$. The theorem solves the stated height-constrained problem;
unrestricted optimality still requires a sufficient height certificate.
Its explicit factor $c=2^h-1$ must be retained when applying such a
certificate. Unequal internal gaps introduce the signed transition
obstruction in Theorem~\ref{thm:slot-gap-checkerboard}. The audit appears
in Appendix~\ref{app:rank-transfer}.

\subsection{Run vectors with arbitrary mixed gaps}
\label{sec:slot-vector}
A complete tree needs a vector of prefix costs rather than every transfer
entry. This removes one factor of the run count and handles unequal gaps
without representing their signed transition matrices explicitly.

\begin{theorem}[Mixed run-vector construction]\label{thm:slot-vector}
Let $n\ge2$ and allow arbitrary nonnegative key and gap weights. Put
$b=\min_{1\le i<n}q_i$, define $w$ by
\eqref{eq:slot-run-gap-gauge}, and put
$g_0=g_n=0$, $g_i=q_i-b$ for $1\le i<n$.
Let $r$ be the number of constant runs in $w$ and
$e=|\{i:g_i>0\}|$. For prescribed $h$ with $c=2^h-1\ge n$, a complete
optimal tree of height at most $h$ can be constructed in
\[
 O\bigl(n+(r+e)c\log(c+1)\bigr)\quad\text{arithmetic work},\qquad
 O\bigl(n+(r+e)c\bigr)\quad\text{words}.
\]
The bounds include preprocessing, backpointers and full tree reconstruction.
At minimum feasible height the work is $O(n+(r+e)n\log(n+1))$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The external-path identity used in Corollary~\ref{cor:slot-run-mixed}, now
retaining the residual internal gaps, gives for every tree
\begin{equation}\label{eq:slot-vector-normalization}
 C_{p,q}(T)=C_{w,g}(T)+bn.
\end{equation}
All transformed weights are nonnegative. Their total mass is $W-b$.
By Theorem~\ref{thm:mixed-slots}, it suffices to maximize their mixed
slot reward and add $bn$ after reconstruction. Write $v(t)=\nu_2(t)$,
set $R=(h-1)(\sum_iw_i+\sum_i g_i)$ and $K=R+1$.
Let $f_k(a)$ be the minimum negative reward of the first $k$ keys ending
at slot $a$. For $k>0$ retain exactly $k\le a\le c$; initially the only
state is $f_0(0)=0$.

Consider a constant run of $m\ge2$ keys of weight $w$, with zero residual
gaps preceding and inside the run. Force its last key to slot $z$ and
let the preceding prefix end at $a$. Define the finite matrix
\begin{equation}\label{eq:slot-vector-forced}
 B_{w,m}(a,z)=K(m-z+a)_+-wv(z)
 -w\sum_{t=1}^{h-1}\min\left(m-1,
          \left\lfloor\frac{z-1}{2^t}\right\rfloor
         -\left\lfloor\frac a{2^t}\right\rfloor\right).
\end{equation}
On $z-a\ge m$, this chooses the best $m-1$ preceding slots in $(a,z)$.
The whole matrix is the window factor for $m-1$ keys, shifted by one
column, plus a column potential. Lemma~\ref{lem:slot-run-factor}'s density
proof applies for this larger positive $K$, so it is finite Monge.

To compute $f_{k+m}(z)$, take row minima of
$f_k(a)+B_{w,m}(a,z)$, with rows $z=k+m,\ldots,c$ and columns
$a=k,\ldots,c-m$ when $k>0$, or just column $0$ when $k=0$.
Transposition and the prefix potential preserve Monge. Each row has a
feasible candidate. An infeasible candidate incurs at least $K$ penalty,
while the prefix and new-run negative rewards together are at least $-R$,
also when $a\ge z$. It therefore costs at least one. A feasible candidate
costs at most zero. The finite row minimum consequently gives exactly the
true prefix optimum and its attaining predecessor. SMAWK~\cite{smawk}
uses $O(c)$ entry evaluations and additional work; each entry of
\eqref{eq:slot-vector-forced} takes $O(h)$ arithmetic operations.
Thus a run pass costs $O(ch)$ work and $O(c)$ working words.

To append a single key of weight $w$ after an arbitrary residual gap $g$,
the exact update is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:slot-vector-gap}
 f_{k+1}(z)=-wv(z)+\min_{k\le a<z}
                  \{f_k(a)-g\min(v(a),v(z))\}.
\end{equation}
For $k=0$, only $a=0$ is present, with $v(0)=0$ and $g=0$.
Sweep $z$ increasingly, inserting $a=z-1$ when that prefix state exists.
Maintain $E_d$, the minimum prefix value at exact valuation $d$, and
$S_d$, the minimum at valuation at least $d$, with leftmost predecessors.
For $t=v(z)$ the minimum in \eqref{eq:slot-vector-gap} is
\[
 \min\left\{\min_{d<t}(E_d-gd),\ S_t-gt\right\},
\]
with absent states omitted. Inserting valuation $d$ updates
$E_d,S_0,\ldots,S_d$; a query scans $t$ lower levels and reads $S_t$.
Every slot is inserted and queried at most once, and
$\sum_{a=1}^c v(a)=c-h<c$. Array initialization, all updates, all queries,
and their predecessors therefore take $O(c+h)=O(c)$ work and $O(c)$
words. This also processes singleton zero-gap runs. No gap-transition
matrix or signed density list is constructed.

Make the key after each positive residual gap a singleton stage.
Otherwise collect a maximal constant-weight segment with zero residual
gaps preceding and inside it. The $e$ exceptional gap positions split the
$r$ original runs into at most $r+e$ segments; isolating their first keys
adds at most $e$ further stages. Hence there are at most $r+2e$ stages.
If $R_2$ stages have length at least two and $S_1$ are singletons, the
more precise bounds are
\begin{equation}\label{eq:slot-vector-refined}
 O\bigl(n+c(1+hR_2+S_1)\bigr)\text{ work},\qquad
 O\bigl(n+c(1+R_2+S_1)\bigr)\text{ words}.
\end{equation}
They imply the theorem because $h=\log_2(c+1)$.

Retain a predecessor array per stage, choose an attaining final slot for
$\min_z f_n(z)$, and trace backward. In each long run choose the $m-1$
largest valuations in its recovered open window, then append its forced
last slot. Valuation buckets and earliest cutoff ties take window length
plus $O(h)$ work. The recovered windows are disjoint, so all slot recovery
costs $O(c+(r+e)h+n)$. The $O(c+n)$ threaded deletion repair of
Theorem~\ref{thm:mixed-slots} produces a complete optimal transformed tree;
\eqref{eq:slot-vector-normalization} proves original mixed optimality.
For minimum feasible height, $c<2(n+1)$. The case $n=1$ is direct.
\end{proof}

For equal internal gaps, $e=0$, so this improves complete construction in
Corollary~\ref{cor:slot-run-mixed} from quadratic to linear run dependence.
The all-entry product and its core bound remain useful separately.
For arbitrary weights, $r\le n$ and $e\le n-2$; if every stage is a
singleton, \eqref{eq:slot-vector-refined} gives $O(nc)$ work. The signed
checkerboard in Theorem~\ref{thm:slot-gap-checkerboard} thus permits fast
vector application despite obstructing a sparse density list. The height
constraint remains explicit; the next subsection supplies a sufficient
height for unrestricted construction. The original interval-core bound and local support
question are unchanged. Independent controls appear in
Appendix~\ref{app:rank-transfer}.

\subsection{Unrestricted height from a small exceptional set}
\label{sec:trimmed-height}
The full mixed rotation inequalities also control height after a chosen set
of outcomes is set aside in the accounting. Every weight remains in the
optimization. Write $\mu$ for the smallest positive singleton-interval mass,
and $z$ for the longest all-zero interval:
\[
 \mu=\min\{p_i+q_i+q_{i+1}:p_i+q_i+q_{i+1}>0\},\qquad
 \ell=\lceil\log_2(z+1)\rceil.
\]
Here $z$ is the longest consecutive run of indices with
$p_i=q_i=q_{i+1}=0$. When the total mass is positive, $\mu$ exists.
Every positive-mass nonempty interval contains a positive singleton
interval: for a positive key use that key, and for a positive gap use
an adjacent key inside the interval. Thus its mass is at least $\mu$.
For $V\ge0$ define
\[
 t(V)=\min\{j\ge0:F_{j+1}\mu>V\},\qquad F_1=F_2=1.
\]

\begin{lemma}[Disjoint charges beyond the light mass]
\label{lem:trimmed-height}
Mark any $k$ key or gap outcomes and let $V$ be the total mass of the
unmarked outcomes. Put
\[
 P=\begin{cases}t(W),&k=0,\\t(V)+k+1,&k\ge1.\end{cases}
\]
Every positive-mass internal subtree in every optimal tree has root depth
less than $P$. Some optimal tree has height at most $\min\{n,P+\ell\}$.
If $W=0$, a balanced tree is optimal directly.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Fix a positive internal subtree at depth $d$ and ascend to the root.
Let $A_0,\ldots,A_d$ be the successive masses and
$B_i=A_i-A_{i-1}$ the mass of the new ancestor key and its off-path
sibling subtree. Lemma~\ref{lem:mass}, including its explicit straight
and bent rotations, gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:trimmed-increments}
 A_j\ge F_{j+1}\mu,\qquad B_i\ge A_{i-2}\quad(2\le i\le d).
\end{equation}
The outcome sets counted by the $B_i$ are disjoint. For any fixed $j$,
all sets $B_i$ with $i>j$ are also disjoint from the subtree of mass $A_j$.
This partition includes each external gap once.

If $k=0$, then $A_d\le W$ implies $d<t(W)$. Suppose $k\ge1$.
When $d<t(V)$ there is nothing to prove. Otherwise let $j$ be the first
index with $A_j>V$. Equation~\eqref{eq:trimmed-increments} ensures
$j\le t(V)$. The subtree of mass $A_j$ contains a marked outcome.
For every $i\ge j+2$, its monotonicity and the same equation give
$B_i\ge A_{i-2}\ge A_j>V$, so each such disjoint increment contains
another marked outcome. If $d\ge j+1$, counting these sets gives
$k\ge1+(d-j-1)=d-j$; when $d=j$ the same conclusion is immediate.
Consequently $d\le j+k\le t(V)+k<P$.
The increment $B_{j+1}$ is deliberately uncharged: its inequality only
involves $A_{j-1}$, which need not exceed $V$.

Replace every maximal zero-mass subtree by a balanced tree on its own
keys. This changes no cost or positive outcome depth. Such a subtree
contains at most $z$ keys and has replacement height at most $\ell$.
It attaches below a positive internal node, giving height at most
$P+\ell$. The trivial height $n$ completes the bound.
\end{proof}

This is a consequence of the sufficient mixed rotation lemma; the
Fibonacci method is prior work~\cite{hlm}. No dominance assumption on
the marked weights, integrality, or separation of their positions is used.
For fixed $k$, selecting the $k$ largest outcome weights minimizes $V$
and hence $t(V)$. Sorting them and scanning all suffix masses finds the
best stated height in $O(n\log(n+1))$ work and $O(n)$ space. Compute
$\mu,z$ in one scan. Generate $\mu F_{j+1}$ only until it exceeds $W$
or $j=n$; the table has $O(n)$ entries and its last entry is at most
$2W$. As the suffix mass decreases, the index $\min\{t(V),n\}$ only
decreases, so all index updates take $O(n)$ work. Truncation at $n$ cannot
falsely improve a bound already capped by the trivial height $n$.

The same global height works for every contiguous interval. Its marked
set and unmarked mass can only decrease, its smallest positive singleton
mass can only increase, and its zero interval length is at most $z$.
Use the stronger $k=0$ case for an interval containing no marked outcome,
and balance an all-zero interval directly. Hence this selected budget
also suffices for convergence of the original terminal-penalty iteration
on the fixed original or normalized instance, with its penalty recomputed
for that height.

\begin{theorem}[Full construction with a selected sufficient height]
\label{thm:trimmed-construction}
Apply the normalization of Theorem~\ref{thm:slot-vector}, and select
height $h$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:trimmed-height} on the normalized weights.
Put $c=2^h-1$. With $r$ normalized key runs and $e$ positive residual
internal gaps, a complete unrestricted optimum can be constructed in
\[
 O\bigl(n\log(n+1)+(r+e)c\log(c+1)\bigr)\text{ work},\qquad
 O\bigl(n+(r+e)c\bigr)\text{ words}.
\]
More precisely, if $R_2$ passes are long runs and $S_1$ are singletons,
choosing between the vector kernel and Knuth before construction gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:trimmed-construction}
 O\!\left(n\log(n+1)+
 \min\{n^2,c(1+hR_2+S_1)\}\right)
\end{equation}
arithmetic work and quadratic worst-case space.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The normalization changes every tree cost by the same constant $bn$.
Thus its sufficient height contains an original unrestricted optimum.
Theorem~\ref{thm:slot-vector} optimizes exactly over that height and
constructs the entire tree. Equation~\eqref{eq:slot-vector-refined}
supplies the precise kernel work and space. Compare its work proxy with
$n^2$ before allocating slot arrays; the selected kernel has the smaller
asymptotic bound. If $h>2\lceil\log_2(n+1)\rceil$, the slot capacity
already exceeds $n^2$ and can be rejected without constructing it.
All-zero normalized input is handled by a balanced tree.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Few exceptional outcomes of arbitrary magnitude]
\label{cor:trimmed-runs}
Let $\varphi=(1+\sqrt5)/2$ and $\alpha=\log_\varphi2\approx1.44042$.
Suppose all normalized keys weigh at least $a>0$. Apart from $s$
exceptional keys of unrestricted size, their weights lie in $[a,Ca]$
for fixed $C$. Suppose the background has $r_0$ constant runs before
these exceptional keys are substituted, and there are $e$ positive
residual gap weights, also of unrestricted size. For fixed $r_0,s,e$,
a complete unrestricted optimum can be constructed in
$O(n^\alpha\log(n+1))$ arithmetic work.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Mark the $s$ exceptional keys and $e$ positive residual gaps. The remaining
mass is $V\le Cna$, every positive singleton has mass at least $a$, and
$z=0$. Since $F_{j+1}\ge\varphi^{j-1}$ for $j\ge1$,
\[
 t(V)\le2+\lceil\log_\varphi(1+V/\mu)\rceil,\qquad
 2^h=O\bigl(2^{s+e}n^\alpha\bigr).
\]
The $k=0$ case has at least as good a bound. Substituting exceptional
keys makes at most $r_0+2s$ key runs. Apply
Theorem~\ref{thm:trimmed-construction}; for fixed $s+e$, $h=O(\log(n+1))$.
The constants depend on $C,r_0,s,e$, not on the exceptional magnitudes.
\end{proof}
The earlier dominant-core construction may be faster when its certificate
passes. This corollary needs no such certificate, and permits unrelated
exceptional magnitudes. Its bound counts exact arithmetic operations;
unbounded integer bit costs are additional. Arbitrarily many runs and
exceptional outcomes remain valid inputs to the full solver, with the
quadratic fallback guarantee. The general arbitrary-weight objective and
the original local interval-core support bound remain open.

\subsection{Valuation-class records without slot expansion}
\label{sec:slot-records}
The prefix vectors admit a second compression: among ending slots of the
same valuation, retain only the first feasible slot and strict value decreases.
This removes the explicit perfect-tree capacity from an integer-mass bound,
including reconstruction of the complete tree.

\begin{lemma}[Prefix records and domination]\label{lem:slot-records}
Fix height $h$, capacity $c=2^h-1$, and normalized weights $w,g$ of total
mass $M$. For each valuation $d$, the prefix cost $f_k(a)$ is nonincreasing
as $a$ increases through feasible slots with $\nu_2(a)=d$. Retaining its
first value and strict decreases suffices for every subsequent run or
singleton transition. If the weights are integers, the number $B_k$ of
retained states satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:slot-record-count}
 B_k\le\min\{c,\ h[1+(h-1)M]\}.
\end{equation}
More generally, mark $t$ outcomes of arbitrary nonnegative weights, and
suppose the unmarked weights are integers of total mass $V$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:slot-record-marked-count}
 B_k\le\min\{c,\ h^{t+1}[1+(h-1)V]\}.
\end{equation}
The empty prefix has one state, at slot zero.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Move only the last slot of an attaining prefix to a later slot of the same
valuation. Its key reward and preceding gap reward remain unchanged, and
order is preserved. This proves monotonicity. If a later state has the same
value as an earlier state of its class, replacing it by the earlier one
preserves a singleton's preceding-gap reward and feasibility. For a long
zero-gap run, it leaves a superset of available inner slots. The earlier
state therefore dominates the later state in both transitions.
For integer weights, each prefix value is an integer in $[-(h-1)M,0]$.
Each of the $h$ classes has at most $1+(h-1)M$ retained values, proving
\eqref{eq:slot-record-count}.
For the marked bound, each marked outcome's reward coefficient is one of
$0,\ldots,h-1$: its slot valuation for a key, the minimum adjacent valuation
for an internal gap, and zero for an outcome outside the prefix. Thus there
are at most $h^t$ marked coefficient vectors. The unmarked reward is an
integer between zero and $(h-1)V$. At most $h^t[1+(h-1)V]$ distinct costs
are possible, regardless of the marked magnitudes or integrality. Multiply
by the $h$ ending-slot classes to obtain \eqref{eq:slot-record-marked-count}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Complete construction from prefix records]
\label{thm:slot-record-construction}
Let $s\le r+2e$ be the number of normalized stages in
Theorem~\ref{thm:slot-vector}, and let $B_1,\ldots,B_s$ count their
retained prefix states, with $B_0=1$. For arbitrary nonnegative weights,
a complete optimal tree subject to a prescribed feasible height bound $h$
can be constructed in
\begin{equation}\label{eq:slot-record-output}
 O\!\left(h^2\left[n+\sum_{j=0}^s B_j\right]\right)
 \text{ arithmetic work},\qquad
 O\!\left(nh+\sum_{j=0}^s B_j\right)\text{ words}.
\end{equation}
For integer normalized weights of total mass $M$, these are at most
\begin{equation}\label{eq:slot-record-integer}
 O\bigl(nh^2+s(M+1)h^4\bigr)\text{ work},\qquad
 O\bigl(nh+s(M+1)h^2\bigr)\text{ words}.
\end{equation}
The bounds include all backpointers and the entire output tree.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For a singleton of weight $w$ following gap $g$, fix destination valuation
$d$ and scan preceding records $(a,f_k(a))$ in increasing $a$. Its candidate
value is $f_k(a)-g\min(\nu_2(a),d)-wd$; it becomes eligible at the first
slot of valuation $d$ strictly greater than $a$. These eligibility positions
are nondecreasing. Record each decrease of the running minimum, combining
improvements at the same position. Repeating over $d$ costs $O(hB_k)$
work before record organization.

For a long run, restrict the finite Monge factor
\eqref{eq:slot-vector-forced} to preceding records $a\le c-m$.
Every row $z\ge k+m$ has a feasible candidate: $a=k$ is the first feasible
slot of its valuation class, hence a record (or $a=0$ for the empty prefix).
The same penalty argument excludes infeasible minima. Adding prefix
potentials and restricting columns preserve Monge, so SMAWK evaluates any
batch of $Q$ destination rows in $O(B_k+Q)$ entry queries. Each query
costs $O(h)$ arithmetic work.

The feasible positions of each destination valuation form an arithmetic
progression. Query its first and last positions. Equal endpoint values
certify a constant interval by Lemma~\ref{lem:slot-records}. For unequal
values, bisect the progression's index interval. Batch all required midpoints
across all classes into a single sorted SMAWK query, then continue only on
children with unequal endpoint values. Adjacent unequal endpoints identify
exactly a strict-decrease record. Retain each class's first position separately.

There are at most $h$ bisection levels. At a fixed level, active intervals
within a class are disjoint, and each contains a distinct strict decrease.
Thus each level queries $O(B_{k+m})$ rows. All levels together take
$O(h(B_k+B_{k+m}))$ entry queries and $O(h^2(B_k+B_{k+m}))$ work.
Sorting row batches and organizing records fit this bound since
$\log(c+1)=h$. Retain only active endpoints and discovered records,
using $O(B_k+B_{k+m}+h)$ working words.

An attaining predecessor stored at a record also works throughout its
constant interval in the same valuation class. With that predecessor
fixed, the candidate cost cannot increase as the destination moves right,
while the global minimum stays equal. The candidate therefore stays equal
too. Store these predecessors per stage and recover an attaining final
slot and all preceding run windows.

For a recovered window $(a,z)$, let
\[
 N_t=\left\lfloor\frac{z-1}{2^t}\right\rfloor-
     \left\lfloor\frac a{2^t}\right\rfloor.
\]
For $m\ge2$, find $d$ with $N_{d+1}<m-1\le N_d$.
Take all multiples of $2^{d+1}$ in the window and the earliest required
odd multiples of $2^d$, then append $z$. Merge the two sorted arithmetic
progressions in $O(m)$ work; the threshold scan costs $O(h)$. All slot
recovery costs $O(n+sh)$, without visiting the unused slots.

To repair the slots into a tree, retain only their ancestor paths in the
height-$h$ perfect tree, at most $nh$ vertices. If $d=\nu_2(x)<h-1$,
the parent of $x$ is $x-2^d$ when bit $d+1$ of $x$ is set, and $x+2^d$
otherwise. Stop a path at its first previously retained ancestor.
Every absent branch contains no selected key and may be collapsed to an
empty tree. On the retained tree, delete each unselected key in increasing
label order, using threaded inorder successors. A two-child deletion
promotes the successor and splices its possible right child upward;
a one-child deletion splices that child upward directly. No surviving
key's depth increases. Thus its comparison length is at most
$h-\nu_2(t_i)$. Gap depths are the maxima of adjacent key comparison
lengths, with the adjacent length at either endpoint. The mixed slot
identity and its converse in Theorem~\ref{thm:mixed-slots} prove exact
optimality of the resulting full tree.

Deterministic ordered dictionaries or sorted arrays organize these
$O(nh)$ vertices in $O(nh^2)$ work and $O(nh)$ words, since the retained
vertices are a subset of $c$ slots. Threaded deletions and iterative output
then cost linear work in the retained size. Summing stage costs proves
\eqref{eq:slot-record-output}; inserting \eqref{eq:slot-record-count}
proves \eqref{eq:slot-record-integer}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Linear integer mass and few mixed runs]
\label{cor:slot-record-linear-mass}
For arbitrary nonnegative integer key and gap weights of total mass
$W=O(n)$, suppose the normalization has a fixed number of key runs and
positive residual gaps. A complete unrestricted optimum can be constructed
in $O(n\log^4(n+1))$ arithmetic work and $O(n\log^2(n+1))$ words.
Zero-weight key runs are permitted.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Normalization preserves integrality and gives $M\le W$. When $M>0$,
the smallest positive singleton mass is at least one. The $k=0$ case of
Lemma~\ref{lem:trimmed-height}, including balanced zero-mass intervals,
supplies $h=O(\log(n+1))$. Selecting a sufficient height costs
$O(n\log(n+1))$. Apply \eqref{eq:slot-record-integer} with $s=O(1)$.
For $M=0$, construct a balanced tree directly.
\end{proof}
\begin{corollary}[Few arbitrarily large integer outcomes]
\label{cor:slot-record-marked}
Suppose the normalized weights are nonnegative integers, with $s=O(1)$
stages. Mark $t$ outcomes of arbitrary magnitude, where $t$ is fixed,
and suppose the remaining mass is $V=O(n)$. A complete unrestricted
optimum can be constructed in
\[
 O\bigl(n\log^{t+4}(n+1)\bigr)\text{ arithmetic work},\qquad
 O\bigl(n\log^{t+2}(n+1)\bigr)\text{ words}.
\]
The constants do not depend on the marked magnitudes; zero weights are allowed.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Every positive singleton has mass at least one. Lemma~\ref{lem:trimmed-height}
gives a sufficient height $h=O(\log(n+1)+t)$, including the balanced
zero-mass intervals. Equation~\eqref{eq:slot-record-marked-count} gives
$B_j=O((V+1)h^{t+2})$ at each stage. Substitution in
\eqref{eq:slot-record-output}, with fixed $s,t$, proves both bounds.
An all-zero instance is balanced directly.
\end{proof}
All bounds count exact arithmetic, including operations on $h$-bit slot
coordinates. Operand bit costs are additional. Arbitrary nonnegative
weights retain the output-sensitive bound \eqref{eq:slot-record-output};
its integer score-range specialization is a separate assumption.
Choosing Knuth when the integer work proxy exceeds $n^2$ retains a
quadratic worst-case bound. The general arbitrary-weight subquadratic
objective and the original interval-core support question remain open.
Lemma~\ref{lem:slot-record-envelope} also gives the sharp universal bound
$B_k\le\sum_{r=0}^{\min(k,h)}\binom hr-k$.
Theorem~\ref{thm:slot-record-barrier} gives exponential explicit record
support at necessary optimal height for strictly positive mixed weights.
Independent state and full-tree controls appear in
Appendix~\ref{app:rank-transfer}.

\subsection{Implicit prefix functions with additive decision diagrams}
\label{sec:slot-dag}
The record bound concerns an explicit list of strict changes. A different
representation shares dyadic blocks whose value functions differ only by
an additive constant. Edge-valued binary decision diagrams are established
data structures of Lai and Sastry and subsequent work; see
Bryant's account~\cite{bryant-dd}. We use their additive sharing principle
with a fixed most-significant-bit-first order on slot coordinates. The
new obligations here are the exclusive-prefix operation, the mixed-gap
transition, and complete tree recovery. No approximation is used.

Represent a function on $\{0,\ldots,2^d-1\}$ with values in
$\mathbb R\cup\{+\infty\}$ by an offset and a normalized binary node.
The offset is its minimum finite value. A node stores its level and its
two normalized child references, with their offsets. Identical triples
are shared. The all-infinite function has a separate sentinel, and the
finite level-zero terminal has value zero. Levels are retained even
when both children coincide. Induction on the level proves that this
representation is canonical up to the chosen node identifiers. Every
reference also stores its exact minimum and maximum, allowing $+\infty$
as the maximum when any position is infeasible.

\begin{lemma}[Shared exclusive prefix minima]\label{lem:dag-prefix}
For a height-$h$ function represented by $D$ reachable nodes, its
exclusive prefix-minimum function
\[
 (\mathcal PF)(b)=\min_{0\le a<b}F(a),\qquad (\mathcal PF)(0)=+\infty,
\]
can be constructed with $O(hD)$ arithmetic operations, node constructions,
and dictionary operations, creating at most $O(hD)$ nodes.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Write $F=L\mathbin\Vert R$ for its two halves. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dag-prefix}
 \mathcal P(L\mathbin\Vert R)
 =\mathcal PL\mathbin\Vert
       \min\{\min L,\mathcal PR\},
\end{equation}
where $\min L$ denotes the constant function with that value. Also
$\mathcal P(F+a)=\mathcal PF+a$, so a normalized input node needs to
be processed only once, regardless of its incoming offset.

Every prefix-minimum function is nonincreasing, including its initial
infinite positions. To cap such a function $G$ by a constant $t$, return
$G$ immediately when $\max G\le t$, and return the constant when
$t\le\min G$. Otherwise recurse on both halves. At each level at most
one half straddles the change from the constant to $G$, since $G$ is
nonincreasing. Thus the cap visits $O(h)$ nodes and creates $O(h)$ nodes.
Equation~\eqref{eq:dag-prefix} performs one cap per distinct input node.
Memoization and normalization give the stated bounds. The all-infinite
case and a level-zero input return the infinite function directly.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Implicit mixed-prefix construction]\label{thm:slot-dag}
For arbitrary nonnegative key and gap weights, every feasible height
$h$ admits an exact construction of all mixed prefix functions and a
complete optimal height-$h$ tree using additive decision diagrams,
without enumerating their record sets or their $2^h-1$ slots.
Setting $h=n$, or using a certified sufficient height, gives an
unrestricted optimum. For an incoming diagram with $D$ nodes, a zero-residual-gap update
uses $O(h^3D)$ arithmetic and dictionary operations. A general gap update
uses $O(h^5D+X)$ such operations, where $X$ counts the distinct expanded
normalized minimum contexts used to combine its $h$ candidate functions.
The parameter $X$ includes relative offsets.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Use the normalization of Section~\ref{sec:slot-vector}, and put
$v(b)=\nu_2(b)$ for $b>0$, with $v(0)=0$. Initialize $f_0(0)=0$
and $f_0(b)=+\infty$ for $b>0$. Let $M_d$ be zero on positions of
valuation $d$ and infinite elsewhere; include position zero in $M_0$.
For the next normalized key $w_k$ and its preceding residual gap $g_k$,
form, for every $0\le d<h$,
\[
 P_d=\mathcal P(f_k+M_d),\qquad
 Q_d(b)=P_d(b)-g_k\min\{d,v(b)\}-w_kv(b).
\]
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dag-mixed-transition}
 f_{k+1}(b)=\min_{0\le d<h}Q_d(b).
\end{equation}
Indeed, the masks partition every feasible predecessor $a<b$ by its
valuation, giving exactly the mixed slot recurrence. When $g_k=0$, use
$f_{k+1}=\mathcal Pf_k-w_kv$ directly.

Masks and functions of valuation have finite shared diagrams, constructed
recursively from their binary suffix conditions. Addition and pointwise
minimum recurse on corresponding halves and normalize the results.
Addition factors out the sum of the incoming offsets. Minimum factors
out their common minimum, but must retain the relative offset in its
memoization key. Omitting that relative offset would identify different
functions. Lemma~\ref{lem:dag-prefix} supplies the remaining operation.
Induction on $k$ proves every represented prefix value.

For the work bound, a valuation mask has $O(h)$ nodes, and an arbitrary
function of valuation has $O(h^2)$ nodes: at each level its exceptional
zero-address value is one of at most $h$ prescribed values. Addition of
two diagrams with $D_1,D_2$ nodes uses $O(D_1D_2)$ node-pair contexts,
since both root offsets factor out. With zero gap, the prefix operation
creates $O(hD)$ nodes and adding the valuation reward takes $O(h^3D)$
work. With a general gap, masking takes $O(hD)$ work per class, prefix
minima take $O(h^2D)$, and the reward addition takes $O(h^4D)$. Summing
over $h$ classes gives $O(h^5D)$. Combining the candidates costs $O(X+h)$:
every newly expanded normalized minimum context makes two recursive
calls and a constant amount of arithmetic and node construction.

Keep the prefix roots. Choose an earliest minimum of $f_n$ by following
minimum-valued child references. To recover a predecessor of an ending
slot $b$ with valuation $e$, minimize $f_k+M_d$ over $[0,b)$ for each
$d$, subtract $g_k\min(d,e)$, and choose an attaining position. A prefix
range decomposes into at most $h$ complete dyadic blocks. Their minima
are stored in their offsets, and descending a minimizing block recovers
an attaining position. Repeating this gives every selected slot. Apply
the sparse ancestor repair of Theorem~\ref{thm:slot-record-construction}
to recover the full tree. The mixed-slot identity proves its optimal cost.
\end{proof}

The diagram algorithms operate on shared nodes and normalized operation
contexts. Their work need not be proportional to the final live graph:
pointwise minima may encounter many relative offsets for the same pair
of node identifiers. The audit therefore counts every created intermediate
node and every recursive operation, separately from final and history
sizes. Exact range pruning is essential to the prefix bound above.
The new constructor supports arbitrary mixed weights; a universal
subquadratic bound on its intermediate work remains open. Independent
prefix and full-tree controls appear in Appendix~\ref{app:rank-transfer}.

\begin{lemma}[Endpoint dominance and binary skyline]\label{lem:dag-skyline}
For every nonempty mixed prefix with nonnegative normalized weights,
\[
 1\le a\le c<2^h,\quad v(a)\le v(c)
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad f_k(c)\le f_k(a).
\]
For $1\le b\le2^h$, write $b-1=\sum_{j=1}^t2^{d_j}$ with
$d_1>\cdots>d_t\ge0$, and let
$c_j=\sum_{i=1}^j2^{d_i}$. Define
$S(b)=\{0,c_1,\ldots,c_t\}$. For every $g\ge0$ and $k\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dag-skyline}
 \min_{0\le a<b}\{f_k(a)-g\min(v(a),v(b))\}
 =\min_{a\in S(b)}\{f_k(a)-g\min(v(a),v(b))\}.
\end{equation}
Here infeasible values are infinite, $v(0)=0$, and the endpoint sentinel
$b=2^h$ has $v(b)=h$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For a finite $f_k(a)$, move only the final selected key from $a$ to $c$.
All earlier selected slots remain smaller. Its key reward cannot decrease,
and its preceding-gap reward cannot decrease because the gap weight is
nonnegative and $\min(v(s_{k-1}),v(c))\ge
\min(v(s_{k-1}),v(a))$. Thus the new feasible prefix costs at most
$f_k(a)$; taking its optimum proves endpoint dominance. Infinite values
make the inequality immediate.

Put $c_0=0$. Each interval $(c_{j-1},c_j]$ has length $2^{d_j}$,
and $c_{j-1}$ is divisible by $2^{d_j+1}$. Its endpoint has valuation
$d_j$ and every interior integer has smaller valuation. Therefore each
positive predecessor $a<b$ has a representative $c_j\ge a$ with
$v(c_j)\ge v(a)$. Endpoint dominance decreases its prefix cost, and
$g\ge0$ ensures that subtracting the new gap reward decreases it again.
The representative remains below $b$. Slot zero is retained separately.
These observations prove both inequalities in \eqref{eq:dag-skyline};
for $k=0$, only its retained zero slot is finite.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[One-path mixed predecessor queries]\label{thm:dag-skyline-query}
Augment every additive diagram node by the value at its first local address,
allowing infinity. This adds constant work and one stored value per created
node. On any actual mixed prefix function, the minimum in
\eqref{eq:dag-skyline} and an attaining predecessor can then be found in
$O(h)$ arithmetic and address operations, with no diagram-node creation.
Given the prefix roots from Theorem~\ref{thm:slot-dag}, all optimal selected
slots can be recovered in $O(nh)$ such operations and $O(n)$ output space,
without creating masked diagrams or invoking pointwise diagram operations.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A normalized node's first-address value is its left child's edge offset
plus that child's first-address value, or infinity if that address is
infeasible. Both are known when the parent is created. An incoming root
offset is added on reading this metadata.

Follow the $h$ bits of $b-1$ from most to least significant. Whenever bit
$d$ is one, the right child begins at exactly the next skyline position
$c_j$. Its stored first-address value, including the accumulated root
offset, is $f_k(c_j)$, and $v(c_j)=d$. Read it in constant work, subtract
$g\min(d,v(b))$, and retain the best feasible candidate. Also consider
$f_k(0)$. This is one path walk with at most $h+1$ candidates, so the
lemma proves the query and its work bound. Ties may select any attaining
skyline candidate; it need not be the earliest minimizer among all slots.

Query $f_n$ with $b=2^h$ and $g=0$ to find an optimal last slot. For each
preceding key, query its prefix root with the current slot and the relevant
residual gap. The current key's reward is constant during this predecessor
choice. The mixed recurrence therefore supplies an attaining predecessor
at every step. There are exactly $n$ queries. The selected positions are
strictly increasing in forward order, and the existing sparse ancestor
repair constructs the complete optimal tree from them.
\end{proof}

The new bound concerns slot traceback. The forward construction still has
the intermediate-size and minimum-context parameters of
Theorem~\ref{thm:slot-dag}. In particular, endpoint dominance has not yet
supplied a general subquadratic bound for constructing the prefix roots.

\subsection{Scalar-carry forward construction}
\label{sec:dag-forward}
Endpoint dominance also controls forward construction. It permits a
single scalar carry per dyadic block. A reset-path cover bounds all
expanded carry states linearly in the input diagram size, with a height
factor for the length of each path.

\begin{lemma}[Reset paths cover the carry states]
\label{lem:dag-carry-reset}
Let $V$ be the reachable nodes of a height-$h$ additive diagram, retaining
all levels, and let $d(u)$ be the remaining depth of node $u$.
Consider the following memoized recursion. On a block $R=L\mathbin\Vert U$
with incoming carry $p$, prune if $R$ is all infinite or $p\le\min R$;
otherwise pass $p$ to $L$ and $\min\{p,\min L,U(0)\}$ to $U$.
Leaves do not expand. Cache by the normalized input node and relative
carry, with infinity represented separately. The number $Z$ of expanded
states satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dag-reset-count}
 Z\le h+\sum_{u\in V}\max\{d(u)-1,0\}=O(h\lvert V\rvert+h).
\end{equation}
Their depth-weighted count satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dag-reset-depth}
 \sum_{s\text{ expanded}}d(s)
 \le \frac{h(h+1)}2+\sum_{u\in V}\frac{d(u)(d(u)-1)}2.
\end{equation}
These bounds permit arbitrary signed finite values and infinite entries;
no OBST realizability assumption is needed for the state count.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Work in the coordinates of a normalized parent input node. Put
$\ell=\min L$, $r=\min U$, and $C=\min\{\ell,U(0)\}$, allowing infinity.
Classify a right-child edge as a reset when $C\le p$, including ties.
Its carry then equals $C$, so if that child expands its normalized state
is $(U\text{ node},C-r)$. This state depends only on the parent input
node, not on $p$ or the parent's external additive offset. An infinite
carry remains a separate symbol; no subtraction of two infinities is used.
There is consequently at most one possible reset seed per input node.

All left-child edges are inherited. A right-child edge that is not a
reset has $p<C\le\ell$, so its left child is pruned. Thus each expanded
state has at most one inherited expanded child. The same statement holds
when some quantities are infinite: a nonreset inequality forces $p$ to
be finite, and an all-infinite child never expands.

Take the root state, when expanded, and every distinct expanded reset
seed. Follow inherited edges from each seed. Every expanded state is
covered: trace any recursion path from the root, starting a new path
after its last reset. Memoization can merge these paths but cannot create
an additional state outside their union. Each edge decreases depth by
one. The root path has at most $h$ states and depth sum $h(h+1)/2$.
A reset from a depth-$d$ input node starts at depth $d-1$, giving at most
$d-1$ states and depth sum $d(d-1)/2$. Summing these allowances, even if
paths overlap or some seeds coincide, proves both inequalities.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Polynomial mixed updates on implicit prefixes]
\label{thm:dag-forward}
Let $F=f_k$ be a feasible mixed prefix function on $2^h$ addresses, with
$D$ reachable additive-diagram nodes. Appending a key of nonnegative
normalized weight $w$ and preceding residual gap $g\ge0$ constructs the
next prefix diagram in $O(h^2D)$ arithmetic and dictionary operations,
without generic pointwise diagram minima. For the empty prefix, use its
required initial gap $g=0$. With incoming diagram sizes
$D_0,\ldots,D_{n-1}$, all forward updates take
\[
 O\!\left(h^2\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}D_k\right)
\]
such operations. The stored prefix roots support the complete optimal
height-$h$ tree construction and the $O(nh)$ slot traceback of
Theorem~\ref{thm:dag-skyline-query}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Consider a dyadic block $[B,B+2^d)$ and put
$p_B=\min_{0\le a\le B}F(a)$. For $B>0$, $v(B)\ge d$.
Every $a\le B$ with $v(a)<d$ and finite value is dominated by $B$,
by Lemma~\ref{lem:dag-skyline}; slot zero is infinite for a nonempty
prefix. Thus $p_B$ has an attainer of valuation at least $d$ whenever
finite. For every interior destination $b$, $v(b)<d$, so all these
outside predecessors contribute exactly $p_B-gv(b)$ after minimization.
At $B=0$, $p_B=+\infty$ for a nonempty prefix. For the empty prefix,
$p_B=0$ and the initial $g=0$ makes the same formula valid. This separate
case is needed because the empty slot has valuation zero.

For a local block function $R$ on $2^d$ positions define
\[
 E_g(R)=\min_{0<t<2^d}\{R(t)-g\nu_2(t)\}.
\]
Exclude the first local address: its global valuation can exceed $d$.
A singleton has summary $+\infty$. If $R=L\mathbin\Vert U$ and
$m_0=U(0)$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dag-forward-summary}
 E_g(R)=\min\{E_g(L),E_g(U),m_0-g(d-1)\}.
\end{equation}
The summary commutes with additive offsets, so memoization by normalized
input node computes all needed summaries in $O(D)$ work.

The recursive output for a block temporarily has $+\infty$ at its first
local address. The parent will supply that boundary's true value. At
midpoint $M=B+2^{d-1}$, the new prefix value and right-child carry are
\begin{align}
 f_{k+1}(M)&=\min\{p_B-g(d-1),E_g(L)\}-w(d-1),
 \label{eq:dag-forward-midpoint}\\
 p_M&=\min\{p_B,\min L,F(M)\}.
 \label{eq:dag-forward-carry}
\end{align}
Indeed, the internal predecessors in $(B,M)$ have valuation below
$d-1$, and the outside contribution has already been established.
The carry formula is the definition of the inclusive prefix minimum.
Recurse on both children and replace the right output's first address
with \eqref{eq:dag-forward-midpoint}. A persistent replacement follows
only the leftmost path, taking $O(d)$ work. The root's first output
address correctly remains infinite.

There is a decisive pruning rule. If $p_B\le\min R$, no internal
predecessor can improve the outside one. For every local $t>0$ the
output is simply $p_B-(g+w)\nu_2(t)$, with infinity at $t=0$.
These templates are shared by depth and require $O(h^2)$ total work;
an all-infinite input block is handled by the same rule. Otherwise let
$m=\min R$ and memoize the recursive state by the input-node identity
and $p_B-m$, representing an infinite carry separately. Translation of
both $R$ and its carry translates the result, proving this normalization.

Lemma~\ref{lem:dag-carry-reset} now gives $O(hD)$ expanded states.
For an explicit operational bound, an expanded state at remaining depth
$d$ uses one node-assembly call and $d-1$ node-assembly calls along the
first-address replacement. Its replacement visits, including the leaf,
also number $d$. Shared templates use at most $h(h+1)/2$ calls of either
kind. Thus each of these two operation counts is at most
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dag-reset-work}
 h(h+1)+\sum_{u\in V}\frac{d(u)(d(u)-1)}2=O(h^2D).
\end{equation}
Here $D\ge1$ because the input prefix is feasible. There are exactly
$2Z+1$ recursive build calls, including memo hits, pruned calls and leaves.
Weighted-summary expansions number at most $D$, with at most $2D+Z$
summary calls. All other scalar and dictionary operations take constant
work per call or newly assembled node. This proves the total
$O(h^2D)$ bound, including all intermediate work, and bounds the number of
new nodes. Induction over the updates proves the represented mixed
prefix functions. The previously proved skyline traceback and sparse
repair return their complete optimal tree. Dictionary costs and exact
operand bit costs are interpreted as in Theorem~\ref{thm:slot-dag}.
\end{proof}

This removes the uncontrolled generic-minimum parameter $X$ from the
per-update guarantee for actual OBST prefixes. A useful bound on the
entire sequence $D_k$, needed for a general subquadratic construction,
remains open. The exponential generic-minimum example in
Theorem~\ref{thm:dag-minimum-barrier} does not contradict this result:
this update uses endpoint dominance and the scalar carry recurrence.
The height factors in the reset-path accounting are sharp for general
diagram inputs by Theorem~\ref{thm:dag-reset-sharp}. Its displayed family
violates endpoint dominance, leaving sharper actual-prefix bounds open.

\subsection{Separating the first entry of each block}
\label{sec:punctured-diagram}
The factor $h$ spent on persistent first-address replacement can be
removed by representing that address separately. The midpoint value then
becomes a scalar stored directly in a node, and a recursive update assembles
its result in constant work.

A \emph{punctured diagram} of remaining depth $d$ represents only the local
addresses $1,\ldots,2^d-1$. At depth zero this domain is empty. At positive
depth its decomposition is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:punctured-node}
 P=(P_L,c,P_R),\qquad c=P(2^{d-1}),
\end{equation}
where $P_L$ and $P_R$ represent the interiors of the two half blocks.
Normalize by subtracting the minimum finite entry from the two child
references and the midpoint scalar. Intern the resulting tuple together
with its depth, retaining every nonempty level. An all-infinite interior
has a null reference. Thus the incoming offset is exactly the interior
minimum, and assembling a node takes constant arithmetic and dictionary
work. The first value of a full block is external to this representation.

\begin{lemma}[Canonical interiors and conversion]
\label{lem:punctured-canonical}
Two interiors of the same depth, each with a finite entry, have the same
normalized punctured node if and only if their functions differ by an
additive constant, with identical infinite positions. From an ordinary additive diagram with $D$ reachable
nodes, including its terminal, its punctured diagram can be constructed
in $O(D)$ work and has at most $D-1$ nodes when the original has a finite entry.
An all-infinite original gives a null punctured diagram.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For canonicity, induct on remaining depth. The minimum fixes the offset;
the left interior, midpoint scalar, and right interior then uniquely fix
the normalized tuple by the induction hypothesis. The reverse implication
follows by evaluating that tuple. For conversion, first memoize the first
value of each ordinary node. An ordinary node $L\mathbin\Vert U$ maps to
$(\operatorname{puncture}(L),U(0),\operatorname{puncture}(U))$.
A terminal maps to an empty interior. Offsets commute with this map, so
memoizing it creates at most one punctured node per ordinary nonterminal,
with constant work per source node. Conversion can merge additional nodes
or produce a null reference, which only decreases the size.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Mixed construction with constant boundary assembly]
\label{thm:punctured-construction}
Let $D_k$ count the punctured nodes of the mixed prefix function $f_k$ on
$2^h$ addresses, with $D_0=0$. One arbitrary nonnegative normalized key/gap
update takes $O(h(D_k+1))$ arithmetic and dictionary operations. For
$n\ge1$ and a prescribed feasible height $h$, a complete optimal mixed
BST can be constructed in
\begin{equation}\label{eq:punctured-full-work}
 O\!\left(h\left[n+\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}D_k\right]\right)
\end{equation}
such operations and at most that many stored words. This includes all
intermediate nodes, selected-slot traceback, and ancestor repair. Choosing
any proved sufficient unrestricted height gives a complete unrestricted
optimum with the same bound, plus the height-selection work.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For a block starting at $B$, the inclusive carry is
$p_B=\min_{a\le B}f_k(a)$. In particular $p_B\le f_k(B)$, so the
omitted first input entry cannot improve that carry. By the endpoint
argument of Theorem~\ref{thm:dag-forward}, outside predecessors contribute
$p_B-g\nu_2(t)$ at an interior destination $t$. For
$P=(P_L,c,P_R)$ of remaining depth $d$, the two recursive carries and the
new midpoint value are therefore
\begin{align}
 p_L&=p_B,&p_R&=\min\{p_B,\min P_L,c\},\label{eq:punctured-carries}\\
 c'&=\min\{p_B-g(d-1),E_g(P_L)\}-w(d-1),\label{eq:punctured-middle}
\end{align}
where $E_g(P)=\min_{t>0}\{P(t)-g\nu_2(t)\}$ and
\[
 E_g(P)=\min\{E_g(P_L),c-g(d-1),E_g(P_R)\}.
\]
This summary takes $O(D_k)$ work by normalized-node memoization.
All minima omit infinite choices, and an empty minimum is infinite.
Recurse on the two interiors and assemble $(P_L',c',P_R')$ directly.
No first-address replacement is performed.

If $p_B\le\min P$, or the interior is all infinite, the output is the
valuation template $p_B-(g+w)\nu_2(t)$ on $t>0$. A template
at depth $d$ is assembled from the depth-$(d-1)$ template on both sides
and midpoint $-(g+w)(d-1)$. Thus all needed templates take at most $h$
node-assembly calls. An infinite carry and infinite interior give an
infinite output. At the root the nonempty prefix has carry $+\infty$;
the empty prefix has no interior, carry zero, and initial gap $g=0$.
This gives the exact first update as well.

Cache each expanded state by its input-node identity and carry relative
to the interior minimum. The reset-path proof of
Lemma~\ref{lem:dag-carry-reset} applies with
$C=\min\{\min P_L,c\}$. A reset right child has a relative carry fixed
by its parent input node. If the right child inherits instead,
$p_B<C\le\min P_L$ prunes the left child. Hence, writing $V_k$ for the
input nodes, the number $Z$ of expanded states obeys
\[
 Z\le h+\sum_{u\in V_k}(d(u)-1).
\]
Each expanded state now makes exactly one node-assembly call. If $T\le h$
templates are built, the exact number of such calls is $Z+T$, at most
\begin{equation}\label{eq:punctured-assembly-bound}
 2h+\sum_{u\in V_k}(d(u)-1)=O(h(D_k+1)).
\end{equation}
There are $2Z+1$ recursive build calls, at most $D_k$ summary expansions,
and at most $2D_k+Z$ summary calls. Template lookups and remaining scalar
operations have the same bound. This accounts for every intermediate
operation and created node, including interned nodes that are reused.

Store the prefix roots. The skyline walk of
Theorem~\ref{thm:dag-skyline-query} still follows the bits of $b-1$.
Whenever it enters a right half, the required candidate is exactly the
midpoint scalar in \eqref{eq:punctured-node}. Thus each predecessor query
takes $O(h)$ work and creates no nodes; all selected slots take $O(nh)$.

For the final repair, collect the union of the selected slots and their
perfect-tree ancestors in a dictionary, stopping each upward walk at an
already collected ancestor. There are at most $nh$ vertices. This set is
ancestor-closed and contains the perfect-tree root. An inorder traversal
visiting only collected children enumerates it in increasing slot order
in linear work, without comparison sorting. At a collected slot $x$ of
valuation $d>0$, its possible children are $x-2^{d-1}$ and $x+2^{d-1}$;
check their membership before visiting them. Build the parent/child and
inorder-neighbor links, then apply the sparse deletion repair of
Theorem~\ref{thm:slot-record-construction}. Every unwanted ancestor is
deleted in constant work using those links, followed by a linear output
traversal. The repair therefore takes $O(nh)$ arithmetic/address and
dictionary operations. Summing updates, traceback, and repair proves
\eqref{eq:punctured-full-work}. The existing exact mixed-slot identity
proves the cost and optimality of the complete tree. As in the preceding
diagram theorems, exact operand bit costs are additional.
\end{proof}

The representation changes the cost of an update without imposing a key
run structure or restricting the gap weights. The all-stage sum
$\sum_kD_k$ remains the obstacle to a general subquadratic guarantee.
Corollary~\ref{cor:punctured-sharp} gives a matching general-diagram
operation family. Theorem~\ref{thm:punctured-size-barrier} proves cubic
final diagrams and quartic shared history on actual positive mixed inputs.
Further savings require avoiding those explicit canonical representations
or exploiting additional input structure.

\subsection{Two positive blocks: the whole above-capacity core}
\label{sec:two-block-core}
The one-block representation extends to two equal positive blocks, including
all positive-endpoint cuts. The complete-block interaction is a finite
Monge product; the cut regions reduce to visibility pairs and two diagonals.

\begin{lemma}[Finite band dominance and a local boundary charge]
\label{lem:band-dominance}
For real sequences $f_1,f_2$ of length $N\ge1$ and an integer $d\ge0$,
define, for $0\le A,B<N$,
\[
 H(A,B)=\max\{f_1(u)+f_2(v):u\le A,\ v\ge B,\ 0\le v-u\le d\},
 \qquad 0\le u,v<N.
\]
On its feasible domain $B\le A+d$, $-H$ has a finite Monge completion
with core size at most $14(N-1)$, constructible in $O(N\log(N+1))$ work.
Any $s\times s$ square of density cells with feasible corners has at most
$22s$ positive densities.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Set $K=1+\max f_1-\min f_1+\max f_2-\min f_2$ and define
\begin{align*}
 L(A,u)&=K\max(u-A,0),\\
 Q(u,v)&=K[\max(u-v,0)+\max(v-u-d,0)]-f_1(u)-f_2(v),\\
 R(v,B)&=K\max(B-v,0).
\end{align*}
All entries are finite. The only densities of $L$ and $R$ are $K$ on
the main diagonal. Matrix $Q$ has density $K$ on each of $v-u=0$ and
$v-u=d$, adding the two contributions when $d=0$. Its row and column
terms do not affect densities. Therefore all three matrices are Monge,
with $\delta(L),\delta(R)\le N-1$ and $\delta(Q)\le2(N-1)$.
Every inadmissible pair in $T=L\otimes Q\otimes R$ incurs at least
$K$ penalty, so its cost exceeds $-\min f_1-\min f_2$. A feasible pair
has cost at most that quantity. Thus $T=-H$ on the feasible domain.
The published inequality $\delta(X\otimes Y)\le2(\delta(X)+\delta(Y))$
and condensed-product algorithm~\cite{coremonge} give
\[
 \delta(T)\le2[2((N-1)+2(N-1))+(N-1)]=14(N-1)
\]
and the construction bound. The operand cores and boundaries are directly
available in $O(N)$ work; no dense conversion is required.

For the local bound let $A\in[a,a']$, $B\in[b,b']$, with at most
$s+1$ indices in each range. Discard $u>a'$ and $v<b$. Aggregate
$u=0,\ldots,a$ into the first row and keep $a+1,\ldots,a'$ as singleton
rows. Keep $b,\ldots,b'-1$ as singleton columns and aggregate
$v=b',\ldots,N-1$ into the last column. Let each entry of $Q'$ be the
minimum of $Q$ on its row-group/column-group rectangle.
Blockwise minima on ordered disjoint groups preserve Monge: take minimizers
in two off-diagonal groups and uncross their coordinates using the Monge
inequality for $Q$. Away from the first row and last column, $Q'$ is a
consecutive submatrix of $Q$ with at most two density diagonals. These
interior diagonals contribute at most $2s$ entries; the first row and last
column contribute at most $s$ each. Hence $\delta(Q')\le4s$.
Finite prefix and suffix selectors on the group indices each have at most
$s$ core entries. They describe exactly the requested prefixes and suffixes;
the same $K$ excludes invalid choices on every feasible entry. Their product
therefore equals $-H$ on the rectangle. Applying the core inequality twice
gives $2[2(s+4s)+s]=22s$. A single-row or single-column case has no density.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Complete above-capacity core for two blocks]
\label{thm:two-block-above-core}
Suppose the two blocks $[z,z+m)$ and $[z+m+g,z+m+g+k)$, with
$m,k\ge1$ and $g\ge0$, have common positive key weight $\alpha$,
and every other key has weight zero. Gap weights may be arbitrary
nonnegative reals. At capacity $c=2^h-1$ with a valid terminal penalty,
the entire ordinary core strictly above capacity has at most $28n$
entries. Any $s\times s$ square strictly above capacity has at most
$40s$ entries. All these entries and their values can be constructed in
$O(n\log(n+1))$ arithmetic work and space.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
If $c\ge n$ the region is empty. Normalize $\alpha$ to one and put
\[
 F(t)=\sum_{r=1}^t\nu_2(r),\qquad R_\ell(r)=F(r+\ell)-F(r).
\]
Above capacity all gap terms cancel from ordinary densities. By binary
saturation, an interval with $P\ge c$ positive keys has score $F(c)$;
otherwise an optimum retains every positive key.

\emph{Both blocks complete.}
If $m+k>c$, the score is constant. Otherwise let $N=c-m-k+1$.
Let $u$ count selected zeros before the first block and $v$ selected zeros
before the second block, excluding the first block's positive keys.
For an interval $[i,j)$ containing both whole blocks, feasibility is exactly
\[
 0\le u,v<N,\quad 0\le v-u\le g,\quad
 u\le A=z-i,\quad v\ge B=c+z+g-j.
\]
Its reward is $R_m(u)+R_k(m+v)$, so Lemma~\ref{lem:band-dominance}
applies with $f_1(u)=R_m(u)$, $f_2(v)=R_k(m+v)$, and $d=g$.
Clip $A$ from above at $N-1$ and $B$ from below at zero; a frozen
endpoint gives zero density. A product-core cell $(a,b)$ maps to
\begin{equation}\label{eq:two-block-core-map}
 i=z-a-1,\qquad j=c+z+g-b-1.
\end{equation}
This region contributes at most $14(N-1)\le14c$ entries globally and
$22s$ in a side-$s$ square.

\emph{Separating the endpoint cases.}
Reserve the at most eight grid lines $i=t-1$ or $j=t-1$ for
$t\in\{z,z+m,z+m+g,z+m+g+k\}$, together with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:two-block-exceptional-diagonals}
 j-i=c+g-1\quad\hbox{or}\quad j-i=c+g.
\end{equation}
They contribute at most $10n$ cells globally and $10s$ locally.
Off these lines, the four-corner endpoints lie within fixed pieces of the
five-piece input: zero, positive, zero, positive, zero. Cells encountering
at most one positive block agree with that one-block input. By
Theorem~\ref{thm:plateau-above-core} and Corollary~\ref{cor:plateau-square},
the two such cores contribute at most $2c$ entries globally and $4s$ locally.

\emph{Exactly one positive endpoint cut.}
Suppose the left endpoint cuts the first block and the right endpoint
lies after the second. Set $a=z+m-i$ and $B=c+z+m+g-j$.
For $a+k\le c$, the first $a$ selected ranks are positive. The rank
offset $t$ before the second block satisfies precisely
\[
 \max(a,B)\le t\le\min(a+g,c-k),
\]
so the score is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:two-block-cut-score}
 F(a)+\max_{\max(a,B)\le t\le\min(a+g,c-k)}R_k(t).
\end{equation}
At $a+k=c$ this is the saturated score $F(c)$.
If $a+k>c$, both left-endpoint choices are saturated, including the
case $a-1+k=c$, and their density is zero.
For the remaining cells, $B-a=c+g-(j-i)$. Away from
\eqref{eq:two-block-exceptional-diagonals}, either $B\le a$ in all four
corners, making the score independent of $j$, or $B>a$ in all four.
In the latter case remove the row term $F(a)$ from
\eqref{eq:two-block-cut-score}. The remaining range maximum has endpoints
$B$ and $\min(a+g,c-k)$. Frozen upper endpoints give zero; all other
positive densities are nonadjacent visibility pairs of $R_k$.
There are at most $c$ globally. In a side-$s$ square their two index
intervals have at most $s$ positions each and are ordered and disjoint,
since their endpoint difference is $j-i-c+1\ge2$. The noncrossing
neighbor-set count in Corollary~\ref{cor:plateau-square} gives at most
$2s$ locally. Reflection, using $\nu_2(2^h-r)=\nu_2(r)$, gives the
same bounds for a right endpoint cutting the second block.

\emph{Both positive endpoints cut.}
Let $a,b$ be the remaining positive lengths. For $a+b\ge c$ the score
is $F(c)$. Otherwise the interval length supplies enough zeros between
them, and the first $a$ and last $b$ selected ranks are positive. Its
score is $F(a)+F(b)$. The four scores separate unless $a+b=c-1$ or
$c$, precisely the two diagonals already reserved in
\eqref{eq:two-block-exceptional-diagonals}. All other densities vanish.
Summing the cases proves
\[
 K_{\rm above}\le14c+2c+2c+10n\le28n,\qquad
 K_{\rm square}\le22s+4s+4s+10s=40s.
\]
Multiplication by $\alpha$ preserves support and scales every density.

\emph{Construction, including the reserved cells.}
The proof enumerates $O(n)$ candidates: the complete-block product core,
two one-block visibility cores, two one-cut visibility cores, and the
reserved lines and diagonals. Filter to valid cells strictly above capacity
and evaluate their four scores. Precompute $F,R_m,R_k$ in $O(c)$ work
with the ruler's even/odd recurrence. Segment trees answer range maxima
in $O(\log(n+1))$ time. Saturated and partial one-block intervals use
$F(c)$ or $F(P)$; two-cut intervals use $F(a)+F(b)$; one-cut intervals
use \eqref{eq:two-block-cut-score} or its reflection. Complete one-block
intervals use \eqref{eq:plateau-reward}, and complete two-block intervals
query the product $T$ above.
For these $O(n)$ product queries use its condensed identity
\[
 T(A,B)=T(A,0)+T(0,B)-T(0,0)
       -\sum_{x<A,\,y<B}D_T(x,y).
\]
Sort queries by $A$, sweep the $O(c)$ core entries in row order, and
maintain column prefix sums in a Fenwick tree. Total query work is
$O(n\log(n+1))$, with $O(n)$ additional space. The sparse products have
the same work bound. The implementation retains ordinary witness maps
inside multiplication, so the conservative implemented space bound is
$O(n\log(n+1))$; no succinct-array assumption is made. Retain exactly
the positive four-corner differences at the enumerated candidates.
\end{proof}
This establishes a boundary charge for interacting equal-weight blocks on
the whole above-capacity side. Unequal positive block weights, additional
blocks, and the below-capacity mixed core remain open cases of the general
size problem.

\subsection{Unimodal key weights and a sparse window envelope}
\label{sec:unimodal-envelope}
A single peak admits a stronger reduction than the constant-block argument.
Here unimodality is in the original key order: weights are nondecreasing up
to a peak and nonincreasing afterward. No keys are reordered.

\begin{lemma}[Contiguous domination]
\label{lem:unimodal-domination}
Every $t$-element subsequence of a unimodal sequence is componentwise
dominated by a contiguous $t$-element block. Consequently, for every
nonnegative reward vector $v_1,\ldots,v_t$ and unimodal $x_0,\ldots,x_{k-1}$,
\[
 \max_{i_1<\cdots<i_t}\sum_{r=1}^t v_rx_{i_r}
 =\max_{0\le a\le k-t}\sum_{r=1}^t v_rx_{a+r-1}.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Choose a peak $z$ and let $b$ selected indices lie at or before $z$.
Move those indices to $z-b+1,\ldots,z$, and the remaining indices to
$z+1,\ldots,z+t-b$. There are enough positions on each side because the
original subsequence already used that many. Each left index moves right
inside the nondecreasing part; each right index moves left inside the
nonincreasing part. Every selected value weakly increases, in the same
order. The new positions are contiguous, including the cases $b=0$ and
$b=t$. The empty subsequence is immediate.
\end{proof}

For arbitrary key weights and capacity $c=2^h-1\le n$, define
\begin{align}
 B_h(a)&=\sum_{r=1}^c p_{a+r-1}\nu_2(r),\qquad 0\le a\le n-c,
 \label{eq:unimodal-windows}\\
 \widehat S_h(i,j)&=\max_{i\le a\le j-c}B_h(a),\qquad j-i\ge c,
 \nonumber\\
 \widehat A_h(i,j)&=M(j-i-c)+hW(i,j)-\widehat S_h(i,j).
 \label{eq:unimodal-envelope}
\end{align}
A contiguous selected block is feasible, so $\widehat A_h\ge A_h$.
Whenever $p[i:j]$ is unimodal, Lemma~\ref{lem:unimodal-domination} gives
equality. This identity includes arbitrary nonnegative gap weights through
$W(i,j)$.

\begin{theorem}[All above-capacity cores for unimodal keys]
\label{thm:unimodal-above}
With a valid terminal penalty, for arbitrary nonnegative unimodal key weights
and arbitrary nonnegative gap weights, a layer's entire ordinary core strictly above capacity has at
most $\max(0,n-c-1)$ entries. Every $s\times s$ square wholly above
capacity has at most $2s-1$ entries. The global leading coefficient one is
asymptotically sharp. All above-capacity cores over every layer with $c\le n$,
including their values, can be constructed in $O(n\log(n+1))$ arithmetic
work and space. The same preprocessing supports an exact layer-value query
and identifies an optimal contiguous selected block for any interval of
length at least $c$, in $O(\log(n+1))$ time.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For $j-i>c$, put $a=i$, $b=j-c+1$, and
$Q=\max_{a<t<b}B_h(t)$. The affine terms in
\eqref{eq:unimodal-envelope}, including every gap contribution, cancel.
Four range maxima give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:unimodal-visibility}
 D_{\widehat A_h}(i,j)
 =\max\{0,\min(B_h(a),B_h(b))-Q\}.
\end{equation}
These are exactly the nonadjacent strict visibility pairs of the
$N=n-c+1$ window values, with mapping
\[
 (a,b)\longmapsto(i,j)=(a,c+b-1).
\]
Every such pair maps to a valid above-capacity cell. The noncrossing count
in Lemma~\ref{lem:range-max-visibility} gives at most $\max(0,N-2)$ entries.
A side-$s$ square maps to two ordered disjoint endpoint ranges, each with
at most $s$ indices. The neighbor-span argument of
Corollary~\ref{cor:plateau-square} gives $2s-1$ entries there.
Global unimodality makes $A_h=\widehat A_h$ on all four corners, proving
both original-core bounds.
For sharpness, let $C=2^h$, $n=2C$, and put a unit key at zero-based
position $C+1$. The one-key count in
Theorem~\ref{thm:plateau-above-core} is $C-2h$, whereas $n-c-1=C$.
Their ratio tends to one.

For construction initialize $B_0(a)=0$, $0\le a\le n$.
Writing $d=2^{h-1}$, the two copies of the preceding ruler word give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:unimodal-window-recurrence}
 B_h(a)=B_{h-1}(a)+B_{h-1}(a+d)+(h-1)p_{a+d-1}.
\end{equation}
There are $O(n)$ entries at each of $O(\log(n+1))$ relevant layers.
The visibility stack constructs every layer's coordinates and densities
in $O(n)$ work. It pushes each index once and pops it at most once.
Thus all cores take $O(n\log(n+1))$ work, without an interval-pair scan.
For each $B_h$, a maximum segment tree retains the earliest maximizing
window start. Its linear preprocessing preserves the construction bound;
a range query costs $O(\log(n+1))$. Prefix sums give $W(i,j)$ in constant
time. The returned start identifies a contiguous optimal selected block;
emitting its $c$ keys or perfect partial skeleton takes $O(c)$ additional
output work. Keeping all arrays, query trees and cores uses
$O(n\log(n+1))$ space.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Certified exact spans in arbitrary inputs]
\label{cor:unimodal-certified}
For arbitrary nonnegative key and gap weights, all positive above-capacity
original densities whose outer key interval is unimodal can be listed in
$O(n\log(n+1))$ work and space over all layers. After $O(n)$ preprocessing,
unimodality of an interval can be tested in $O(1)$ time. In particular,
an above-capacity tile $[a,a+s)\times[b,b+s)$ whose key span $[a,b+s)$
is unimodal has at most $2s-1$ original positive densities.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Put $\Delta_t=p_{t+1}-p_t$ and let
\[
 V_t=\max\{u:u<v\le t,\ \Delta_u<0,\ \Delta_v>0\},
\]
using $-1$ for an empty set. Maintain the last negative-difference index
and update $V$ whenever a positive difference occurs. This computes all
$V_t$ in one scan. An interval $[i,j)$ is unimodal exactly when
$j-i\le1$ or $V_{j-2}<i$: a decrease followed by a later increase is
precisely a violation, including intervening flat positions.

Construct the global envelope \eqref{eq:unimodal-envelope} for the arbitrary
input. Equation~\eqref{eq:unimodal-visibility} still gives at most $O(n)$
entries per layer. Filter its cell $(i,j)$ by the unimodality of $[i,j+1)$.
On every retained outer span, all four corner intervals have exact envelope
values. Conversely, any positive original density with such an outer span
must occur in the envelope core. Thus filtering gives the complete claimed
set. If a tile's whole key span is unimodal, the same equality holds at
every corner in it, and the local visibility count applies.
\end{proof}
This supplies the boundary charge on certified spans even when the full
input has many peaks. Positive cells outside this certified part require a
nonunimodal outer span. The remaining interactions and the below-capacity
mixed region still require separate size bounds.

\subsection{Valley weights and logarithmic implicit queries}
\label{sec:valley-queries}
A valley is nonincreasing up to a minimum and nondecreasing afterward,
in the fixed key order. Its above-capacity scores have a different compact
representation from the single-peak case. Section~\ref{sec:valley-dense}
shows that their explicit cores can nevertheless be quadratic.

\begin{lemma}[Universal end-block domination]\label{lem:valley-domination}
For every $c$, one $c$-element subsequence of a valley componentwise
dominates every other $c$-element subsequence. It consists of the $c$
largest weights as a prefix and suffix, resolving ties toward the left.
It simultaneously maximizes every nonnegative rank-reward vector.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Split a valley $x_0,\ldots,x_{k-1}$ at a minimum position $z$, assigning
the minimum to the nondecreasing right part. If a subsequence selects $t$
keys from the left part, move them to the first $t$ positions and the
remaining keys to the last $c-t$ positions. Each selected value weakly
increases within its monotone wing. Let $X(t)$ be this end-block word.
Its feasible splits satisfy
\[
 \max(0,c-(k-z))\le t\le\min(c,z).
\]
The words $X(t)$ and $X(t+1)$ differ only at rank $t+1$, replacing
$x_{k-c+t}$ by $x_t$. On this feasible range, $x_t$ is nonincreasing and
$x_{k-c+t}$ is nondecreasing. Their difference is therefore nonincreasing.
Move through all nonnegative differences and stop before the first negative
one. The stopping word dominates every preceding and subsequent $X(t)$,
and hence every original subsequence. It selects the largest $c$ values
from the two decreasing end lists. Binary search finds its split in
$O(\log(k+1))$ comparisons, including ties and empty wings.
\end{proof}

For arbitrary key weights define the ruler-prefix score
\[
 P_p(a,t)=\sum_{r=1}^{t}p_{a+r-1}\nu_2(r),\qquad P_p(a,0)=0.
\]
Retain the arrays $B_k$ from \eqref{eq:unimodal-windows}, with $B_0=0$.
For $2^k\le t<2^{k+1}$ the ruler identity gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:valley-prefix}
 P_p(a,t)=B_k(a)+k p_{a+2^k-1}+P_p(a+2^k,t-2^k).
\end{equation}
Scanning decreasing powers evaluates this in $O(\log(n+1))$ arithmetic
work. The construction assumes no constant-time population-count operation.

\begin{theorem}[Implicit above-capacity queries for valley keys]
\label{thm:valley-queries}
For nonnegative valley key weights and arbitrary nonnegative gap weights,
$O(n\log(n+1))$ preprocessing and space support an exact layer-value
query for any $h\ge1$, $c=2^h-1\le j-i$, with a valid terminal penalty,
in $O(\log(n+1))$ work. A query returns two end blocks identifying an
optimal selected subsequence; emitting its keys or perfect partial skeleton
costs $O(c)$ additional output work. All layers share the preprocessing.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Build the perfect-ruler windows for $p$ and its reversal $\bar p$, using
\eqref{eq:unimodal-window-recurrence}. Their total construction and storage
are $O(n\log(n+1))$. Every interval of a valley is a valley. Find its
optimal split $t$ using Lemma~\ref{lem:valley-domination}, clipping the
two wings to the interval before binary search. Ruler symmetry
$\nu_2(c+1-r)=\nu_2(r)$ yields
\begin{align}
 S_h(i,j)&=P_p(i,t)+P_{\bar p}(n-j,c-t),\nonumber\\
 A_h(i,j)&=M(j-i-c)+hW(i,j)-S_h(i,j).
 \label{eq:valley-query}
\end{align}
The split search and two prefix queries take logarithmic work; prefix
mass sums give $W(i,j)$ in constant time. The witness is $[i,i+t)$ followed
by $[j-(c-t),j)$. No interval table or explicit core is constructed.
\end{proof}
This gives exact implicit queries even when the original above-capacity
core is dense. Below-capacity mixed intervals and efficient composition
of these representations remain requirements for a general full-tree
algorithm.

\subsection{Individual-weight charges for valley cores}
\label{sec:valley-core-mass}
The valley query representation also supports a direct charge to individual
integer key weights, without the height factor in a density-mass estimate.
Let $T_c(i,j)$ be the sum of the $c$ largest key weights in $[i,j)$, and put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:valley-top-density}
 \Gamma_c(i,j)=T_c(i,j)+T_c(i+1,j+1)-T_c(i,j+1)-T_c(i+1,j)
 \qquad(j-i>c).
\end{equation}
For $c\ge2$, write $x=p_i$, $y=p_j$, and let $z,w$ be the $c$th and
$(c-1)$st largest inner weights in $[i+1,j)$. Only these two smallest
retained values can be displaced by adjoining the endpoints, giving
\begin{equation}\label{eq:valley-top-thresholds}
 \Gamma_c(i,j)=\max\{0,\min(x,y,w)-z\}.
\end{equation}
For $c=1$ take $w=+\infty$. Thus $\Gamma_c\ge0$ for arbitrary input weights.
