\begin{lemma}[Valley support inclusion]\label{lem:valley-support}
For nonnegative real valley key weights, arbitrary nonnegative gaps, and
$c=2^h-1$, every strictly above-capacity cell satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:valley-support}
 0\le D_{A_h}(i,j)\le(h-1)\Gamma_c(i,j).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
At $h=1$ the ruler score vanishes. Otherwise, Lemma~\ref{lem:valley-domination}
makes a largest-$c$ selection optimal at every corner. All largest-$c$
selections have the same value word: the dominating word has the same sum,
so its componentwise inequalities must all be equalities.

If $x\le z$, adjoining $x$ leaves the inner largest-$c$ sum and value word
unchanged. Adjoining $y$ can only raise the cutoff, so $x$ remains
ineffective afterward. Both ruler-score changes vanish. Reflection treats
$y\le z$. Suppose now $x,y>z$ but $x<w$. Every inner value on the left
wing is at most $x$, so at most one selected value is on that wing; every
retained value except $z$ is at least $w>x$. Adjoining $x$ replaces the
first selected value $z$ by $x$. The endpoint $y$ is at least $w$, since
$w$ is on its wing, and adjoining $y$ raises the cutoff to at least $w$.
Thus the four-corner ruler difference is $(x-z)\nu_2(1)=0$.
Reflection treats $y<w$.

It remains that $x,y\ge w$. Choose adjacent smallest retained values
$z\le w$ in the valley word, choosing within its trough if they tie.
Each one-end extension replaces $z$, and the two-end extension replaces
$z,w$. If $z$ has selected rank $r$, cancellation gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:valley-support-rank}
 D_{A_h}(i,j)=(w-z)\nu_2(r).
\end{equation}
Explicitly, let $L_a,R_b$ be the inner left and reversed-right ruler-prefix
scores, $a+b=c$, and let primes adjoin the corresponding endpoint.
If $w,z$ are both left, the scores in order (inner, left, right, both) are
\[
 L_a+R_b,\quad L'_a+R_b,\quad L_{a-1}+R'_{b+1},\quad
 L'_{a-1}+R'_{b+1}.
\]
Their difference is
$(L'_a-L'_{a-1})+(L_{a-1}-L_a)=(w-z)\nu_2(a)$.
If $z$ is left and $w$ right, only the last expression changes to
$L'_a+R'_b$. The difference becomes
$w\nu_2(b+1)-z\nu_2(a)=(w-z)\nu_2(a)$, since $b+1=2^h-a$.
Reflection covers the right-minimum cases by ruler symmetry; the expressions
also give zero when $w=z$. Now $\nu_2(r)\le h-1$, and
\eqref{eq:valley-top-thresholds} equals $w-z$ in this last case.
The affine and gap terms cancel, proving the lemma.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Valley boundary-mass support and construction]
\label{thm:valley-core-mass}
Let the valley key weights be nonnegative integers of mass $P=\sum_i p_i$;
gap weights may be arbitrary nonnegative reals. Every layer's strictly
above-capacity core has at most $P$ entries. Any rectangle there with row
set $I$ and column set $J$ has at most
\begin{equation}\label{eq:valley-boundary-mass}
 \min\left\{\sum_{i\in I}p_i,\ \sum_{j\in J}p_j\right\}
\end{equation}
positive entries. All above-capacity cores over every layer can be constructed
in $O((n+P)\log^2(n+1))$ arithmetic work and
$O((n+P)\log(n+1))$ space, including their density values.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let $z_i(j)$ be the $c$th largest inner weight in $[i+1,j)$. For arbitrary
input weights the endpoint difference is
\[
 \Delta_i(j)=T_c(i,j)-T_c(i+1,j)=\max\{0,p_i-z_i(j)\}.
\]
The cutoff $z_i(j)$ is nondecreasing with $j$, and
$\Gamma_c(i,j)=\Delta_i(j)-\Delta_i(j+1)$. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:valley-row-charge}
 \sum_{j=i+c+1}^{n-1}\Gamma_c(i,j)\le p_i.
\end{equation}
Reversal gives the column charge $\sum_i\Gamma_c(i,j)\le p_j$.
For integer keys every positive $\Gamma_c$ is at least one. Apply
Lemma~\ref{lem:valley-support} to obtain the global and rectangle bounds.

Preprocess Theorem~\ref{thm:valley-queries}. Binary search in the two
monotone wings also finds a $c$th-largest cutoff or counts values strictly
above a threshold in logarithmic work. In row $i$, start at $j=i+c+1$
and compute $z=z_i(j)$. If $z\ge p_i$, no auxiliary density remains.
Otherwise let $g$ count inner values greater than $z$ and set $m=c-g$,
the multiplicity of the smallest retained value. Find the first future
right-wing position $k$ with $p_k>z$. All intervening values are at most
$z$, and all values after $k$ are greater than $z$. Exactly $m$ such
insertions are needed before the cutoff increases; its next event is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:valley-core-event}
 j'=k+m-1.
\end{equation}
If $j'\ge n$, finish the row. Otherwise query $z'=z_i(j'+1)>z$,
emit $\Gamma_c(i,j')=\min(p_i,z')-z$, and continue at $j=j'+1$.
If $j$ is still on the decreasing wing, every later key on that wing is
at most the current cutoff, justifying the same right-wing search.
The multiplicity in \eqref{eq:valley-core-event} skips all zero changes
caused by repeated weights. Every positive auxiliary cell is emitted once.

Each event and final row termination take logarithmic work. Evaluate four
ruler queries at each emitted cell and retain its original density when
positive. Lemma~\ref{lem:valley-support} proves completeness; height one
has zero ruler core and is skipped. With $L=\lceil\log_2(n+1)\rceil$ and
$\delta$ total auxiliary events, the work is $O((nL+\delta)L)$ and space
is $O(nL+K)$ for $K$ original output entries. Equation
\eqref{eq:valley-row-charge} gives $K\le\delta\le PL$, proving the bounds.
\end{proof}
This proves the local boundary-plus-mass support target on above-capacity
valley spans using only endpoint mass. The normalized rational dense family
has fractional auxiliary densities, consistent with the support inclusion
and row charge while escaping the integer support count. Below-capacity
mixed cores still require a separate argument.

The mass dependence is necessary up to constants. In the integer family
\eqref{eq:valley-family}, $P=n(n+1)/2$ and one layer has at least
$n^2/32$ entries. On its displayed square the row-endpoint mass is
$3s^2+s$, the column-endpoint mass is $3s^2$, and the core has $s^2/2$
entries. Thus the local bound \eqref{eq:valley-boundary-mass} is attained
within a factor of six by the proved family.

\subsection{An exact periodic quotient for the full mixed problem}
\label{sec:periodic-obst}

Periodicity offers a different way to avoid most interval states. Both types
of weights are retained, and the construction uses the unrestricted optimum.

\begin{theorem}[Cyclic phase construction]\label{thm:periodic-obst}
Let $n\ge1$. If the key and gap weights are restrictions of nonnegative
real sequences with common period $b$, a complete optimal BST can be
constructed in $O(nb)$ arithmetic work and space. The dynamic program
examines exactly $b(2n-1)$ root candidates. The input length need not be a
multiple of $b$. Given arbitrary explicit weights, their shortest common
finite period can be detected in $O(n)$ work; it is at most $n+1$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Extend both period words indefinitely. Translation by $b$ preserves the
cost of every ordered tree. Let $C_a(\ell)$ denote the optimum for length
$\ell$ and starting phase $a\in\{0,\ldots,b-1\}$, and let $\rho_a(\ell)$
be its leftmost optimal root relative to the interval start. Then
$C_a(0)=0$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:periodic-recurrence}
 C_a(\ell)=W_a(\ell)+\min_{0\le r<\ell}
 \left\{C_a(r)+C_{(a+r+1)\bmod b}(\ell-r-1)\right\}.
\end{equation}
Process lengths in increasing order. Knuth's monotone-root theorem
\cite{knuth}, with consistent leftmost ties, restricts the search at
$\ell\ge2$ to
\begin{equation}\label{eq:periodic-root-range}
 \rho_a(\ell-1)\le r\le 1+\rho_{(a+1)\bmod b}(\ell-1).
\end{equation}
The endpoints lie in $[0,\ell-1]$. For $a=b-1$, the right neighbor begins
at absolute position $b$; translation identifies it with phase zero.
The total number of candidates at any fixed length $\ell\ge2$ is therefore
\begin{equation}\label{eq:periodic-telescope}
 \sum_{a=0}^{b-1}
 \left(2+\rho_{(a+1)\bmod b}(\ell-1)-\rho_a(\ell-1)\right)=2b.
\end{equation}
Length one uses $b$ candidates, giving exactly $b(2n-1)$ overall.
Individual phase ranges can be large; the bound uses the whole cycle.
In particular, the algorithm computes all phases at every length, including
synthetic intervals extending beyond the original input. Omitting those
states would invalidate this cyclic cancellation.

A prefix sum $U$ of $p_t+q_t$ over $n+b$ consecutive positions gives
\[
 W_a(\ell)=U(a+\ell)-U(a)+q_{(a+\ell)\bmod b}
\]
in constant work. Every lookup in \eqref{eq:periodic-recurrence} uses a
shorter length. Thus candidate evaluation, mass queries, and minimizer
maintenance take $O(nb)$ work. Cost and relative-root tables each store
$(n+1)b$ entries. For reconstruction, interval $[i,j)$ uses absolute root
$i+\rho_{i\bmod b}(j-i)$. An explicit stack emits its two child intervals,
visiting every key and gap once. This includes the degenerate all-zero case,
whose leftmost optimum can have height $n$.

For period detection, form the signature word
\begin{equation}\label{eq:periodic-signature}
 (p_i,q_i,q_{i+1}),\qquad 0\le i<n.
\end{equation}
The prefix-function algorithm finds its shortest finite period
$d=n-\pi_{n-1}$ in linear work. If $d<n$, signature equality gives every
required key and gap equality, including the final gap, so $b=d$.
If $d=n$, use $b=n$ when $q_n=q_0$, and $b=n+1$ otherwise. Every common
period below $n$ is a period of \eqref{eq:periodic-signature}, proving
minimality. For $b=n+1$, set the unobserved padding key $p_n=0$ and use
period words $(p_0,\ldots,p_{n-1},0)$ and $(q_0,\ldots,q_n)$.
Their restriction to the input is exact. The empty input is handled
separately with cost zero.
\end{proof}

For constant $b$ this is linear construction with arbitrary nonnegative
real magnitudes inside the two periods. More generally $b=O(n^\alpha)$,
$\alpha<1$, gives $O(n^{1+\alpha})$ work. A defect in the final gap can
invalidate an otherwise repeating pattern; both adjacent gaps in
\eqref{eq:periodic-signature} are needed. The method is a direct cyclic
application of Knuth's theorem, independent of the integer-mass analysis
and the later lower-bound certificate. Its general nonperiodic worst case
remains quadratic. Verification is in Appendix~\ref{app:periodic-obst}.

\subsection{Arbitrary fringes around a periodic middle}
\label{sec:fringe-obst}

An exact central oracle can absorb arbitrary boundary weights without
recomputing its central intervals. The needed linear primitive is the
concave least-weight-subsequence algorithm of Wilber~\cite{wilber}.

\begin{lemma}[Exact boundary extension]\label{lem:fringe-extension}
Suppose all unrestricted mixed OBST costs $C(i,r)$ for $0\le i\le r<J$
are available in constant time. The complete new column $C(i,J)$,
$0\le i\le J$, and optimal roots can be computed in $O(J)$ arithmetic
work and space. Reflection gives the same bound for a new left row.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Write $D(i)=C(i,J)$. The original recurrence is
\[
 D(J)=0,\qquad
 D(i)=W(i,J)+\min_{i\le r<J}\{C(i,r)+D(r+1)\}.
\]
Set $F(t)=D(J-t)$ and identify predecessor $s<t$ with root $r=J-s-1$.
This gives $F(0)=0$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fringe-lws}
 F(t)=\min_{0\le s<t}\{F(s)+w(s,t)\},\qquad
 w(s,t)=C(J-t,J-s-1)+W(J-t,J).
\end{equation}
Every old interval has right endpoint at most $J-1$; its value is already
available. Prefix masses make each valid arc query constant time.
For $s<s'<t<t'$, put $a=J-t'$, $b=J-t$, $c=J-s'-1$, and $e=J-s-1$.
Then $a<b\le c<e$. The ordinary mixed OBST Monge inequality gives
\[
 C(a,c)+C(b,e)\le C(a,e)+C(b,c).
\]
The mass terms cancel, proving the precise concavity condition
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fringe-quadrangle}
 w(s,t)+w(s',t')\le w(s,t')+w(s',t).
\end{equation}
The case $b=c$ uses $C(b,b)=0$ and is included.

Wilber's published statement is that ``the concave least weight subsequence
problem can always be solved in $O(n)$ time''~\cite[p.~418]{wilber}.
Its input is a finite real arc function on $0\le s<t\le N$ satisfying
\eqref{eq:fringe-quadrangle}. With constant-time arc access, the algorithm
computes every $F(t)$ and predecessor witnesses in $O(N)$ work and space
\cite[pp.~420--424]{wilber}; no difference-of-weight condition is required.
Here $N=J$, all valid arcs are finite, and \eqref{eq:fringe-lws} supplies
exactly this interface. A leftmost predecessor gives a rightmost original
root. Reflecting keys and inclusive gaps proves the left-row statement.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Periodic middle with arbitrary fringes]\label{thm:fringe-obst}
Let $[l,r)$ be a nonempty middle of $m=r-l$ keys in an $n$-key input.
Suppose its key weights and inclusive gap weights $q_l,\ldots,q_r$ have
common period $b\le m$. With $d=n-m$ arbitrary fringe keys and arbitrary
nonnegative real weights throughout, a complete unrestricted optimal BST
and a constant-time oracle for every interval cost and an optimal root
can be constructed in $O(mb+nd)$ arithmetic work and space.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Build the periodic middle oracle by Theorem~\ref{thm:periodic-obst}, using
exactly $b(2m-1)$ root candidates. Append the right fringe one key at a
time by Lemma~\ref{lem:fringe-extension}, then prepend the left fringe
by reflection. Extension $t$ creates exactly $m+t$ nonempty states, so
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fringe-states}
 S_{\mathrm{boundary}}=\sum_{t=1}^{d}(m+t)
 =dm+\frac{d(d+1)}2
 =\frac{n(n+1)-m(m+1)}2\le nd.
\end{equation}
Store each completed boundary in an array. An old interval either lies
wholly in the middle, ends in a stored right column, or starts in a stored
left row. The last case takes precedence when both endpoints are outside.
These cases give constant-time lookup, including empty intervals of cost
zero. During left extension, every old left endpoint is strictly larger
than the new one, so the queried rows already exist.

Explicitly, for a new left row at $i$, let the reversed endpoint be
$N=n-i$. An old reversed interval $[a,u)$ queries $C(n-u,n-a)$;
the new interval mass is $W(i,n-a)$; and reversed root $u$ maps to
original root $n-u-1$. Thus reflection preserves the inclusive gap indices
as well as key order. No unfinished row or column is queried.

Summing the linear boundary costs using \eqref{eq:fringe-states}, and adding
the central oracle, gives the claimed bounds. Prefix preprocessing and
complete reconstruction cost $O(n)$, absorbed because $mb+nd\ge n$.
An explicit stack follows the exact stored roots and visits every key and
gap once, with no height assumption. The representation covers every
original interval, including those unused by the final tree.
\end{proof}

More generally, any exact constant-time central interval oracle with
construction and storage cost $T$ can be extended at cost $O(T+nd+n)$.
The periodic quotient is one concrete source of such an oracle. A final-gap
defect fits inside a one-key fringe. Neither boundary magnitudes nor total
mass enter \eqref{eq:fringe-states}.

\begin{corollary}[Automatic middle recognition]\label{cor:fringe-recognition}
Let $b_0$ be the shortest whole-input joint period, allowing $b_0=n+1$.
For $1\le b\le B<n$, let $m_b>b$ be the longest middle with common
period $b$, omitting periods with no such middle. There is an automatic
exact construction with work
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fringe-auto}
 O\!\left(n(B+1)+\min\left\{nb_0,\
       \min_{1\le b\le B}\bigl[m_b b+n(n-m_b)\bigr]\right\}\right).
\end{equation}
With supplied constant $b,d$, construction is linear; default recognition
with $B=\min\{n-1,\lceil\log_2(n+1)\rceil\}$ adds $O(n\log(n+1))$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Use signatures $s_i=(p_i,q_i,q_{i+1})$. A middle $[l,r)$ of length greater
than $b$ has joint period $b$ exactly when $s_i=s_{i-b}$ for every
$l+b\le i<r$. On a failed comparison at $i$, an eligible new left endpoint
must exceed $i-b$; set it to $i-b+1$. Between failures, the longest current
suffix supplies the longest periodic middle. This costs $O(n)$ per $b$
and includes the final gap $q_r$. For fixed $b<n$, the proxy
$mb+n(n-m)$ decreases with $m$, so a longest middle is best.
Compare all these proxies with $nb_0$, and use the corresponding constructor.
If $b_0\le B$, the whole-period route already costs $O(nB)$ and the scan
can be skipped. This proves \eqref{eq:fringe-auto}, also with $B=0$ and
an omitted inner minimum. Empty input is handled separately.
\end{proof}

The LWS primitive is established prior work; the boundary reduction and
its composition with the central quotient supply the bounds above.
A small number of arbitrary interior defects need not leave a long middle,
so the general nonperiodic worst case remains quadratic. Independent
verification and implementation details are in Appendix~\ref{app:fringe-obst}.

\subsection{Positive affine translation of both weight sequences}
\label{sec:affine-period}

Literal repetition can be replaced by a transformation that preserves all
optimal shapes. The key and gap offsets must have opposite signs.

\begin{lemma}[Affine shape-cost identity]\label{lem:affine-shape-cost}
For a tree $T$ on $\ell$ keys, replacing every key weight by
$\lambda p+\alpha$ and every gap weight by $\lambda q-\alpha$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:affine-shape-cost}
 \operatorname{cost}_{\mathrm{new}}(T)
 =\lambda\operatorname{cost}_{\mathrm{old}}(T)-\alpha\ell.
\end{equation}
For $\lambda>0$, all minimizing shapes and their leftmost roots are preserved.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If $I$ is internal path length, key comparison lengths sum to $I+\ell$
and gap depths sum to $I+2\ell$. The latter identity follows inductively:
joining the two subtrees increases the internal sum by $\ell-1$ and the
external sum by $\ell+1$, giving difference $2\ell$. The offset contribution
is therefore $\alpha(I+\ell)-\alpha(I+2\ell)=-\alpha\ell$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Finite affine phase construction]\label{thm:affine-period}
Let $n\ge1$ and suppose the nonnegative input weights satisfy, for some
$1\le b\le n$, $\lambda>0$, and $\alpha\in\mathbb R$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:affine-weight-law}
 p_{i+b}=\lambda p_i+\alpha\quad(0\le i<n-b),\qquad
 q_{j+b}=\lambda q_j-\alpha\quad(0\le j\le n-b).
\end{equation}
A complete unrestricted optimal BST, with constant-time access to every
interval cost and leftmost optimal root, can be constructed in $O(nb)$
arithmetic work and space. The smallest admissible $b$ can be found within
the same bound. Positivity outside the finite input is not assumed.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let $P_k=\lambda^k$ and $G_k=\sum_{t=0}^{k-1}\lambda^t$, with
$P_0=1,G_0=0$. Tabulate these by multiplication and addition.
Lemma~\ref{lem:affine-shape-cost} implies, for $i=a+kb$ and $0\le a<b$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:affine-lookup}
 C(i,i+\ell)=P_k C(a,a+\ell)-\alpha\ell G_k,
\end{equation}
whenever the intervals lie in the input. Their relative leftmost roots
are equal. All intermediate translations also lie inside the input.

Put $m=n-b+1$. For each length $1\le\ell\le m$, all $b$ phase intervals
$[a,a+\ell)$ are actual input intervals. Compute their costs $C_a(\ell)$
and relative roots $\rho_a(\ell)$ by the original recurrence, using
\eqref{eq:affine-lookup} for child costs. Every child has smaller length.
Actual prefix masses give each interval weight in constant time.
Knuth's theorem~\cite{knuth} restricts roots at $\ell\ge2$ to
\[
 \rho_a(\ell-1)\le r\le1+\rho_{(a+1)\bmod b}(\ell-1).
\]
For $a=b-1$, the right neighbor begins at $b$ and ends at
$b+\ell-1\le n$. Its roots equal those of phase zero by
\eqref{eq:affine-lookup}. Thus every monotonicity argument uses actual
nonnegative input weights. Summing the inclusive ranges around all phases
gives $2b$ candidates per length, hence exactly $b(2m-1)$ overall.

For the remaining lengths $\ell=m+1,\ldots,n$, compute the
$t=n-\ell+1<b$ actual intervals directly. Ordinary Knuth bounds telescope:
if $\rho_i$ is the relative root of $[i,i+\ell-1)$, the total number of
candidates at length $\ell$ is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:affine-tail-candidates}
 2t+\rho_t-\rho_0\le2t+\ell-2=2n-\ell.
\end{equation}
Both end roots lie in $[0,\ell-2]$. The last $b-1$ diagonals therefore use
at most $(b-1)n+(b-1)(b-2)/2$ candidates and store exactly $b(b-1)/2$
nonempty states. Short child intervals use \eqref{eq:affine-lookup};
long children use the already completed terminal diagonals. The total
number of stored nonempty states is exactly
\begin{equation}\label{eq:affine-states}
 bm+\frac{b(b-1)}2=nb-\frac{b(b-1)}2\le nb.
\end{equation}
This proves the work and space bounds. Prefixes and power sums cost $O(n)$.
An explicit stack follows the stored phase or terminal root, visiting every
key and gap once, including degenerate zero-weight chains.

It remains to recognize the law. For a candidate $b<n$, set
$X_i=p_i+q_i$ and $Y_i=p_i+q_{i+1}$. Equation~\eqref{eq:affine-weight-law}
implies $X_{i+b}=\lambda X_i$ and $Y_{i+b}=\lambda Y_i$.
Conversely, put $e_j=q_{j+b}-\lambda q_j$. These two proportionality
conditions give $p_{i+b}-\lambda p_i=-e_i=-e_{i+1}$, so all $e_j$ are
constant and $\alpha=-e_0$. A positive source component determines
$\lambda$ by its target/source ratio; zero components require zero targets.
Reject a nonpositive ratio and verify all original equations. If all paired
components are zero, nonnegativity makes all constrained weights zero;
$\lambda=1,\alpha=0$ works. This is a complete $O(n)$ test.
For $b=n$, $\lambda=1,\alpha=q_0-q_n$ always works. Testing
$b=1,2,\ldots$ until the first success $b_*$ costs $O(nb_*)$, including
recognition and construction. The empty input has cost zero separately.
\end{proof}

The finite boundary is essential to this proof. The valid input
$p=(1,2,2,3,3)$, $q=(2,2,1,1,0,0)$ has
$b=2,\lambda=1,\alpha=1$, but its continued gap sequence becomes negative.
The construction uses complete cycles only through $m=4$ and then computes
the last length directly. It never applies nonnegative-weight root
monotonicity to that continuation.

For $b=1$, exactly $2n-1$ root candidates suffice. This includes pure
geometric mixed weights, and the strictly positive opposing arithmetic
sequences $p_i=i+1$, $q_j=n+1-j$. The latter have total mass $(n+1)^2$
and admit $O(n)$ full construction with $O(\log n)$-bit words. Geometric
weights can have growing bit lengths; the general theorem counts exact
arithmetic operations. Ordinary periodicity is $\lambda=1,\alpha=0$.
The established boundary-extension lemma also permits an affine-period
middle with arbitrary fringes in $O(mb+nd+n)$ work. Arbitrary interior
changes and the general nonperiodic subquadratic problem remain open.
Verification is in Appendix~\ref{app:affine-period}.

\subsection{Certified refinement of coarse optimal trees}
\label{sec:coarse-face}
The integer Monge construction also supplies a structural certificate for
nearby arbitrary weights. The coarse optimum may have ties: retain roots
shared by every coarse optimum, then resolve the remaining intervals with
the original key and gap weights. The resulting work depends on those
ambiguous intervals, rather than on the magnitude or precision of the
original weights.

Let $(\bar p,\bar q)$ be nonnegative integer weights on the same $n\ge1$
keys and $n+1$ gaps as the arbitrary nonnegative weights $(p,q)$. For
$\delta>0$, define the total signed-perturbation magnitude
\begin{equation}\label{eq:coarse-error}
 E_\delta=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}|p_i-\delta\bar p_i|
              +\sum_{j=0}^n|q_j-\delta\bar q_j|.
\end{equation}
The word coarse refers to the integer base, not to any reordering of outcomes.

Perturbation arguments preserving optimality already occur in search-tree
work: Chrobak et al.~\cite{chrobak-perturb} use infinitesimal changes to
resolve ties in two-way comparison trees. Here the integer cost gap gives
an explicit finite signed-error certificate, and the common-root partition
supplies the construction bound.

\begin{lemma}[Preservation of the optimal family]\label{lem:coarse-stability}
If
\begin{equation}\label{eq:coarse-certificate}
 (n-1)E_\delta<\delta,
\end{equation}
then every optimum for $(p,q)$ is also optimal for $(\bar p,\bar q)$.
The errors in \eqref{eq:coarse-error} may have either sign, and the coarse
optimum need not be unique.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For a nonempty $n$-key tree, every key comparison length and every gap
depth lies in $[1,n]$. Write $C_e(T)$ for the cost of the signed error
vector. For any two full trees $T,U$,
\[
 |C_e(T)-C_e(U)|\le(n-1)E_\delta.
\]
If $U$ is coarse-optimal and $T$ is not, integrality gives
$C_{\bar p,\bar q}(T)-C_{\bar p,\bar q}(U)\ge1$. Therefore
\[
 C_{p,q}(T)-C_{p,q}(U)
 \ge\delta-(n-1)E_\delta>0.
\]
Such a $T$ cannot be fine-optimal. This proves the assertion simultaneously
for all coarse ties. The empty input has its sole tree separately.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Unrestricted optimal-root extremes]\label{lem:coarse-extremes}
Let $\bar S$ be the aggregate core bound for the integer base in
Theorem~\ref{thm:aggregate-cores}, including its linear boundary terms.
In $O(\bar S\log(n+1))$ work one can prepare queries for the smallest
and largest unrestricted optimal root of any nonempty interval. Each query
takes $O(\log(n+1))$ work, without constructing a dense interval table.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
First compute the final unrestricted cost matrix $C^*$ in condensed form
using the Monge construction. Its globally sufficient height applies to
every interval. Make one further product whose root-$r$ candidate on
$[i,j)$ is $C^*(i,r)+C^*(r+1,j)$, using the same finite padding and
boundary conventions as the main construction. The interval mass is
independent of $r$, so the published exact leftmost witness gives the
smallest unrestricted optimal root. Repeat on the reversed key and gap
sequences; reflection of its smallest root gives the largest original root.

The two extra products cost $O((n+K_H)\log(n+1))$ each, where $K_H$ is
the corresponding final core size. Their output sizes are $O(K_H)$ by
the product bound. Both are covered by the aggregate preparation bound,
and the published witness queries have the stated cost.
\end{proof}

Using unrestricted child values in that last product is essential.
A sufficient height preserves some optimum, rather than every tied optimum.
For $n=2^h-1$ with every coarse weight zero, the only tree of height at
most $h$ is perfect, while every full BST is unrestricted-optimal.
Two height-limited reconstructions could therefore suggest false uniqueness.
The witnesses of Lemma~\ref{lem:coarse-extremes} are reused at every
subtree depth, with no decreasing height budget.

\begin{theorem}[Common-root refinement]\label{thm:coarse-face}
Assume \eqref{eq:coarse-certificate}. Starting at $[0,n)$, query the two
root extremes of each visited nonempty interval. If they coincide, retain
that root and recurse on its two children. Otherwise stop at that interval.
Let the stopped interval sizes be $m_1,\ldots,m_t$, and put
$b=\max_j m_j$, or $b=0$ if $t=0$.

A complete optimum for the original weights can be constructed in
\begin{equation}\label{eq:coarse-face-work}
 O\left(\bar S\log(n+1)+n+\sum_{j=1}^t m_j^2\right)
\end{equation}
arithmetic and dictionary operations. In particular, if the integer coarse
mass $\bar W=\sum\bar p_i+\sum\bar q_j$ is $O(n)$, the bound is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:coarse-face-linear-mass}
 O\bigl(n^{3/2}\log^{3/2}(n+1)+nb\bigr).
\end{equation}
After preparation, only $O(n)$ data need be retained. Each further
certified perturbation of the same base, with any positive scale $\delta$,
can be solved completely in $O(n+\sum_jm_j^2)$ work. If the coarse
optimum is unique, this is $O(n)$ per original-weight tree.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
At a coincident pair of extreme roots, every coarse-optimal tree has that
root. Induction from the full interval shows that every such tree contains
the retained skeleton. By Lemma~\ref{lem:coarse-stability}, every fine
optimum contains it as well. There are at most $n$ visited nonempty
intervals. Partition extraction therefore costs $O(n\log(n+1))$;
the stopped intervals are disjoint in keys and satisfy $\sum_jm_j\le n$.
There are no stopped intervals exactly when the coarse optimum is unique.

Solve each stopped interval by ordinary Knuth on its original key weights
and its original gaps, including both endpoint gaps. Attach these subtrees
to the retained roots. A subtree's contribution from ancestors outside it
is its fixed total outcome mass times its fixed attachment depth. This
term is independent of the subtree shape, so all payload optima combine
independently. The family with the retained roots contains a global fine
optimum; its computed minimum is consequently a global fine optimum.

The payload work is $O(\sum_jm_j^2)$, and writing, attaching, and
traversing all original keys and gaps costs $O(n)$. Lemma~\ref{lem:coarse-extremes}
absorbs partition extraction into the preparation term, proving
\eqref{eq:coarse-face-work}. Since $\sum_jm_j^2\le nb$, the main
linear-mass bound proves \eqref{eq:coarse-face-linear-mass}.

Once the partition is extracted, discard the matrices and witnesses.
Retain only the $O(n)$ base weights, skeleton entries, interval endpoints
and construction metadata. Each new signed-error certificate costs $O(n)$;
the same independent payload argument then proves the reuse bound.
The integer coarse optimum being unique gives $t=0$.
\end{proof}

With the published succinct witnesses, preprocessing uses $O(\bar S)$
space. The retained implementation uses ordinary maps, which can require
$O(\bar S\log(n+1))$ peak space. Sequential payload solves add peak
$O(b^2+n)$ space. The arithmetic bounds exclude operand bit costs.
No run, period, monotonicity, or integrality condition is imposed on the
original fine-weight sequences.

\begin{corollary}[An automatic full constructor]\label{cor:coarse-automatic}
Let $X$ be the largest original weight. For $X>0$, test
$\delta=X/m$ for $m=1,\ldots,16$, round each original weight to its
nearest nonnegative integer multiple of $\delta$, and check
\eqref{eq:coarse-certificate}. Use $\delta=1$ when $X=0$.
At the first success apply Theorem~\ref{thm:coarse-face}; if none succeeds,
use ordinary Knuth. This is an exact full mixed-weight constructor with
$O(n^2)$ worst-case arithmetic work. On accepted inputs its work satisfies
\eqref{eq:coarse-face-linear-mass}, hence is subquadratic when $b=o(n)$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
There are a fixed number of $O(n)$ scale tests. At scale $X/m$, every
rounded coarse weight is in $\{0,\ldots,m\}$, so
$\bar W\le16(2n+1)$. The certificate and refinement theorem give
correctness on acceptance, and Knuth gives correctness on rejection.
Use $b\le n$ in \eqref{eq:coarse-face-linear-mass} for the overall
quadratic bound. Half-integer ties may be rounded upward; every comparison
and certificate is exact.
\end{proof}

An explicit limitation remains even for arbitrarily small perturbations.
For uniform unit coarse weights at $n=2^h$, $h\ge1$, start with the
perfect $(n-1)$-key tree and expand either its leftmost or rightmost bottom
gap. Both trees minimize internal path length, hence coarse cost, and their
root ranks differ. The partition stops at the full interval and uses the
entire quadratic payload table. The theorem therefore makes its ambiguity
parameter explicit. Verification and complete operation counts are in
Appendix~\ref{app:coarse-face}.

\subsection{Adaptive refinement through the reachable optimal grammar}
\label{sec:adaptive-coarse-grammar}
Root ambiguity can occur at the full interval while only a small number of
subproblems participate in any coarse optimum. We can retain precisely those
subproblems and optimize the original weights over their root choices.
The certificate remains Lemma~\ref{lem:coarse-stability}; no additional
restriction is placed on individual fine-weight errors.

For a fixed integer base, start at $[0,n)$. At every reached nonempty
interval $[i,j)$ retain every unrestricted optimal root $r$, with children
$[i,r)$ and $[r+1,j)$. Deduplicate intervals. Write $V$ for the number
of reached nonempty intervals, $A$ for the number of retained triples
$(i,j,r)$, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:adaptive-grammar-scan}
 Q=\sum_{[i,j)\text{ reached}}
       \bigl(r_{\max}(i,j)-r_{\min}(i,j)+1\bigr).
\end{equation}
Thus $V\le A\le Q$. A root strictly between the two extremes need not
be optimal, so $Q$ counts unsuccessful candidate tests as well.

\begin{theorem}[Adaptive optimal-grammar refinement]
\label{thm:adaptive-coarse-grammar}
The reachable grammar can be prepared in
\begin{equation}\label{eq:adaptive-grammar-preparation}
 O\bigl((\bar S+V+Q)\log(n+1)\bigr)
\end{equation}
arithmetic and comparison operations, using the aggregate coarse-core
quantity $\bar S$ of Lemma~\ref{lem:coarse-extremes}.
After preparation retain $O(n+V+A)$ data. Each arbitrary nonnegative
fine-weight input satisfying \eqref{eq:coarse-certificate} can then be
solved completely in
\begin{equation}\label{eq:adaptive-grammar-refinement}
 O(n+V+A)
\end{equation}
arithmetic operations, including certification and full tree reconstruction.
Peak preparation space with the retained ordinary-map implementation is
$O(\bar S\log(n+1)+n+V+A)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Prepare unrestricted root extremes by Lemma~\ref{lem:coarse-extremes}.
Also index the final condensed cost matrix $C^*$. For a core entry
$(a,b,d_{a,b})$, the condensed representation gives
\[
 C^*(i,j)=C^*(i,0)+C^*(0,j)-C^*(0,0)
                   -\sum_{a<i,\ b<j}d_{a,b}.
\]
Maintain a persistent binary sum tree on column coordinates after each
core-row prefix. Inserting a density copies $O(\log(n+1))$ nodes.
There are $O(n)$ versions and $K_H$ insertions, so this index costs
$O(n+K_H\log(n+1))$ work and space. A column-prefix query in version
$i$ evaluates any required entry in $O(\log(n+1))$ work. In particular,
the zero diagonal represents empty-child cost exactly.

Process each reached interval once. Query its root extremes. If they
coincide, retain the single rule. Otherwise test every root between them
by the exact equality
\begin{equation}\label{eq:adaptive-grammar-test}
 C^*(i,r)+C^*(r+1,j)+\bar W(i,j)=C^*(i,j).
\end{equation}
Coarse interval masses use prefix sums. Every retained root schedules its
nonempty children. A dictionary on endpoint pairs, implemented if desired
by a balanced search tree, deduplicates intervals in $O(\log(n+1))$
work per operation. There are $O(V+Q)$ such operations and tests.
The final core index is covered by the $\bar S\log(n+1)$ term. This
proves \eqref{eq:adaptive-grammar-preparation}.

The resulting grammar describes exactly the full coarse-optimal trees.
Every coarse-optimal tree uses an optimal root and optimal children at
each of its intervals, and hence appears in the grammar by induction.
Conversely, combining any retained root with any optimal children attains
the parent's coarse optimum by \eqref{eq:adaptive-grammar-test}.
Induction on interval length proves the reverse inclusion, including
all combinations of child choices.

Bucket the reached intervals by their integer lengths $1,\ldots,n$ in
$O(n+V)$ work. Assign array indices in that order, replacing child
endpoints by indices and using a sentinel for an empty child. Each child
has smaller length. This compilation takes $O(A\log(n+1)+n+V)$ work
with the same dictionary and is absorbed by the preparation bound.
Discard the cost index, matrices and witness structures.

For each new input check \eqref{eq:coarse-certificate}, form original
weight prefix sums, and compute in increasing length
\[
 D(i,j)=W(i,j)+
       \min_{r:\,(i,j,r)\text{ retained}}
              \{D(i,r)+D(r+1,j)\},\qquad D(i,i)=0.
\]
Use original gaps at both endpoints. The compiled child indices make
each root evaluation constant work with array access; no query-time
dictionary is needed. Store an attaining root with its two child indices.
The grammar contains every fine optimum by the certificate, so its
minimum is the full original optimum. Evaluate $V$ states and $A$ rules,
then traverse the selected rules to write all $n$ keys. Every selected
child interval is strictly smaller and the two child key sets are
disjoint, so full traceback costs $O(n)$. This proves
\eqref{eq:adaptive-grammar-refinement} and the retained-space bound.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Counted expansion and the automatic constructor.}
Apply the scale tests of Corollary~\ref{cor:coarse-automatic}. On acceptance,
attempt grammar extraction with a budget of
$4n\lceil\log_2(n+1)\rceil$ charges, one for each processed interval,
candidate root and retained rule. Check the budget before each charge;
each accepted rule schedules at most two children. If the budget is
exhausted, discard the partial grammar and use ordinary Knuth on the
original weights. A certificate rejection also uses Knuth.
Thus all failed expansion work is counted and bounded.

The accepted coarse mass is $O(n)$. Coarse preparation, the cost index
and the bounded extraction together take
$O(n^{3/2}\log^{3/2}(n+1)+n\log^2(n+1))$ work.
A completed plan has $V+A=O(n\log(n+1))$, so its first full construction
is subquadratic and subsequent certified queries use
$O(n+V+A)$ work. The fallback gives an exact $O(n^2)$ worst-case
constructor. For a supplied integer base of unrestricted mass, retain
the explicit $\bar S$ term instead. Operand bit costs are additional.
The dense uniform family of Theorem~\ref{thm:coarse-grammar} explains
why the budget can be necessary.

\begin{theorem}[Two distant roots with a linear reachable grammar]
\label{thm:two-root-grammar}
Let $m=2^k$ with $k\ge1$, and put $n=3m-1$, $a=m-1$, $c=2m-1$.
Assign unit weight to every key and gap, then increase the weights of
keys $a,c$ to two. The total coarse mass is $2n+3$. There are exactly
two coarse-optimal full trees. Their roots are $a$ and $c$, and their
reachable grammar has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:two-root-grammar-counts}
 V=n+1,\qquad A=n+2,\qquad Q=4m.
\end{equation}
The common-root partition has a single ambiguous payload of size $n$.
The adaptive grammar instead gives $O(n)$ work for every certified
fine-weight query after generic coarse preparation.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For unit mixed weights the cost is $2I+3n$. Every minimum-IPL tree at
this size fills the perfect upper skeleton with $2m-1$ keys and expands
exactly $m$ of its $2m$ bottom gaps. Its root has rank between $a$ and
$c$. Rank $a$ requires no left-half expansion and all $m$ right-half
expansions; rank $c$ requires the reverse. Each extreme therefore
determines a unique full shape.

The two extra unit key weights contribute the sum of the comparison
lengths of $a,c$. Distinct keys have comparison-length sum at least
three. Both extreme shapes attain three: one marked key is the root,
and the other is its child. Any other minimum-IPL shape has neither
marked key at the root and contributes at least four. A tree with larger
IPL already has a strictly larger baseline cost and cannot offset it
with a contribution below three. Consequently the two extreme shapes
are exactly the coarse optima.

Let $P_0,P_1,P_2$ be the perfect $(m-1)$-key trees on
$[0,a)$, $[a+1,c)$ and $[c+1,n)$, respectively. The two trees are
\[
 T_a=a(P_0,c(P_1,P_2)),\qquad
 T_c=c(a(P_0,P_1),P_2).
\]
Their common perfect subtrees contribute $3(m-1)$ distinct nonempty
intervals. The full interval and the two different $(2m-1)$-key
intervals contribute three more, giving $V=3m=n+1$.
Every proper reached interval has a unique optimal subtree: an alternative
would replace that subtree in a full optimum and create a third full
optimum. Thus the full interval has two rules and each of the other
$V-1$ intervals has one, yielding $A=V+1=n+2$.
The full root range contains $m+1$ candidates, and every proper interval
contributes one. Hence $Q=(m+1)+(3m-1)=4m$.
The different full roots make the common-root partition stop immediately.
\end{proof}

For this explicitly recognized base, even coarse Monge preparation can
be omitted. Construct the three perfect subtrees and choose between the
two displayed trees. For arbitrary original weights their cost difference
is exactly
\begin{equation}\label{eq:two-root-fine-choice}
 C_{p,q}(T_a)-C_{p,q}(T_c)
   =p_c+W(c+1,n)-p_a-W(0,a).
\end{equation}
The middle subtree has the same depth in both trees; the outer subtrees
and the two marked keys change depth by one. The certificate, mass sums,
choice and complete output therefore take $O(n)$ work in total.
The errors at all original key and gap positions may vary independently,
subject to nonnegativity and the one total-error certificate. Verification
and separate preparation/query counts appear in
Appendix~\ref{app:adaptive-coarse-grammar}.


\subsection{Screening roots by coarse cost and height}
\label{sec:coarse-screen}
Coarse weights can also screen roots when the small-error certificate
\eqref{eq:coarse-certificate} fails. Retain every root whose coarse regret
is within a height-based allowance for the original errors. An optimum
for the original weights survives this screening. A hierarchy of
compressed root-range products enumerates the surviving roots without
scanning every position between their extremes.

\begin{lemma}[A hierarchy of root-range minima]\label{lem:root-range-index}
Let $n\ge1$ and let $C$ be a finite $(n+1)\times(n+1)$ Monge matrix in condensed form,
with $K$ positive densities, and put $L=\lceil\log_2(n+1)\rceil$.
In $O((n+K)L^2)$ arithmetic and comparison operations and space one can
index a balanced binary hierarchy of ranges $I=[\ell,u)\subseteq[0,n)$.
For every hierarchy node, a query
\begin{equation}\label{eq:root-range-minimum}
 D_I(i,j)=\min_{r\in I}\{C(i,r)+C(r+1,j)\}
\end{equation}
takes $O(L)$ work. Given any threshold $t$ and contiguous valid-root
range $J\subseteq[0,n)$, all $a$ roots in $J$ satisfying
$C(i,r)+C(r+1,j)\le t$ can be enumerated in $O((a+1)L^2)$ work.
The bounds include all products and indexing, rather than assuming
constant-time access to a condensed entry.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Split each nonsingleton range at its integer midpoint; this gives
$2n-1$ nodes and depth at most $\lceil\log_2 n\rceil$, also when $n$
is not a power of two. Build the persistent entry index used in
Theorem~\ref{thm:adaptive-coarse-grammar} for the input $C$.

At $I=[\ell,u)$, write $d_{a,b}$ for a density of $C$. The restricted
first factor has exactly the densities with $\ell\le b<u-1$;
let their number be $K_A$. The restricted second factor has those
with $\ell+1\le a<u$; let their number be $K_B$. Keep row coordinates
\[
 R_I=\{0\}\cup\{a+1:d_{a,b}>0,\ \ell\le b<u-1\}
\]
in the first factor, and column coordinates
\[
 T_I=\{0\}\cup\{b+1:d_{a,b}>0,\ \ell+1\le a<u\}
\]
in the second. Let $\rho(i)$ and $\kappa(j)$ be the largest retained
coordinates no greater than $i,j$. Telescoping the intervening densities
gives, for every $r\in I$,
\begin{align*}
 C(i,r)-C(\rho(i),r)&=C(i,\ell)-C(\rho(i),\ell),\\
 C(r+1,j)-C(r+1,\kappa(j))
   &=C(\ell+1,j)-C(\ell+1,\kappa(j)).
\end{align*}
For the first identity, a density with row between $\rho(i)$ and $i-1$
and column in $[\ell,u-1)$ would have inserted a retained coordinate
in $(\rho(i),i]$, contradicting the choice of $\rho(i)$. Densities outside
that column range contribute the same row offset for every $r\in I$.
The second identity follows by the transposed argument.

Multiply the compressed factors
$C[R_I,I]$ and $C[I+1,T_I]$, retaining their product $P_I$.
There are at most $K_A+1$ outer rows and $K_B+1$ outer columns,
with $|I|$ inner positions. Increasing-coordinate selection preserves
Monge structure. The published product theorem~\cite{coremonge} gives
at most $K_A+K_B$ product-core entries. Store their persistent entry
index and the two offset boundary arrays. Then
\begin{align}
 D_I(i,j)={}&P_I(\rho(i),\kappa(j))
     +C(i,\ell)-C(\rho(i),\ell)\nonumber\\
     &+C(\ell+1,j)-C(\ell+1,\kappa(j)).
     \label{eq:root-range-recovery}
\end{align}
Here product coordinates are identified with their retained original
coordinates. Binary searches locate those coordinates; the two original
entry queries and the compressed product query take $O(L)$ work.
The other two entries are stored offsets.

At any hierarchy depth, the inner ranges are disjoint and their total
length is at most $n$. Each original density belongs to at most one
first-factor range and one second-factor range at that depth.
Consequently the sum of $K_A+K_B$ at a depth is at most $2K$.
Splitting a range drops densities crossing the new inner boundary;
it never copies a density into both children of the same factor.
Thus all factor dimensions and core sizes together are $O((n+K)L)$.
Sort and deduplicate only the retained coordinates. Obtain factor
boundaries from the original index and its top row and left column.
The product, coordinate sorting and entry-index costs add at most
one further factor $L$, proving the preparation and space bounds.
In particular, no node allocates a fresh full-length outer boundary.

For enumeration, skip a node disjoint from $J$, and prune a node when
its range minimum exceeds $t$. Otherwise descend, emitting a surviving
singleton. Fully contained nodes that survive pruning have a qualifying
leaf below them. Their visited descendants can be charged to the at most
$L$ ancestors of each emitted root and their pruned siblings.
Partially intersecting nodes lie on the two boundary paths of $J$.
There are therefore $O((a+1)L)$ visited nodes, each using at most one
$O(L)$ range query. The same bound applies to an interrupted enumeration
prefix, with the number of emitted roots plus one.
\end{proof}

Let the coarse nonnegative integer weights be $(\bar p,\bar q)$ and
the original arbitrary nonnegative weights be $(p,q)$. Fix any $\delta>0$.
For a nonempty interval $I=[i,j)$, include all its keys and both endpoint
gaps when defining
\[
 E_+(I)=\sum_{o\in I}(x_o-\delta\bar x_o)_+,\qquad
 E_-(I)=\sum_{o\in I}(\delta\bar x_o-x_o)_+.
\]
Here $y_+=\max\{y,0\}$, and the notation $o\in I$ refers to those
outcomes. Let $H_f$ bound the height of some full original optimum.
Let $H_c$ bound the height of some coarse optimum on every interval.
The existing height lemma supplies $H_f$, with the trivial choice
$H_f=n$ always available. The number of completed coarse Monge layers
supplies $H_c$: their final values are unrestricted and are attained
within that many layers on every interval.

\begin{lemma}[A signed coarse-regret allowance]\label{lem:coarse-regret}
For $m=j-i>0$, put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:coarse-regret-allowance}
 \eta(I)=(\min\{m,H_c\}-1)E_+(I)
                 +(\min\{m,H_f\}-1)E_-(I).
\end{equation}
There is a full original optimum all of whose subtree roots $r$ satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:coarse-root-screen}
 \bar C(i,r)+\bar C(r+1,j)+\bar W(i,j)
       \le \bar C(i,j)+\eta(I)/\delta,
\end{equation}
where $\bar C$ is the unrestricted coarse optimum table.
This conclusion holds for every error magnitude.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Choose a full original optimum $T$ of height at most $H_f$.
Its subtree $T_I$ on any of its intervals is original-weight optimal
there. Compare it with a coarse-optimal tree $U_I$ of height at most
$\min\{m,H_c\}$. Every outcome comparison length in a nonempty
$m$-key tree lies in $[1,m]$. Positive errors can increase
$C_e(U_I)-C_e(T_I)$ by at most
$(\min\{m,H_c\}-1)E_+(I)$, while negative errors can increase it by
at most $(\min\{m,H_f\}-1)E_-(I)$. Original optimality therefore gives
\[
 \delta\bigl(C_{\bar p,\bar q}(T_I)-\bar C(i,j)\bigr)
       \le C_e(U_I)-C_e(T_I)\le\eta(I).
\]
The least coarse cost with the same root $r$ is no larger than the
coarse cost of $T_I$. Substituting that root-constrained minimum proves
\eqref{eq:coarse-root-screen} simultaneously at every subtree of $T$.
Only existence of one height-bounded full optimum is required; the
screen need not preserve every tall tied optimum.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Full construction through coarse-regret screening]
\label{thm:coarse-screen}
Prepare the coarse Monge construction, with aggregate core quantity
$\bar S$, final completed core size $K$, and $H_c$ as above. In
\begin{equation}\label{eq:coarse-screen-preparation}
 O\bigl(\bar S L+(n+K)L^2\bigr)
\end{equation}
work, prepare the root-range index. For any original nonnegative input,
start at $[0,n)$, retain precisely the valid roots satisfying
\eqref{eq:coarse-root-screen}, and recurse on their children,
deduplicating intervals. Let $V$ and $A$ be the reached nonempty
interval and retained root counts.

Using the sufficient-height selector of Lemma~\ref{lem:trimmed-height},
the complete original optimum can be constructed in
\begin{equation}\label{eq:coarse-screen-query}
 O\bigl(nL+(V+A)L^2\bigr)
\end{equation}
additional arithmetic and comparison operations. Preparation can be
reused for unrelated fine-weight inputs and any positive scale.
Retained index and query space is $O((n+K)L^2+V+A)$; ordinary coarse
witness maps add $O(\bar S L)$ during preparation.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Index the finite completed coarse matrix by Lemma~\ref{lem:root-range-index}.
For each reached interval, query with valid roots $[i,j)$ and threshold
$\bar C(i,j)-\bar W(i,j)+\eta(I)/\delta$.
Prefix sums compute both signed error masses, coarse interval masses
and original interval masses. The entry $\bar C(i,j)$ costs $O(L)$.
Summing the enumeration bound over reached intervals gives
$O((V+A)L^2)$ work. A balanced endpoint dictionary adds only
$O((V+A)L)$ comparisons.

Every reached interval retains at least a coarse-optimal root because
$\eta(I)\ge0$. Thus the retained acyclic grammar has a feasible
completion. By Lemma~\ref{lem:coarse-regret}, it also contains the chosen
full original optimum: its root survives, then each of its children
is reached and its root survives, inductively. Evaluate the original
root recurrence on retained rules in increasing interval length and
trace an attaining full tree. The result cannot be more expensive than
the surviving original optimum, and every output is a feasible original
BST, so its cost is exactly optimal. It need not have height at most
$H_f$; that height was used only to prove that an optimum survives.
State ordering, recurrence evaluation and traceback fit the stated
bound. Height selection costs $O(nL)$; all other input scans are linear.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{A low-mass screening attempt for every input.}
For a nonzero input set $b=L^3$, $\delta=X/b$, where $X$ is its
largest weight, and round each outcome to the nearest nonnegative
integer multiple of $\delta$. Then $\bar W\le(2n+1)L^3$ regardless
of the original magnitudes. Check a budget of $4nL$ charges before
each reached state and each retained root. If exhausted, stop expansion
and run ordinary Knuth on the original input.
Uniform key and gap sequences have their direct linear construction.

This is an exact full constructor on all nonnegative inputs. Its
coarse preparation is subquadratic in the arithmetic model. In fact,
the final completed core satisfies
$K\le n+L\bar W$ by \eqref{eq:core-mass}, while
$H=O(L)$ for this polynomially bounded coarse mass.
The aggregate theorem and \eqref{eq:coarse-screen-preparation} give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:coarse-screen-automatic}
 O\bigl(n^{3/2}L^3+nL^7\bigr)=o(n^2)
\end{equation}
preparation work. The budget bounds a successful or interrupted screening
attempt by $O(nL^3)$ additional work, including partial enumeration.
Consequently a successful full construction is subquadratic, and the
global worst case, including every failed attempt, is $O(n^2)$.
The final core bound is used only for preprocessing this index; the
early-layer odd-ramp obstruction remains unchanged.

The remaining issue is the size of the screened grammar. A coarse-optimal
root has zero regret and always survives, so the dense uniform family
can still force quadratic states and cubic rules without a budget.
The improvement is that coarse screening now applies to every original
weight vector, with an explicit regret allowance, rather than stopping
when the small-error certificate fails. All bounds exclude operand bit
costs. Independent inclusion, range-product and full-construction checks
are reported in Appendix~\ref{app:coarse-screen}.


\subsection{Ordered references without explicit cores}
\label{sec:ordered-reference}
The root-range index in Section~\ref{sec:coarse-screen} can be replaced
by an implicit cost oracle on a certified ordered class. A second use of
the same exchange argument gives a corridor around nonlinear reference
profiles. Both constructions retain the original key and gap costs.

\begin{lemma}[A uniform baseline forces the shape class]
\label{lem:baseline-shape}
For nonnegative weights on $n\ge1$ keys, let
\[
 a=\min_i p_i,\qquad b=\min_j q_j,\qquad
 E_0=\sum_i(p_i-a)+\sum_j(q_j-b).
\]
If
\begin{equation}\label{eq:baseline-shape}
 a+b>(n-1)E_0,
\end{equation}
every optimal tree on every interval has minimum internal path length.
If $E_0=0$, such an optimum also exists, including when all weights vanish.
For a fixed root, a best tree has minimum-IPL children. Moreover, whenever
two fixed-root classes have different minimum internal path lengths, their
best costs have the same strict ordering under \eqref{eq:baseline-shape}.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
On an $m$-key interval, the uniform part has cost
$(a+b)I+(a+2b)m$. Every outcome has comparison length in $[1,m]$.
The residual-cost difference between any two trees is at most
$(m-1)E_0(I)\le(n-1)E_0$ in absolute value. An integer increase of at
least one in $I$ therefore cannot improve the total cost. This also
applies with a root fixed, and to comparisons between the least-IPL
members of two fixed-root classes. For $E_0=0$, use the uniform identity
directly. The all-zero case permits every tree.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Implicit interval and root-range costs]
\label{thm:ordered-range-oracle}
Suppose the weights satisfy Lemma~\ref{lem:baseline-shape} and the
leftward exchange condition \eqref{eq:ruler-condition}, with
$h=\lfloor\log_2(n+1)\rfloor$, or its reflection. Put
$L=\lceil\log_2(n+1)\rceil$. In $O(nL)$ arithmetic work and space one
can prepare an index with the following exact queries:
\begin{enumerate}
\item the unrestricted interval optimum $C(i,j)$ in $O(L)$ work;
\item the minimum of $C(i,r)+C(r+1,j)$ over any nonempty contiguous
      root range $J\subseteq[i,j)$ in $O(L)$ work;
\item all $t$ roots in such a range whose cost is at most a given
      threshold, in $O(L^2+t)$ work.
\end{enumerate}
The preparation stores dyadic-stride prefix sums, without constructing
an interval table or listing any Monge densities.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
It suffices to treat the leftward condition. It holds on every subinterval:
its skeleton height is at most $h$, and the maximum of $d$ and $hd$
bounds $td$ for every integer $1\le t\le h$. The exchange theorem and
Lemma~\ref{lem:baseline-shape} therefore make the left-filled shape
optimal on every interval.

Write $P(u,v)=\sum_{k=u}^{v-1}p_k$ and
$Q(u,v)=\sum_{k=u}^{v-1}q_k$. For $m=j-i>0$ put
$h_m=\lfloor\log_2(m+1)\rfloor$, $c=2^{h_m}-1$, and $s=m-c$.
Define, for positive integer limits,
\[
 R(o,d;u,v)=\sum_{t=u}^{v}(1+\nu_2(t))p_{o+dt},
\]
with an empty sum equal to zero. The exact full interval cost is
\begin{align}
 C(i,j)={}&(h_m+1)P(i,j)+h_m Q(i,j+1)+Q(i,i+2s)\nonumber\\
 &-R(i-1,2;1,\min\{s,c\})
  -R(i+s-1,1;s+1,c).
 \label{eq:ordered-interval-oracle}
\end{align}
Indeed, skeleton key $t$ occupies input position
$i+t-1+\min\{s,t\}$ and has comparison length $h_m-\nu_2(t)$.
The added keys have length $h_m+1$. The first $2s$ gaps have length
$h_m+1$ and the other gaps length $h_m$. Subtracting the skeleton's
savings from the common key length $h_m+1$ gives the formula.

For each power-of-two stride $2^d\le n$, store
\[
 Z_d(k)=\sum_{\substack{t\ge0\\k-t2^d\ge0}}p_{k-t2^d}
 \quad(0\le k<n).
\]
These arrays require $O(nL)$ additions. A sum over a contiguous part of
one residue class is a difference of two entries. Expand
$1+\nu_2(t)=\sum_{d\ge0}[2^d\mid t]$ in each $R$ query.
There are $O(L)$ resulting stride sums, proving the interval-query bound.

For root ranges, let $G(r)=C(i,r)+C(r+1,j)$ and put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ordered-root-pivot}
 r_* = i+2^{h_m-1}-1+\min\{s,2^{h_m-1}\}.
\end{equation}
We claim that $G$ is nonincreasing up to $r_*$ and nondecreasing after it.
The least IPL with left size $\ell=r-i$ is
$m-1+I_{\min}(\ell)+I_{\min}(m-1-\ell)$.
Since $I_{\min}(k)-I_{\min}(k-1)=\lfloor\log_2 k\rfloor$,
this sequence is convex. Its minimum plateau consists precisely of
\[
 \ell=2^{h_m-1}-1+u,\qquad
 \max\{0,s-2^{h_m-1}\}\le u\le\min\{s,2^{h_m-1}\}.
\]
Outside that plateau the strict cost ordering follows from
Lemma~\ref{lem:baseline-shape}. Within it, the best tree with that root
fills the first $u$ bottom slots on the left and the first $s-u$ on the
right. Increasing $u$ by one moves one expansion from the right block
to the next empty left slot. This transformation is a sequence of swaps
$01\to10$; each is nonincreasing by \eqref{eq:ruler-exchange} and the
assumed condition. Thus $G$ is nonincreasing along the plateau, proving
the claim. The uniform zero-cost case is immediate.

Clamp $r_*$ to the queried root range. Evaluating $G$ there gives its
minimum using two interval queries. If the minimum passes the threshold,
binary searches on the two monotone sides find the first and last
qualifying roots. All intervening roots qualify. The searches use
$O(L)$ interval-query pairs, followed by $O(t)$ enumeration. Reflection
reverses the indices and preserves all bounds.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Screening through an implicit ordered reference]
\label{cor:ordered-screen}
Let coarse weights satisfy Theorem~\ref{thm:ordered-range-oracle}.
For arbitrary nonnegative original weights and any scale $\delta>0$,
use the allowance of Lemma~\ref{lem:coarse-regret}, with $H_c=L$.
Preparation takes $O(nL)$ work and space. If screening reaches $V$
nonempty intervals and retains $A$ root rules, complete exact original
construction takes
\[
 O(nL+VL^2+AL)
\]
additional deterministic arithmetic and comparison operations, and
$O(nL+V+A)$ space. The same preparation serves unrelated original inputs.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Each interval threshold is formed from an exact coarse cost and error
prefix sums. The oracle enumerates its retained rules in $O(L^2+t)$.
Balanced endpoint dictionaries account for the $AL$ term during
scheduling and evaluation; this term is not treated as constant-time
hashing. The inclusion and full-tree correctness proof is exactly that
of Theorem~\ref{thm:coarse-screen}. A $4nL$ state-and-rule budget bounds
a successful or interrupted query by $O(nL^3)$; ordinary Knuth on budget
exhaustion gives an exact quadratic worst-case constructor, including
all failed work. Preparation is independent of coarse core size and
weight magnitudes. Operand bit costs are additional.
\end{proof}

\subsection{A nonlinear exchange corridor with linear-time fitting}
\label{sec:nonlinear-exchange-corridor}
An exchange margin can also control the whole shape optimization, avoiding
interval-graph expansion. This extends the affine corridor of
Section~\ref{sec:legacy-22-1} to a reference profile that need not be affine.

Let $h=\lfloor\log_2(n+1)\rfloor$, $S=2^h$, $s=n-S+1$, and $z=S-s$.
A minimum-IPL shape is a length-$S$ binary expansion word with $s$ ones.
Its inversion count is the number of pairs consisting of a zero followed
by a one. Suppose every leftward adjacent exchange of a reference input
reduces its cost by at least $\gamma>0$. Let original weights differ from
that reference by total absolute key error $E_p$ and gap error $E_q$.

\begin{lemma}[An inversion allowance from exchange margin]
\label{lem:exchange-inversion-allowance}
Put $V=hE_p+E_q$ and $D=\lfloor V/\gamma\rfloor$.
Every original optimum within the minimum-IPL class has at most $D$
inversions. In particular, every prefix with $a$ zeros already used and
$b$ ones still required satisfies $ab\le D$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Sorting an expansion word leftward takes exactly its inversion count in
adjacent swaps. Its reference cost therefore exceeds the left-filled
reference cost by at least $\gamma$ times that count. Among minimum-IPL
trees, each key length varies by at most $h$ and each gap length by at
most one. Original errors can compensate for at most $hE_p+E_q$ of that
reference excess. An original class optimum cannot cost more than the
left-filled candidate, which proves the inversion bound. Every already
used zero precedes every required future one, giving $ab$ inversions.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Nonlinear reference corridor construction]
\label{thm:nonlinear-exchange-corridor}
An original minimum-IPL optimum can be constructed using at most
\begin{equation}\label{eq:nonlinear-corridor-states}
 S+1+\min\{sz,D\lceil\log_2(z+1)\rceil\}
\end{equation}
states and constant work per state, in addition to fitting and linear
input/output work. For arbitrary nonnegative original weights, a unit-margin
reference can be fitted in $O(n)$ work: set all reference gaps to $\min q$,
and recursively set
\[
 \bar p_0=p_0,\qquad \bar p_i=\max\{p_i,\bar p_{i-1}+1\}.
\]
Compute $E_p,E_q$ directly and use $\gamma=1$. One may also fit the
reflected input and choose the smaller allowance. If the original input
passes Lemma~\ref{lem:baseline-shape}, the resulting tree is a full
unrestricted optimum. Otherwise ordinary Knuth gives an exact fallback.
The complete constructor has $O(n^2)$ worst-case arithmetic work and space.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The fitted key sequence increases by at least one and its gap sequence
is constant. Formula~\eqref{eq:ruler-exchange} makes every leftward
exchange improve reference cost by at least one. The fit and error scans
are linear.

Retain precisely the feasible expansion-grid states satisfying $ab\le D$.
The axes contain $S+1$ states. For each $1\le a\le z$, at most
$\min\{s,\lfloor D/a\rfloor\}$ positive $b$ survive. Dyadic grouping
of $a$ bounds their sum by $D\lceil\log_2(z+1)\rceil$; the full
rectangle has only $sz$ such states. This proves
\eqref{eq:nonlinear-corridor-states}.

On layer $g$, after $g$ slots and $e$ expansions, $a=g-e$ and $b=s-e$.
Both factors decrease with $e$, so the retained expansion counts form a
single integer interval. Its lower endpoint is nondecreasing with $g$;
a pointer advances at most $s$ times over the whole computation.
Every retained state is reachable by placing its used ones first,
then its used zeros: all preceding products are at most $ab$. Completing
with the required ones before the remaining zeros also stays in the corridor.
Use compact arrays for each layer and one predecessor choice per retained
state. There is no dictionary or full-width array per layer.

For the next slot, put $i=g+e$ and $d=h-\nu_2(g+1)$.
Choosing no expansion charges $h q_i$ and, if $g<S-1$, $d p_i$.
Choosing an expansion charges
$(h+1)(p_i+q_i+q_{i+1})$ and, if $g<S-1$, $d p_{i+1}$.
These are the original weights. Each state has at most two outgoing
transitions. Every original class optimum survives by
Lemma~\ref{lem:exchange-inversion-allowance}; retained extra paths are
feasible minimum-IPL trees and cannot invalidate the optimum. Traceback
produces the expansion word, its key comparison lengths, and the full
tree in linear output work. The baseline lemma supplies global
optimality on acceptance. Uniform inputs have their direct linear
construction. On rejection only linear checks precede Knuth, and the
entire expansion grid itself has $O(n^2)$ states.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Bounded errors around arbitrary increasing profiles]
\label{cor:nonlinear-bounded-errors}
Let $f_0=0$, $f_{i+1}-f_i\ge1$, and $F=\sum_i f_i$. For arbitrary
errors $|u_i|,|v_j|\le U$, let
\[
 p_i=B+f_i+u_i,\qquad q_j=B+v_j,
\]
with nonnegative resulting weights. If
\begin{equation}\label{eq:nonlinear-baseline-family}
 2B-2U>(n-1)\bigl(F+2U(2n+1)\bigr),
\end{equation}
the automatic constructor of Theorem~\ref{thm:nonlinear-exchange-corridor}
takes $O(n+Un\log^2(n+1))$ arithmetic work and space, including fitting,
certification and full reconstruction. The same holds after reflection.
For fixed $U$ this is $O(n\log^2(n+1))$. The profile $f$ is arbitrary
subject to its increments; for example $f_i=i+\lfloor i^2/n\rfloor$
is nonlinear. Original perturbations can occur independently on every
key and every gap, and need not preserve their ordering.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The prefix-envelope fit obeys
\[
 B+f_i-U\le\bar p_i
   =\max_{0\le k\le i}(p_k+i-k)\le B+f_i+U,
\]
because $f_i-f_k\ge i-k$. Since $\bar p_i\ge p_i$, this gives
$E_p\le2Un$. The constant gap fit gives $E_q\le2U(n+1)$.
Hence $V\le2U(hn+n+1)$, and
\eqref{eq:nonlinear-corridor-states} gives the work bound.
The original minimum key and gap weights are at least $B-U$;
their total residual mass is at most $F+2U(2n+1)$.
Condition~\eqref{eq:nonlinear-baseline-family} therefore implies
\eqref{eq:baseline-shape}, completing the unrestricted certificate.
The implementation infers its reference from the given weights; it
requires neither $f$, $B$, nor $U$ as an additional input.
\end{proof}

These two ordered-reference constructions address different costs: the
interval oracle supports reusable arbitrary-weight screening, while the
inversion corridor supplies a proved small state count for a nonlinear
perturbation family. The unrestricted subquadratic objective still requires
a representation or certificate covering arbitrary weight profiles.
Verification appears in Appendix~\ref{app:ordered-reference}.


\subsection{Range-sensitive shape certification}
\label{sec:shape-range}
The total residual mass in Lemma~\ref{lem:baseline-shape} can be replaced
by a shortening-capacity bound when only a full-interval shape certificate
is required. This gives an alternative baseline requirement for the nonlinear
corridor, and an exact universal threshold at perfect-tree sizes.

For $n\ge1$, put $L=\lceil\log_2(n+1)\rceil$ and define
\[
 K_L=2^L-L-1,\qquad
 G_L=\begin{cases}0,&L=1,\\2^{L-1},&L\ge2.\end{cases}
\]
Write $\ell_i(T)$ for key comparison lengths and $d_j(T)$ for gap depths.

\begin{lemma}[Shortening capacities]\label{lem:shortening-capacities}
For every full $n$-key tree $T$,
\[
 \sum_i(L-\ell_i(T))_+\le K_L,\qquad
 \sum_j(L-d_j(T))_+\le G_L.
\]
If $n=2^h-1$, the stronger gap bound
\begin{equation}\label{eq:perfect-gap-shortening}
 \sum_j(h-d_j(T))_+\le I(T)-I_{\min}(n)
\end{equation}
holds.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
At most $2^t-1$ keys have comparison length at most $t$. Summing this
bound for $t=1,\ldots,L-1$ gives $K_L$.
For an integer $u\ge1$, $u\le2^{u-1}$. Thus each gap with $d_j<L$
contributes at most $2^{L-1}2^{-d_j}$. Kraft equality
$\sum_j2^{-d_j}=1$ gives the gap bound; for $L=1$ both gaps have depth one.

At perfect size put $k_j=d_j-h$, and let
$A=\sum_j(-k_j)_+$ and $Z=\sum_j(k_j)_+$.
Kraft equality, multiplied by $2^h$, gives
\[
 \sum_{k_j<0}(2^{-k_j}-1)
   =\sum_{k_j>0}(1-2^{-k_j}).
\]
The left side is at least $A$, while the right side is at most $Z/2$,
using $2^t-1\ge t$ and $1-2^{-t}\le t/2$ for positive integers $t$.
Hence $Z\ge2A$. External path length differs from internal path length
by $2n$, so $I(T)-I_{\min}=\sum_j k_j=Z-A\ge A$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[A full shape certificate from weight ranges]
\label{cor:range-shape}
Let $a=\min p$, $b=\min q$, $P=\max p-a$, and $Q=\max q-b$.
If
\begin{equation}\label{eq:range-shape}
 a+b\ge P K_L+Q G_L,
\end{equation}
a minimum-IPL optimum is a full unrestricted optimum. The certificate
is computable in $O(n)$ work. It can be combined with the earlier
mass certificate by using the smaller sufficient threshold.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Every minimum-IPL tree $S$ has all key and gap lengths at most $L$.
For any $T$ with excess $e=I(T)-I_{\min}\ge1$, nonnegative residuals
and Lemma~\ref{lem:shortening-capacities} imply
\[
 C(T)-C(S)\ge(a+b)e-PK_L-QG_L\ge0.
\]
Thus optimizing within the class gives a global optimum. Equality in
the certificate is sufficient; an outside tree may tie.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Sharp universal box threshold at perfect sizes]
\label{thm:perfect-box}
Let $n=2^h-1$, $h\ge2$, and let $a,b,P,Q\ge0$.
The perfect tree is optimal for every fixed-position assignment
\[
 p_i\in[a,a+P],\qquad q_j\in[b,b+Q]
\]
if and only if
\begin{equation}\label{eq:perfect-box}
 a+b\ge(n-h)P+Q.
\end{equation}
On acceptance it can be constructed in $O(n)$ work and space, including
reading the weights and producing the full tree. This is a universal
statement about the entire box; particular assignments may remain optimal
when the inequality fails. For $n=1$ there is only one tree.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The perfect tree $S$ is the unique minimum-IPL shape. For any other $T$
put $e=I(T)-I_{\min}\ge1$. The key shortening bound is $K_h=n-h$;
\eqref{eq:perfect-gap-shortening} bounds gap shortening by $e$. Therefore
\[
 C(T)-C(S)\ge(a+b-Q)e-P(n-h).
\]
Under \eqref{eq:perfect-box}, this is at least $P(n-h)(e-1)\ge0$.
Necessity follows from the explicit one-unit-excess construction in
Theorem~\ref{thm:perfect-box-sharpness}, which attains cost difference
$a+b-(n-h)P-Q$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Nonlinear corridor with a range baseline]
\label{cor:range-nonlinear}
Use the nonlinear profile and error assumptions of
Corollary~\ref{cor:nonlinear-bounded-errors}, and write $M=f_{n-1}$.
Its complete $O(n+Un\log^2(n+1))$ construction bound also holds under
\begin{equation}\label{eq:range-nonlinear}
 2B-2U\ge(M+2U)K_L+2U G_L.
\end{equation}
In particular $B=n(M+4U)+U+1$ suffices. For the curved profile
$f_i=i+\lfloor i^2/n\rfloor$ and fixed $U$, a baseline $B=O(n^2)$
suffices, whereas the earlier total-mass condition requires order $n^3$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The original baselines are at least $B-U$, and their ranges are at most
$M+2U$ and $2U$. Apply Corollary~\ref{cor:range-shape}.
The same automatic prefix fit still gives $E_p\le2Un$ and
$E_q\le2U(n+1)$, so the corridor state and construction bounds are unchanged.
Since $K_L<2n$ and $G_L\le n$, the displayed sufficient choice of $B$
satisfies \eqref{eq:range-nonlinear}. No profile or error bound needs to
be supplied to the implementation.
\end{proof}

A full constructor first scans the ranges and the earlier mass threshold.
At a perfect size it also tests \eqref{eq:perfect-box}; acceptance returns
the perfect tree directly. Otherwise, acceptance permits the original-cost
nonlinear corridor, and rejection invokes ordinary Knuth. Every successful
and failed route is included in the quadratic worst-case work and space
bound. The all-interval root-range oracle retains its separate hypotheses.
The exact box threshold does not resolve arbitrary-profile construction;
it identifies precisely the worst perturbation scale for this shape class
at perfect sizes. Verification is in Appendix~\ref{app:shape-range}.

\section{Centering around large constant baselines}
\label{sec:legacy-20}

The total-mass bound can be pessimistic when large constant key and gap
weights dominate smaller variations. We retain the original weights in the
solver and center only the analysis of its matrix representation. Let
\[
 a=\min_i p_i,\quad b=\min_j q_j,\quad
 u_i=p_i-a,\quad v_j=q_j-b,\quad R=\sum_i u_i+\sum_j v_j.
\]
All quantities remain nonnegative integers. The two baselines may differ
arbitrarily; the deviations may occur at arbitrary key and gap positions.
Write $L=\lceil\log_2(n+1)\rceil$ as before.

\begin{lemma}[Normalized height budget]\label{result:legacy-30}\label{lem:normalized-height}
For $W>0$, let $s$ be the smallest positive singleton mass
$p_i+q_i+q_{i+1}$. Put $z=0$ if every singleton mass is positive and $z=L$
otherwise. Every interval has an optimal tree with height at most
\[
 H=\min\{n,f(\lfloor W/s\rfloor)+z\}.
\]
For $W=0$, use $H=L$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Every positive-mass nonempty interval contains a positive-mass singleton:
choose a positive key, or an adjacent key within the interval if a gap
supplies its positive mass. Thus every positive internal subtree has mass
at least $s$. Lemma~\ref{result:legacy-8} gives $W\ge F_{d+1}s$ for such a subtree at depth $d$
in an optimal tree. The positive part has the resulting bounded depth;
zero-mass subtrees can be balanced, adding at most $L$ levels without
changing the cost. If all singleton masses are positive no nonempty zero
subtree exists. The global $W,s,L$ bounds also apply to every subinterval.
The trivial height $n$ and the all-zero balanced case complete the argument.
\end{proof}

Use this $H$, $M=(H+1)W+1$, and the same finite completion and penalty
iteration. All previous preservation arguments apply. Reconstruction is
complete because any unfinished nonempty terminal costs at least $M$,
whereas an optimal tree of height at most $H$ costs at most $HW$.

\subsection{The exact baseline layer and its exceptional diagonals}\label{sec:legacy-20-1}
Let $I(k)$ be the minimum unweighted internal path length for $k$ keys.
For $k\ge1$, with $d=\lfloor\log_2 k\rfloor$,
\[
 I(k)=dk-2^{d+1}+d+2,\qquad I(0)=0.
\]
The familiar baseline identity is
$(a+b)I(T)+(a+2b)k$ for a full $k$-key tree. It was already used by the
minimum-path-length certificate; the following application concerns every
partial-tree layer.

\begin{lemma}[Centered partial-tree formula]\label{result:legacy-31}\label{lem:centered-layer}
With $c_h=2^h-1$, define
\[
 F_h(k)=
 \begin{cases}
 (a+b)I(k)+(a+2b)k,&k\le c_h,\\
 M(k-c_h)+(a+b)h(k+1)-ac_h,&k>c_h.
 \end{cases}
\]
Then the full-weight layer admits
\[
 A_h(i,j)=F_h(j-i)+U_h(i,j),\qquad
 0\le U_h(i,j)\le hR(i,j),
\]
where $R(i,j)=\sum_{i\le r<j}u_r+\sum_{i\le r\le j}v_r$.
The baseline's density at interval length $k\ge1$ is
\[
 F_h(k+1)+F_h(k-1)-2F_h(k)=
 \begin{cases}
 a+b,&k=2^t-1,\quad 1\le t<h,\\
 M-b,&k=2^h-1,\\
 0,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The penalty forces exactly $\min(k,c_h)$ explicit keys. If $k\le c_h$,
a full minimum-path-length tree fits in $h$ levels. Otherwise a partial
tree exposing $c_h$ keys must fill every position in the first $h$ levels.
Its explicit keys have baseline cost
$a\sum_{t=0}^{h-1}(t+1)2^t$; unfinished keys cost $ah(k-c_h)$, and all
$k+1$ gaps cost $bh(k+1)$. This gives the second branch of $F_h$.
Nonnegative deviations cannot reduce the baseline optimum. Conversely,
a baseline-optimal partial tree charges every deviation at most $h$ times,
proving the residual bounds. The displayed densities follow by taking
second differences of the two branches. In particular, changes in the
full-tree slope occur only at $k=2^t-1$.
\end{proof}

The residual $U_h$ need not be Monge. The actual layer is Monge, and on
any square whose density cells avoid the exceptional diagonals, its density
equals the residual density. Baseline magnitudes do not increase the number
of exceptional diagonals.

\subsection{A weighted packing argument}\label{sec:legacy-20-2}
Remove all density cells on $j-i=2^t-1$ within the domain, and the main
diagonal, accounting for $O(nL)$ cells. Tile the remainder with maximal
aligned dyadic squares that avoid those lines and the outer boundary.
For a side-$s$ tile $T=[x,x+s)\times[y,y+s)$, let
$J_T=[x,y+s]$ be its spanning input interval, including both gap endpoints.

Lemma~\ref{lem:dyadic-boundary} gives $O(nL/s)$ tiles at scale $s$.
\begin{lemma}[Per-position packing]\label{lem:per-position-packing}
At a fixed side length $s$, every input position belongs to the spanning
intervals of $O(n/s)$ accepted tiles. Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{eq:centered-cover}
 \sum_{T:\,\mathrm{side}(T)=s} R(J_T)=O(nR/s).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Fix an input position and charge each
tile to a boundary met by its parent. A tile charged to a diagonal at
distance $d\ge s$ lies within $O(s)$ of that diagonal. For its spanning
interval to contain the fixed position, its row start lies in an interval
of length $O(d+s)$. Row starts are spaced by $s$, with only $O(1)$ eligible
column starts per row near that diagonal. Thus it contributes
$O(1+d/s)$ covers. The distances $d=2^t-1$ are geometric, so summing these
bounds gives $O(n/s)$. All distances $d<s$ lie together in an $O(s)$-wide
strip near the main diagonal and contribute only $O(1)$ covers of the fixed
position. Outer-boundary tiles number $O(n/s)$ in total. Charging each
nonnegative deviation to its covering intervals proves~\eqref{eq:centered-cover}.
The outer-boundary case is covered by Lemma~\ref{lem:dyadic-boundary}, including arbitrary $n$.
\end{proof}

On an ordinary tile the density sum telescopes to
\[
 U_h(x,y+s)+U_h(x+s,y)-U_h(x,y)-U_h(x+s,y+s)
 \le 2hR(J_T).
\]
The true densities are nonnegative integers. The number of nonzero entries
on a tile is therefore bounded both by $s^2$ and by that density sum.
Using the tile count and~\eqref{eq:centered-cover}, the number across all
tiles of side $s$ is $O(\min\{nLs,hnR/s\})$. Splitting the dyadic geometric
sum at $s\asymp\sqrt{hR/L}$ gives $O(n\sqrt{hRL})$; for $R=0$ every
ordinary density vanishes. No cancellation of negative residual densities
on the removed lines is assumed.

\begin{theorem}[Centered core construction]\label{result:legacy-32}\label{thm:centered-core}
For arbitrary nonnegative integer mixed weights, the normalized-height
penalty construction has completed layer cores bounded by
\[
 K_c=O\!\left(n+\min\{n^2,nL+n\sqrt{HRL}\}\right).
\]
Using the published compact product and witness algorithms, a complete
optimum can be constructed in $O(HK_c\log(n+1))$ arithmetic operations.
Published succinct witnesses use $O(HK_c)$ words; the implemented ordinary
maps can require $O(HK_c\log(n+1))$ words.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Add the $O(nL)$ removed cells and $n$ completion densities to the ordinary
tile bound, and use $h\le H$. The initial layer has only $O(n)$ core entries.
The quadratic cap is immediate. All products, conversion steps, and root
queries operate on the actual condensed matrices, so the already established
product reduction applies. The previous $O(n+n\sqrt{WL})$ bound is also
available; either bound may be used when smaller.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Arbitrarily large baselines with polynomial deviation]\label{result:legacy-33}
If $R$ is polynomial in $n$, exact full construction takes
\[
 O\!\left(n(1+\sqrt R)\log^3(n+1)\right)
\]
arithmetic operations, independently of the magnitudes of $a,b$ in the
operation count. In particular, $R=O(n)$ gives
$O(n^{3/2}\log^3(n+1))$, and $R=O(n^{2-\varepsilon})$ gives
$O(n^{2-\varepsilon/2}\log^3(n+1))$ for fixed $0<\varepsilon\le2$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
If $a+b=0$, then $W=R$, and $s\ge1$ whenever $W>0$. Otherwise every
singleton has mass at least $a+2b\ge1$, and
\[
 W=na+(n+1)b+R\le n(a+2b)+R,\qquad W/s\le n+R.
\]
Thus $H=O(\log(n+R+1))=O(\log(n+1))$ for polynomial $R$, including the
possible additional $L$ and the all-zero case. Substituting in the theorem
gives the result.
\end{proof}

For example, $p_i=n^c+u_i$ and $q_j=n^d+v_j$, with fixed nonnegative
integer $c,d$ and bounded nonnegative integer deviations, satisfy the
linear-$R$ condition even when $W$ is superlinear. Intermediate integers
then still use $O(\log n)$ bits. Arbitrary larger baselines retain a bit-cost
dependence on their representation lengths. These families permit nonuniform
weights on both keys and gaps.  The earlier total-mass result has a smaller
logarithmic factor when $W=O(n)$.



\section{A common certificate and its constructive corollaries}\label{sec:certificates}
The following pattern applies to all the exact shortcuts in this section.
Every candidate and bound uses the original fixed-position key and gap
objective, or a stated tree-independent cost transfer.
\begin{theorem}[Certificate pattern]\label{thm:certificate-pattern}
Let $\mathcal T$ be the full set of feasible ordered search trees.
Suppose an algorithm constructs a candidate $T$ of cost $V$ and a lower
bound $B\le\min_{S\in\mathcal T}C(S)$. If $V=B$, the candidate is optimal.
