so this is a bijection with the $\binom Ss$ slot subsets.

The skeleton key of rank $r$ among the ones has comparison length
$h-\nu_2(r)$; a cherry has length $h+1$. A gap has length $h+1$ exactly
when adjacent to a cherry, and otherwise length $h$. Put
$c_i=q_i+q_{i+1}$. Since zeros are nonadjacent, no deep gap is counted
twice. The full original cost of a feasible word $x$ is therefore
\begin{align}
 C(x)&=A-\sum_{i:x_i=1}\bigl((1+\nu_2(r_i))p_i+c_i\bigr),
       \label{eq:run-word-cost}\\
 A&=(h+1)\sum p_i+h\sum q_i+\sum c_i.\nonumber
\end{align}
Both weight types remain in this identity. It does not apply to arbitrary
taller shapes.

Partition $(p_i,c_i)$ into its $k$ maximal constant runs. In a block of
length $L$ with signature $(a,c)$, define
\[
 U(r)=(2a+c)r-a\operatorname{popcount}(r).
\]
The sum of $\nu_2(t)$ through $r$ is $r-\operatorname{popcount}(r)$.
Consequently every feasible block increasing the skeleton count from
$r_0$ to $r_1$ earns exactly $U(r_1)-U(r_0)$. Its internal arrangement
affects only feasibility, not reward.

Let $t$ be the previous key's bit and $u$ the final bit of a nonempty
block. Its possible number of ones is every integer in $[\ell,b]$:
\[
\begin{array}{cc|cc}
t&u&\ell&b\\\hline
1&1&\lceil L/2\rceil&L\\
0&1&\lfloor L/2\rfloor+1&L\\
1&0&\lfloor L/2\rfloor&L-1\\
0&0&\lceil L/2\rceil&L-1
\end{array}
\]
Empty ranges are infeasible. These bounds follow by separating zeros
with ones, respecting the first and last bits; every intermediate count
is attained. Use virtual initial bit $t=1$ and either final bit.

\subsubsection{Sliding transfers and linear-space reconstruction}\label{sec:legacy-23-2}
For a prefix reward profile $D_t(r_0)$, the exact next profile is
\begin{equation}
 D'_u(r)=U(r)+\max_{t\in\{0,1\}}
       \max_{r-b\le r_0\le r-\ell}\{D_t(r_0)-U(r_0)\}.
 \label{eq:run-transfer}
\end{equation}
Bounds $\ell,b$ depend on $t,u$. Exclude unreachable and out-of-range
entries. The inner maximum is a sliding window; a standard monotone
deque evaluates all ranks in linear time. Each candidate is inserted
and removed at most once. There are four bit pairs. Backward profiles
use the analogous window over $r_1\in[r+\ell,r+b]$ with values
$B'_u(r_1)+U(r_1)$, then subtract $U(r)$. Popcounts are tabulated once.

\begin{theorem}[Exact joint-signature optimizer]\label{result:legacy-38}
For arbitrary nonnegative mixed weights, let $k$ be the number of runs
of $(p_i,q_i+q_{i+1})$. Exact optimization over all minimum-path-length
shapes, including full reconstruction, takes $O(nk)$ arithmetic operations
and $O(n)$ auxiliary space for $n\ge1$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
An initial sweep finds the maximum final reward at rank $m$. For traceback,
split the block list in half and compute its left forward and right
backward profiles between fixed endpoint ranks and bits. Their sum selects
an optimal middle state. Release both profiles before recursively solving
the two halves. A one-block leaf explicitly places its required count of
nonadjacent zeros using the boundary rules. The complete word yields all
comparison lengths and the existing linear Cartesian reconstruction.
This uses Hirschberg's established recomputation principle~\cite{hirschberg}.

At recursion level $d$, an active subproblem has at most
$\lceil k/2^d\rceil$ blocks. The sum of active rank-interval widths is
at most $m+2^d$, because endpoint rank spans partition the original span.
Summing sweep work gives $O(km+k\log(k+1))$, plus $O(n)$ preprocessing
and output. Since $m=\Theta(n)$ and $k\le n$, this is $O(nk)$.
Only a constant number of profiles is retained at once; releasing them
before recursion prevents a stack of profiles. All other summaries and
the output occupy $O(n)$ space.
\end{proof}

A perfect shape or a single signature block is constructed directly.
The potential and transfer use arbitrary block values; they do not assume
that an affine approximation is accurate or that the reference tree stays
optimal.

\subsubsection{Certification and full mixed-weight guarantees}\label{sec:legacy-23-3}
The mixed residual-Huffman gate remains
\[
 C_*\le(a_0+b_0)(I_{\min}(n)+1)+(a_0+2b_0)n+H,
 \qquad a_0=\min p,\quad b_0=\min q.
\]
It can also be built from the compressed signatures. Each block contains
one key weight. Its gap weights obey $q_{i+1}=c-q_i$, so they alternate
between at most two values. Record the block's $L$ keys as one weighted
multiplicity and its $L$ left gaps as at most two; finally add $q_n$ once.
There are at most $k$ key records and $2k+1$ gap records. Sort these
records, expand their multiplicities, and run the two-queue bound. The
total work is $O(k\log(k+1)+n)$ and space is $O(n)$. Gaps at block
boundaries occur once. Thus an accepted full solve retains the theorem's
time and space bounds. Failed gates use exact Knuth; the fixed-profile
preflight rejection also remains available.

\begin{corollary}[Arbitrary mixed block values above a baseline]\label{result:legacy-39}
For $n\ge1$, $M\ge1$, $0\le u_i,v_i\le M$, set
\[
 B=4(n+1)^2M,\qquad p_i=B+u_i,\qquad q_i=B+v_i.
\]
If $(u_i,v_i+v_{i+1})$ has $k$ runs, the standalone signature constructor
returns a full optimal mixed-weight BST in $O(nk)$ arithmetic operations
and $O(n)$ auxiliary space. Constant $k$ gives linear construction and
$k=o(n)$ gives subquadratic construction.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The residual mass above $a_0,b_0$ is at most $(2n+1)M$. Every class
candidate has residual cost at most $(h+1)(2n+1)M<2B\le a_0+b_0$.
A tree outside the class pays an additional baseline cost of at least
$a_0+b_0$. Hence the gate passes even with $H=0$, and preflight cannot
reject. The exact class optimum is a full optimum. The preceding theorem
and compressed certificate give the stated work and space.
\end{proof}

The weights are positive and both types may be nonconstant. Key values
can change arbitrarily between blocks, and gap weights can alternate at
every position. No small perturbation budget or affine-closeness assumption
is imposed on $u,v$. If $M$ is polynomial in $n$, all weights and costs
have $O(\log n)$ bits, giving the corresponding word-RAM bound. For
arbitrarily large $M$, bit costs remain additional. The large baseline
is a real restriction; few signatures alone do not guarantee the full gate.
For example, $p=(100,1,1,1)$ and $q=(1,1,1,1,1)$ have only two signatures,
but their best minimum-path-length shape costs 218 and their full optimum
is 121. Direct enumeration of all fourteen trees, cubic DP, and Knuth agree;
the separate gate correctly rejects the class candidate.

\subsubsection{Bounded routing and its space qualification}\label{sec:legacy-23-4}
Generic transfer sweeps and recomputation have substantial constants when
$k=n$. The initial matched experiment exposed roughly tenfold regressions
against Knuth in this regime. The standalone kernel remains an explicit
control. The implemented portfolio first scans signatures and selects it
only when $k\le\max\{16,\lfloor n/32\rfloor\}$; otherwise it calls the
existing affine solver and its exact fallback. The empirical threshold is
not a correctness assumption or a claim of optimal routing.

On the preceding positive family, portfolio work is still $O(nk)$: the
signature route certifies, while rejected block counts satisfy $k>n/32$,
making even quadratic fallback $O(nk)$. The portfolio does not preserve
the standalone kernel's linear-space guarantee on every such input;
the affine or Knuth route may use more space. The separate standalone
constructor retains the theorem's time and space bounds. All scans,
certificates, and output reconstruction are included in the implementation
and matched measurements.


\subsection{Global alternating-gap bounds and linear profile construction}
\label{sec:legacy-24}

The preceding signature representation can have $k=n$ even on a short
alternating pattern. We now avoid its rank table by recognizing attainment
of a global key-depth lower bound. The crucial gap inequality removes the
large-baseline restriction on the recognized family.

\subsubsection{A gap lower bound valid for all tree shapes}\label{sec:legacy-24-1}
For $n\ge1$, set $h=\lfloor\log_2(n+1)\rfloor$, $S=2^h$, and
$s=n+1-S$. Suppose $q_i$ alternates between arbitrary nonnegative values
$a,b$. Every minimum-internal-path-length shape has gap cost
\[
 G(q)=h\sum_{i=0}^n q_i+s(a+b).
\]
\begin{lemma}[Alternating-gap component bound]\label{result:legacy-40}
Every BST on these keys, including every taller tree, has gap contribution
at least $G(q)$. Every minimum-internal-path-length shape attains equality.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Fix one parity of the $n+1$ inorder gaps. Give its $H$ leaves unit weight
and the other $Z=n+1-H$ leaves zero weight. Delete a zero leaf and contract
its parent into its sibling. The sibling contains a unit-weight leaf:
remaining zero leaves are separated in inorder by unit leaves, and a
subtree's leaves are contiguous. An all-zero sibling would contradict
that separation. Deleting only zero leaves preserves separation.
Each contraction decreases the selected depth sum by at least one.
After all $Z$ contractions, a full tree with $H$ unit leaves remains.
Its external path length is at least $E_{\min}(H)$.

Here $S/2\le H\le S$, so the standard balanced-leaf formula gives
$E_{\min}(H)=(h+1)H-S$, valid at both endpoints. Thus the original selected
depth sum is at least
\[
 E_{\min}(H)+Z=(h+1)H-S+n+1-H=hH+s.
\]
A minimum-path-length shape has gaps at depths $h,h+1$. Each of its $s$
bottom cherries has two adjacent gaps, one of each parity, at depth $h+1$.
Thus its selected depth sum is exactly $hH+s$. Apply the inequality to
both parities and multiply by the nonnegative weights $a,b$.
\end{proof}

Let $p_{(1)}\ge\cdots\ge p_{(n)}$ be the key weights sorted by value,
ignoring their fixed positions only for a lower bound. There are at most
$2^{d-1}$ keys at comparison length $d$. Consequently the sorted lengths
of every BST dominate $\operatorname{bitlength}(1),\ldots,
\operatorname{bitlength}(n)$ componentwise. Rearrangement yields the
globally valid bound
\[
 K(p)=\sum_{r=1}^n p_{(r)}\operatorname{bitlength}(r),\qquad
 \OPT(p,q)\ge K(p)+G(q).                                      \tag{28}
\]
An ordered tree attaining both components is globally optimal. This
compares with all shapes; it does not assume that an optimum is nearly
complete. Finding a tree attaining $K(p)$ is the remaining constructive task.

\subsubsection{Two key levels reduce to interlaced intervals}\label{sec:legacy-24-2}
Suppose key weights have two distinct levels $\ell<u$, and let $H$ count
the high keys. Put $m=S-1$ and assume $S/2-1\le H\le m$.
In a minimum-path-length shape, even skeleton ranks have comparison length
less than $h$, odd skeleton ranks have length $h$, and cherries have length
$h+1$. Attainment of $K(p)$ is therefore equivalent to retaining every high
key in the skeleton and placing high keys at every even skeleton rank.
Low keys may occupy odd ranks or cherries. The inorder word must have $m$
ones and no adjacent zeros, as in the preceding section.

Read the high/low pattern from left to right. A high key must be retained;
a low key can be retained only when its new rank is odd. At a boundary
immediately after a high key, the previous bit is one. Encode reachable
even ranks as $2A$ and odd ranks as $2B+1$. The virtual initial boundary
has $A=\{0\}$, $B=\varnothing$.

\begin{lemma}[Interlaced parity intervals]\label{result:legacy-41}
The sets $A,B$ are integer intervals, possibly empty. When both are
nonempty, their lower endpoint difference and their upper endpoint
difference each belong to $\{0,1\}$. Four endpoints and empty flags thus
represent the exact reachable state.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $L$ low keys precede the next high key. The exact transformations are
\[
\begin{array}{c|cc}
L&A'&B'\\\hline
0&B+1&A\\
1&(A\cup B)+1&A\\
2\text{ or }3&A+1&\varnothing\\
\ge4&\varnothing&\varnothing
\end{array}
\]
For $L=1$, remove the low key or retain it at an odd rank. For $L=2$,
the low word is $01$ or $10$; for $L=3$ it is $010$. These require an
even incoming rank. A low-only run can retain at most one key, because
two retained lows would occupy consecutive skeleton ranks, one even.
At most one removed key can lie on either side, so $L\ge4$ is impossible.

For $L=0$, each endpoint difference becomes one minus its previous value.
For $L=1$ with both intervals nonempty, their union is
$[\min B,\max A]$. The new lower difference is
$1+\min B-\min A\in\{0,1\}$ and the upper difference is one.
If only $A$ is nonempty, the pair becomes $(A+1,A)$; if only $B$ is
nonempty, only $B+1$ survives as $A'$. The remaining cases are immediate.
This proves the invariant inductively.
\end{proof}

For a terminal low run, write $R$ for all incoming ranks and $E$ for its
even subset. The final ranks are $R$ for $L=0$, $R\cup(E+1)$ for $L=1$,
$E+1$ for $L=2,3$, and empty otherwise. Save each high boundary's intervals.
After selecting final rank $m$, trace backward: each step has at most two
candidate predecessor ranks, and the short words above realize the chosen
transition. Concatenating the words and applying the comparison-length
Cartesian reconstruction returns the full tree in linear time and space.
Parity cannot be discarded: the high-key pattern $010100$ has reachable
final ranks $\{3,5\}$, so a single ordinary interval would invent rank four.

\begin{corollary}[Full linear construction on attaining two-level patterns]\label{result:legacy-42}
For arbitrary nonnegative two-level key weights with $S/2-1\le H\le S-1$
and arbitrary nonnegative alternating gap weights, the recognizer decides
whether a minimum-path-length shape attains the stated key profile and
constructs a witness in
$O(n)$ arithmetic operations and $O(n)$ auxiliary space. Every accepted
witness is a full optimal mixed-weight BST, without a baseline assumption.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The interval transitions and traceback take constant work per high boundary
and linear total input/output work. Only two key multiplicity records are
ordered, so computing $K(p)$ also takes linear work. The recognized word
assigns the high keys to the shortest $H$ comparison lengths, attaining
$K(p)$. Its minimum-path-length shape attains $G(q)$. Equality in (28)
certifies global optimality, including comparison with every taller tree.
\end{proof}

One key level is handled directly. At perfect sizes $s=0$, the implementation
constructs and evaluates the unique class shape. Equality in (28) certifies
it; otherwise the earlier mixed-Huffman gate may certify it. Failure invokes
exact Knuth. Rejected recognition does not imply that every minimum-path-length
shape is suboptimal. The standalone solver and routed portfolio both remain
exact, with quadratic worst-case fallback. The linear-space guarantee applies
to the successful route; general fallback can use quadratic space.

\subsubsection{An explicit alternating family with no baseline}\label{sec:legacy-24-3}
\begin{corollary}[Alternating keys and gaps]\label{result:legacy-43}
For $n\ge1$ with $n+1$ not a power of two, strictly alternating two-level
nonnegative key weights and alternating nonnegative gap weights admit full
exact $O(n)$-time, $O(n)$-space construction. The relative weight magnitudes
are unrestricted. Equal key levels are handled by the uniform route.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Here $s>0$. Let $A=1$ if the first key is high and $A=0$ otherwise.
If $s\bmod2=A$, remove the first $s$ low keys. Otherwise remove the first
$s-1$ lows and the last low. There are enough lows, and their positions
are nonadjacent. Every high key remains. Before the initially removed lows
end, retained keys are high. After removing $t$ initial lows, a high key's
skeleton-rank parity is $A-t$. The suffix therefore has high keys at even
ranks when $t\bmod2=A$. In the second construction, removing the final low
affects at most one subsequent high, which may occupy an odd rank.
Every even skeleton rank is high. The high count lies in the theorem's
range, so the recognizer succeeds and (28) proves the full claim.
\end{proof}

This family has $k=n$ joint signatures when the key levels differ. The
interval representation therefore escapes the preceding $O(nk)$ bound
without invoking a small-residual assumption. Nonperiodic accepted patterns
are also possible: choose any minimum-path-length shape, mark all even
skeleton ranks high, choose the odd skeleton labels freely, and label every
cherry low. Recognition receives only the resulting weights, not this witness.

The perfect-size exception matters. For $p=(100,1,100)$ and four zero gaps,
the unique minimum-path-length tree costs 401 while the full optimum is 303.
Cubic DP and Knuth agree. Thus the corollary cannot include every size and
orientation. Arbitrary integer magnitudes also retain their bit costs;
polynomially bounded weights yield the corresponding word-RAM bounds.


\subsection{Threshold runs and a linear Cartesian certificate}
\label{sec:legacy-25}

\label{sec:threshold-certificate}
We now allow arbitrary nonnegative gap sequences and arbitrary key values
in the certificate. The returned tree is optimal for the full mixed
objective; failure still requires an exact fallback. This extends the
preceding global alternating-gap argument rather than restricting the
problem to alphabetic coding.

\subsubsection{Binary indicators and arbitrary weighted layers}\label{sec:legacy-25-1}
For $H\ge1$, write
\[
 E(H)=(\lfloor\log_2 H\rfloor+2)H
       -2^{\lfloor\log_2 H\rfloor+1}.
\]
This is the minimum external path length of a full tree with $H$ unit
leaves; in particular $E(1)=0$.

\begin{lemma}\label{result:legacy-44}\label{lem:indicator-runs}
Give $H>0$ of $N$ ordered leaves weight one and the others weight zero.
Let $R$ be the number of contiguous zero runs, including endpoint runs.
The exact minimum weighted external path length is $E(H)+R$.
For $H=0$ the optimum is zero.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Remove every maximal zero-only subtree. Each lies within one zero run,
and every run contains at least one such subtree. Each removed subtree's
parent has a selected descendant in its other child, by maximality.
Suppressing the resulting unary nodes therefore decreases the selected
depth sum by at least one per removed subtree. What remains is a full
$H$-leaf unit tree, with cost at least $E(H)$. This proves the lower bound.

For attainment, start with any optimal $H$-leaf unit tree. Assign each
interior zero run to the selected leaf immediately to its left; assign an
initial run to the first selected leaf's left and a final run to the last
selected leaf's right. Each selected leaf has at most one right run, and
only the first can also have a left run. Expand each such leaf to a small
ordered tree accommodating the required zero-only subtrees. One assigned
run increases its selected depth by one; two runs increase it by two.
The zero-only subtrees have arbitrary required sizes and zero cost.
All original leaves and their order are preserved, with total cost $E(H)+R$.
\end{proof}

For arbitrary $q=(q_0,\ldots,q_{N-1})$, let $Q=\max_i q_i$.
At threshold $0<t\le Q$, let $H_t$ count weights at least $t$ and $R_t$
count runs of weights below $t$. Lemma~\ref{lem:indicator-runs} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:threshold-G}
 \operatorname{gapcost}(T,q)\ge G(q)
 :=\int_0^Q\bigl(E(H_t)+R_t\bigr)\,dt
 \qquad\hbox{for every tree }T.
\end{equation}
These integrals are exact finite layer sums. The minimum and sum need not
commute: $q=(100,10,1,10)$ has $G(q)=152$, but alphabetic optimum $153$.

Let $q_{[r]}$ denote the $r$th largest weight. Since
$E(r)-E(r-1)=\lceil\log_2 r\rceil+1$ for $r\ge2$, the bound becomes
\begin{align}
 F(q)&=\sum_{r=2}^{N}(\lceil\log_2 r\rceil+1)q_{[r]},\label{eq:threshold-F}\\
 D(q)&=Q-q_0+\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\max(q_{i-1}-q_i,0),\label{eq:threshold-D}\\
 G(q)&=F(q)+D(q).\nonumber
\end{align}
For~\eqref{eq:threshold-D}, a zero run starts at position zero for
$q_0<t\le Q$, or at position $i>0$ for $q_i<t\le q_{i-1}$.
Equivalently,
\[
 D(q)=Q-\frac{q_0+q_{N-1}}2
       +\frac12\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}|q_i-q_{i-1}|.
\]
Variation terms are familiar in alphabetic-code bounds, including upper
bounds reviewed by Bruno et al.~\cite{bruno}. The present lower-bound
derivation and equality certificate do not by themselves establish priority.

\subsubsection{Dyadic selection avoids sorting the gap weights}\label{sec:legacy-25-2}
\begin{theorem}\label{result:legacy-45}\label{thm:threshold-linear}
For arbitrary nonnegative weights, $G(q)$ can be computed in $O(N)$
arithmetic and comparison operations and $O(N)$ auxiliary space.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The variation term is one scan. For a current multiset of $M>1$ weights,
let $k$ be the largest power of two strictly less than $M$.
Every rank $k+1,\ldots,M$ has coefficient $\log_2 k+2$ in
\eqref{eq:threshold-F}. Use deterministic linear selection to retain the
$k$ largest values and charge this coefficient times the discarded sum.
Repeat on the retained multiset; the last single weight contributes zero.
After the first step the retained sizes halve. The total sizes processed,
$M,k,k/2,\ldots,2$, sum to less than $3M$. Standard median-of-five
selection~\cite{bfprt}, partitioning, summation, and copying are linear
in each processed size. Their total is therefore linear, with linear
auxiliary storage. Equal values at a partition boundary may be allocated
to either side to obtain exactly the required cardinality.
\end{proof}

The implementation also uses a cheaper scan-only formula when applicable.
Put $h=\lfloor\log_2 N\rfloor$, $S=2^h$, $s=N-S$, and $\beta=\min_iq_i$.
If at least $S/2$ gaps have weight $Q$ and at least $s$ have weight $\beta$,
then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:threshold-extrema}
 F(q)=(h+1)\sum_iq_i-SQ+s\beta.
\end{equation}
Indeed, for $\beta<t\le Q$, $S/2\le H_t\le S$, and
$E(H_t)=(h+1)H_t-S$. The part below $\beta$ is $\beta E(N)$,
where $E(N)=hN+2s$; integration gives~\eqref{eq:threshold-extrema}.
Uniform weights and $N=1$ also satisfy the formula. These checked
multiplicities choose an evaluation method, not an optimality certificate.

\subsubsection{Checking the key profile without sorting}\label{sec:legacy-25-3}
The preceding key capacity bound remains
\[
 K(p)=\sum_{r=1}^n p_{[r]}\lfloor\log_2 r+1\rfloor,
 \qquad \OPT(p,q)\ge K(p)+G(q).
\]
Let $H$ be the number of positive keys in a candidate tree and $d_i$ their
comparison lengths. Define $M(H)=\sum_{r=1}^H\lfloor\log_2 r+1\rfloor$.

\begin{lemma}\label{result:legacy-46}\label{lem:key-profile-linear}
A candidate's key cost equals $K(p)$ if and only if
\begin{enumerate}
\item $\sum_{i:p_i>0}d_i=M(H)$; and
\item every positive weight at a shallower depth is at least every
positive weight at a deeper depth.
\end{enumerate}
Both conditions are testable in linear work and space.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The sorted positive comparison lengths dominate the capacity profile
$\lfloor\log_2 r+1\rfloor$ componentwise. All weights being paired with
these lengths are strictly positive. Thus any excess length makes even
the rearranged cost strictly larger than $K(p)$. Equality of the
unweighted sums forces equality of the complete length profiles.
The second condition is precisely equality in the rearrangement step:
there is no strict weight inversion between different lengths.
A traversal collects the length sum and each positive depth's minimum
and maximum weight; adjacent nonempty depth levels suffice for the
second test. When $H=0$ the conditions are vacuous.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{result:legacy-47}\label{thm:cartesian-certificate}
Construct the max-Cartesian tree on $p$, with fixed leftmost priority for
ties. If its key cost equals $K(p)$ and gap cost equals $G(q)$, it is a
globally optimal full mixed-weight BST. Candidate construction, testing,
and successful output take $O(n)$ arithmetic work and auxiliary space.
If all keys have positive pairwise distinct weights, this test succeeds
if and only if some tree attains $K(p)+G(q)$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The two lower bounds hold for every tree, so their simultaneous attainment
proves optimality. Standard monotone-stack Cartesian construction~\cite{vuillemin} is
linear; use Lemma~\ref{lem:key-profile-linear} for the key test and
Theorem~\ref{thm:threshold-linear} for the gap test. Iterative traversal
and reverse traversal construct all output nodes in linear work, even
when the candidate has linear height.

For distinct positive weights, any attaining tree has weights ordered
by depth, so every parent has strictly greater weight than its children.
The inorder max-heap with distinct priorities is the unique Cartesian
tree. Hence any attaining tree must be the tested candidate.
\end{proof}

With ties, failure does not prove nonattainment. For $p=(1,1,1)$ and
$q=(1,1,1,1)$ the balanced tree attains cost $13$, whereas leftmost
Cartesian tie-breaking produces a chain. Even without this candidate
issue, simultaneous attainment is not universal: for $p=(2,1,1)$ and
$q=(100,10,1,10)$, the mixed bound is $158$ and full optimum $160$.
The standalone solver uses exact Knuth fallback. The portfolio first
tries the previous profile certificate, then the Cartesian certificate,
then the earlier signature/affine portfolio. The unrestricted worst
case remains quadratic. All linear claims here count exact arithmetic
operations; they are not bit-complexity or instruction-count bounds.

\subsubsection{A positive family with many key and gap levels}\label{sec:legacy-25-4}
\begin{corollary}\label{result:legacy-48}\label{thm:threshold-family}
Start with a perfect skeleton of $S-1$ keys, $S=2^h$, and expand any
$s$ odd-indexed bottom slots to cherries, where $0\le s\le S/2$.
Give every even slot gap weight $Q>0$, every unexpanded odd slot an
arbitrary weight in $[\beta,Q]$, and both gaps of each expanded slot
weight $\beta$, where $0<\beta\le Q$. Give all keys positive weights
strictly decreasing between depth layers, with arbitrary weights within
each layer. The Cartesian certificate constructs a full optimal tree in
$O(n)$ arithmetic work and space, where $n=S-1+s$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
For $\beta<t\le Q$, every even slot is selected. All selected gaps have
depth $h$. If $H$ are selected, zero runs number $S-H$: every zero slot
is odd and separated from the others by selected even slots. Expanding
a zero slot into two zero gaps does not change this run count. Since
$S/2\le H\le S$, we have $E(H)+(S-H)=hH$, exactly the candidate cost.
For $0<t\le\beta$, all $N=S+s$ gaps are selected and the minimum-path-length
shape attains $E(N)$. The candidate therefore attains every threshold
optimum and thus $G(q)$. Its key lengths form the capacity profile, and
the assigned weights decrease between depths, so it attains $K(p)$.
Strict ancestor inequalities make the Cartesian construction recover
this tree, even if some same-depth weights coincide.
\end{proof}

For the explicit tested family let $s=S/4$, $\beta=1$, $Q=n^2+2$,
give unexpanded odd slot $j$ weight $j+2$, and assign
\[
 p_i=(h+2-d_i)(n+1)+i.
\]
All $n$ key weights are distinct, and there are $S/4+2$ gap levels.
Both alphabets have $\Theta(n)$ values, gaps need not alternate, and
there is no large common baseline. The weights have $O(\log n)$ bits.
Independent positive rescaling of the key and gap vectors preserves the
two equalities; the theorem requires no dominance between their masses.
The solver receives only $p,q$, without the reference tree or slot pattern.


\subsection{Shifted alphabetic certificates beyond key-capacity equality}
\label{sec:legacy-27}

\begin{theorem}\label{result:legacy-50}\label{thm:shifted-alphabetic}
Let $m=\min p$, with $m=0$ when $n=0$. Define
\[
 L_m=K(p-m)+A(q+m)-mn.
\]
Then $K(p)+G(q)\le K(p)+A(q)\le L_m\le\OPT(p,q)$.
Among all admissible uniform transfers, $m$ gives the largest such
separate key/exact-gap bound. An alphabetic optimum $T$ for $q+m$
with original full cost $C(T;p,q)=L_m$ is globally optimal.
The bound, candidate, certificate, and complete tree construction take
$O(n\log(n+1))$ arithmetic work and $O(n)$ space.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The transfer identity gives
$L(a)=K(p-a)+A(q+a)-an\le\OPT$ for all admissible $a$.
Uniform subtraction preserves ranks and $K(p-a)=K(p)-aM(n)$, so
\[
 L(a)=K(p)+\min_T\left\{\sum_iq_id_i(T)
                  +a\left(\sum_i d_i(T)-E(n+1)\right)\right\}.
\]
Every affine term has nonnegative slope. Their minimum is nondecreasing,
so the largest admissible $a=m$ maximizes the bound. Taking $a=0$ gives
the claimed chain of inequalities. Equality of a feasible full cost
with a lower bound proves optimality. Equivalently, the alphabetic
candidate must attain $K(p-m)$ in the original residual key order.

Implement Hu--Tucker compatible merging using a leftist heap of internal
items in each gap between consecutive original leaves, and a priority
queue of each group's best compatible pair. Each pair is selected among
at most four items: two boundary leaves and two heap minima. Removing
a leaf melds its incident groups. There are $O(n)$ groups and events,
each meld or priority operation takes $O(\log(n+1))$, and stale events
are discarded. Recovering depths, alphabetic stack reconstruction, and
full original-cost evaluation are linear. Sorting residual keys costs
$O(n\log(n+1))$. No interval table is used on acceptance.
\end{proof}

The tie convention exactly reproduces infinitesimal original-leaf weights
$2^i$ without storing those large integers. Active items partition the
leaves; at equal mass, individual items compare by their largest original
index. Two pairs compare lexicographically by the larger, then the smaller,
of their items' largest indices. A shared item cancels; otherwise the
largest differing leaf determines the symbolic sum order. Only ordinary
indices are stored. The implementation separately counts heap-item,
priority-entry, and key-sort comparisons, as well as all events, melds,
reconstruction, cost visits, and fixed scans. These are arithmetic/logical
counts, with bit costs additional.

The six-key obstruction has $L_1=213$, and its shifted alphabetic candidate
is the full optimum. Thus normalization repairs the bound when the exact
alphabetic optimum replaces $G$, even though $K+G$ is invariant.
Tied alphabetic optima can hide an attaining candidate, and rejection
does not prove suboptimality or nonattainment. The standalone implementation
uses exact Knuth fallback.

\subsubsection{A positive nonuniform family with linear capacity deficit}\label{sec:legacy-27-1}
\begin{corollary}\label{result:legacy-51}\label{thm:shifted-family}
Let $r=2^h$, $n=7r-1$, and repeat the original seven-gap vector
$(18,2,13,3,17,1,2)$ exactly $r$ times. Place the six-key witness tree
above into every leaf of a perfect $r$-leaf skeleton. Give its $r$
deepest keys weight $1$, and every other key weight $1$ plus a positive
residual decreasing between depth layers. The shifted alphabetic
certificate constructs a full optimal tree in $O(n\log(n+1))$ work.
Its original key contribution exceeds $K(p)$ by exactly $r$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The $6r-1$ residual-positive keys fill all capacity levels above the last
occupied level, with $2r$ keys in that last level. Their weights decrease
between levels, so they attain $K(p-1)$. The $r$ residual-zero keys have
length $h+4$, one greater than their original unit-weight capacity slots
at length $h+3$. This gives the deficit $r$.

The shifted gap block is $(19,3,14,4,18,2,3)$. In every block compatible
merging performs $2+3=5$, $3+14=17$, $17+4=21$, and $18+5=23$.
Each stage finishes in all blocks before the next: cross-block pairs
do not undercut these costs. Per block remain an original leaf $19$
and internal items $21,23$. For even $r$, all original leaves pair at
$38$ before any competing remaining pair, removing all barriers.
Then $21$ pairs to $42$ and $23$ to $46$, giving $r/2$ copies of twice
$(19,21,23)$. Repeat until a single triple remains, with final merges
$19+21=40$ and $40+23=63$. For $r=1$ these are already compatible.
All same-stage ties give the same depths. Thus the ordered gap depths
are $h+(2,4,4,3,2,3,3)$ per block, with alphabetic cost $r(169+63h)$.
Reconstruction gives the stated tree, which attains both shifted terms.
\end{proof}

For a concrete integer family, if $\ell_i$ is its key comparison length,
set $p_i=1$ at length $h+4$, and otherwise
\[
 p_i=1+(h+4-\ell_i)(n+1)+i.
\]
This gives $6r$ distinct key weights, six distinct gap weights, strictly
positive outcomes, common key minimum $1$, and $O(\log n)$-bit weights.
No key-versus-gap dominance assumption is needed for the equality proof.
The family is outside the earlier original-capacity certificate for every
$r$: its full cost exceeds $K(p)+G(q)$ by at least $r$.


\subsection{Endpoint prices and the limit of additive transfers}
\label{sec:legacy-28}

The endpoint identities $\ell_0=d_0$ and $\ell_{n-1}=d_n$ permit transfers
that the preceding uniform normalization does not exploit.
\begin{theorem}\label{result:legacy-52}\label{thm:all-transfers}
For $n\ge3$, every additive weight change whose cost difference is
independent of the tree has the following form. Put $L=p_0+q_0$ and
$R=p_{n-1}+q_n$, and choose
\begin{align*}
 p'&=(x,p_1-a,\ldots,p_{n-2}-a,y),\\
 q'&=(L-x,q_1+a,\ldots,q_{n-1}+a,R-y).
\end{align*}
Then $C(T;p',q')=C(T;p,q)+an$. Nonnegativity is exactly
\[
 -\min_{1\le j<n}q_j\le a\le\min_{1\le i<n-1}p_i,
 \qquad 0\le x\le L,\quad 0\le y\le R.
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The depth identity and endpoint equalities prove sufficiency. For necessity
write $U_i=\delta p_i+\delta q_i$ and
$V_i=\delta p_i+\delta q_{i+1}$. A two-key rotation gives
$U_i=V_{i+1}$. Compare a rotation on keys $i+1,i+2$ with and without
key $i$ in its outer left subtree: subtraction gives $U_i=0$ for
$0\le i\le n-3$. The symmetric comparison gives $V_i=0$ for
$2\le i<n$. The two-key identities then supply $V_1=0$ and $U_{n-2}=0$.
All these rotations can be embedded in full trees on the original keys.
Consequently all interior gap changes equal one $a$, all interior key
changes are $-a$, and the endpoint key/gap sums are unchanged. The
resulting constant is $an$.
\end{proof}
This characterizes additive transfers on the given outcome coordinates,
not arbitrary encodings or all possible lower bounds.

\begin{theorem}\label{result:legacy-53}\label{thm:endpoint-face}
Let $F(a,x,y)=K(p')+A(q')-an$. The maximum of this valid lower bound over
the entire admissible box is attained on the face
$a=a_*:=\min_{1\le i<n-1}p_i$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Increase $a$ by $\delta$ while replacing $x,y$ by
$\max(0,x-\delta),\max(0,y-\delta)$. On a segment with $e\in\{0,1,2\}$
endpoint residual keys clamped at zero, the other $n-e$ positive keys
decrease uniformly until $a_*$. Thus $K$ has slope $-M(n-e)$.
All gaps except those $e$ endpoints increase uniformly. By
Lemma~\ref{lem:indicator-runs}, their minimum depth sum is
$E(n+1-e)+e=M(n-e)+n$. Every affine tree term in $F$ therefore has
nonnegative slope. Their minimum is nondecreasing; continuity joins the
segments. Moving any feasible point to the maximal face cannot reduce $F$.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{An exact bounded two-price search}\label{sec:legacy-28-1}
Fix $a=a_*$. An alphabetic optimum for $q'$ and a decreasing assignment
of $p'$ to capacity lengths provide the value $F(x,y)$. If the assigned
key lengths are $\lambda_i$ and the alphabetic gap depths are $d_j$, they
also give a global supporting plane
\[
 F(X,Y)\le c+(\lambda_0-d_0)X+(\lambda_{n-1}-d_n)Y.
\]
The plane is an upper bound on the concave \emph{bound function}; the
sampled $F(x,y)$ is a lower bound on the full optimum. The alphabetic
tree evaluated with the original ordered weights gives a feasible upper
bound on that optimum. These directions are distinct.

Maintain the planes and maximize their minimum over $[0,L]\times[0,R]$.
The implementation enumerates vertices of the three-dimensional hypograph
using integer determinants and Cramer's rule. Feasibility and objective
comparisons use exact cross-products; prices and bound values are rational.
There is no floating-point tolerance. Retain the largest sampled bound
and the smallest original tree cost across calls. For integer original
weights, equality of the latter with the ceiling of the former certifies
full optimality. Equality between the LP upper bound and best sampled
bound instead proves completion of the price maximization. An early full
certificate or a call-budget stop need not complete that maximization.

With $b$ alphabetic calls, all preprocessing, exact pricing, and candidate
construction take
\[
 O\bigl(bn\log(n+1)+b^5\bigr)\text{ arithmetic work},\qquad O(n+b)\text{ space}.
\]
Each LP call checks $O(b^3)$ triples against $O(b)$ constraints. The
default $b=\max(2,\operatorname{bitlength}(n))$ gives
$O(n\log^2(n+1)+\log^5(n+1))$, asymptotically $O(n\log^2 n)$, for
the attempted certificate. Rejection uses Knuth; the unrestricted worst
case remains quadratic. Supporting slopes have magnitude $O(n)$ and
integer intercepts $O(nW)$, so vertex denominators are $O(n^2)$ and the
scaled alphabetic weights use only $O(\log n)$ additional bits. Bit costs
remain additional. The uncapped diagnostic has no proved small iteration
bound here.

\subsubsection{A positive family with distinct gaps and light endpoint keys}\label{sec:legacy-28-3}
\begin{corollary}\label{result:legacy-55}\label{thm:endpoint-family}
Let $h\ge3$, $B=2^h$, $n=B-1$, and let
$\ell_i=h-v_2(i+1)$ be the perfect tree's key comparison lengths.
Initially set $p_i=B+(h+1-\ell_i)B+i$, then replace
$p_0=p_{n-1}=1$ and $p_2=B$. Set $q_0=B$, $q_n=B+n$, and $q_j=j+1$
for $1\le j<n$. The first endpoint-price call constructs a full optimum
in $O(n\log n)$ arithmetic work and $O(n)$ space, with exact cost
\[
 \frac{(8h-11)B^2+(13-7h)B}{2}-1.
\]
There are $n-1$ distinct key weights and $n+1$ distinct gap weights,
all positive and with $O(\log n)$ bits. The preceding uniform-shift
bound is at least three below the optimum for every such size.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
At $a_*=B$, $x=y=0$, exactly three bottom residual keys are zero.
The other $n-3$ keys attain capacity and decrease between depth layers.
The transformed gaps are $B+j+1$, all in $[B+1,2B]$. Unrestricted
Huffman merging pairs these $B$ outcomes level by level: at each level
every newly generated mass exceeds every unmerged same-level mass since
the maximum weight is less than twice the minimum. All leaves have depth
$h$. The perfect alphabetic tree attains that unrestricted optimum and
both transformed lower bounds, proving full mixed optimality. Summing
the perfect lengths, their squares, and the three bottom-key corrections
gives the formula.

The preceding uniform shift uses only $\min p=1$. Its residual key bound
is already attained by the perfect tree. In the last four shifted gaps,
the two left weights sum to $2B-3$, while the rightmost weight is $2B$.
Changing the balanced four-leaf subtree to the right-heavy alternative
saves three in the gap objective. Thus the old bound is strictly below
the perfect full optimum, regardless of which candidate it constructs.
\end{proof}

Twelve complete family constructions reach $n=262143$, with $262142$
distinct keys, $262144$ distinct gaps, and full cost $4569830391807$.
The preceding bound is $4561240391642$, a deficit of $8590000165$.
The largest run uses one alphabetic call, $262143$ merges, $7111153$
priority comparisons, $8924411$ heap-item comparisons, and $1482369$
key-sort comparisons. It constructs every key with no LP vertices or
interval-DP cells. Seven sizes through $511$ match Knuth; three also
match cubic. The formula and an independently built perfect tree verify
all larger sizes. All scans, depth recovery, events, and output visits
are retained in the source-hashed results.


\subsection{Root/nonroot coupling beyond the transfer barrier}
\label{sec:legacy-29}

The four-key obstruction proved in Theorem~\ref{result:legacy-54} loses the coupling between one residual
interior key and the surrounding gap tree. A partition by the identity of the
root retains part of that coupling at the cost of two additional alphabetic
constructions. It applies to arbitrary mixed input weights; successful
certification, rather than the validity of the bound, is conditional.

\begin{theorem}\label{result:legacy-56}
Let $n\ge2$, let $r$ index a largest key weight $w=p_r$, and put
$v=\max_{i\ne r}p_i$, $P=\sum_i p_i$, and $Q=\sum_j q_j$. Define
\begin{align*}
N_r&=K(p)+A(q)+w-v,\\
R_r&=P+Q+K(p_{[0,r)})+K(p_{(r,n)})
              +A(q_{[0,r]})+A(q_{[r+1,n]}).
\end{align*}
Then
\[
 K(p)+A(q)\ \le\ \min(N_r,R_r)\ \le\ \OPT(p,q).
\]
Here $K$ is the free key-capacity bound and $A$ the exact alphabetic gap optimum;
empty key subvectors have bound zero.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
If the root is not $r$, let its weight be $u\le v$, and let the comparison
length of $r$ be $\ell\ge2$. Exchanging those two weights reduces the key
cost by $(w-u)(\ell-1)\ge w-v$. The exchanged cost is at least $K(p)$,
so the original key cost is at least $K(p)+w-v$. Adding $A(q)$ proves $N_r$.
The exchange is solely a lower-bound argument; it does not change the ordered
input or construct a different search tree.

If the root is $r$, the recurrence pays $P+Q$ and the two ordered subproblem
costs. Bounding their keys and gaps separately gives $R_r$. These cases
partition all trees. Moreover, the root-constrained free key placement costs
at least $K(p)$, and the root-constrained alphabetic tree costs at least
$A(q)$. Hence each case bound dominates the original separable bound.
\end{proof}

The implementation first applies Theorem~\ref{result:legacy-52} with
$a=\min_{1\le i<n-1}p_i$ and endpoint residuals
$x=\max(0,p_0-a)$, $y=\max(0,p_{n-1}-a)$. It subtracts $an$ from transformed
bounds and evaluates every candidate using the original mixed weights.
One alphabetic call constructs the global gap optimum. A single decreasing
key sort supplies $K$ and, by scanning its left and right subsequences,
both conditional capacity sums. If the global candidate already meets
$K+A$, the attempt ends. Otherwise two alphabetic calls construct the gap
optima on either side of $r$, and a second candidate joins them at root $r$.
The smaller original candidate cost is compared with $\min(N_r,R_r)-an$.
Equality certifies full optimality.

At most three alphabetic calls process a total of $2(n+1)$ leaves. Sorting,
normalization, rebasing, reconstruction, and cost evaluation give
$O(n\log(n+1))$ arithmetic work and $O(n)$ space for the complete attempt.
The constant-size $n<3$ case is solved directly. Rejection uses Knuth and
retains the unrestricted quadratic worst case. The fixed endpoint choice
need not preserve certificates found at another price; the preceding solvers
remain separate and unchanged.

For Theorem~\ref{result:legacy-54}, normalization gives $p'=(0,0,1,0)$ and
$q'=(2,3,2,2,2)$ with additive constant four. Here $K=1$, $A=26$,
$r=2$, and both $N_r$ and $R_r$ equal 28. The new original bound is therefore
24, exactly the full optimum, while the maximum over the entire preceding
transfer class is 23.

\subsubsection{A positive family outside every separable additive transfer}\label{sec:legacy-29-1}
\begin{corollary}\label{result:legacy-57}
Let $h\ge3$, $N=2^h$, $n=N-1$, and let
$\ell_i=h-\nu_2(i+1)$ be the perfect tree's key comparison lengths. Put
\[
 r=N/4-1,\qquad s=N/2-1,\qquad C=4hN,\qquad w=hN^2/2.
\]
Initially set $p_i=1+(h-\ell_i)N+i+1$, then replace
\[
 p_0=p_2=p_{n-1}=1,\qquad p_r=w+1.
\]
Set $q_j=C+j-1$ for $0\le j<N$. The root/nonroot certificate constructs a
full optimum in $O(n\log n)$ arithmetic work and $O(n)$ space. Its exact cost is
\[
 \OPT=\frac{(8h^2+6h-7)N^2+(12-5h)N}{2}+1-3h.
\]
Every weight is positive and has $O(\log n)$ bits. There are $n-2$ distinct
key weights and $n+1$ distinct gap weights. Nevertheless, the maximum
$K+A$ bound over every admissible additive cost-preserving transfer falls
short by exactly
\[
 D=\frac{hN^2-(2h-1)N}{2}>0.
\]
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The maximal interior transfer is $a=1$. At endpoint prices zero, residual
keys $\widehat p_i=p_i-1$ have largest value $w$ at $r$, next-largest value
$v=(h-\tfrac12)N$ at the perfect root $s$, and three zero bottom keys.
All other weights decrease strictly between perfect depth layers. The
perfect tree attains the nonroot key bound $K(\widehat p)+w-v$: exchanging
the weights at $r$ and $s$ attains the free capacity profile.

Transformed gaps are $\widehat q_j=C+j$, with maximum less than twice the
minimum. Since $N$ is a power of two, unrestricted Huffman merging pairs
nodes level by level. Every gap has depth $h$, and the perfect alphabetic
tree attains the unrestricted minimum. Consequently
\[
 A(\widehat q)=h\left(CN+\frac{N(N-1)}2\right).
\]
If $r$ is the root, its two sides contain $N/4$ and $3N/4$ gaps. The minimum
unit-weight total depth, including that root, is $Nh+N/4$. All transformed
gap weights are at least $C$, so the root-$r$ alphabetic cost exceeds
$A(\widehat q)$ by at least
\[
 \frac{CN}{4}-\frac{hN(N-1)}2=w+\frac{hN}{2}>w-v.
\]
Its conditional key bound is at least $K(\widehat p)$. Thus $R_r>N_r$,
and the perfect candidate attains $N_r$, proving full optimality.

For the cost formula, use
$\sum_i\ell_i=(h-1)N+1$,
$\sum_i\ell_i^2=N(h^2-2h+3)-3$, and
$\sum_i(i+1)\ell_i=(N/2)\sum_i\ell_i$.
After the three zero-key corrections and the replacement at $r$, the perfect
residual key cost is
$(5h-7)N^2/2+(7-2h)N-3h$.
Adding $A(\widehat q)$ and subtracting $n$ yields the displayed full cost.

Finally, Theorem~\ref{result:legacy-53} reduces all admissible transfers to $a=1$ and two
endpoint prices. At $x=y=0$, both zero endpoint keys occupy capacity slots
of length $h$, and both endpoint gaps also have depth $h$. The supporting
plane from Section~\ref{sec:legacy-28-1} therefore has both slopes zero and value
$K(\widehat p)+A(\widehat q)$. This bounds the entire price objective from
above and is attained at zero. The best original separable bound is exactly
$K(\widehat p)+A(\widehat q)-n=\OPT-(w-v)$, giving the stated deficit.
\end{proof}


\subsection{A sufficient exchange condition for a wide last level}\label{sec:legacy-21-2}
Let $h=\lfloor\log_2(n+1)\rfloor$, $c=2^h-1$, and $s=n-c$.
As proved earlier, minimum internal-path-length shapes expand $s$ of the
$2^h$ external slots of a perfect $c$-key skeleton. Consider adjacent
skeleton slots $g,g+1$ containing one expansion. If their first input
index is $i$, moving the expansion from the second slot to the first
changes the weighted cost by exactly
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ruler-exchange}
 (1+\nu_2(g+1))(p_i-p_{i+1})+q_i-q_{i+2}.
\end{equation}
Only the intervening skeleton key, added key, and three gaps change their
comparison lengths; all later positions are unchanged. The coefficient
$1+\nu_2(g+1)$ lies between one and $h$.

\begin{samepage}
\begin{theorem}[Exchange-certified minimum-path-length shape]\label{result:legacy-34}
If
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ruler-condition}
 \max\{p_i-p_{i+1},\ h(p_i-p_{i+1})\}\le q_{i+2}-q_i
 \quad(0\le i<n-1),
\end{equation}
then filling the last level from the left minimizes weighted cost among
all minimum internal-path-length shapes. The reflected condition gives
the corresponding right-filled shape. For $s=0$ the shape is unique
without any condition.
\end{theorem}
\end{samepage}
\begin{proof}
The maximum in~\eqref{eq:ruler-condition} bounds the key term
in~\eqref{eq:ruler-exchange}, whether its difference is positive or
negative. Every adjacent swap of expansion pattern $01$ to $10$ is
therefore nonincreasing in cost. Repeated swaps move every expansion
leftward, yielding the claimed shape. Reflection proves the other case.
\end{proof}

In particular, nondecreasing key and gap weights satisfy the condition;
both lists nonincreasing satisfy its reflection. Nonmonotone keys are also
permitted when the gap increments compensate for their decreases.
This proves optimality \emph{within the shape class}. Global optimality
still requires the earlier residual-Huffman gate. For candidate value $V$,
minimum weights $a,b$, and residual mixed-node lower bound $H_{\rm res}$,
the sufficient condition is
\[
 V\le(a+b)(I_{\min}(n)+1)+(a+2b)n+H_{\rm res}.
\]
The exchange constructor constructs the candidate,
checks this gate, and otherwise falls back to Knuth. Accepted instances
take $O(n\log n)$ time and $O(n)$ auxiliary space, including sorting for
the bound. If both weight lists are monotone, scans/reversal and the
existing two queues make the whole accepted route $O(n)$. No optional-slot
width appears. Sorted geometric controls pass the shape condition but fail
the global gate, preventing an incorrect general claim about balanced trees.


\section{Negative results and representation barriers}\label{sec:negative}
These results identify information lost by particular relaxations or
costs imposed by particular representations. Each statement specifies its
model of failure. None is an unrestricted computational lower bound.

\subsection{A useful relaxation that failed as a general solver}
\label{sec:legacy-15}
\label{sec:unordered}
Allow arbitrary assignment of key weights to internal positions and gap
weights to external positions. In this relaxed problem, repeatedly take
the two smallest current external weights $a,b$ and the smallest remaining
internal weight $p$, replace them by $a+b+p$, and add that sum to the cost.

To prove the rule, take a deepest internal node of an optimum.
Its children are leaves. Swapping the smallest internal weight into it
and the two smallest external weights into its children cannot increase
cost. Contract that cherry and induct. Sorting and a heap give
$O(n\log n)$ time. This elementary extension of the Huffman exchange
argument is not claimed as novel.

The relaxed cost is a valid lower bound for full OBST. Equality with a
feasible ordered tree certifies optimality. It agreed with an independent
shape-enumeration and rearrangement oracle on 22,143 exhaustive and 2,700
random instances in the basic harness.

However, using the relaxation on demand in an exact sparse DP was poor
on random inputs. At $n=128$ it visited roughly 4,300--4,600 exact intervals
instead of Knuth's 8,256, but spent approximately 3.9--4.1 million weight
comparisons constructing bounds and inspected over 100,000 candidate roots.
Fewer exact states did not mean less computation.

Nor does the relaxation's interval matrix inherit the required quadrangle
inequality. For $p=(9,1,12)$, $q=(5,1,18,10)$, its costs $H$ satisfy
\[
 H(1,2)+H(2,3)=60>54=H(1,3)+H(2,2).
\]
Thus applying Knuth-style matrix optimization directly to this bound
would be unjustified.

Two additional exact counterexamples exclude tempting local certificates.
With $p=(3,5,18)$, $q=(13,14,4,8)$, the tree with root 2 and left subtree
$0(\varnothing,1)$ costs 127, every single-rotation neighbor costs at least 134,
yet the balanced tree costs 125.
With $p=(18,8,18)$, $q=(17,11,1,13)$, the balanced tree costs 164 and every
distinct tree obtained by promoting a descendant to the root of an existing
subtree costs at least 169; the tree $0(\varnothing,2(1,\varnothing))$ costs 157.
These are barriers to those local certificates, not complexity lower bounds.


\subsection{An ordered depth-chain lower bound}
\label{sec:legacy-16}

Let $l_i$ be the comparison length of key $i$ and $g_i$ the depth of gap $i$.
For every valid BST, adjacent keys have different lengths and
\[
 g_0=l_0,\quad g_n=l_{n-1},\quad
 g_i=\max(l_{i-1},l_i)\quad(0<i<n).
\]
This identity is existing work; see Becker~\cite{becker}, Lemma~1.
Let $K_d$ count keys of length $d$ and $G_d$ count gaps at depth $d$.
The root and child-count identities are
\[
 K_1=1,\qquad G_d+K_{d+1}-2K_d=0\quad(1\le d\le n),
 \qquad K_{n+1}=0.
\]

Permit any sequence in $\{1,\ldots,n\}^n$ with unequal adjacent entries.
For arbitrary multipliers $\lambda_0,\ldots,\lambda_n$, minimize
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dual}
 \sum_i p_i l_i+\sum_i q_i g_i
 +\lambda_0(K_1-1)
 +\sum_{d=1}^n\lambda_d(G_d+K_{d+1}-2K_d).
\end{equation}
Call the minimum $L(\lambda)$. Every actual tree is permitted and has zero
penalty, so $L(\lambda)\le\OPT$ for every multiplier vector.
The expression is a chain with key energy
$U_i(b)=p_i b+\lambda_{b-1}-2\lambda_b$, gap energy
$V_i(b)=q_i b+\lambda_b$, and constant $-\lambda_0$.
For an interior transition into length $b$, take the smaller of
\[
 \min_{a<b}F(a)+V_i(b),\qquad
 \min_{a>b}\{F(a)+V_i(a)\}.
\]
Prefix and suffix minima give $O(H)$ work per key for a domain of $H$ lengths.
Using $H=n$ would cost quadratic time even for one bound evaluation.

\begin{theorem}[Exact affine-tail compression]\label{result:legacy-21}
Fix $h\ge1$, arbitrary $\lambda_0,\ldots,\lambda_h$, and a slope
$0\le s\le\min_i p_i$. Extend
$\lambda_d=\lambda_h+s(d-h)$ for $d>h$.
The minimum in~\eqref{eq:dual} is unchanged if lengths are restricted to
$1,\ldots,\min(n,h+3)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For $b\ge h+1$, the unary key energy has slope $p_i-s\ge0$ and the gap
energy has slope $q_i+s\ge0$. Both are nondecreasing in this tail.
If some $l_i>h+3$, replace it by a member of
$\{h+1,h+2,h+3\}$ unequal to its current neighbors. At most two values
are forbidden. The key energy does not increase. Each adjacent maximum
is either unchanged or decreases within the affine tail, so neither gap
energy increases. Endpoint gaps obey the same argument. Repeat for all
large lengths. The restricted domain is a subset of the original domain,
and every original sequence has a no-more-expensive restricted sequence;
therefore the minima are equal.
\end{proof}

Taking $h=\lceil\log_2(n+1)\rceil+2$ gives an $O(n\log n)$ oracle.
This is a statement about minimization of the relaxed energy, with no height
assumption on the unknown optimal BST. Optimizing only the head multipliers
requires folding all tail constraint violations into the derivative for
$\lambda_h$. The implementation uses exact integer multipliers and costs
scaled by 1024. Its subgradient ascent is heuristic; the bound is valid
at every iteration regardless of convergence.

A stronger oracle requires exactly one length1 key. For each candidate
root, the left and right chains then contain only lengths at least2.
One forward and one reverse pass supply their closing costs for every
root position, giving all forced-root bounds in $O(nH)$ total time.
The projection above preserves the unique root, so tail compression remains
valid for each marginal. Retaining the maximum bound seen for each root,
then taking the minimum over roots, is also a valid lower bound.

\paragraph{A structural gap.}
The sequence $l=(3,1,2,3,2)$ has
$g=(3,3,2,3,3,2)$, a unique root, and all the level-count equalities.
It is not a valid BST depth sequence. For
\[
 p=(3,27,21,16,12),\qquad q=(18,22,27,22,12,22),
\]
it costs 470, while the true optimum is 472. Its penalty is zero for every
multiplier vector. Hence neither oracle family can ever exceed 470 on
this instance. More optimization iterations cannot remove this obstruction.


\subsection{Exact depth ordering and an arbitrary-window obstruction}
\label{sec:legacy-17}

Call a positive integer sequence \emph{ranked} if every nonempty contiguous
interval has a unique minimum. Equivalently, equal labels have a smaller
label between them. This is vertex ranking on a path with the label order
reversed; vertex ranking and bounded-path variants are existing
notions~\cite{ranking,localranking}. The following results explain the
ordering information absent from the preceding relaxation; no novelty claim
is made for this characterization.

\begin{theorem}[Depth characterization]\label{result:legacy-22}
A sequence $l\in\{1,\ldots,n\}^n$, $n\ge1$, is the inorder
key-comparison-length sequence of a BST if and only if it is ranked,
$K_1=1$, and every level-flow equality holds, with gaps derived from $l$
as above.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Necessity follows from the unique shallowest key in each inorder interval
and the two children of every internal node. For sufficiency, delete all
labels at most $d$. Let $U_d$ count the remaining nonempty runs, and let
$A_d$ count the deleted labels. If the deleted labels form $R$ runs and
include $e$ endpoints, then the number of gaps of length at most $d$ is
$A_d-R+e$, whereas $U_d=R+1-e$. Thus
\[
 G_{\le d}=A_d+1-U_d.
\]
Summing the level-flow equalities from 1 through $d$, and using $K_1=1$,
also gives
\[
 G_{\le d}=A_d+1-K_{d+1}.
\]
Hence $U_d=K_{d+1}$. Ranking permits at most one label $d+1$ per remaining
run, so equality forces exactly one. Starting with the unique length1
root, every remaining nonempty interval therefore receives its root at
the next level. This constructs the tree with exactly the supplied lengths.
\end{proof}

Ranking has a linear recognizer: scan with an increasing stack, pop larger
labels, reject an equal top, and otherwise push. A rejected pair has no
smaller intervening label; conversely every such pair causes rejection.
Each entry is pushed once and popped at most once. This establishes cheap
recognition, not cheap minimization over all valid sequences.

\begin{theorem}[Bounded-window obstruction]\label{result:legacy-23}
For every integer $w\ge2$ and $t\ge2$, there is a strictly positive full-OBST
instance with $n=w+3$ keys and an inadmissible depth sequence satisfying
all level-flow equalities, a unique length1 root, and ranking on every
interval of at most $w$ keys. Its cost is at most
$(7/8+1/(8t))\OPT$. Consequently every lower bound obtained by pricing
the level-flow equalities over this window-constrained domain has the same
upper limitation, regardless of the multipliers.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Take
\[
 l=(3,1,2,3,4,\ldots,w+1,2).
\]
The ascending middle portion has $w-1$ entries. Its two enclosing length2
labels form the only interval with a repeated minimum, of width $w+1$.
Every shorter window passes. Direct substitution into the gap formula
verifies $K_1=1$ and every level-flow equality. The sequence is invalid:
the unique root is key1, but its singleton left subtree has length3,
and two keys to its right both claim length2.

Give every gap weight1 and every key weight1 except
\[
 p_1=3B,\qquad p_2=p_{n-1}=B.
\]
The unit-weight outcomes contribute
$E=w^2+4w+10$ in the inadmissible sequence, whose cost is $7B+E$.
Every actual tree costs at least $8B$. If key1 is the root, the two
$B$-weight keys lie to its right and their comparison lengths sum to at
least5, giving $3B+5B$. Otherwise key1 has length at least2 and each
$B$-weight key has length at least1, giving $6B+2B$.

Set $B=tE$. The inadmissible cost satisfies
\[
 7B+E=\left(\frac78+\frac{1}{8t}\right)8B
       \le\left(\frac78+\frac{1}{8t}\right)\OPT.
\]
Its level-flow penalty is zero for every multiplier vector, so every
priced minimum is at most this cost.
\end{proof}

For $t=16$ the upper ratio is $113/128$, independently of $w$; as $t$
increases it approaches $7/8$. This rules out universal exactness of the
specified window-minimum repair, not other local constraints, adaptive
nonlocal cuts, or subquadratic full-OBST algorithms.
Equivalently, at every $n\ge5$ the construction passes all windows of up
to $n-3$ keys.

\paragraph{Burying the obstruction below a forced prefix.}
Let a current instance have mass $W$ and a feasible tree of cost $U$.
Prepend a key of weight $A=W+U+2$ and a new leftmost gap of weight1.
Using the new key as root costs $A+1+W+U=2A-1$, while every other root
costs at least $2A$ from that key alone. Hence the new root is forced and
$\OPT_{\rm new}=A+1+W+\OPT_{\rm old}$.
The transformation $l\mapsto(1,l_0+1,\ldots,l_{n-1}+1)$ preserves zero
flow and local window ranking, and changes the inadmissible cost by the
same $A+1+W$. Repeating this $D$ times places the strict gap beneath the
true optimal top-$D$ right-chain prefix. Thus fixing any constant number
of top levels cannot make the remaining window/flow relaxation exact.
The constant relative gap is not claimed after this embedding, because
the forcing weights add a potentially large common cost.


\subsection{Endpoint responses: insufficiency and representation size}
\label{sec:legacy-18}

An intact subtree interacts with its ancestors through its relative cost
and total mass. If its interior is not yet fixed, one tempting richer
summary is the response to added weights at its endpoint gaps:
\[
 \mathcal R(x,y)=\OPT(p,q+xe_0+ye_n),\qquad x,y\ge0.
\]
For a fixed tree the response is $C(T)+xd_0(T)+yd_n(T)$, so
$\mathcal R$ is a concave lower envelope of affine planes. We distinguish
whether this function contains enough information for composition from
how many pieces an explicit representation requires. Neither result below
is a general lower bound for optimal-BST construction; priority remains
unestablished.

\subsubsection{Equal responses need not be interchangeable}\label{sec:legacy-18-1}
\begin{theorem}[Insufficient endpoint-value interface]\label{result:legacy-24}\label{thm:response-insufficient}
An interval's endpoint response, length, total mass, and endpoint gap
weights do not determine its optimal cost in an arbitrary surrounding
search problem. This remains true if its full vector of key weights
and its multiset of gap weights are also retained.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Consider two four-key intervals with
\[
 p=(1,1,2,3),\quad q^A=(1,4,2,1,1),\quad q^B=(1,4,1,2,1).
\]
Both have mass 16 and endpoint gap weights one. Enumerating the 14
four-key shapes gives the following least intercepts for each possible
endpoint-depth pair:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{crr}
\toprule
Depths & $A$ & $B$\\
\midrule
$(1,2)$ & 45 & 45\\
$(1,3)$ & 42 & 42\\
$(1,4)$ & 44 & 45\\
$(2,1)$ & 43 & 43\\
$(2,2)$ & 36 & 36\\
$(2,3)$ & 36 & 37\\
$(3,1)$ & 39 & 39\\
$(3,2)$ & 36 & 36\\
$(4,1)$ & 43 & 42\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Each plane with slopes $(1,4),(2,3),(3,2)$, or $(4,1)$ is pointwise
dominated on the nonnegative quadrant by a common plane whose slopes
and intercept are no larger. Hence both responses are exactly
\begin{align*}
 \mathcal R(x,y)=\min\{&45+x+2y,\ 42+x+3y,\ 43+2x+y,\\
                      &36+2x+2y,\ 39+3x+y\}.
\end{align*}
This proves equality for all parameters, not just sampled ones.

Prepend keys of weights $(7,1)$, append keys of weights $(1,7)$, and
give the four new gaps weight one. The full inputs are
\begin{align*}
 P&=(7,1,1,1,2,3,1,7),\\
 Q^A&=(1,1,1,4,2,1,1,1,1),\\
 Q^B&=(1,1,1,4,1,2,1,1,1).
\end{align*}
Using the exact recurrence, the least full costs conditional on each
root are
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrrr}
\toprule
Root & 0 & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5 & 6 & 7\\
\midrule
$A$ & 108 & 108 & 103 & 101 & 101 & 101 & 111 & 110\\
$B$ & 108 & 108 & 103 & 101 & 100 & 101 & 111 & 110\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Thus the optima are 101 and 100. Direct enumeration of all 1,430
eight-key trees independently verifies both rows. Equal summaries
with the identical surrounding context therefore have unequal answers.
\end{proof}

In particular, exact prepend/append updates closed on the stated summary
cannot exist: identical starting summaries and identical updates would
remain indistinguishable. The theorem does not exclude extra shape
information or richer interfaces. Section~\ref{sec:signed-boundary}
proves that non-exposed depth states and signed-price responses do retain
the original gap vector with the stated metadata. It does not assert a distinction between mixed and alphabetic
algorithms; it rejects this summary even on small positive mixed inputs.

The lost information includes internal alternatives with identical boundary
depths. Both intervals have minimum cost 36 at depths $(2,2)$. The shape
rooted at key 2, with left child 0 (whose right child is 1) and right
child 3, also has depths $(2,2)$, but costs 37 in $A$ and 36 in $B$.
The least intercept erases this distinction; the surrounding tree can
make the additional alternative valuable.

\subsubsection{The legal domain loses information; signed prices recover it}
\label{sec:signed-boundary}

The information boundary can be located more precisely. Allowing all
nonnegative endpoint assignments still loses information, while a bounded
line of signed assignments determines the original interval.

\begin{theorem}[Insufficiency on the full legal endpoint domain]
\label{thm:legal-response-collision}
Even the response to every nonnegative reassignment of the two endpoint
gaps, together with the key vector, total mass, original endpoint gaps,
and gap multiset, does not determine arbitrary contextual optima.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Use $p=(2,1,2,4)$ and interior gaps $(4,2,1)$ or $(4,1,2)$.
With endpoints set to $u,v\ge0$, both responses equal
\[
 \min\{45+u+2v,42+u+3v,43+2u+v,36+2u+2v,39+3u+v\}.
\]
Indeed, move each unit outer gap in Theorem~\ref{thm:response-insufficient}
to its adjacent endpoint key. Their depths agree in every isolated tree,
so the same complete intercept table and domination certificate apply.
Choose actual endpoints one: both inputs are strictly positive, have mass
18, and have all the listed metadata equal. Prepending keys $(1,6)$,
appending $(4,3)$, and giving the four new gaps weight one yields keys
$(1,6,2,1,2,4,4,3)$ and full gap vectors
$(1,1,1,4,2,1,1,1,1)$ and $(1,1,1,4,1,2,1,1,1)$.
The root-conditioned costs are respectively
\[
 (128,106,103,101,101,101,109,120),\qquad
 (128,106,103,101,100,101,109,120).
\]
Their optima are 101 and 100. Thus equality holds on the entire legal
domain, including zero endpoints, yet the same context distinguishes them.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Extreme boundary states determine all gaps]
\label{lem:chain-recovery}
For nonnegative real weights, the complete endpoint-depth profile determines
the original gaps when the key vector $p$, total mass $W$, and left gap
$q_0$ are retained. Only its $n$ states with endpoint-depth sum $n+1$
are needed, and recovery takes $O(n)$ arithmetic operations.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If the endpoint depths are $a,b$, their two paths contain $a+b-1$ distinct
keys. Equality $a+b=n+1$ forces every key onto these paths. For each root
$r$, there is exactly one such tree: a decreasing left chain and increasing
right chain, with endpoint depths $(r+1,n-r)$. Let its cost be $C_r$.
Every outcome depth changes by at most one between adjacent chain trees,
so $|C_r-C_{r-1}|\le W$. Direct differencing gives
\begin{align}
 C_{r-1}-2C_r+C_{r+1}&=2p_r+q_r+q_{r+1}\ge0,
       &&1\le r\le n-2,\label{eq:chain-curvature}\\
 C_1-C_0&=2(p_0+q_0)+q_1-W.\label{eq:chain-first-difference}
\end{align}
For example, key comparison length is $|i-r|+1$; interior gap $j$ has
depth $r-j+2$ for $j\le r$ and $j-r+1$ for $j>r$.
The outer gaps have depths $r+1,n-r$. These formulas prove both identities.
For $n\ge2$, recover
\begin{align}
 q_1&=W+C_1-C_0-2(p_0+q_0),\nonumber\\
 q_{r+1}&=C_{r-1}-2C_r+C_{r+1}-2p_r-q_r,
       &&1\le r\le n-2,\label{eq:chain-gap-recovery}\\
 q_n&=W-\sum_i p_i-\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}q_j.\nonumber
\end{align}
For $n=1$, the last mass identity suffices. The chain coefficients themselves
are computable in linear work: initialize
$C_0=\sum_i(i+1)p_i+q_0+nq_n+\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}(j+1)q_j$,
then use \eqref{eq:chain-first-difference} and \eqref{eq:chain-curvature}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Recovery from a bounded signed response]
\label{thm:signed-recovery}
Define $F(x,y)=\min_T\{\operatorname{cost}(T)+xd_0(T)+yd_n(T)\}$
for arbitrary real $x,y$. Given $p,W,q_0$, a bounded line of this signed
response determines all gaps. For nonnegative integer input on $n\ge2$
keys, at most $n$ exact value-only oracle queries recover them, with
$O(n)$ additional arithmetic work and space. Query coordinates have
magnitude $O(n(W+2))$, denominator at most $n^2-1$, and bit length
$O(\log(n(W+2)))$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Put $M=n+1$ and $K=4(n+1)(W+2)$. On the line
$x=-K+t$, $y=-K$, $|t|\le W+2$, any non-chain tree has endpoint-depth
sum at most $n$ and value at least $-Kn-n|t|$.
Any chain has value at most $nW-K(n+1)+n|t|$.
Since $K>nW+2n|t|$, a chain always wins. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:signed-chain-line}
 G(t):=F(-K+t,-K)+K(n+1)
      =\min_{0\le r<n}\{C_r+(r+1)t\}.
\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq:chain-curvature}, $C$ is convex, and its first differences
lie in $[-W,W]$. Thus every coefficient is exposed, possibly with ties:
\[
 C_r=\sup_{|t|\le W+1}\{G(t)-(r+1)t\}.
\]
Together with Lemma~\ref{lem:chain-recovery}, this proves determination
for real input weights.

For integer input, take a rational $t_0=A/B$ in lowest terms, $B>0$,
and query at $t=t_0+1/(BM)$. Unperturbed scores are multiples of $1/B$;
the perturbation changes each pairwise difference by less than $1/B$.
It therefore selects the smallest index among the minimizers at $t_0$.
If that index is $r$, put $a=r+1$. The returned value gives an integer
\[
 z=BM G(t)=BM C_r+a(AM+1),\qquad z\bmod M=a.
\]
Because $1\le a\le n<M$, the residue recovers $r$, and then
$C_r=G(t)-at$ is exact. No minimizer witness or numerical limit is requested.

Queries with $t_0=W+1$ and $-W-1$ recover the two extreme coefficients.
For known endpoints $l<r$ with $r-l\ge2$, query at the negative chord
slope $t_0=(C_l-C_r)/(r-l)$. If the recovered unperturbed score equals
the endpoint score, convexity puts every intermediate coefficient on the
chord; fill them by interpolation. Otherwise the recovered index lies
strictly inside $(l,r)$; recurse on the two resulting intervals.
In the collinear case, the decoded minimizer can lie outside $[l,r]$;
score equality, rather than index membership, is the stopping test.

Each nonterminal query discovers at least one previously unknown interior
coefficient, and the recursive interiors are disjoint. Including the first
two queries, at most $n$ queries suffice. Interpolation, recursion, and
\eqref{eq:chain-gap-recovery} take $O(n)$ further work and space.
Chord denominators satisfy $B\le n-1$, so $BM\le n^2-1$.
Their slopes have magnitude at most $W$; the initial queries have magnitude
$W+1$ before perturbation. All requested points satisfy the stated bounds.
\end{proof}

The oracle's signed domain is part of its specification: endpoint weights
may become negative. Ordinary nonnegative-weight Knuth monotonicity is not
used for those queries. For the legal-domain collision, the absolute
assignment $(u,v)=(0,-1)$ already gives distinct costs 33 and 34.
The complete endpoint-depth profile is therefore sufficient with the stated
metadata, whereas its envelope on legal endpoint weights is not.
A known chain sequence supports \eqref{eq:signed-chain-line} by binary
search in $O(\log(n+1))$ comparisons after linear preprocessing.
These are representation and recovery results; an efficient general
composition rule still has to be supplied. Verification is in
Appendix~\ref{app:signed-boundary}.

\subsubsection{Quadratic explicit envelopes with linear factorizations}\label{sec:legacy-18-2}
For any $n\ge1$, the endpoint paths share only the root. If their depths
are $a,b$, their union has $a+b-1\le n$ keys. At most one least
intercept per depth pair is needed, giving at most $n(n+1)/2$ affine
pieces. The quadratic order can occur already for a single interval.

\begin{theorem}[Explicit-facet obstruction]\label{result:legacy-25}\label{thm:response-facets}
There are strictly positive mixed-weight instances on $n=2m+1$ keys
whose endpoint response has at least $m^2$ affine pieces on the
nonnegative quadrant. Consequently, explicitly listing all such pieces
can require $\Omega(n^2)$ entries.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Set $K=4m$ and $P_i=K8^i$ for $0\le i<m$. First consider these $m$
keys with all gaps one and added left endpoint weight $x$. Write
$S_j=\sum_{i=0}^jP_i$ and $x_j=2S_j+2m$.

An endpoint key and its adjoining outer gap are forced to the root
if their combined weight exceeds the mass of all other outcomes.
Indeed, each rotation of that key toward the root saves one comparison
on both outcomes and adds at most one to each other outcome. The strict
inequality makes every rotation improve cost. At $x_j$, for every $k>j$,
\[
 P_k+1>S_{k-1}+x_j+k+1,
\]
while in the remaining prefix
\[
 P_0+1+x_j>(S_j-P_0)+(j+1).
\]
The geometric series and $K=4m$ imply these inequalities. For example,
$S_{k-1}<P_k/7$ and $x_j\le2S_{k-1}+2m$ for $k>j$, whereas
$P_k\ge32m$. Thus every optimum first uses roots
$m-1,m-2,\ldots,j+1$, then key 0, giving left endpoint depth $m-j$.
All $m$ possible one-sided slopes occur, with strict inequalities
persisting in open neighborhoods of the probes.

Mirror this instance about a central key:
\[
 p=(P_0,\ldots,P_{m-1},R,P_{m-1},\ldots,P_0),\quad q=(1,\ldots,1).
\]
Let $X=x_{m-1}$, $S=S_{m-1}$,
$B=2S+2m+2+2X$, and $R=(2m+2)B$.
For $0\le x,y\le X$, the mass outside the central key is at most $B$.
A center-root tree costs at most $R+nB<2R$; any other root charges
the central key at least $2R$. Thus the center root is forced.
At $(x_j,x_k)$, every optimum has endpoint depths
\[
 (m-j+1,m-k+1).
\]
These are $m^2$ different gradients. Since there are finitely many
trees and all minimizing trees at each probe have the same gradient,
that probe has an open affine neighborhood. Different gradients require
different pieces.
\end{proof}

The weights have $O(n)$ bits each. This is an arithmetic/output-size
obstruction to explicit facet enumeration, not a word-RAM lower bound
with $O(\log n)$-bit weights, nor a lower bound for a single optimum query.
The same example also explains why counting facets cannot rule out
implicit representations.

\begin{lemma}[Exact hinge factorization]\label{result:legacy-26}\label{lem:response-hinges}
For the one-sided family above, let
\[
 C_0=\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}(m-i)P_i+m+\frac{m(m+1)}2.
\]
Its response for every $x\ge0$ is
\[
 H_m(x)=C_0+mx-\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\max\{0,x-(P_i-P_0)\}.
\]
Within the forced-center box the two-sided response equals
\[
 R+2S+2m+2+x+y+H_m(x)+H_m(y).
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
At $x_j$, the remaining right subtree of key 0 is a decreasing-key
chain, by the same strict endpoint condition. Passing from $j$ to
$j+1$ rotates key 0 above key $j+1$. At base weights the intercept
increases by $P_{j+1}-P_0$, while the endpoint slope decreases by one.
At $j=0$ the intercept is $C_0$. Every possible endpoint depth occurs
among these $m$ trees, and optimality at a probe proves its intercept
is minimal for that depth. Their successive intersections are the
increasing thresholds $P_i-P_0$, giving the hinge formula. The center-root
recurrence gives the two-sided identity.
\end{proof}

The factorization extends to the entire quadrant. Set
\begin{align*}
 W&=R+2S+2m+2, & V&=W-P_0-1,\\
 L&=C_0-mP_0-m-1, & J&=R+2(S-P_0)+2m+2L.
\end{align*}
Here $L$ is the fixed cost of the remaining one-sided keys
$1,\ldots,m-1$, and $J$ is the fixed inner cost after both outer keys
and outer gaps are removed.

\begin{theorem}[Full-quadrant factorization]\label{result:legacy-27}\label{thm:response-global}
For the geometric family and every $x,y\ge0$,
\begin{align*}
 \mathcal R(x,y)=W+x+y+\min\{&H_m(x)+H_m(y),\\
 &V+y+L+H_m(y),\ V+y+J,\\
 &V+x+L+H_m(x),\ V+x+J\}.
\end{align*}
This function has exactly $m^2+2m+2$ affine pieces. It admits $O(m)$
arithmetic preprocessing and storage, $O(\log m)$ value queries, and
$O(m)$ complete tree construction per query.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Any root other than keys $0,m,2m$ gives both added endpoint weights
depth at least two and the central key comparison length at least two.
A center-root candidate with the two outer keys as children also puts
both endpoints at depth two, with base cost at most
$R+n(W-R)<2R$. It therefore strictly beats such a root for all $x,y$.
If root 0 is used, its remaining one-sided problem similarly has only
the center or the other outer key as a possible root; symmetrically for
root $2m$. Expanding these five cases gives the formula. The fixed
remaining intervals are geometric chains on either side of a forced
central root, giving the stated $L,J$.

The center-root term supplies $m^2$ slopes, all exposed by the original
probes. For root 0, the same $2R$ argument forces the center in the
right problem throughout the original probe range, exposing all $m$
pieces inherited from $H_m(y)$. As $y$ grows unbounded, one final piece
appears when the outer key becomes its root. Taking $x$
sufficiently large forces root 0, exposing these $m+1$ pieces globally.
Their slopes are $(1,2),\ldots,(1,m+2)$. Reflection gives another
$m+1$ pieces with second slope one. All slopes are distinct. The formula
has no further candidate slopes, proving the count.

Prefix sums of the ordered thresholds let two binary searches evaluate
$H_m(x)$ and $H_m(y)$. Four comparisons select the outer minimum.
The selected case and one-sided chain indices reconstruct
exactly $2m+1$ keys in linear work.
\end{proof}

Thus $2(m-1)$ hinge terms and five alternatives represent a quadratic
number of affine regions. Endpoint-dominance shortcuts already solve this
family. It is not a hard-query example, and the facet theorem is not an
expression-size lower bound. A lossless factorization also does not repair
Theorem~\ref{thm:response-insufficient}: the value function itself omits
information needed by some surrounding contexts.



\paragraph{A favorable bound independent of the added weights}\label{sec:legacy-18-3}
\begin{lemma}[Endpoint mass-ratio bound]\label{result:legacy-28}\label{lem:response-ratio}
Let the original total mass be $W$, with $q_0,q_n>0$. The entire response
for arbitrary $x,y\ge0$ has
\[
 O\!\left(\log(2+W/q_0)\log(2+W/q_n)\right)
\]
affine pieces, also bounded by $O(n^2)$. Polynomial endpoint mass ratios
therefore give $O(\log^2 n)$ pieces.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If an optimum has left endpoint depth $a\ge2$, its last internal
ancestor is at depth $a-2$ in the root's left subtree. This subtree
is optimal and excludes the opposite endpoint and its added weight $y$.
Its mass is at most $W+x$, while the last ancestor's subtree has mass
at least $q_0+x$. Lemma~\ref{lem:mass} gives
\[
 F_{a-1}\le\frac{W+x}{q_0+x}\le\frac{W}{q_0},\qquad F_1=F_2=1.
\]
The symmetric bound is $F_{b-1}\le W/q_n$ for right depth $b\ge2$.
Fibonacci growth bounds the number of possible slopes in each direction;
only a least intercept per pair can be required.
\end{proof}

The added parameters do not occur in this bound. The quadratic example
has exponential original mass ratio, so the two results are consistent.
This lemma bounds output size, not the work to discover the pieces.
Moreover, the composition counterexample has just five pieces: a small
response need not be a sufficient interface.



\subsection{Ruler patterns and a dense-core representation barrier}
\label{sec:legacy-21}

\label{sec:ruler}
The geometric-weight control has a dense core whose entries are constant
along each row. This motivates asking whether another finite difference
could restore sparse support. The answer is negative even for a smooth,
positive, polynomial-weight family that has an exact compact formula.
Before proving that barrier, we isolate the ruler pattern underlying both
the penalty layers and a new sufficient exchange condition. Priority of
these identities and conditions is unestablished.

\subsubsection{Saturated layers as weighted subsequences}\label{sec:legacy-21-1}
Write $c_h=2^h-1$ and let $\nu_2(t)$ be the exponent of two dividing
the positive integer $t$. Use the penalty recurrence above with $M>HW$.
For any interval of length $k=j-i\ge c_h$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ruler-layer}
 A_h(i,j)=M(k-c_h)+hW(i,j)
 -\max_{i\le s_1<\cdots<s_{c_h}<j}
       \sum_{t=1}^{c_h}\nu_2(t)p_{s_t}.
\end{equation}
Indeed, the penalty forces exactly $c_h$ exposed keys: losing one exposure
costs more than any possible reduction in the ordinary weighted cost.
Every position in the first $h$ levels is occupied. The exposed skeleton is
