Otherwise it is $(Q+1)2^{d+1}+2^d$. In that case
$\operatorname{pc}(Q+1)\le\operatorname{pc}(Q)+1$ and the old remainder
already had its $d$th bit set, giving the same bound. Starting at zero,
the earliest ending position after $k$ replacements has at most $k$ one bits.

If an optimal prefix ended at $b$ with $\operatorname{pc}(b)>k$, this
replacement would end strictly earlier in the same valuation class with
the same reward. Lemma~\ref{lem:slot-records}'s monotonicity then gives
equal prefix values, so $b$ cannot be a record. Count all positive $h$-bit
integers with at most $k$ one bits and remove the $k-1$ positions below
$k$; each of those also satisfies the popcount condition. This gives
\eqref{eq:slot-record-envelope}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Lexicographic ruler endpoints]\label{lem:lex-ruler-endpoints}
Let $m\ge1$, and suppose positive weights satisfy
$w_i>(h-1)\sum_{j>i}w_j$. For $m\le U<2^h$, let
\[
 G_m(U)=\max_{1\le t_1<\cdots<t_m\le U}
                  \sum_{i=1}^m w_{i-1}\nu_2(t_i).
\]
The maximizing tuple is unique. Its last slot is $U$ exactly when
$\operatorname{pc}(U)\le m$. Consequently, for $U>m$,
\[
 G_m(U)>G_m(U-1)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \operatorname{pc}(U)\le m.
\]
The first feasible window $U=m$ is an initial record.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
All valuations lie in $\{0,\ldots,h-1\}$. The first differing coordinate
therefore determines the weighted comparison, so optimization is
lexicographic in key order. With $r$ slots still required after the previous
slot $a$, choose the largest valuation in $[a+1,U-r+1]$. That maximum is
unique: two positions of equal maximal valuation would enclose a position
of larger valuation. Repeating this feasible greedy choice gives the unique
maximizing tuple.

Write $U=P+s$, where $P$ is its largest power of two and $0\le s<P$.
If $m\le s+1$, the first greedy choice is $P$. For $m=1$, it ends at $U$
exactly when $s=0$, equivalently $\operatorname{pc}(U)=1$.
For $m>1$, the suffix is the same problem on $[1,s]$ with $m-1$ slots,
since $\nu_2(P+x)=\nu_2(x)$ for $1\le x\le s$. Induction and
$\operatorname{pc}(U)=1+\operatorname{pc}(s)$ prove the claim.
If $m>s+1$, every choice while $r>s+1$ lies below $P$, because its
feasibility upper endpoint $U-r+1$ is below $P$. When $r=s+1$, the slot
$P$ becomes feasible and is selected. All following slots through $U$
are then required. Here $\operatorname{pc}(U)\le s+1<m$, proving the
claim in this case as well.

If the unique optimum ends at $U$, no equally good tuple was feasible
at $U-1$, so the value rises strictly. Otherwise the same optimum was
already feasible there. The initial window contains only one tuple.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact exponential record support]\label{thm:slot-record-barrier}
For $n\ge2$, put $A=n+1$ and set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:slot-record-barrier-family}
 p_i=A^{n-i}-1\quad(0\le i<n),\qquad q_j=1\quad(0\le j\le n).
\end{equation}
All weights are positive integers. The unique optimal tree is the right
chain, so its necessary height is $h=n$. At this height, the records of
its normalized $k$-key prefix are exactly
\begin{equation}\label{eq:slot-record-binary-support}
 \{b:k\le b\le2^n-1,\ \operatorname{pc}(b)\le k\}.
\end{equation}
Their exact count and total across all prefixes are
\begin{equation}\label{eq:slot-record-barrier-count}
 B_k=\sum_{r=0}^k\binom nr-k,\qquad
 B_n=2^n-n,\qquad
 \sum_{k=1}^n B_k=(n+2)2^{n-1}-1-\frac{n(n+1)}2.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Common-gap normalization gives $w_i=A^{n-i}$, zero residual gaps, and
the additive cost $n$ for every tree. For every suffix,
\[
 n\sum_{j>i}w_j=w_i-A<w_i.
\]
A right chain on that suffix costs less than $2w_i$. Any tree with its
first key below the root costs at least $2w_i$ from that key alone.
The first key must therefore be the root; induction forces the unique
right chain. Its original cost is $n+\sum_i(i+1)w_i$, and its height is $n$.

The same weights satisfy the strict dominance assumption of
Lemma~\ref{lem:lex-ruler-endpoints}. For $m=k-1$, a prefix forced to end
at $b$ has negative reward
\[
 f_k(b)=-G_m(b-1)-w_m\nu_2(b),
\]
where $G_0$ is constantly zero. Fix valuation $d$ and write
$b=(2j+1)2^d$. Between consecutive feasible slots of this valuation,
the argument of $G_m$ passes through
\[
 [b-2^{d+1},\ b-1].
\]
The minimum popcount in this interval is $\operatorname{pc}(j)$.
Indeed, its right half contains $j2^{d+1}$ and has that minimum; its
left half has minimum $\operatorname{pc}(j-1)+1$, which is at least
$\operatorname{pc}(j)$. Thus $f_k$ decreases strictly at $b$ exactly
when $\operatorname{pc}(j)\le m$. As
$\operatorname{pc}(b)=\operatorname{pc}(j)+1$, this is precisely
$\operatorname{pc}(b)\le k$.

The first feasible slot of each class obeys the same test. Its index is
$j=\max\{0,\lceil(k-2^d)/2^{d+1}\rceil\}\le k-1$, so
$\operatorname{pc}(b)=1+\operatorname{pc}(j)\le k$.
For $k=1$, only the first slot of each valuation class is retained,
consistent with $G_0=0$. This proves \eqref{eq:slot-record-binary-support}.
There are $\sum_{r=0}^k\binom nr-1$ positive $n$-bit integers with at most
$k$ one bits. Every positive integer below $k$ satisfies that test;
removing those $k-1$ positions proves $B_k$. Summing the binomial
coefficients over $k$ gives the last identity in
\eqref{eq:slot-record-barrier-count}.
\end{proof}

The support calculation requires only lexicographic dominance. With any
prescribed height $h$, base $A=h+1$, and at most $2^h-1$ keys, the same
argument attains $E_h(k)$ at every prefix. Thus
Lemma~\ref{lem:slot-record-envelope} is sharp.

The largest input integer has $O(n\log(n+1))$ bits. Thus a polynomial-size
input forces exponential explicit prefix lists, even at the minimum optimal
height. Retaining every stage's predecessors requires $\Theta(n2^n)$
records on this family. Its support nevertheless has the succinct binary
description \eqref{eq:slot-record-binary-support}. Moreover, the original
mixed weights satisfy period-one affine translation with
$\lambda=1/A$ and $\alpha=1/A-1$. Theorem~\ref{thm:affine-period} therefore
constructs their full optimum using exactly $2n-1$ root candidates.
This is a barrier to enumerating prefix records; the general OBST problem
and implicit representations remain separate. The integer-mass and
fixed-exception guarantees of Section~\ref{sec:slot-records} are unaffected.
Independent controls appear in Appendix~\ref{app:rank-transfer}.

\subsection{Monotone functions can require exponential diagram minima}
\label{sec:dag-minimum-barrier}
The prefix operation of Lemma~\ref{lem:dag-prefix} has a bound in the
input diagram size. General pointwise minimum has no such polynomial
bound, even for strictly decreasing inputs in the same address order.

\begin{theorem}[Exponential minimum of two monotone diagrams]
\label{thm:dag-minimum-barrier}
Let $m\ge1$, $N=2^m$, and write a $2m$-bit address as $b=NX+Y$,
where $0\le X,Y<N$. The two strictly decreasing functions
\[
 F(b)=-2b+X,\qquad G(b)=-2b+Y
\]
each have an additive decision diagram with $2m+1$ nodes, including
its terminal, in most-significant-bit-first address order. Their
pointwise minimum has exactly $3\cdot2^m-2$ nodes in that same order,
including $2^m$ distinct normalized nodes at level $m$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Both functions are weighted sums of individual address bits. A single
shared node per bit, with the corresponding coefficient on one edge,
represents each function; the terminal supplies the extra node. Increasing
$b$ by one changes $F$ by $-2$ or $-1$. It changes $G$ by $-1$ within
a low-bit block and by $-(N+1)$ across its boundary. Thus both are
strictly decreasing.

Their minimum is
\[
 H(b)=-2b+\min(X,Y).
\]
Fixing the high $m$ bits to $X=x$ gives a low-bit residual function
$-2Y+\min(x,Y)$, up to the constant $-2Nx$. Its minimum is at $Y=N-1$.
After subtracting that minimum the normalized residual is
\[
 H_x(Y)=2(N-1)-2Y+\min(x,Y)-x.
\]
For $0\le Y<N-1$ its consecutive difference is $-1$ when $Y<x$
and $-2$ when $Y\ge x$. These $N$ functions are pairwise distinct,
including $x=0$ and $x=N-1$. No two can share the same normalized node.
Every high-bit assignment must therefore reach a different level-$m$
node.

For the exact total, consider a remaining low-bit level $d\le m$.
After fixing the preceding bits, the residual has the form
$-2t+\min(x-z,t)$ up to a constant, where $z$ is the fixed low-bit
block's starting address. Normalization depends only on the clipped
threshold $\max\{0,\min\{x-z,2^d-1\}\}$. There are exactly $2^d$
distinct thresholds, and all occur by taking $z=0$ and $x$ in that range.
Thus levels $1,\ldots,m$ contain $2^{m+1}-2$ nodes in total.
At level $m+r$, $1\le r\le m$, there are $2^{m-r}$ high-bit prefixes.
They remain distinct up to an additive constant: setting the remaining
$r$ bits of $X$ to zero gives different thresholds $X=a2^r$ in the
low-bit function. Hence those upper levels contain $2^m-1$ nodes.
Adding the single terminal gives $3\cdot2^m-2$.
\end{proof}

The theorem concerns the fixed address order used by the prefix algorithm.
It shows why a polynomial bound for a generic minimum operation cannot
follow from small operand diagrams and monotonicity alone. The displayed
functions have not been realized as mixed-OBST prefix transitions, so this
is a barrier to that generic complexity argument. Bounding the specific
functions generated by \eqref{eq:dag-mixed-transition} remains a separate
structural question. Independent exact checks appear in
Appendix~\ref{app:rank-transfer}.

\subsection{Sharp reset-path work on general diagram inputs}
\label{sec:dag-reset-sharp}
The reset-path lemma bounds the mechanical recursion even outside the
OBST domain. The following family makes both its state bound and the
persistent-replacement work sharp. Its failure of endpoint dominance is
explicit, so the result does not assert tightness on actual OBST prefixes.

\begin{theorem}[A compact input with sharp carry and replacement counts]
\label{thm:dag-reset-sharp}
Let $m\ge1$, $N=2^m$, $h=2m$, and
$A(X)=2X-\operatorname{popcount}(X)$. On $0\le X,Y<N$ define
\[
 F(NX+Y)=-\frac{A(X)}{m+1}-Y.
\]
Its additive diagram has exactly $D=2m+1$ nodes, including the terminal.
Run the mechanical recursion in Theorem~\ref{thm:dag-forward}, initialized
with carry $F(0)$, using key and gap parameters $w=g=1$. The number of
expanded states is
\[
 Z=\frac{3m(m+1)}2=\Theta(hD).
\]
With the stated persistent first-address replacement and shared templates,
the number of node-assembly calls, and separately the number of replacement
visits including leaves, is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dag-reset-sharp-work}
 M=\frac{m(7m^2+15m+2)}6=\Theta(h^2D).
\end{equation}
This input cannot be a mixed OBST prefix.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The identity
$A(X)=\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}(2^{j+1}-1)x_j$
shows that $F$ is a weighted sum of individual bits. Every residual of
the same length differs only by an additive constant, so there is exactly
one normalized node at each level and one terminal.
For $X>0$, setting $s=\nu_2(X)$ gives
\[
 A(X)-A(X-1)=s+1,\qquad
 \delta_s=\frac{s+1}{m+1}\in(0,1).
\]
Within each $X$ block the values decrease by one, and its last value is
$\delta_s$ below the preceding block's last value. Thus the incoming
inclusive carry at the start of block $X>0$ is that preceding minimum.
Relative to the current block minimum, it is exactly $\delta_s$.
Every position before $Y=N-1$ has larger value than that carry. Only the
rightmost path expands inside this block, with the same relative carry
$\delta_s$ at each remaining low-bit depth $d=1,\ldots,m$.
Each $s=0,\ldots,m-1$ occurs, by taking $X=2^s$.

In the first block $X=0$, every depth-$d$ subblock starts with its own
first value as inclusive carry, giving relative carry $2^d-1$.
This is distinct from every $\delta_s<1$. All low-bit contexts have
therefore been enumerated: at each depth $1\le d\le m$ there are exactly
$m+1$ states.

At an upper depth $m+r$, $1\le r\le m$, a block starts at an $X$ divisible
by $2^r$. For $X>0$, its valuation $s$ ranges over $r,\ldots,m-1$.
The block minimum is at its last address, and its relative carry is
\[
 \frac{A(2^r-1)+s+1}{m+1},\qquad
 A(2^r-1)=2^{r+1}-r-2.
\]
These $m-r$ values are distinct and all occur. The block starting at zero
has relative carry $A(2^r-1)/(m+1)+N-1$, a further distinct value.
All these carries are positive, so every such context expands. There are
exactly $m-r+1$ contexts at depth $m+r$. Summing the low and upper levels
gives
\[
 Z=m(m+1)+\sum_{r=1}^m(m-r+1)=\frac{3m(m+1)}2.
\]
The expanded-state depth sum is
\[
 S=(m+1)\sum_{d=1}^m d+
       \sum_{r=1}^m(m+r)(m-r+1)
   =\frac{m(m+1)(7m+5)}6.
\]
Every upper block expands. Inside each later $X$ block the left child
of each rightmost-path state is pruned, with largest depth $m-1$.
Thus templates of precisely depths $1,\ldots,m-1$ are assembled once;
for $m=1$ this set is empty. Their assembly work and replacement visits
both sum to $m(m-1)/2$. Adding this to $S$ proves
\eqref{eq:dag-reset-sharp-work}, counting all assembly calls even when
interning reuses an existing node.

Finally,
\[
 F(N)=-\frac1{m+1}>-(N-1)=F(N-1),
 \qquad \nu_2(N)=m>\nu_2(N-1)=0.
\]
This contradicts the endpoint-dominance condition of
Lemma~\ref{lem:dag-skyline}. Hence no actual mixed prefix has this function.
The construction proves sharpness of the general recursion's accounting;
it supplies no lower bound for the full OBST problem.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Sharp punctured-diagram update count]
\label{cor:punctured-sharp}
Puncturing the input in Theorem~\ref{thm:dag-reset-sharp} gives exactly
$D=2m$ nodes at height $h=2m$. The recursion of
Theorem~\ref{thm:punctured-construction} expands
$Z=3m(m+1)/2$ states and makes exactly
\[
 Z+m-1=\frac{3m(m+1)}2+m-1=\Theta(hD)
\]
node-assembly calls. Thus its general-diagram work bound is sharp.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
At each positive depth the input remains a weighted sum of residual bits,
restricted to nonzero addresses, so there is exactly one normalized node
per level and no terminal. Every nontrivial input block has its minimum
at its last address; omitting its first address preserves that minimum.
The omitted value is at least the inclusive carry, so removing it from
the right-carry formula changes no carry. Consequently the expanded
relative carries and every level count are precisely those computed in
Theorem~\ref{thm:dag-reset-sharp}. The same depths $1,\ldots,m-1$ need
pruning templates, now costing one assembly call each. Every expanded
state also costs one assembly call, proving the formula. The earlier
endpoint-dominance violation still excludes this family from OBST prefixes.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Cubic actual-prefix diagrams and quartic shared history}
\label{sec:punctured-size-barrier}
The generic operation family above violates endpoint dominance. The next
family consists of actual mixed OBST prefixes and gives a size obstruction
for the canonical representation itself. Its optimum has necessary height
$n$, and all original outcome weights are strictly positive.

\begin{theorem}[Actual-prefix diagram size]\label{thm:punctured-size-barrier}
For $n\ge2$, put $A=4n+1$, $w_i=A^{n-i}$, and
\[
 p_i=w_i-1\quad(0\le i<n),\qquad q_j=1\quad(0\le j\le n).
\]
At the necessary optimal height $h=n$, let $D_k$ count the canonical
punctured nodes of the $k$-key prefix, and let $\mathcal H$ count distinct
nodes in the union of all prefix diagrams, allowing full sharing across
stages. Define
\[
 Q=2\lceil n/2\rceil,\qquad
 d_0=\lceil\log_2(Q+1)\rceil+2,\qquad N=\max\{0,n-d_0\}.
\]
Then
\begin{align}
 D_k&\ge L_k:=\sum_{t=1}^{N}(N-t+1)\min\{t,(k-3)_+\},
       \label{eq:punctured-stage-lower}\\
 D_n&\ge\binom{N+2}{3}=\Omega(n^3),
       \label{eq:punctured-final-lower}\\
 \mathcal H&\ge(n-2)\binom{N+2}{3}-\binom{N+3}{4}
       =\Omega(n^4).\label{eq:punctured-history-lower}
\end{align}
The unique optimum is the right chain. Its cost is
$n+\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(i+1)w_i$, and the complete tree has a linear-work
construction and certificate.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Common-gap normalization gives $w$, zero residual gaps, and additive cost
$n$. The inequality $n\sum_{j>i}w_j<w_i$ forces the first key to be the
root in every suffix, by the dominance argument in
Theorem~\ref{thm:slot-record-barrier}. This proves the chain, cost, and
necessary height assertions. It also gives lexicographic optimization of
slot valuations, as in Lemma~\ref{lem:lex-ruler-endpoints}.

We first record why scalar differences preserve valuation information.
Two differences of valuation vectors have coordinates in $[-(n-1),n-1]$.
If they disagree first at coordinate $i$, their weighted difference has
leading magnitude at least $w_i$, whereas its remaining magnitude is at most
\[
 2(n-1)\sum_{j>i}w_j
 <\frac{2(n-1)}{A-1}w_i<w_i.
\]
Thus equality of their exact scalar scores implies equality of every
coefficient. Padding shorter vectors with zeros preserves this statement.

For each quadruple $(d,s,r,k)$ with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:punctured-witness-range}
 d_0\le d\le s<n,\qquad 1\le r\le\min\{n-s,k-3\},
\end{equation}
choose a block boundary $B$ with exactly $r$ one bits, its lowest at
position $s$. One choice uses the top $r-1$ bit positions and bit $s$.
This is a multiple of $2^d$, and its whole punctured block
$B+t$, $1\le t<2^d$, lies within the height-$n$ slots. Use three local
addresses
\begin{equation}\label{eq:punctured-three-addresses}
 t_*=1,\qquad t_-=2^{d-2}+Q,\qquad t_+=2^{d-1}+Q.
\end{equation}
Since $2^{d-2}>Q\ge n$, all are interior and all corresponding prefix
entries are finite. Let $v(t)$ be the valuation vector of the unique
optimal $k$-slot sequence forced to end at $B+t$. Its first $k-1$ slots
are obtained by successive feasible greedy choices, and
$f_k(B+t)=-\sum_iw_iv_i(t)$.

At $t_-$ and $t_+$, the first $r$ choices are the successive partial sums
of the high bits of $B$. Each is the unique highest-valuation feasible
position. Feasibility holds because at least $n$ positions remain after
$B$, and still more remain after each earlier partial sum. There remain
$m=k-1-r\ge2$ predecessor slots after $B$. Their first feasible upper
endpoint, relative to $B$, is $t-m$. As
$0<Q-m<2^{d-2}$, their next greedy choice has valuation $d-2$ at $t_-$
and $d-1$ at $t_+$. Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{eq:punctured-identify-r}
 \min\{i:v_i(t_+)\ne v_i(t_-)\}=r,
 \qquad v_r(t_+)-v_r(t_-)=1.
\end{equation}

At $t_*=1$, the first $r-1$ partial sums remain feasible and are still
greedy maxima. Put $P=B-2^s$. There are $j=k-r\ge3$ predecessor slots
left in $(P,B]$. The first two greedy choices are
\[
 P+2^{s-1},\qquad P+3\cdot2^{s-2},
\]
with valuations $s-1$ and $s-2$. For the first choice $B$ is infeasible
because $j>1$, while the midpoint is feasible because $2^{s-1}\ge j-1$.
For the second, $B$ is still infeasible because $j>2$, while the quarter
point is feasible because $2^{s-2}\ge j-2$. These margins follow from
$2^{s-2}\ge2^{d-2}>Q\ge n$. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:punctured-identify-s}
 v_r(t_*)-v_r(t_-)=(s-2)-(d-2)=s-d.
\end{equation}
The forced ending key has valuation zero at $t_*$ and
$\nu_2(Q)\ge1$ at $t_-$. The latter follows from
$0<Q<2^{d-2}$. After zero padding, this gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:punctured-identify-k}
 \max\{i:v_i(t_*)\ne v_i(t_-)\}=k-1.
\end{equation}

If two same-depth blocks shared a normalized punctured node, their scalar
value differences at the three addresses would agree. Coefficient
uniqueness would then give the same vector differences.
Equation~\eqref{eq:punctured-identify-k} recovers $k$,
\eqref{eq:punctured-identify-r} recovers $r$, and
\eqref{eq:punctured-identify-s} recovers $s$. Thus every quadruple in
\eqref{eq:punctured-witness-range} gives a different canonical node,
even across prefix stages. Different depths are distinct by the definition
of the representation.

For fixed $s$, write $t=n-s$. There are $N-t+1$ eligible depths and
$\min\{t,(k-3)_+\}$ choices of $r$, proving
\eqref{eq:punctured-stage-lower}. At $k=n$, summing gives
$\binom{N+2}{3}$. For each $r$, eligible stages are $k=r+3,\ldots,n$,
numbering $n-r-2$. Counting their distinct shared nodes gives
\[
 \sum_{t=1}^{N}(N-t+1)\sum_{r=1}^{t}(n-r-2)
 =(n-2)\binom{N+2}{3}-\binom{N+3}{4}.
\]
Finally $d_0=O(\log n)$, so the two formulas have leading terms
$n^3/6$ and $n^4/8$, respectively. This proves the lower bounds.
\end{proof}

Theorem~\ref{thm:punctured-construction}'s full work bound remains valid,
but universally small prefix diagrams or shared history cannot make this
representation subquadratic. The witnesses give lower bounds, not exact
node counts or matching upper bounds. The final-stage obstruction survives
discarding earlier roots; the history obstruction already accounts for
cross-stage sharing. The family's directly certified chain solution
separates this representation barrier from the complexity of full OBST.

\subsection{Positive affine normalization does not remove the size barrier}
\label{sec:affine-diagram-barrier}
A more permissive representation could share blocks that differ by a
positive scale as well as an additive shift. The next result gives the
same size obstruction under that equivalence, using finite integer weights
with polynomial binary encoding.

\begin{lemma}[Separating quadratic scores]\label{lem:quadratic-score-separation}
Let $n\ge2$, $A=16n^2+1$, $e_t=t+2nt^2$ for $1\le t\le n$, and
$w_i=A^{e_{n-i}}$ for $0\le i<n$. For integer vectors $u,v,u',v'$ whose
coordinates have magnitude at most $n-1$, equality
\[
 (u\cdot w)(v'\cdot w)=(u'\cdot w)(v\cdot w)
\]
implies the polynomial identity
$(u\cdot X)(v'\cdot X)=(u'\cdot X)(v\cdot X)$.
If $u,v$ are linearly independent and $u'\ne0$, this forces
$u'=\lambda u$ and $v'=\lambda v$ for some nonzero rational $\lambda$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
All unordered sums $e_a+e_b$ are different. Equality with $e_c+e_d$
implies
\[
 a+b-c-d=-2n(a^2+b^2-c^2-d^2).
\]
The left side has absolute value at most $2n-2$, so both sides vanish.
Equality of sums and sums of squares gives $ab=cd$, hence equality of
the unordered pairs.

Every coefficient in the difference of the two quadratic polynomials
has magnitude at most $4(n-1)^2$. Substitution of the $w_i$ sends distinct
monomials to distinct powers of $A$. If the substituted polynomial were
nonzero, its highest nonzero term would dominate all lower terms, since
$4(n-1)^2<A-1$. Thus scalar equality implies polynomial identity.
For the last assertion, the independent linear forms $U=u\cdot X$ and
$V=v\cdot X$ are coprime over the rationals. From $UV'=U'V$, $U$ divides
$U'$. Both are nonzero linear forms, so $U'=\lambda U$; cancellation
gives $V'=\lambda V$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Size under affine block equivalence]
\label{thm:affine-diagram-barrier}
Use the weights of Lemma~\ref{lem:quadratic-score-separation}, and put
$p_i=w_i-1$ and $q_j=1$. Let $D_k^{\mathrm{aff}}$ count equivalence classes
of nonnull, depth-tagged dyadic block interiors in the $k$-key prefix,
where functions may share whenever $f=\alpha g+\beta$ for $\alpha>0$.
Let $\mathcal H^{\mathrm{aff}}$ count their union across all prefixes,
allowing the same equivalence across stages. With $N,Q,d_0,L_k$ as in
Theorem~\ref{thm:punctured-size-barrier},
\begin{align}
 D_k^{\mathrm{aff}}&\ge L_k,\qquad
 D_n^{\mathrm{aff}}\ge\binom{N+2}{3}=\Omega(n^3),
       \label{eq:affine-diagram-final}\\
 \mathcal H^{\mathrm{aff}}&\ge
 (n-2)\binom{N+2}{3}-\binom{N+3}{4}=\Omega(n^4).
       \label{eq:affine-diagram-history}
\end{align}
All original weights are positive. The unique optimum is the right chain
and its necessary height is $n$. The largest input weight has
$O(n^3\log(n+1))$ bits, and the complete input has
$O(n^4\log(n+1))$ bits.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Consecutive normalized weights differ by a factor at least $A$.
Thus $n\sum_{j>i}w_j<w_i$, proving the chain and necessary height by
suffix dominance. The same inequality gives lexicographic optimization
of valuations. The exponent and bit-length bounds follow directly from
$e_n=2n^3+n$ and $\log A=O(\log(n+1))$.

Use the witnesses $(d,s,r,k)$ and three interior addresses from
\eqref{eq:punctured-witness-range}--\eqref{eq:punctured-three-addresses}.
All their feasibility and valuation identities depend only on lexicographic
dominance, so they hold for these weights. Zero-pad the valuation vectors
to $n$ coordinates and write
\[
 u=v(t_+)-v(t_-),\qquad z=v(t_*)-v(t_-).
\]
The first nonzero coordinate of $u$ is $r$ and equals one.
Also $u_{k-1}=0$, whereas the last nonzero coordinate of $z$ is $k-1$
and equals $-\nu_2(Q)\ne0$. Thus $u,z$ are linearly independent.
Their coordinates have magnitude at most $n-1$, and $z_r=s-d$.

For the actual block function $f$, define its three-entry invariant
\begin{equation}\label{eq:affine-three-entry-invariant}
 R(f)=\frac{f(t_*)-f(t_-)}{f(t_+)-f(t_-)}
     =\frac{z\cdot w}{u\cdot w}.
\end{equation}
The denominator is nonzero by dominance. This ratio is unchanged by every
positive affine transformation. If two same-depth witnesses have equal
ratios, cross multiplication and
Lemma~\ref{lem:quadratic-score-separation} give
$u'=\lambda u$ and $z'=\lambda z$. The first nonzero coefficient of each
$u$ is one, forcing equal $r$ and $\lambda=1$. The last nonzero coordinate
of $z$ then recovers $k$, and its $r$th coordinate recovers $s$.
Consequently every witness remains distinct under affine equivalence,
including across stages. The counts in
\eqref{eq:affine-diagram-final}--\eqref{eq:affine-diagram-history}
are the same counts as before.
\end{proof}

The result concerns the coarsest exact positive affine equivalence, without
assuming an implementation or charging for canonicalization. It also holds
for blocks that include their first address: whole-block equivalence would
imply equality of the three strictly interior invariants. Thus adding a
scalar multiplier to normalized references cannot supply a universally
small diagram. The same family still has a linear arithmetic-work chain
construction and certificate; bit-operation costs are additional.
A useful replacement must share more than affine copies of fixed dyadic
blocks, avoid these prefix functions, or exploit additional input structure.

\subsection{Lazy prefix evaluation with a rearrangement bound}
\label{sec:lazy-prefix-barrier}
One can avoid materializing a prefix function by evaluating only the
predecessor states requested by its skyline recurrence. The following
relaxation supplies exact lower bounds for that search. Its failure theorem
identifies an ordering constraint that the relaxation loses.

For normalized nonnegative weights $w_0,\ldots,w_{n-1}$ and residual gaps
$g_0,\ldots,g_n$, with $g_0=g_n=0$, recall that the reward of slots
$s_0<\cdots<s_{k-1}=b$ is
\[
 R=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}w_i v(s_i)
   +\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}g_i\min\{v(s_{i-1}),v(s_i)\},
 \qquad v(s)=\nu_2(s).
\]
Its maximum is $-f_k(b)$; feasibility means $k\le b<2^h$.
Put $a_i=2w_i+g_i+g_{i+1}$, and let $T_t(r)$ be the sum of the $t$
largest values among $a_0,\ldots,a_{r-1}$, with $T_0(r)=0$.

\begin{lemma}[Mixed-prefix rearrangement bound]\label{lem:lazy-rearrangement}
For every feasible nonempty prefix,
\begin{align}
 U_2(k,b)&=(2w_{k-1}+g_{k-1})v(b)
 +\sum_{d=1}^{h-1}T_{\min\{k-1,\lfloor(b-1)/2^d\rfloor\}}(k-1),
       \label{eq:lazy-rearrangement}\\
 2f_k(b)&\ge -U_2(k,b).\nonumber
\end{align}
After $O(n\log(n+1))$ preprocessing and space, each bound can be
computed in $O(h\log(n+1))$ arithmetic and dictionary operations.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Use $2\min(x,y)\le x+y$ to distribute each residual gap to its two
endpoints. This gives
\[
 2R\le\sum_{i=0}^{k-2}a_i v(s_i)
             +(2w_{k-1}+g_{k-1})v(b).
\]
The final coefficient excludes $g_k$, which is absent from this prefix.
For the other $k-1$ keys, drop the ordering constraint and choose slots
from $[1,b-1]$. Nonnegative coefficients permit choosing the largest
available valuations. An exchange of an inverted pair changes the dot
product by $(a-a')(v-v')\ge0$, so pairing coefficients and valuations
in the same decreasing order is optimal for this relaxation.
Exactly $\lfloor(b-1)/2^d\rfloor$ available slots have valuation at
least $d$. Summing these threshold contributions gives
\eqref{eq:lazy-rearrangement}, including the case $k=1$.

Sort and compress the coefficient values. Maintain a persistent binary
count-and-sum tree after each prefix insertion. This uses
$O(n\log(n+1))$ nodes and work. Descending through the larger-value child
first computes any $T_t(r)$ in $O(\log(n+1))$ work, taking an entire
child's sum when its count fits the remaining quota. There are at most
$h-1$ such queries per bound. Rational coefficients require no rounding;
comparisons with a cost incumbent are made after doubling that incumbent.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{The specified search.}
For $k\ge2$, Lemma~\ref{lem:dag-skyline} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lazy-skyline-recursion}
 f_k(b)=\min_{\substack{a\in S(b)\\a\ge k-1}}
       \{f_{k-1}(a)+c_k(a,b)\},\qquad
 c_k(a,b)=-w_{k-1}v(b)-g_{k-1}\min\{v(a),v(b)\}.
\end{equation}
The base is $f_1(b)=-w_0v(b)$. Add a sentinel state $(n+1,2^h)$,
with zero key and gap reward, whose value is the minimum of $f_n$.
At an expanded state, form its at most $h$ feasible skyline candidates
and their lower bounds $-U_2(k-1,a)/2+c_k(a,b)$. Process candidates
in increasing lower-bound order, memoizing exact states. A candidate
is discarded when its lower bound is at least the current feasible
incumbent. Once that comparison holds, all remaining candidates may
be discarded together. Store an attaining predecessor with each exact
value. An explicit stack suffices because every call decreases $k$.
Traceback and sorting-free ancestor repair produce the original-weight tree.

If $V$ states are expanded, including the sentinel, and $Q$ distinct
bounds are computed, then $Q\le Vh$ and complete work is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lazy-work}
 O\bigl(n\log(n+1)+Qh\log(n+1)+Vh\log(h+1)+nh\bigr).
\end{equation}
Candidate enumeration costs $O(Vh)$, candidate sorting costs
$O(Vh\log(h+1))$, and each distinct bound has the cost of
Lemma~\ref{lem:lazy-rearrangement}. Storage is
$O(n\log(n+1)+Q+V+nh)$, including the stack's at most $n$ candidate
lists and full repair. These bounds count exact arithmetic and dictionary
operations; operand bit costs are additional.

For unrestricted construction, choose a certified sufficient height
$h\le n$. A fixed $8n^2$ budget on the counted search work, followed
by ordinary Knuth if exhausted, bounds full work by $O(n^2)$. This
includes unsuccessful search, height selection, normalization, and full
tree reconstruction. The next theorem shows why the unbudgeted search
itself has no polynomial guarantee from this bound.

\begin{theorem}[Exponential obstruction to rearrangement pruning]
\label{thm:lazy-exponential}
For $n\ge6$, let $p_i=(n+1)^{n-i}-1$ and $q_j=1$, and use their
necessary optimal height $h=n$. The completed unbudgeted search above expands at least
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lazy-forced-count}
 \sum_{r=1}^{n-3}\binom{n-2}{r}=2^{n-2}-2=\Omega(2^n)
\end{equation}
distinct actual-prefix states. The same lower bound holds with any
candidate processing order, if pruning uses only the stated bound and
a feasible incumbent for the current state. Memoization does not remove
these states. The original input is positive and has
$O(n^2\log(n+1))$ bits in total.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Common-gap normalization gives $w_i=(n+1)^{n-i}$, zero residual gaps,
and cost shift $n$. The unique optimum is the right chain by
Theorem~\ref{thm:slot-record-barrier}, so $h=n$ is necessary.
The largest weight has $O(n\log(n+1))$ bits. For every index $r$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lazy-lex-dominance}
 w_r>(n-1)\sum_{i>r}w_i.
\end{equation}
Consequently weighted reward strictly respects lexicographic order of
valuation vectors with coordinates in $[0,n-1]$.

First consider a candidate child $f_m(a)$ satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lazy-unprunable-region}
 m\ge4,\qquad a>3\cdot2^{n-2}.
\end{equation}
The first three keys are free in its relaxation. The three highest
valuations among slots before $a$ are $n-1,n-2,n-2$, at positions
$2^{n-1},2^{n-2},3\cdot2^{n-2}$. Since the weights strictly decrease,
these valuations are assigned in that order to $w_0,w_1,w_2$.

No increasing slot sequence has this initial valuation triple. A first
valuation $n-1$ forces slot $2^{n-1}$. A following valuation $n-2$
then forces slot $3\cdot2^{n-2}$, after which no valuation-$n-2$ slot
remains. Thus every actual prefix valuation vector is lexicographically
smaller within its first three coordinates. At the first difference $r$,
its loss of at least $w_r$ exceeds all possible gains at later coordinates
by \eqref{eq:lazy-lex-dominance}.

This argument compares the candidate's relaxed bound with the optimum
of its \emph{parent}, as required for pruning. For an ordinary parent,
append its forced final key to the relaxed reward vector; this is exactly
adding the transition reward, because all residual gaps vanish. All
coordinates after the first difference, including the appended coordinate,
still lie in $[0,n-1]$. The relaxed reward is therefore strictly greater
than the reward of every feasible parent prefix. For the root sentinel,
no coordinate is appended and the same comparison applies to all $n$ keys.
Equivalently, the candidate lower bound plus its transition cost is
strictly below the true parent optimum. Every feasible incumbent is at
least that optimum, so this candidate can never be pruned by the stated
rule, independently of processing order.

Now choose any nonempty proper subset $B\subsetneq\{0,\ldots,n-3\}$.
Start at $(k,b)=(n+1,2^n)$ and process its elements $d$ in descending
order, following
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lazy-subset-path}
 (k,b)\longmapsto(k-1,b-2^d).
\end{equation}
Previously subtracted powers have larger exponents, so $b$ is divisible
by $2^{d+1}$. Hence $b-2^d$ is precisely the leading-bit prefix of
$b-1$ ending at bit $d$: it is a genuine skyline predecessor.
The sum $x$ of all subtracted powers is less than $2^{n-2}$, so every
child endpoint $2^n-x$ exceeds $3\cdot2^{n-2}$. Its key count is at
least four, since $|B|\le n-3$, and no greater than its endpoint, proving feasibility
and \eqref{eq:lazy-unprunable-region}. Every edge must therefore be
processed unless its child has already been evaluated and cached.
Induction down these paths forces evaluation of all their endpoints
\[
 \left(n-|B|+1,\ 2^n-\sum_{d\in B}2^d\right).
\]
Distinct subsets give distinct states, all with at least four keys, so
each is expanded at least once. Counting all nonempty proper subsets gives
\eqref{eq:lazy-forced-count}.
\end{proof}

The family retains its linear arithmetic-work chain constructor and
certificate. The obstruction concerns this particular recurrence with
this particular relaxation. It pinpoints the information needed by a
stronger pruning bound: the order in which high valuations can be attained.
Exact finite controls and complete-construction operation counts appear
in Appendix~\ref{app:rank-transfer}.

\subsection{The size and rotation structure of a coarse optimal family}
\label{sec:coarse-structure}
The coarse certificate in Section~\ref{sec:coarse-face} suggests replacing an
ambiguous payload by all intervals and root choices reachable through coarse
optima. The following exact counts delimit that representation, even when
all coarse weights are positive and their total mass is linear.

For fixed coarse weights, define the \emph{reachable optimal-interval grammar}
as follows. Start with $[0,n)$. At each reached nonempty interval $[i,j)$,
include every root $r$ attaining its unrestricted coarse optimum, and reach
both children $[i,r)$ and $[r+1,j)$. Identify repeated intervals. A root
triple is $(i,j,r)$, not a complete tree. By optimal substructure, this grammar
contains exactly the intervals and root triples occurring in full coarse
optima: each reached choice can replace the corresponding optimal subtree
without changing the full cost.

\begin{theorem}[Quadratic intervals and cubic root choices]
\label{thm:coarse-grammar}
Let $B=2^k$, $k\geq1$, and $n=6B-1$. Give every coarse key and every coarse
gap weight one. The reachable optimal-interval grammar contains the
$(B+1)^2$ distinct intervals
\begin{equation}\label{eq:coarse-grammar-intervals}
 [B+u,\,2B-1+u+v),\qquad 0\leq u,v\leq B.
\end{equation}
The number of its root triples on just these intervals is exactly
\begin{equation}\label{eq:coarse-grammar-triples}
 (B+1)(B/2+1)^2.
\end{equation}
Consequently the explicit reachable grammar has $\Theta(n^2)$ nonempty
intervals and $\Theta(n^3)$ root triples in the worst case, at coarse total
mass $2n+1$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Uniform mixed cost is $2I+3n$, so the coarse optima are exactly the trees
of minimum internal path length. Take a perfect upper skeleton with
$4B-1$ keys, and expand $2B$ of its $4B$ bottom gaps. Every resulting tree
fills all levels through depth $k+1$ and has its remaining $2B$ keys at depth
$k+2$; hence it has minimum internal path length.

The four subtrees at depth two each begin as a perfect $(B-1)$-key tree
with $B$ expandable bottom gaps. Let their expansion counts be
$x_0,x_1,x_2,x_3\in[0,B]$, with sum $2B$. Set $x_0=u$, $x_1=v$.
The remaining sum $2B-u-v$ is always in $[0,2B]$, so choose
$x_2=\min\{B,2B-u-v\}$ and $x_3=2B-u-v-x_2$.
The second subtree then has exactly the interval in
\eqref{eq:coarse-grammar-intervals}. Its start determines $u$, and its
length determines $v$, proving distinctness and full-tree reachability.

Write its start as $i=B+u$. Its length is $B-1+v$. Every minimum-path-length
tree of that size has a perfect $(B-1)$-key upper skeleton and $v$ bottom
expansions. If $t$ expansions lie in the left half, its root is
$i+B/2-1+t$, where precisely
\[
 \max\{0,v-B/2\}\leq t\leq\min\{v,B/2\}.
\]
Every such $t$ is realizable, and replacing the subtree preserves full
optimality. Thus there are $\min\{v,B-v\}+1$ roots on that interval. Summing
first over $v$ and then over $u$ gives
\[
 (B+1)\sum_{v=0}^{B}(\min\{v,B-v\}+1)
   =(B+1)(B/2+1)^2.
\]
There are at most $n(n+1)/2$ nonempty intervals and
$n(n+1)(n+2)/6$ interval-root triples in any BST recurrence, proving the
matching orders.
\end{proof}

The counts concern a representation prepared from the coarse weights.
They cannot be reduced by discarding coarse-optimal trees on the assumption
that small perturbations will never select them. The following standard
exposing-weight idea makes this precise. Onishi and Hoshi~\cite{onishi-regions}
study optimality regions of key-weighted BSTs and establish that every tree
has a nonempty region; they also distinguish their adjacency from single
rotations. We give the elementary mixed-weight consequence needed here.

\begin{lemma}[Every coarse optimum can be selected uniquely]
\label{lem:coarse-expose}
For any coarse-optimal tree $T$ on $n\geq2$ keys and any $\delta>0$, there
are arbitrarily small positive key perturbations, with all gap perturbations
zero, satisfying \eqref{eq:coarse-certificate} for which $T$ is the unique
fine-weight optimum.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Put $A=n^2+1$, and give key $i$ perturbation
$e_i=\varepsilon A^{-d_T(i)}$, where $d_T(i)$ is its depth in $T$.
In any subtree of $T$, let its root perturbation be $w$.
Every proper descendant has perturbation at most $w/A$.
The perturbation cost of the prescribed subtree is therefore at most
$w+n(n-1)w/A<2w$. Any competing subtree with a different root costs at
least $2w$ just for that root key. Induction for equal roots proves that
$T$ uniquely minimizes perturbation cost among all full trees.
It also minimizes coarse cost, so it uniquely minimizes their positive
scaled sum. Finally $E\leq n\varepsilon$, and taking
$0<\varepsilon<\delta/[n(n-1)]$ gives the certificate. The choice may be
made arbitrarily small.
\end{proof}

For Theorem~\ref{thm:coarse-grammar}, every counted interval and root triple
therefore occurs in a uniquely selected fine optimum for some certified
perturbation of the same positive coarse input. This does not require one
fine input to select all the counted triples simultaneously. Nor does it
force an algorithm to materialize the grammar: a different implicit
representation remains possible.

\paragraph{Root holes and a strict rotation trap.}
Small exact examples rule out two further shortcuts. For
$p=(4,1,4)$ and $q=(1,1,1,1)$, the three root-constrained optimum costs are
$24,25,24$. Knowing the smallest and largest optimal roots therefore does
not imply that all roots between them are optimal.

For a near-uniform rotation example, write $r(L,R)$ for a tree rooted at
zero-based key $r$, and omit the children of a leaf. Let
\[
 T=1\bigl(0,\,3(2,4)\bigr),\qquad
 U=2\bigl(1(0,\varnothing),\,3(\varnothing,4)\bigr).
\]
Give every coarse key and gap weight one, and take fine weights
\begin{equation}\label{eq:coarse-rotation-weights}
 p=(D,D+1,D+1,D,D),\qquad q=(D,D,D,D,D,D),\qquad D>8.
\end{equation}
Here $E=2$ and $(n-1)E=8<D$, so the coarse-family certificate holds.
The coarse optimum is $27$. Direct depth sums give
\[
 C(T)=27D+4,\qquad C(U)=27D+3.
\]
The four single rotations from $T$, respectively at edges
$(1,3)$, $(3,2)$, $(3,4)$ and $(1,0)$, have costs
\[
 27D+5,\qquad29D+3,\qquad29D+5,\qquad33D+6.
\]
Thus $T$ is a strict local minimum under every single rotation, while $U$
is better. The global optimum is exactly $27D+3$: among the six coarse
optima, the perturbation costs are $4,4,4,3,3,5$, and the certificate
excludes every other tree. The six trees correspond to choosing two of
the four bottom gaps of the perfect three-key upper skeleton.
Their coarse-cost-preserving rotation graph has components of sizes two
and four: the two root-$1$/$3$ trees form one edge, while the four root-$2$
trees independently choose each two-key child's orientation. Rotating
the root of a root-$2$ tree leaves the minimum-path-length family.
After dividing \eqref{eq:coarse-rotation-weights} by $D$, these are
arbitrarily small perturbations of strictly positive uniform weights.

\paragraph{Descendant promotions combined with bottom-gap exchange.}
Strict local minima also occur under the combined richer move set.
For nine keys, start with the perfect seven-key upper skeleton and expand
bottom gaps 6 and 7, numbered from zero. Give every outcome coarse weight
one and choose fine weights $p_i=D+e_i$, $q_j=D+f_j$, where
\[
\begin{split}
e&=(2,5,4,4,5,2,0,4,4),\\
f&=(3,4,4,6,8,7,1,1,7,2),\qquad D>584.
\end{split}
\]
The total error is 73, so the certificate holds. All 28 coarse optima
come from expanding two of the eight bottom gaps and have cost 59.
The selected tree has fine cost $59D+219$, whereas expanding gaps 0
and 5 gives the global optimum $59D+212$.
Consider promoting any descendant to the root of any existing subtree,
or exchanging one expanded bottom gap for one unexpanded gap.
Among the resulting trees, the fourteen distinct neighbors still in the
coarse family all cost at least $59D+224$ by direct depth sums.
Every remaining promotion leaves the coarse family and is more expensive
by the certificate. Thus the selected tree is a strict local minimum
under the combined move set. Exact neighbor values and independent
full-tree checks appear in Appendix~\ref{app:adaptive-coarse-grammar}.


The theorem obstructs explicit coarse-family expansion; the five-key
example obstructs descent using only single rotations. Neither is a
lower bound for arbitrary full-OBST algorithms. Independent checks and
complete original-weight controls appear in Appendix~\ref{app:coarse-structure}.

\section{Core-size tightness and further questions}\label{sec:tightness}
Theorem~\ref{thm:ramp-core-lower} rules out an $O(n\,\operatorname{polylog}n)$
bound for all intermediate cores at linear integer mass. The remaining
size question is the $\sqrt{\log n}$ factor between this lower bound and
$O(n\sqrt{W L})$ when $W=n$. A stronger upper bound must respect the
explicit odd-ramp density formula, or a stronger lower bound must force
additional nonzero cells. The family arose from the adversarial search;
its theorem follows from the deletion lemma and the closed-form density,
independently of the search outcomes.

The aggregate bound of Theorem~\ref{thm:aggregate-cores} already removes
one logarithmic factor from construction time by charging individual
outcomes across geometrically spaced layer boundaries. It leaves the
per-layer $\sqrt{\log n}$ gap above unresolved.
Theorem~\ref{thm:local-tile-obstruction} shows why simply removing the
height factor from each tile charge cannot close it: even one weight unit
can cause a logarithmic number of positive entries in one tile, although
Corollary~\ref{cor:one-key-core} bounds its entire layer core linearly.

The bound concerns an explicit density representation. The unrestricted
subquadratic construction problem remains open, as does the possibility
of computing with a more compact representation of these structured dense
cores. The quadratic ruler example already shows why representation size
alone cannot establish a general algorithmic lower bound.

\subsection{A boundary term in the local support charge}
The remaining gap suggests the following open local hypothesis. For an
absolute constant $A$, every accepted early-layer tile $T$ of side $s$
and inclusive outcome span $J_T$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:boundary-tile-hypothesis}
 K(T)\le A\bigl(s+W(J_T)\bigr)
 \qquad\text{for nonnegative integer input weights.}
\end{equation}
Here $K(T)$ counts positive densities, rather than their sum. The side
term accommodates the known one-key obstruction. Integer normalization
is essential: rescaling an input preserves support while changing mass.
The hypothesis remains unproved. The following one-key bound explains
its compatibility with the logarithmic local obstruction.

\begin{lemma}[Single-key square support]\label{lem:one-key-square}
With one positive key and zero gap weights, any $s\times s$ square of
ordinary density cells lying wholly on one side of the capacity line
has at most $1+\lfloor\log_2s\rfloor\le s$ positive entries. The square
need not be maximal or dyadically aligned. The logarithmic dependence
has sharp leading coefficient one.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Use the ruler-pair classification in
Section~\ref{sec:local-tile-obstruction}. Each positive density corresponds
to consecutive multiples $u<v$ of $2^r$, for some $1\le r<h$.
Below capacity the mapping is $i=x-u$, $j=c+x+1-v$; above it the mapping
is $i=x+1-v$, $j=c+x-u$. For a square wholly off capacity, the possible
$u$ and $v$ values occupy ordered disjoint integer intervals $I,J$, each
with at most $s$ values, and gap $g=\min J-\max I\ge2$. Clipping to the
allowed ruler range only shortens these intervals.

Every contributing pair spans a common point between $I$ and $J$.
At each scale, intervals between consecutive multiples have disjoint
interiors, so at most one pair at that scale contributes. Their lengths
lie between $g$ and $g+2s-2$, whose ratio is at most $s$. There are at
most $1+\lfloor\log_2s\rfloor$ powers of two in that range. This also
handles $s=1$. Multiplying the positive key weight preserves support.
Finally Theorem~\ref{thm:local-tile-obstruction} has $s=2^{h-2}$ and
$K(T)=h-3=\log_2s-1$, proving sharpness of the leading logarithmic term.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Exact single-gap densities]\label{lem:unit-gap-density}
Fix $h\ge1$, $C=2^h$, $c=C-1$, and put a unit weight at gap $x$,
with all other weights zero. Ordinary off-capacity densities above
$j-i=c$ vanish. Below capacity put $g=c-(j-i)>0$. The density is one
exactly in the following cases, and zero otherwise:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $x=i$ or $x=j+1$, and $g+1$ is a power of two;
\item $i<x\le j$, $g+2=2^r$, and
$x-i-1\equiv2^{r-1}\pmod{2^r}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For an interval $[i,j)$ containing the gap with $k=j-i\le c$, put
$u=x-i$ and $v=u+c-k=x+c-j$. Its maximal gap reward $F(u,v)$ is the
largest $a$ for which $[u,v]$ contains an aligned integer block
$[z,z+2^a-1]$, with $2^a\mid z$. Its cost is $h-F(u,v)$.
Indeed, the block represents $2^a$ consecutive external slots of a
collapsed perfect subtree, whose $2^a-1$ internal slots are omitted.
The $u$ preceding keys fit exactly when $z\ge u$; the $k-u$ following
keys fit exactly when $z+2^a-1\le u+c-k=v$.
The mixed-slot deletion repair gives a tree with no greater gap depth.
Conversely, a gap at depth $h-a$ in an embedded height-$h$ tree occupies
such an aligned block. This also covers empty intervals, where
$[u,v]=[0,c]$ and $F=h$.

Write $F=\sum_{a=1}^h I_a$, where $I_a$ indicates that an aligned
block of length $d=2^a$ fits. For an interior gap the density is
\[
 F(u,v)+F(u-1,v-1)-F(u,v-1)-F(u-1,v).
\]
For $I_a$ this difference is $-1$ when the outer interval $[u-1,v]$
is exactly one aligned $d$-block, $+1$ when it is exactly two consecutive
$d$-blocks, and zero otherwise. To check every endpoint case, removing
the right endpoint changes $I_a$ only if that endpoint finishes a block
and no earlier complete block is present. Moving the left endpoint
from $u-1$ to $u$ changes this event only if the removed endpoint
starts that same block or its immediate predecessor. These give the
one-block and two-block cases respectively.
The outer interval has length $g+2$. At $g+2=2^r$, the positive
contribution from level $r-1$ cancels the negative contribution from
level $r$ when $u-1\equiv0\pmod{2^r}$; otherwise it survives precisely
when $u-1\equiv2^{r-1}\pmod{2^r}$.

For $x=i$ or $x=j+1$, the endpoint gap cost is
$h-\lfloor\log_2(g+1)\rfloor$. The two contributing interval costs
give density $\lfloor\log_2(g+1)\rfloor-\lfloor\log_2g\rfloor$.
Gaps outside the four intervals contribute zero. Above capacity, every
gap has comparison length $h$, whose interval sums have zero density.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Universal unit-outcome support envelope]
\label{lem:unit-support-envelope}
Define $\mathcal E_h$ on ordinary off-capacity cells by
\[
\begin{array}{ll}
j-i<c:&g=c-(j-i)>0,\quad g+1\text{ or }g+2\text{ is a power of two};\\
j-i>c:&g=(j-i)-c>0,\quad g+1\text{ is a power of two}.
\end{array}
\]
The support of every possible single positive key or gap is contained
in $\mathcal E_h$. Every dyadically aligned square of side $s$, wholly
in the ordinary region and on one side of capacity, contains at most
$2s$ envelope cells; below capacity the bound is $2s-1$.
Maximality is unnecessary. The leading coefficient two is sharp for
the union over all unit outcomes.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The preceding lemma handles gaps. The key ruler-pair classification in
Section~\ref{sec:local-tile-obstruction} gives capacity distance $2^r-1$.
Scaling a single outcome preserves its support.

The claim is immediate for $s=1$. Otherwise write the square as
$[a,a+s)\times[b,b+s)$, with $a,b$ multiples of $s$.
Successive interval lengths have multiplicities $1,2,\ldots,s,\ldots,2,1$.
Below capacity, the smallest gap is $g_0=C-(b-a+s)>0$.
Since $b-a\ge s$, we have $C\ge4s$, so $g_0$ is a positive multiple
of $s$. The gap range is $[g_0,g_0+2s-2]$. A contributing power $d$
with $g=d-1$ or $d-2$ exceeds $s$ and is a multiple of $s$.
Only $d=g_0+s$ or $d=g_0+2s$ can contribute. They cannot both be
powers of two: the first is at least $2s$ and their difference is $s$.
The first contributes diagonals of lengths $s$ and $s-1$; the second
contributes only the final corner. This proves the bound $2s-1$.

Above capacity, $g_0=b-a-s-C+2$, and only $g=d-1$ contributes.
If $C\ge s$, write $g_0=ms+2$; positivity forces $m\ge0$.
For $m\ge1$, contributing powers are at least $2s$, so a window of
width $2s-2$ contains at most two, each contributing at most $s$ cells.
For $m=0$, powers $d\le s$ contribute $d-2$ cells each, and $d=2s$
contributes two. Their sum is $2s-2\log_2s\le2s$.
If $C<s$, write $g_0=ms-C+2$. Since $2\le C\le s/2$, positivity
forces $m\ge1$. Contributing powers are at least $s$, and again at
most two fit in the distance window. Restricting a padded grid to
arbitrary $n$ only removes cells.

For sharpness take $s\ge2$, $n=C=4s$, and
$T=[0,s)\times[2s,3s)$. Its envelope diagonals $j-i=2s$ and $2s+1$
contain $s$ and $s-1$ cells. A cell on the first is realized by a unit
key at one-based position $i+1$; a cell on the second by a unit gap at
$x=i+s+1$, using Lemma~\ref{lem:unit-gap-density}.
The parent meets capacity, so the tile is accepted and maximal.
This attains $2s-1$ for the union over different inputs.
\end{proof}
For early layers $h<L$, the envelope uses $O(L)$ diagonals and therefore
$O(nL)$ cells per layer, or $O(nL^2)$ over all early layers.
Theorem~\ref{thm:off-envelope-interaction} shows that support outside
this envelope still requires accounting for joint outcomes across scales.

\begin{lemma}[Conditional improvement from a boundary charge]
\label{lem:boundary-tile-conditional}
If \eqref{eq:boundary-tile-hypothesis} holds uniformly, then the aggregate
completed core size satisfies
\[
 S=O_A\bigl(nH+(n+W)L^2+n\sqrt W+HWL\bigr).
\]
At $W=O(n)$ this is $O(n^{3/2})$, and the existing Monge construction
would take $O(n^{3/2}\log(n+1))$ arithmetic operations, including full
reconstruction.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
At early layer $h<L$, put $c=2^h-1$. The established tile count and
inclusive-position overlap bounds give at most $24n/s$ side-$s$ tiles
and total span mass at most $60W(1+c/s)$. Combining the hypothesis with
the area bound yields
\[
 K_{h,s}\le\min\{24ns,\ A[24n+60W(1+c/s)]\}.
\]
Separate the constant-per-scale term from $Wc/s$, and sum the geometric
minimum over dyadic scales. Including the exceptional and completion
diagonals gives
\[
 K_h=O_A\bigl((n+W)L+\sqrt{nWc}\bigr).
\]
Now $\sum_{h<L}\sqrt{c_h}=O(\sqrt n)$, so the early layers contribute
$O_A((n+W)L^2+n\sqrt W)$. Late layers retain their proved bound $n+LW$,
giving the claimed aggregate expression. At linear mass $H=O(L)$, and
$nL^2=O(n^{3/2})$. Product, conversion, and succinct reconstruction costs
are $O(S\log(n+1))$.
\end{proof}
This conditional core scale matches the odd-ramp lower bound. Establishing
or refuting \eqref{eq:boundary-tile-hypothesis} is therefore a direct
local route to deciding the remaining logarithmic gap; the main theorem
continues to use the proved height-dependent charge.

\appendix\section{Independent verification and operation counts}\label{app:verification}
The theoretical statements above do not depend on these finite tests.
Independent cubic recurrences, literal tree enumeration, direct original-cost
evaluation, and operation counters provide falsification controls.

The per-outcome overlap audit checks 766 padded dyadic partitions, 4508
scales, and 1,891,752 position/scale incidences, including sizes immediately
below and above powers of two. An independent all-root recurrence checks
290 mixed-weight inputs: 1977 original/padded layer equalities, 452,644
local residual bounds, and 28,013 weighted tiles. It examines 685,551
dense diagnostic states and 8,636,426 root candidates. The tests include
all binary inputs through three keys, zero mass, uniform and random mixed
weights, geometric weights, and mass at either endpoint gap. The executed
source and operation counts are recorded in
\texttt{aggregate\_core\_audit.json}. These dense verification operations
are separate from the sparse construction's theoretical work bound.

Reproduction code and source-hashed records are available in the
\href{https://github.com/falseywinchnet/papers_please/tree/codex/obst-research/obst}{accompanying repository}.
The filenames below identify those records; they are not definitions needed
by the proofs.

\subsection{Implemented condensed constructors}\label{sec:legacy-19-5}
The compact path is now implemented in Python and independently in C++.
The product algorithm is the published compression, balanced core split,
staircase merge, and decompression method of~\cite{coremonge}. We credit
that primitive separately from the OBST reduction. A matrix stores its top
row, left column, and positive density triples. Two linear bucket passes
canonicalize the triples. Boundary extraction uses one-dimensional sweeps;
the product merge uses adjacent-entry recomputation along its staircase.
Each relevant core row or column is scanned only a constant number of times
per merge. None of these operations enumerates the full interval table.

\paragraph{Exact early stopping.}
The constructors compare consecutive completed layers by their top rows
and canonical core triples; the left column is fixed. Equality is therefore
equality of the whole table. A fixed point satisfies the ordinary OBST
recurrence with zero diagonal. Induction on interval length proves that
this recurrence has a unique solution, namely $C$. Stopping at such a fixed
point is exact. Equality of the single full-interval cost is not used.
Retained layer witnesses reconstruct a complete optimum: any unfinished
nonempty terminal would contribute at least $M>HW$, exceeding the
cost of an optimal tree of height at most $H$. All charges are nonnegative.
Reconstruction uses an explicit stack and visits every original key.

\paragraph{Implementation costs and arithmetic.}
Both implementations retain ordinary integer coordinate maps and recursive
witness nodes. Their witness storage is bounded by
$O(H(n+K)\log(n+1))$ words, where
$K=O(n+\min\{n^2,n\sqrt{WL}\})$ bounds the completed cores. This can
exceed the succinct storage bound in Theorem~\ref{thm:penalty-reduction}
by a logarithmic factor. Compression bounds recursive dimensions by core
size, and the core count halves along a recursive branch, so the time bound
is preserved. No succinct rank/select implementation is claimed.





The selected perturbation sweep certified amplitude 8 around 1,000 at
$n=256$, amplitude 4 at $n=512$, and amplitude 1 at $n=1,024$; larger tested
amplitudes at those sizes failed. These are seeded observations, not
universal thresholds. Ordinary random inputs failed the certificate.
Sizes 384, 768, and 1,000 deliberately skip the shortcut because their
minimum-$I$ shape space exceeds the logarithmic width budget.




\subsection{Exact bounded-excess construction by dyadic intervals}
\paragraph{Validation, failed precursor, and prior work.}
The implementation passed $1{,}200$ restricted-optimum comparisons against
all shapes through eight keys with budgets $0,\ldots,4$, and $3{,}000$
full mixed-weight checks against cubic DP. It separately checks direct
tree costs, both residual-bound implementations, and exact fallback.
An additional $104$ family checks include zeros, geometric weights,
spikes, and key-only/gap-only controls. The seeded size sweep is retained.
A precursor substituting ordered depth bounds into the old certificate
passed $480$ exhaustive checks but rescued none of $24$ near-uniform
fixtures at its $32$-iteration budget. This negative experiment did not
prove optimal multiplier search useless; its cost and the failed
perfect-tree hypothesis motivated expanding the candidate class.

On the $1{,}023$-key periodic fixture with weights $16$ and $17$, the
excess-two DP finds cost $312{,}154$ and excludes other shapes with bound
$312{,}185$. It uses $27{,}570$ states, $137{,}835$ transition checks,
$6{,}052$ sorting comparisons, and $1{,}022$ merge comparisons, versus
Knuth's $523{,}776$ states and $1{,}046{,}532$ root candidates.
At $n=1{,}024$, a seeded amplitude-four perturbation uses $48{,}003$ states
and $240{,}000$ transition checks, plus $13{,}784$ sorting and $1{,}264$
merge comparisons. It certifies cost $19{,}477{,}996$.



The dyadic/prefix-code viewpoint and sequential frontier DP are established
ideas. Becker~\cite{becker} gives a sequential mixed-weight DP for a
near-minimal \emph{height} restriction in quadratic time. The band above
uses excess \emph{total unweighted path length}, a different restriction;
it does not improve Becker's stated problem. Priority of this particular
potential, band bound, and exclusion combination remains unestablished.


\subsection{A split-height construction covering every input size}
\paragraph{Exact verification and operation evidence.}
The harness passed 450 exhaustive weighted height-class checks through
eight keys, 44 exhaustive tall-path minima, 2,000 cubic-checked mixed inputs
with complete fallback (917 successful certificates), and 104 additional
adversarial-family inputs. An independent all-root height recurrence checked
520 candidates and 261,482 stored short-table entries; the same 520 cases
also compared SMAWK with a retained divide-and-conquer matrix search.
The larger sweep has 36 fixtures at three height budgets each. Every
near-uniform fixture certified, including previously skipped sizes
384, 768, 1,000, and 1,536. All random, geometric, and gap-spike fixtures
failed the certificate, sometimes despite an optimal restricted candidate.

At $n=1{,}000$, the minimum-height candidate and full optimum are both
$18{,}973{,}413$, below the exclusion bound $18{,}974{,}193$. The optimizer
uses 117,453 short states, 220,483 short root candidates, 84,937 genuine
matrix queries, 10,268 padding queries, 16,016 prefix scans, and 5,630 coarse
transitions. The bound adds 10,648 sorting and 1,335 merge comparisons,
plus 1,000 contractions; all keys are reconstructed. Knuth uses 500,500
states and 1,002,162 root candidates. These distinct operation types are
reported separately, without treating them as equal-cost instructions.


\subsection{A variable interval frontier for exact full construction}
\paragraph{Independent verification.}
The harness passed 960 weighted exhaustive-shape comparisons through
eight keys on arbitrary nondecreasing contours, including infeasible
classes. It checked 914 interval costs independently and 90,193 top
minima by brute search. Another 2,400 full solves, using both modes on
1,200 inputs through 40 keys, matched cubic DP and reconstructed costs.
All 24,600 internal subtrees in the cubic-optimal trees satisfied
Lemma~\ref{lem:mass}. Families include zeros, ties, geometric variants,
spikes, and key-only and gap-only controls. A further 56 fixtures through
2,048 keys matched Knuth in both modes, including ordinary random inputs
that failed the preceding height certificates.

At 2,048 random keys, the adaptive version chose depth six and returned
cost 21,053,524. It used 309,261 interval cells and 616,642 root candidates,
versus Knuth's 2,098,176 cells and 4,215,527 candidates. Additional work
included 24,035 contour mass checks across seven parameter depths,
1,245,431 genuine matrix queries, 149,319 padding queries, 131,136 scans
of each row/column type, and 107,859 coarse transitions. The uniform
version stored 337,550 cells. On log-uniform weights the corresponding
cell counts were 308,487 versus 431,951; on a single heavy key they were
1,048,576 versus 1,573,376. Geometric inputs selected the full table.
All operation categories and parameter levels are retained separately.


\subsection{Dominant-gap separators and local mass frontiers}
\paragraph{Verification and complete work accounting.}
The harness exhaustively checks 22,140 weight assignments in $\{0,1,2\}$
through four keys against every tree shape. It checks 3,211 qualifying gap
certificates and their cost identities, 2,036 endpoint certificates, and
380 key certificates. A further 3,280 full solves, using both frontier and
Knuth terminal strategies on 1,640 inputs through 40 keys, match independent
cubic costs and direct tree evaluation. Inputs include empty, zero, tied,
geometric, spike, key-only and gap-only controls. Both strategies and the
direct global frontier also match Knuth on 40 fixtures through 2,048 keys.

At $n=2048$, the direct and local frontier interval counts are:
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrr}
\toprule
Fixture & direct frontier & local frontier & terminal components\\
\midrule
Random & 308,115 & 308,115 & 1\\
Near-uniform & 309,290 & 309,290 & 1\\
Geometric & 2,098,176 & 0 & 0\\
Heavy key & 1,048,576 & 153,660 & 2\\
Heavy gap & 1,047,552 & 153,582 & 2\\
Random background, heavy gap & 1,047,552 & 153,362 & 2\\
Hierarchical gaps & 522,242 & 22,333 & 64\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Cell counts do not include all work. On hierarchical gaps, 63 gap separators
require 254 endpoint checks, 6,142 index-build comparisons and 1,476 range
query comparisons. Terminal solves use 42,885 root candidates, 39,284 genuine
top-matrix queries, 6,261 padding queries, 8,122 contour mass checks, 6,952
scans of each row/column type, and 1,792 coarse transitions. Their 1,922 keys
are reconstructed and relabeled. On the heavy-gap fixture, 305,118 terminal
root candidates and 1,214,092 genuine top queries accompany the reduced
interval table. All counter categories are retained; metadata such as local
selected depths and actual heights are summed over terminals, not reported
as a global tree height. Geometric input uses 2,048 endpoint separators and
4,095 endpoint checks, with no index or terminal work.


\subsection{Joint heavy-outcome cores}
\paragraph{Independent checks and operation counts.}
Every nonempty selected-outcome subset and every tree shape through four keys
were examined. In all 1,937 non-core-closed cases a core-closed tree has
componentwise no-greater selected depths and at least one strict improvement.
On 22,140 inputs with weights in $\{0,1,100\}$, all 17,054 qualifying
nontrivial certificates match the optimum over every shape and the composition
identity. Another 2,640 full solves on 1,320 inputs through 32 keys, with both
kernels, match independent cubic DP and reconstructed costs. On 520 small
inputs the threshold sweep matches direct enumeration of every threshold and
payload partition. Both kernels also match Knuth and the previous separator
constructor on 30 larger fixtures through 2,048 keys.

At $n=2048$, the following cell counts include both core and payload solves,
or the complete fallback when certification fails:
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrr}
\toprule
Fixture & previous separator & joint core/frontier & core keys\\
\midrule
Random & 309,201 & 309,201 & ---\\
Near-uniform & 309,290 & 309,290 & ---\\
Two heavy gaps, unit background & 2,098,176 & 10 & 4\\
Two heavy gaps, random background & 2,098,176 & 306,506 & 4\\
45 heavy gaps & 304,486 & 23,965 & 90\\
45 moderate gaps & 304,486 & 23,695 & 90\\
45 mixed heavy outcomes & 305,421 & 24,016 & 68\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The moderate-gap fixture sets each selected weight to exactly
$(H+L)W_0=42{,}395{,}178$, with $H=9$, $L=12$, and $W_0=2{,}018{,}818$.
The old size-based condition would require $(90+12)W_0$ and fails.
Work includes 63,549 sorting comparisons, 2,047 Fibonacci additions, 3,024
height checks, 1,958 activations, 1,912 unions, and 1,511 find steps. Kernels
use 45,365 root candidates, 116,338 genuine top queries, 23,904 padding queries,
12,336 contour checks, and 17,488 scans each for rows and columns. All 2,048
keys are reconstructed. Local kernel metadata such as heights and chosen
depths are sums across calls, not global tree heights. The uniform two-gap
case still pays selection, 2,044 payload-node constructions and relabelings,
and ten core DP cells; it does not perform only ten total operations.


\subsection{Joint heavy-outcome cores}
\paragraph{Full construction beyond the quadratic-table scale.}
On the unit-background two-heavy-gap family, full trees were constructed at
$n=512,2048,8192,32768,65536$. Each exact cost matched an independent cubic
solve of the four-key instance
\[
 p=(1,1,1,1),\qquad q=(1,n^3,2n-7,n^3,1),
\]
plus $2I_*(n-4)+3(n-4)$ for the uniform payload, and direct evaluation of the
full reconstructed tree. The uniform closed form was separately checked against
cubic DP through 16 keys. At $n=65536$, the exact cost is
$1{,}407{,}374{,}885{,}846{,}941$. The construction used ten core DP cells and fifteen core root candidates.
It also used 196,799 sorting comparisons, 65,535 Fibonacci additions, 65,535
height checks, 65,532 activations, 65,531 unions, 65,529 find steps, and 196,609
uniformity comparisons. All 65,532 payload keys were constructed and relabeled,
and the four core keys were reconstructed.




\subsection{Residual-cost certificates for heavy-outcome cores}
\paragraph{Independent exact checks.}
On all 22,140 assignments in $\{0,1,4\}$ through four keys, every one of
17,054 nontrivial threshold cases passed the excluded-class lower-bound
check against all tree shapes. There were 13,669 passing witness tests
and 15,181 passing exact-candidate tests, including 1,512 cases rescued
by refinement. Another 14,912 excluded-tree checks used arbitrary selected
subsets, weights in $\{1,3\}$, and up to three keys. These latter selections
need not be weight thresholds.

Both kernel choices and both refinement settings passed 6,560 full solves
on 1,640 inputs through 40 keys against independent cubic DP and direct
tree evaluation. All 1,640 candidate portfolios matched direct enumeration
of the two representatives in each core-size band. Forty-eight larger
fixtures and nine additional fixtures with independently varying positive
key and gap weights matched Knuth through 2,048 keys. The varying backgrounds
are integers in $[1,1000]$ and their selected outcomes have weight $4000n$;
the unit-background success rows below use weight $4n$. These are finite tests,
not an assertion that every such random instance passes certification.

At $n=2048$, selected operation categories are:
\begin{center}\small

\midrule

45 linear-weight gaps, unit background & 304,486 & 1,394 & 4,095\\
45 mixed outcomes, unit background & 305,421 & 1,250 & 2,346\\
Two gaps, varying background & 951,851 & 307,541 & 30,941\\

New intervals count all executed full-weight kernel cells, including fallback,
but exclude the separately displayed protected-core proof cells. The proof
column sums all attempted cores. Selected-state metadata is not counted again
as executed cells. Sorting, profile scans, lower-bound contractions, witness
construction, kernel matrix work, and reconstruction have separate raw
counters; this table is not a total instruction count. The random control
fails all ten witnesses and pays additional proof work before fallback.


\subsection{Residual-cost certificates for heavy-outcome cores}
\paragraph{Full large constructions and a remaining certificate gap.}
For every $n=2^h$, $3\le h\le16$, the full $\theta=4n$ construction matches
Equation~\eqref{eq:linear-gap-optimum}, direct evaluation of the entire
output tree, and an independent four-key cubic core plus the uniform
payload formula. At $n=65,536$, the optimum is $3,604,381$. The proof core
and full-weight core each use ten cells and fifteen root candidates. Other
counts include 196,799 selection-sort comparisons, 131,204 bound-sort
comparisons, 65,536 bound contractions, 65,535 two-queue comparisons,
65,532 activations, 65,531 unions, and 65,529 find steps. All 65,536 witness
keys are constructed and evaluated, then all output payload and core keys
are constructed. The lower bound is $3,604,482$, giving a certificate margin
of 101.
The previous method was only planned at this size: it rejects the core and
selects 146,498,625 frontier cells at depth 11.

Failure remains informative. At $n=2048$ and selected gap weight $2n$, the
exact core-closed cost $71,611$ equals the unrestricted optimum, but the
exclusion lower bound is only $67,586$; even refinement cannot certify it.
At weight $n$, the closed candidate costs $61,371$ while the unrestricted
optimum is $59,332$. Thus some failures reflect a weak certificate and
others reflect a genuinely suboptimal proposed structure. Improving the
bound must preserve this distinction.

Raw results and source hashes are in \texttt{core\_exclusion*.json} under
\texttt{obst/results}. An initial full-harness failure was a metadata bug:
list-valued fallback fields were passed to arithmetic counter update. Direct
assignment fixed it, and the final full harness was rerun. A completed
single-threshold experiment was superseded after it missed several useful
cores; the published selector and every final result use the bounded
portfolio. The general subquadratic objective remains unresolved.


\subsection{Coupled two-gap skeleton certificates}
\paragraph{Verification and measured limits}\label{sec:legacy-13-2}
Independent forest enumeration passed 11,880 checks. Exhaustive small
weight inputs numbered 24,188, with 51,654 pair-exclusion checks and
separate checks for both first and tail lower bounds. Among these are
3,288 exact closed candidates accepted by the coupled bound but rejected
by $B_{\rm basic}$. Complete construction passed 6,560 comparisons with
independent cubic DP, covering both payload kernels and refinement modes.
Larger tests at $n=128,512,2048$ include random, near-uniform, geometric,
single-spike, adjacent-pair, interior-pair, and varying-weight inputs.
Neither favorable examples nor these exhaustive finite checks replace
the preceding proofs. Raw counts and source hashes are stored in
\texttt{pair\_skeleton*.json}.

The nonuniform-family harness checks 31 placement/size combinations,
including clustered, spread, and random placements; 28 are compared with
Knuth and eight with cubic DP. All satisfy the exact residual formula
and the proved certificate margins. Fourteen unit-background cases
extend through $n=65,536$. At $n=2048$ with unit keys and selected gap
weight $2n$, the earlier bound $67,586$ is replaced by $71,619$, which
certifies the exact cost $71,611$. At gap weight $n$, the closed
candidate remains suboptimal and is correctly rejected.

At $n=16,384$ the seeded nonuniform instance has 1,023 doubled key
weights. Full construction returns cost $767,240$, with witness
$768,946$, first bound $780,208$, and tail bound $795,651$.
Its 7,758,912 short states, 3,922,465 top states, and 20 total core
states give 11,681,397 DP states versus 134,225,920 in a full Knuth
table. Parameter-selection metadata is not counted a second time.
There are also 42,351,013 top-matrix queries, 15,502,473 short-root
candidates, 261,181 forest sorting comparisons, 49,143 forest merges,
and 256,673 contour mass checks. The complete tree is reconstructed
and its cost evaluated directly. The largest instance is certified by the theorem and construction,
not compared with an independently executed quadratic table.





The chief unresolved issue is how to retain useful path-union information
when no constant-size core can be certified. The present method does
not bound the number of necessary boundary states for arbitrary mixed
input. Its general worst-case construction remains quadratic.


\subsection{An ordered depth-chain lower bound}
\paragraph{Preliminary strength and cost.}
At $n=256$, normalized gaps $(\OPT-\mathrm{LB})/W$ for the chain bound
versus order-free Huffman were 0.15003 versus 0.22080 on a random mixed
instance, and 0.00873 versus 0.49294 on an alternating instance.
However, 300 ascent iterations used 1,082,368 key-depth cells including
initialization, compared with 32,896 interval states for Knuth.
The unique-root variant uses two passes and still leaves 254 of 256
potentially improving roots on the random instance, and 255 on the
alternating instance. Only two survive on the gap-spike instance.
These results identify a lower-bound component and its limitations;
they do not establish a faster general exact solver.


\subsection{Exact depth ordering and an arbitrary-window obstruction}
\paragraph{Independent verification.}
The harness enumerated all 873,612 sequences in $\{1,\ldots,n\}^n$ for
$1\le n\le7$. The characterization accepted exactly the independently
generated BST depth sequences: respectively 1, 2, 5, 14, 42, 132, and 429.
The stack recognizer agreed with direct interval scans on every sequence.
Width3 and exact flow already admit four invalid sequences at $n=6$ and
34 at $n=7$. The positive family was checked at $t=16$ for all 127 widths
$2\le w\le128$: exact costs, window validity, zero flow, and the tree-cost
lower bound all passed. Knuth and direct tree evaluation checked every
instance; independent cubic DP also checked $w\le24$.
Another 120 cases combine $2\le w\le16$ with forced prefix depths
$1\le D\le8$. Cubic DP, Knuth, direct costs, and inspection of the optimal
prefix verify the exact additive identity and preserved strict gap.


\subsection{Endpoint responses: insufficiency and representation size}
\paragraph{Independent checks and remaining question.}
The exact-arithmetic harness checks 875 response queries against cubic DP
on 175 inputs through seven keys. Rational half-plane clipping checks
exposed pieces; 725 retained planes with positive endpoint weights satisfy
the Fibonacci bound. The geometric family passes 408 hinge queries,
including thresholds and neighboring integers; 1,496 two-parameter probes
through 33 keys; 204 full cubic comparisons; 30 all-shape checks that
every optimum has the prescribed depth pair; and 680 strict endpoint
inequalities. The product checks execute 887,808 Knuth root candidates.
Another 304 queries expose all outer-root pieces, and 384 queries test
arbitrary parameters and the global transition threshold. All 2,184
factored complete constructions agree with Knuth and direct evaluation,
and construct exactly $n$ keys. Full-quadrant envelopes through nine
keys have 5, 10, 17, and 26 pieces, agreeing with the proved formula.
The factored constructions use 14,492 threshold comparisons, 8,736
outer-minimum comparisons, and construct 52,232 keys in total; these
categories exclude the independently executed reference solvers.

The collision search found an initial surrounding context with outer
weights 16; reducing them to seven preserves the one-unit optimum
difference. The published certificate independently checks domination
of discarded planes and enumerates both full eight-key tree sets.
It does not depend on the clipping implementation or sampled parameters.
Code, raw results, and the harness source hash are in
\texttt{boundary\_response.py/json}.

\paragraph{Legal-domain collision and signed recovery.}\label{app:signed-boundary}
The independent recovery audit checks 800 integer inputs: all binary mixed
vectors through four keys and seeded inputs through 24 keys, including
geometric, uniform, gap-only, key-only, and zero weights. It compares 3968
signed value queries against a fresh all-root recurrence using 1,926,832
root candidates, with negative endpoint weights permitted. Literal shape
evaluation supplies 107,608 further signed cost controls. The boundary-path
classification has 19,184 shape controls and 2792 unique-chain checks.
All recovered gap vectors agree exactly, and each instance checks the
query-count and rational-denominator bounds.

The whole legal quadrant is certified by two complete intercept tables and
18 exact domination checks. Independent enumeration evaluates all 2860
ordered trees across the two eight-key contexts and agrees with the
root-conditioned costs. The signed absolute probe $(0,-1)$ is checked by
the original recurrence. Twelve larger recoveries reach 65,536 keys using
the separately proved chain-line oracle. Random and uniform instances at
that size use 65,536 value queries each; the all-zero instance uses three.
These larger rows verify complete recovery from the chain representation;
they have no independent large signed-DP oracle. The result record retains
counts, the exact collision, verification scopes, and five source hashes.
The recovery theorem bounds calls to its specified oracle and does not
provide a faster general OBST solver.
